#tags column:3
#notetype:Basic
#deck:AI and LLM Scientist Handbook
[Q1] What does cosine similarity measure, and when does it equal the dot product?	The angle between two vectors, ignoring their length. It equals the dot product when both vectors are L2-normalised.	bank level::basic
[Q2] What is the SVD and why does it matter for ML?	Any matrix factors as UΣVᵀ; keeping the top k singular values gives the best rank-k approximation. It underlies PCA, matrix factorisation and the low-rank idea behind LoRA.	bank level::basic
[Q3] State Bayes' rule and give an ML use.	P(A|B) = P(B|A)P(A)/P(B). Naive Bayes classifiers; turning a test's sensitivity and base rate into the probability of fraud given a flag.	bank level::basic
[Q4] MLE vs MAP?	MLE picks parameters maximising the likelihood of the data; MAP adds a prior and maximises the posterior. L2 regularisation is MAP with a Gaussian prior; L1 with a Laplace prior.	bank level::intermediate
[Q5] Why is cross-entropy the loss for classification?	It is the negative log-likelihood of the labels under the model's predicted distribution, so minimising it is maximum likelihood; it also gives well-behaved gradients (p − y) with softmax or sigmoid.	bank level::intermediate
[Q6] What is KL divergence and where does it appear in LLM work?	A non-symmetric measure of how one distribution differs from another. It appears in RLHF (penalty keeping the policy close to the reference model), distillation (matching teacher distributions), and variational methods.	bank level::intermediate
[Q7] Explain the chain rule's role in training neural networks.	Backpropagation applies the chain rule to compute the gradient of the loss with respect to every parameter, layer by layer from the output back.	bank level::basic
[Q8] What is entropy, intuitively?	The average surprise of a distribution: high when outcomes are uncertain, zero when one outcome is certain. Decision trees use it to measure impurity.	bank level::intermediate
[Q9] Define a 95% confidence interval correctly.	A procedure that, over repeated samples, produces intervals containing the true value 95% of the time; not a 95% probability for this specific interval.	bank level::basic ch::2
[Q10] What is the difference between type I and type II errors?	Type I: a false positive (claiming an effect that is not there), probability α. Type II: a false negative, probability β; power is 1 − β.	bank level::basic ch::2
[Q11] How does sample size depend on the minimum detectable effect?	Inversely with its square: halving the MDE needs four times the sample.	bank level::basic ch::2
[Q12] What is CUPED?	Variance reduction using a pre-experiment covariate: Y − θ(X − mean X) with θ = cov(Y,X)/var(X); variance falls by 1 − ρ² without bias.	bank level::intermediate ch::2
[Q13] Why is peeking at A/B results a problem?	Repeated significance checks inflate the false positive rate far above α; use a fixed horizon or a sequential method built for interim looks.	bank level::intermediate ch::2
[Q14] What is sample ratio mismatch and what do you do?	The observed traffic split differs significantly from the design; stop interpreting results and find the assignment or logging bug.	bank level::intermediate ch::2
[Q15] Bonferroni vs Benjamini-Hochberg?	Bonferroni controls the chance of any false positive (strict); BH controls the expected share of false discoveries (more power), better for many metrics or segments.	bank level::intermediate ch::2
[Q16] How do you test a change to courier dispatch?	Not by user randomisation, because arms share couriers. Switchbacks over city × time slots, or geo experiments, analysed at the slot level with clustered errors and carryover buffers.	bank level::senior ch::2
[Q17] An A/B test shows +4% but leadership doubts it. What do you check?	SRM, data quality, novelty over time, segment consistency, power and the winner's curse, interference, guardrails, and whether the effect replicates in a holdout or follow-up test.	bank level::senior ch::2
[Q18] What assumption does difference-in-differences rely on?	Parallel trends: without treatment, treated and control groups would have changed alike; check pre-period trends.	bank level::intermediate ch::2
[Q19] Bias vs variance?	Bias: error from too simple a model (underfitting). Variance: error from sensitivity to the training sample (overfitting). Regularisation, data and model choice trade them.	bank level::basic ch::3
[Q20] Why can't you use the test set to choose hyperparameters?	It then becomes part of training decisions and no longer gives an unbiased estimate.	bank level::basic ch::3
[Q21] L1 or L2 regularisation for feature selection?	L1, because it drives some weights exactly to zero.	bank level::basic ch::3
[Q22] Name four types of leakage.	Target-derived features, future information in time-based features, preprocessing fitted on all data, and the same entity across train and test.	bank level::intermediate ch::3
[Q23] How would you cross-validate a model predicting next week's demand?	Walk-forward (time-based) folds with a gap equal to the horizon and point-in-time features.	bank level::intermediate ch::3
[Q24] How does Adam differ from SGD with momentum?	Adam adds per-parameter adaptive step sizes from a running average of squared gradients, with bias correction. Each coordinate moves by about η·m̂/√v̂, close to η·sign(gradient) when the gradient is consistent, so parameters with tiny gradients (norm gains, rare embedding rows) still move. AdamW decouples weight decay: it shrinks weights by ηλ directly, instead of adding λw to the gradient, where Adam's 1/√v̂ scaling would weaken the decay on exactly the parameters with large gradients. Transformers favour Adam because gradient scale and curvature differ by orders of magnitude between embeddings, attention, MLPs and norms, so any single SGD learning rate is too large for some blocks and too small for others. Cost: two fp32 moments, 8 extra bytes per parameter. Follow-up: "Does SGD ever win?" On many vision CNNs it generalises as well or better, with less memory.	bank level::intermediate ch::3
[Q25] How do you handle a categorical feature with 50,000 values?	Out-of-fold smoothed target encoding, hashing, frequency encoding, or learned embeddings in a neural network; CatBoost handles it natively.	bank level::intermediate ch::3
[Q26] What do you do first with heavy class imbalance?	Choose the right metric (PR-AUC, recall at a precision), then class weights and threshold tuning; resample only training folds; correct probabilities afterwards.	bank level::intermediate ch::3
[Q27] Offline AUC 0.99. Your reaction?	Suspect leakage: inspect top features, timestamps, splits and duplicates; compare with simple baselines.	bank level::senior ch::3
[Q28] When should a problem not be solved with ML?	When rules suffice, labels or feedback do not exist, errors are unacceptable and unexplainable, or nobody will act on predictions.	bank level::senior ch::3
[Q29] How does a decision tree choose a split?	It tries features and thresholds and picks the split with the largest reduction in impurity (Gini, entropy or variance).	bank level::basic ch::40
[Q30] Random forest vs gradient boosting?	Forests average deep, decorrelated trees in parallel (variance reduction); boosting adds shallow trees sequentially fitting the gradient (bias reduction), usually more accurate when tuned.	bank level::basic ch::40
[Q31] Why does a small learning rate help gradient boosting?	Shrinkage regularises: each tree corrects a little, and with early stopping the ensemble generalises better at the cost of more trees.	bank level::intermediate ch::40
[Q32] Which models need feature scaling?	Linear and logistic regression with regularisation, SVMs, kNN, k-means, PCA and neural networks; trees do not.	bank level::intermediate ch::40
[Q33] What is the kernel trick?	Computing dot products in a high-dimensional feature space via a kernel function, so linear methods learn non-linear boundaries.	bank level::intermediate ch::40
[Q34] How do you choose k in k-means?	Elbow, silhouette, stability, and usefulness for the task; with k-means++ initialisation and restarts.	bank level::intermediate ch::40
[Q35] When would you choose logistic regression over LightGBM in production?	Interpretability or regulation, very small data, critical calibration, extreme latency limits, or when the accuracy gain does not justify complexity.	bank level::senior ch::40
[Q36] Why do trees still beat deep learning on most tabular data?	They handle heterogeneous, irregular features and uninformative features well, need little tuning, and deep nets' smoothness bias fits tabular targets poorly; deep models win with huge data, sequences or high-cardinality IDs.	bank level::senior ch::40
[Q37] Precision vs recall?	Precision: share of flagged that are positive. Recall: share of positives that are flagged.	bank level::basic ch::4
[Q38] What does ROC-AUC mean?	The probability a random positive scores above a random negative.	bank level::basic ch::4
[Q39] Why prefer PR-AUC for rare positives?	ROC-AUC stays high when false positives are many but small relative to the huge negative class; PR-AUC exposes poor precision.	bank level::intermediate ch::4
[Q40] What is calibration and how do you fix it?	Predicted probabilities matching observed rates; fix with Platt scaling, isotonic regression or temperature scaling on held-out data.	bank level::intermediate ch::4
[Q41] MAE, RMSE or WAPE for demand forecasting?	WAPE for aggregate accuracy across items of different volumes; MAE for interpretability; RMSE when large misses are costly; always report bias.	bank level::intermediate ch::4
[Q42] Micro vs macro F1?	Micro pools all decisions (dominated by frequent classes); macro averages per-class F1 (rare classes count equally).	bank level::intermediate ch::4
[Q43] How do you compare two models' AUCs fairly?	Paired bootstrap over the independent unit (users, queries) on the same test set, or DeLong's test; report the interval of the difference.	bank level::senior ch::4
[Q44] Offline metric up, online flat: list causes.	Proxy mismatch, different candidate sets, leakage, train-serve skew, feedback-loop bias in logs, low power, or opposing segment effects.	bank level::senior ch::4
[Q45] Why do neural networks need non-linear activations?	Without them, stacked layers collapse into one linear function.	bank level::basic ch::5_6
[Q46] Why ReLU (or GELU) instead of sigmoid in hidden layers?	Sigmoid saturates, making gradients vanish in deep networks; ReLU-family activations keep gradients alive and are cheap.	bank level::basic ch::5_6
[Q47] Why does initialisation matter? Name a scheme.	Bad scales make activations and gradients explode or vanish through layers. Xavier/Glorot for tanh, He/Kaiming for ReLU keep variance roughly constant.	bank level::intermediate ch::5_6
[Q48] Batch norm vs layer norm?	Batch norm normalises each feature across the batch (CNNs, stable large batches); layer norm normalises across features per example (transformers, variable lengths, small batches).	bank level::intermediate ch::5_6
[Q49] What does dropout do?	Randomly zeroes activations in training, preventing co-adaptation; acts like an ensemble; disabled at inference (with scaling handled in training).	bank level::intermediate ch::5_6
[Q50] Your loss is NaN after a few steps. What do you check?	Learning rate too high, missing gradient clipping, log of zero or division by zero, fp16 overflow (use bf16 or loss scaling), bad input data.	bank level::intermediate ch::5_6
[Q51] The model does not learn at all. How do you debug?	Overfit a single batch first; check labels and inputs line up; verify loss and output shapes; check the learning rate; confirm optimizer.zero_grad(), model.train() and that gradients flow (no detached tensors).	bank level::intermediate ch::5_6
[Q52] What does model.eval() change?	Disables dropout and makes batch norm use running statistics; it does not turn off gradients (use torch.no_grad() for that).	bank level::basic ch::5_6
[Q53] What is mixed-precision training?	Computing in bf16 or fp16 while keeping critical values (master weights, reductions) in fp32; faster and smaller with near-identical results.	bank level::intermediate ch::5_6
[Q54] What is a learning-rate warm-up and why use it?	Start with a small learning rate and increase it over the first steps, avoiding unstable early updates when Adam's statistics are poor; standard for transformers.	bank level::intermediate ch::5_6
[Q55] How does word2vec learn embeddings?	Predicting context words from a word (skip-gram) or the reverse, with negative sampling; words in similar contexts get similar vectors.	bank level::basic ch::8_9
[Q56] Why did LSTMs replace vanilla RNNs, and why did transformers replace LSTMs?	LSTM gates ease vanishing gradients over long sequences; transformers removed sequential computation, allowing parallel training and direct long-range attention.	bank level::intermediate ch::8_9
[Q57] What is BPE tokenisation?	Start from characters or bytes and repeatedly merge the most frequent pair into a new token, giving a sub-word vocabulary that handles rare words.	bank level::basic ch::8_9
[Q58] Why do tokenisers matter for cost and multilingual quality?	Cost and context are counted in tokens; languages under-represented in the tokeniser's training (often Arabic, among others) need more tokens per word, so they are slower, costlier and often lower quality.	bank level::intermediate ch::8_9
[Q59] Write the attention formula.	softmax(QKᵀ/√dₖ + mask)V.	bank level::basic ch::10
[Q60] Why the √dₖ?	Keeps dot products from growing with dimension and saturating softmax. If the components of q and k are independent with mean 0 and variance 1, q·k is a sum of d_k products each of variance 1, so Var(q·k) = d_k and its standard deviation is √d_k, about 11 at d_k = 128. Dividing by √d_k brings the logits back to unit variance. Without it, the largest of 8 softmax weights averages about 0.9 instead of about 0.36, so attention is nearly one-hot at initialisation and its gradients are tiny. Follow-up: the scaling fixes initialisation only; logits can still grow during training, which QK-norm addresses (Chain 1).	bank level::basic ch::10
[Q61] What does each part of a transformer block do?	Attention mixes information across positions; the feed-forward network transforms each position; residuals and normalisation keep deep training stable.	bank level::intermediate ch::10
[Q62] What is RoPE?	Rotary position embeddings rotate queries and keys by position-dependent angles so attention depends on relative distance.	bank level::intermediate ch::10
[Q63] What is the KV cache?	Stored keys and values of previous tokens, per layer, so each new token computes only its own key and value and attends to the cached ones instead of recomputing the prefix. Size = 2 (K and V) × layers × KV heads × head dim × tokens × bytes per value × batch. For a Llama-3-8B-like model (32 layers, 8 KV heads under GQA, head dim 128, bf16) that is 2 × 32 × 8 × 128 × 2 bytes = 128 KiB per token, so 16 GiB for one 128k-token sequence, as much as the weights; with 32 KV heads (no GQA) it would be 64 GiB. That is why the cache, not the weights, caps batch size at long context, and why GQA, MLA, cache quantisation and paged allocation (vLLM) exist.	bank level::intermediate ch::10
[Q64] MQA and GQA?	Query heads share key/value heads (all in MQA, in groups in GQA), shrinking the KV cache and speeding decoding.	bank level::intermediate ch::10
[Q65] Why is attention quadratic, and what are the main ways around it?	Every token attends to every other: O(T²). FlashAttention reduces memory traffic (still exact); sliding-window, sparse or linear attention and state-space models reduce the computation itself.	bank level::senior ch::10
[Q66] Encoder vs decoder models: which for what?	Encoders (BERT) for classification, extraction and embeddings; decoders (GPT) for generation and general LLM use.	bank level::basic ch::12_15
[Q67] What is a mixture-of-experts model?	Each token is routed to a few expert feed-forward networks, so total parameters are large but compute per token is small; needs load balancing.	bank level::intermediate ch::12_15
[Q68] What do scaling laws say?	Loss falls predictably as a power law in parameters, data and compute; Chinchilla showed compute-optimal training uses roughly 20 tokens per parameter, so many earlier models were under-trained.	bank level::intermediate ch::12_15
[Q69] What are the stages of training a chat LLM?	Pre-training on large text corpora (next-token prediction), supervised fine-tuning on instructions and conversations, then preference optimisation (RLHF or DPO), often followed by reinforcement learning for reasoning.	bank level::basic ch::12_15
[Q70] How does RLHF work?	Collect human preference pairs, train a reward model on them, then optimise the policy with RL (classically PPO) to maximise reward with a KL penalty to stay close to the SFT model.	bank level::intermediate ch::12_15
[Q71] What does DPO change?	It optimises the policy directly on preference pairs with a classification-style loss derived from the RLHF objective, with no separate reward model or RL loop.	bank level::intermediate ch::12_15
[Q72] What are reasoning models and how are they trained?	Models that generate long reasoning before answering, trained largely with reinforcement learning on tasks with verifiable rewards (maths, code tests), so they learn to search, check and correct; controlled at inference with a reasoning budget. The usual recipe: a strong base, often a cold-start SFT on long chain-of-thought traces, then RLVR: sample a group of 8–16 answers per prompt, score each with a checker (answer match, unit tests), and raise the probability of above-average answers with GRPO or a PPO variant (Q213). Response length grows during training as the model learns to verify and backtrack. The second axis is test-time compute: accuracy rises roughly with the log of thinking tokens or samples, so accuracy is bought per query (Q251). Trade-off: latency and cost grow with the budget and easy queries waste tokens, which is why hybrid models expose an effort or thinking-budget control. Follow-up: "Does RL teach new skills or sharpen old ones?" See Q217.	bank level::senior ch::12_15
[Q73] What is distillation?	Training a smaller student to imitate a larger teacher's outputs (labels, soft probabilities or generated text), keeping much of the quality at lower cost.	bank level::intermediate ch::12_15
[Q74] Small fine-tuned model or large general model for a production task?	Compare on your evaluation set with cost and latency: large models for broad, changing or low-volume tasks; small fine-tuned models for narrow, high-volume, latency-sensitive tasks, often distilled from the large one.	bank level::senior ch::12_15
[Q75] Temperature, top-k and top-p?	Temperature scales logits (lower is more deterministic); top-k samples from the k most likely tokens; top-p from the smallest set whose probability mass reaches p.	bank level::basic ch::21
[Q76] Greedy decoding vs beam search for chat models?	Beam search finds high-likelihood sequences but tends to be bland and repetitive for open-ended text; chat uses sampling; beam search suits translation-like tasks.	bank level::intermediate ch::21
[Q77] What is continuous batching?	Adding new requests to the running batch at each decoding step instead of waiting for the whole batch to finish, keeping GPUs busy and raising throughput (vLLM, TGI).	bank level::intermediate ch::21
[Q78] What is speculative decoding?	A small draft model proposes k tokens; the large model scores all of them in one forward pass. With sampling, each drafted token x is accepted with probability min(1, p(x)/q(x)), where p is the target's distribution and q the draft's; at the first rejection a token is resampled from the residual norm(max(0, p − q)) and the rest of the draft is discarded, and if all k are accepted the target's pass yields one extra token for free. This makes the output exactly distributed as p: the chance of emitting x is q(x)·min(1, p(x)/q(x)) + (1 − α)·norm(max(0, p − q))(x) = p(x), where α = Σ min(p, q) is the acceptance rate. "Accept the matching prefix" is only the greedy special case. With α = 0.8 and k = 4, a target pass yields (1 − α^(k+1))/(1 − α) ≈ 3.4 tokens on average; the wall-clock speed-up must pay for the k draft passes (about 2.8× if each costs 5% of a target pass). Follow-up: gains shrink at large batch, where decoding is no longer memory-bound.	bank level::intermediate ch::21
[Q79] What does quantisation trade off?	Lower precision (int8, int4: GPTQ, AWQ) cuts memory and raises speed, with some quality loss that grows at lower bits and for smaller models; measure on your tasks.	bank level::intermediate ch::21
[Q80] Why is decoding memory-bandwidth-bound?	Each step generates one token per sequence but must read all weights and the KV cache; batching amortises the reads. At batch 1 a bf16 matrix–vector product does 2 FLOPs (one multiply-add) per weight and reads 2 bytes per weight: about 1 FLOP per byte. An H100 needs about 989 TFLOP/s ÷ 3.35 TB/s ≈ 295 FLOP/byte to be compute-bound, so at batch 1 most of its compute idles. An 8B model in bf16 reads 16 GB per token, which caps it at about 210 tokens/s at batch 1 however fast the arithmetic is. Batching B sequences raises the weight matmuls' intensity to about B FLOP/byte, but each sequence reads its own KV cache, which does not amortise, so long-context decoding stays memory-bound. Follow-up: prefill is compute-bound, because thousands of prompt tokens share each weight read (Q252).	bank level::intermediate ch::21
[Q81] What are time to first token and tokens per second, and what drives each?	TTFT is dominated by prefill (prompt length, queueing); tokens per second by decode speed (model size, batching, bandwidth, speculative decoding).	bank level::intermediate ch::21
[Q82] Estimate the monthly cost of an LLM feature.	Calls per month × (input tokens × input price + output tokens × output price), minus caching and batch discounts; compare with self-hosting (GPU hours at the needed throughput and utilisation).	bank level::senior ch::21
[Q83] Your offline LLM classification job is too expensive. Options?	Batch API (often around half price), prompt caching, shorter prompts, a smaller model or a cascade, and distilling into a small model; a before-and-after cost figure from your own work is the strongest answer.	bank level::senior ch::21
[Q84] When would you self-host an open model instead of using an API?	High, steady volume where GPUs stay busy; data residency or privacy requirements; need for fine-tuning or custom decoding; latency control. Against: operational cost, scaling, and keeping up with model releases.	bank level::senior ch::21
[Q85] Zero-shot vs few-shot?	Instructions only vs instructions plus examples; add examples when format or boundary cases are ambiguous.	bank level::basic ch::22
[Q86] How do you get reliable JSON?	Structured-output or tool-calling modes with a schema, validation with Pydantic, one retry with the error, and a fallback.	bank level::basic ch::22
[Q87] When does chain of thought help?	Multi-step reasoning; not simple extraction. Reasoning models do it internally under a budget.	bank level::intermediate ch::22
[Q88] How do you version and test prompts?	Version template, model and parameters together; run a labelled evaluation set on each change; log versions with outputs.	bank level::intermediate ch::22
[Q89] How do you design a prompt for caching?	Stable content first (system prompt, instructions, examples, tools), byte-identical across calls; per-request content last.	bank level::intermediate ch::22
[Q90] What is indirect prompt injection and how do you defend?	Instructions hidden in content the model reads (documents, emails, tool output). Least privilege, policies in code, human approval for risky actions, untrusted content handled by a tool-less reader, filtering and red-teaming.	bank level::senior ch::22
[Q91] Prompt, RAG or fine-tune?	Prompt first; RAG for knowledge that changes or must be cited; fine-tune for consistent format or style, narrow high-volume tasks, or domain language.	bank level::basic ch::20
[Q92] Explain LoRA in one sentence.	Freeze W and learn a low-rank update BA (scaled by α/r), training well under 1% of parameters and merging afterwards at no latency cost.	bank level::basic ch::20
[Q93] Why is B initialised to zero in LoRA?	So the update starts at zero and training begins exactly from the pretrained model.	bank level::intermediate ch::20
[Q94] What makes QLoRA possible?	4-bit NF4 quantisation of the frozen base, double quantisation and paged optimisers, with bf16 LoRA adapters receiving gradients.	bank level::intermediate ch::20
[Q95] How do you limit catastrophic forgetting?	PEFT, low learning rate, few epochs, replay of general data, and a regression suite.	bank level::intermediate ch::20
[Q96] How would you evaluate whether a fine-tune was worth it?	Against the best prompted baseline on a held-out set, plus format reliability, a general-ability and safety regression suite, and cost and latency; include maintenance cost when the base model changes.	bank level::senior ch::20
[Q97] How are sentence embeddings trained?	Contrastively: pairs that should match are pulled together and in-batch negatives pushed apart (InfoNCE).	bank level::basic ch::29
[Q98] What does the temperature do in InfoNCE?	Sharpens or softens the softmax over similarities; low temperature focuses on hard negatives.	bank level::intermediate ch::29
[Q99] What are false negatives in hard-negative mining?	Mined "negatives" that are actually relevant; filter them with a cross-encoder or margin, or skip top ranks.	bank level::intermediate ch::29
[Q100] What are Matryoshka embeddings?	Embeddings trained so that prefixes (first 64, 128, … dimensions) are good embeddings too, allowing cheap truncated search.	bank level::intermediate ch::29
[Q101] How do you choose an embedding model?	Shortlist from MTEB for your task and languages, evaluate on your own labelled queries, compare latency, dimension, cost and licence, consider fine-tuning.	bank level::senior ch::29
[Q102] BM25 in one sentence.	Keyword scoring summing IDF-weighted, saturating, length-normalised term frequencies.	bank level::basic ch::30
[Q103] Why hybrid search?	Keyword search catches exact names and codes; embeddings catch paraphrases; fused (RRF) they cover each other's misses.	bank level::basic ch::30
[Q104] How does HNSW work?	A layered proximity graph searched greedily from sparse top layers down to the full bottom layer; efSearch trades recall for latency.	bank level::intermediate ch::30
[Q105] What is product quantisation?	Splitting vectors into sub-vectors, each stored as the id of its nearest of 256 centroids; scored with lookup tables; compressed and approximate.	bank level::intermediate ch::30
[Q106] Bi-encoder vs cross-encoder?	Bi-encoders embed separately (fast, indexable, for retrieval); cross-encoders read query and document together (accurate, slow, for re-ranking).	bank level::intermediate ch::30
[Q107] How would you handle per-user permissions in vector search?	Filter in retrieval (pre-filtering or a partition per tenant), never by prompt instructions; test that restricted documents never reach the context.	bank level::senior ch::30
[Q108] Draw the RAG pipeline.	Offline: load, parse, chunk, embed and index. Online: rewrite query, hybrid retrieval with filters, re-rank, assemble context, generate with citations, verify.	bank level::basic ch::31
[Q109] How do you choose chunk size?	Empirically: structure-aware chunks with headings, two or three sizes compared on retrieval recall and answer quality.	bank level::intermediate ch::31
[Q110] What is HyDE?	Embedding an LLM-written hypothetical answer to search with, because it resembles documents more than a short question does.	bank level::intermediate ch::31
[Q111] What is contextual retrieval?	Prepending a short LLM-written description of each chunk's place in its document before embedding and BM25 indexing, so chunks are self-explanatory.	bank level::intermediate ch::31
[Q112] RAG answers are wrong 20% of the time. How do you find the cause?	For a sample of failures, check whether the right passage was retrieved, whether it reached the final context, and whether the answer used it; fix the dominant stage.	bank level::senior ch::31
[Q113] When would you use GraphRAG or agentic RAG?	GraphRAG for global or multi-hop questions over a stable entity-rich corpus; agentic RAG for multi-part questions mixing documents and live data. Both cost more; start with a fixed pipeline.	bank level::senior ch::31
[Q114] Workflow or agent?	Workflow when steps are known; agent when the path depends on findings. Prefer the simplest that works.	bank level::basic ch::33
[Q115] What makes a good tool for an agent?	Clear description with units and limits, strict schema, concise output, actionable errors, idempotency, and policy checks inside the tool.	bank level::intermediate ch::33
[Q116] Why do multi-step agents fail more often?	Errors compound: per-step success p gives roughly pⁿ over n steps.	bank level::intermediate ch::33
[Q117] What is MCP?	An open protocol exposing tools, resources and prompts from servers to AI applications through a standard interface.	bank level::intermediate ch::33
[Q118] How do you take an agent to production safely?	Sandbox evaluation on real cases over repeated runs, guardrails and budgets, audit logs, shadow mode against humans, gradual automation by risk tier, monitoring of success, cost and overrides.	bank level::senior ch::33
[Q119] Why are public benchmarks insufficient?	They do not reflect your inputs, criteria or failure costs, and can be contaminated.	bank level::basic ch::24_25
[Q120] Where do you start evaluating a new LLM feature?	Error analysis: read 50–100 traces, write notes, group into failure modes, build an evaluation per frequent mode.	bank level::intermediate ch::24_25
[Q121] How do you validate an LLM judge?	Against expert labels: Cohen's kappa, true positive and true negative rates, compared with human-human agreement, on a held-out set.	bank level::intermediate ch::24_25
[Q122] Name three LLM-judge biases.	Position, verbosity, self-preference.	bank level::intermediate ch::24_25
[Q123] Faithfulness vs correctness?	Faithfulness: claims supported by the retrieved context. Correctness: claims true against a reference. A faithful answer from a wrong document is still wrong.	bank level::intermediate ch::24_25
[Q124] How many examples do you need to compare two prompts?	Depends on the difference: use paired comparisons on the same cases (McNemar); 100 cases give about ±7 points on a single rate near 85% (±10 near 50%), small differences need hundreds.	bank level::senior ch::24_25
[Q125] How do you evaluate an agent?	End-state task success, trajectory checks, efficiency, robustness to tool failures, repeated runs (pass^k), safety tests.	bank level::senior ch::24_25
[Q126] What is hallucination, and how do you reduce it in products?	Confident, unsupported output. Ground on retrieved sources with citations, allow abstention, verify claims, constrain outputs, and hand off when unsure.	bank level::basic ch::28
[Q127] How do you handle PII in an LLM pipeline?	Minimise what is sent, redact or pseudonymise before calls, contractual and regional controls with providers, no PII in logs or training data without consent, output filters.	bank level::intermediate ch::28
[Q128] How would you moderate user reviews with ML?	Policy definitions first; a fast classifier for clear cases, an LLM for nuanced ones, human review for borderline and appeals; precision and recall per policy category, per language; monitoring for new abuse patterns.	bank level::intermediate ch::28
[Q129] What does the EU AI Act mean for a practitioner?	A risk-based framework: prohibited practices, high-risk systems with obligations (risk management, data governance, documentation, human oversight, accuracy and robustness), transparency duties (for example disclosing AI interaction and AI-generated content), and obligations for general-purpose AI model providers. Know which tier your system falls into and document accordingly. Dates matter: the Act entered into force in August 2024, the prohibitions applied from February 2025 and the general-purpose model obligations (technical documentation, copyright policy, training-data summary, plus evaluation and incident reporting for models with systemic risk) from August 2025. The high-risk deadlines (originally August 2026 and 2027) were the subject of a late-2025 proposal to postpone them, so check the current timetable before quoting one.	bank level::senior ch::28
[Q130] Why multi-stage recommenders?	Cheap high-recall retrieval over the catalogue, then filtering, then a heavy ranker on hundreds, then page-level re-ranking, to meet latency and cost.	bank level::basic ch::43
[Q131] Implicit vs explicit feedback?	Explicit ratings are rare and biased; implicit behaviour (impressions, clicks, orders) is abundant but has no explicit negatives.	bank level::basic ch::43
[Q132] How does a two-tower model train and serve?	User and item towers trained with in-batch softmax (plus logQ correction); item vectors precomputed into an ANN index; the user vector computed per request.	bank level::intermediate ch::43
[Q133] What is position bias and how do you correct it?	Items shown higher get more clicks regardless of relevance; use position as a training feature removed at serving, or inverse propensity weighting with propensities from randomisation.	bank level::intermediate ch::43
[Q134] How do you handle cold start for a new restaurant?	Content features in the item tower, ID dropout in training, exploration impressions, borrowing from similar restaurants.	bank level::intermediate ch::43
[Q135] Explain Thompson sampling.	Keep a posterior per option (Beta for click rates); sample once from each; pick the highest; update with the outcome.	bank level::intermediate ch::43
[Q136] Offline NDCG up 3%, A/B flat. Why?	Candidate set mismatch, label vs goal mismatch, leakage, position bias in test labels, re-ranking rules overriding, low power, or segment effects.	bank level::senior ch::43
[Q137] Where would you use LLMs in a recommender?	Offline item enrichment and cold-start features, calibrated LLM judges for offline evaluation, conversational surfaces, semantic IDs; not for scoring thousands of candidates per request.	bank level::senior ch::43
[Q138] Pointwise, pairwise and listwise?	Per document, per pair, per list; the last two optimise order directly.	bank level::basic ch::44
[Q139] Explain LambdaMART.	RankNet's pairwise gradients scaled by the |ΔNDCG| of swapping each pair (LambdaRank), fitted by gradient-boosted trees.	bank level::intermediate ch::44
[Q140] Why must ideal DCG use all relevant documents?	Otherwise a system that retrieves few documents is not penalised for what it missed.	bank level::intermediate ch::44
[Q141] How do you handle a navigational query for a closed restaurant?	Show it first, labelled closed with opening time, then similar open alternatives.	bank level::intermediate ch::44
[Q142] What is team-draft interleaving?	Alternately drafting results from two rankers into one list and crediting clicks to the ranker that supplied them; a sensitive comparison.	bank level::intermediate ch::44
[Q143] Search conversion dropped 2%. Debugging plan?	Instrumentation and traffic mix first, then segment by query type, language, city and platform; zero-result and reformulation spikes; supply changes; recent ranking and index changes; compare with browse conversion.	bank level::senior ch::44
[Q144] Why is seasonal naive a strong forecasting baseline?	Delivery demand has strong weekly patterns; the same hour last week captures them for free.	bank level::basic ch::45
[Q145] How do you forecast with stock-outs in the history?	Treat sales during stock-outs as censored: exclude or impute those periods and model unconstrained demand.	bank level::intermediate ch::45
[Q146] What quantile should a staffing forecast target?	The newsvendor quantile q = cost of under-forecasting / (cost of under + cost of over).	bank level::intermediate ch::45
[Q147] Decompose an ETA prediction.	Accept, prep time, courier assignment and travel to the restaurant, wait, travel to the customer, hand-over; pickup is the later of food ready and courier arrival.	bank level::intermediate ch::45
[Q148] How would you rebalance courier supply?	Forecast need per zone, solve a min-cost transport LP from surplus to deficit, turn moves into incentives or suggestions, evaluate with switchbacks.	bank level::senior ch::45
[Q149] Name the eight steps of the ML system design framework.	Clarify, frame, data, features, model, evaluation, serving, monitoring.	bank level::basic ch::46_47
[Q150] What is train-serve skew and how do you prevent it?	Features computed differently in training and serving; prevent with a feature store or shared code paths, logging online features, and comparing with offline recomputations.	bank level::intermediate ch::46_47
[Q151] Shadow, canary or A/B: when each?	Shadow to validate behaviour and latency without user impact; canary to limit blast radius; A/B to measure effect.	bank level::intermediate ch::46_47
[Q152] What is data drift vs concept drift?	Data drift: input distribution changes. Concept drift: the relationship between inputs and target changes. Monitor inputs, predictions and delayed labels.	bank level::intermediate ch::46_47
[Q153] What do you monitor for an LLM feature?	Traces of chained calls, token usage and cost, latency, error and refusal rates, sampled judged quality, user feedback, and drift in input topics.	bank level::intermediate ch::46_47
[Q154] Back-of-the-envelope: 5M daily users, 4 feed loads each. Peak QPS?	5M × 4 / 86,400 ≈ 230 average; with a peak factor of 3, about 700 QPS.	bank level::senior ch::46_47
[Q155] How would you roll out a new ranking model safely?	Offline evaluation, shadow traffic for latency and sanity, a small canary with guardrails, an A/B or interleaving test, gradual ramp, monitoring and a rollback switch.	bank level::senior ch::46_47
[Q156] What is a point-in-time join?	Joining each training row only with feature values known at its timestamp (for example merge_asof backward), preventing leakage.	bank level::basic ch::48
[Q157] How do you sessionise events in SQL?	Flag events whose gap from the previous event (LAG) exceeds a threshold, then take a running sum of the flags per user.	bank level::intermediate ch::48
[Q158] How do you measure label quality?	Inter-annotator agreement (Cohen's or Fleiss' kappa), expert audits of samples, and checking confidently disagreeing model predictions.	bank level::intermediate ch::48
[Q159] When does pandas stop being enough?	When data no longer fits comfortably in memory or jobs take too long: push work to SQL, use Polars or DuckDB, or Spark for distributed processing.	bank level::intermediate ch::48
[Q160] How would you set up data contracts for an ML pipeline?	Agreed schemas, types, freshness and quality rules between producers and consumers, enforced by automated checks that fail the pipeline or alert before bad data reaches training or serving.	bank level::senior ch::48
[Q161] What are the main steps of a web pretraining-data pipeline?	URL filtering, HTML extraction, language ID, heuristic filters, deduplication, model-based quality filtering, PII and toxicity removal, decontamination, tokenisation and sharding with provenance metadata.	bank level::intermediate ch::13_16
[Q162] How does MinHash estimate document similarity?	Hash each shingle with many hash functions and keep the minimum per function; the fraction of matching minima estimates the Jaccard similarity.	bank level::intermediate ch::13_16
[Q163] How do you choose the LSH bands and rows?	The candidate probability 1 − (1 − sʳ)ᵇ is an S-curve with its threshold near (1/b)^(1/r); choose b and r to place it at the similarity you want, balancing missed duplicates against false candidates.	bank level::senior ch::13_16
[Q164] Why might global deduplication across all crawl snapshots hurt?	It removes good documents that recur across years and leaves a higher share of low-quality unique pages; per-snapshot deduplication performed better in FineWeb's ablations.	bank level::senior ch::13_16
[Q165] What does a quality classifier like DCLM's or FineWeb-Edu's do, and what is the risk?	Scores documents by similarity to a high-quality reference (or LLM-rated educational value); risk of narrowing style and topics and of biases against dialects and regions.	bank level::intermediate ch::13_16
[Q166] How would you choose a data mixture?	Proxy-model ablations, learned weights (DoReMi) or fitted mixing laws, constrained by epochs per source; validate per domain and at two scales.	bank level::senior ch::13_16
[Q167] What is annealing on high-quality data?	Switching to a curated mixture during the final learning-rate decay; also a cheap way to measure a data source's value.	bank level::intermediate ch::13_16
[Q168] What are the risks of synthetic pretraining data?	Narrowed distribution, inherited errors, contamination via the generator, and collapse when models repeatedly train on their own outputs without fresh data.	bank level::senior ch::13_16
[Q169] Derive the compute-optimal allocation from L = E + A/N^α + B/D^β with C = 6ND.	Substitute D = C/(6N) to write the loss in N alone: L(N) = E + A·N^(−α) + B·(6N/C)^β. Differentiate and set to zero: −αA·N^(−α−1) + βB·(6/C)^β·N^(β−1) = 0; multiplying by N gives αA·N^(−α) = βB·(6N/C)^β, so N^(α+β) ∝ C^β. Hence N_opt ∝ C^(β/(α+β)) and D_opt = C/(6N_opt) ∝ C^(α/(α+β)). With Chinchilla's approach-3 fit, α ≈ 0.34 and β ≈ 0.28, so N_opt ∝ C^0.45 and D_opt ∝ C^0.55; their other two methods give about 0.5 each, the source of the "20 tokens per parameter at every scale" rule. With the fitted constants (E = 1.69, A = 406.4, B = 410.7), C = 10²³ gives about 15B parameters and 1.1T tokens. Follow-up: the exponents are fragile; 0.45 against 0.50 changes the recommended size by 1.4× over three decades of compute (Chain 8).	bank level::intermediate ch::13_16
[Q170] Why are small open models trained far past 20 tokens per parameter?	Inference cost: minimising training plus lifetime serving compute favours smaller models trained longer.	bank level::intermediate ch::13_16
[Q171] How much is repeated data worth?	Nearly as good as fresh data up to about 4 epochs, sharply diminishing beyond about 16 (Muennighoff et al.).	bank level::senior ch::13_16
[Q172] Are emergent abilities real?	Many apparent jumps come from discontinuous metrics like exact match; continuous metrics usually show smooth improvement, though some multi-step tasks still appear abruptly.	bank level::senior ch::13_16
[Q173] Write the AdamW update.	m = β₁m + (1−β₁)g; v = β₂v + (1−β₂)g²; bias-correct both; p ← p(1 − ηλ) − η m̂/(√v̂ + ε).	bank level::basic ch::7_17
[Q174] Why does Adam need warm-up?	Early second-moment estimates are poor, so every coordinate moves by about the full learning rate in a noisy direction.	bank level::intermediate ch::7_17
[Q175] What is the WSD schedule's advantage?	Any checkpoint in the constant phase can be decayed into a good model, so runs can be extended and branched cheaply for experiments.	bank level::intermediate ch::7_17
[Q176] What does the gradient noise scale tell you?	Roughly the critical batch size: below it, larger batches save steps almost linearly; it grows as the loss falls.	bank level::senior ch::7_17
[Q177] What does Muon do?	Orthogonalises the momentum of each hidden weight matrix with Newton–Schulz iterations, equalising update size across directions; embeddings and heads stay on AdamW.	bank level::senior ch::7_17
[Q178] What problem does µP solve?	Under the standard parametrisation the optimal learning rate shifts with width; µP scales initialisation and per-layer learning rates so hyperparameters tuned on small models transfer.	bank level::senior ch::7_17
[Q179] Why keep fp32 master weights in bf16 training?	bf16 has 8 bits of precision, so small updates to weights near 1 round away; accumulating in fp32 preserves them.	bank level::intermediate ch::7_17
[Q180] What makes fp8 training hard?	Tiny dynamic range requiring fine-grained scaling, sensitive operations kept in higher precision, and silent quality loss that appears late.	bank level::senior ch::7_17
[Q181] What do z-loss and QK-norm prevent?	Growth of output logits and of attention logits respectively, common causes of late-training instability.	bank level::intermediate ch::7_17
[Q182] How much memory does Adam training need per parameter in mixed precision?	About 16 bytes: bf16 weights and gradients plus fp32 master weights and two moments.	bank level::basic ch::7_17
[Q183] Compare ZeRO stages 1, 2 and 3.	Shard optimiser states; plus gradients; plus weights. Stages 1–2 add no communication; stage 3 gathers weights per layer, about 1.5× DDP traffic.	bank level::intermediate ch::7_17
[Q184] Why is tensor parallelism kept inside a node?	It all-reduces activations in every layer, needing NVLink-class bandwidth.	bank level::intermediate ch::7_17
[Q185] What is the pipeline bubble?	Idle time at batch start and end, (p − 1)/(m + p − 1); reduced by more micro-batches and interleaved or zero-bubble schedules.	bank level::intermediate ch::7_17
[Q186] How does context parallelism compute attention?	Each GPU holds a sequence block; key–value blocks circulate around a ring while each GPU attends its queries, with load balancing for causal masks.	bank level::senior ch::7_17
[Q187] How do you estimate training time?	6ND FLOPs divided by GPUs × peak FLOP/s × MFU.	bank level::basic ch::7_17
[Q188] Your training throughput fell 20% overnight. What do you check?	Per-rank step times for stragglers, node and network health, data-loader I/O, checkpoint timing, recent configuration changes.	bank level::senior ch::7_17
[Q189] How does a top-k MoE layer change compute and memory?	Compute per token scales with k active experts; memory with all experts.	bank level::intermediate ch::10_11_14
[Q190] Why do MoEs need load balancing, and what are two methods?	Routers collapse onto few experts: a slightly favoured expert receives more tokens, learns faster and is favoured more, while the rest stay untrained. Two methods are the Switch auxiliary loss α·E·Σ_e f_e·P_e and auxiliary-loss-free per-expert selection biases. In the loss, E is the number of experts, f_e the fraction of the batch's tokens dispatched to expert e by the hard top-k choice, P_e the mean router probability for expert e over the batch, and α a small coefficient (0.01 in Switch). E·Σ f·P equals 1 when routing is uniform (f_e = P_e = 1/E) and grows with imbalance: with 8 experts, one taking half the tokens and 40% of the probability, it is about 1.9. f is not differentiable, so the gradient flows through P, pushing probability away from overloaded experts. The bias method (DeepSeek-V3) adds a per-expert bias to the scores used only for top-k selection, raised for under-loaded experts after each step, so balancing does not fight the language-model loss. Follow-up: capacity factor and dropped tokens (Chain 7).	bank level::intermediate ch::10_11_14
[Q191] What are fine-grained and shared experts?	Many small experts with more active per token, plus always-on experts for common knowledge.	bank level::senior ch::10_11_14
[Q192] Explain MLA and why it needs decoupled RoPE.	Cache a low-rank latent per token and reconstruct keys and values; absorbing up-projections into other matrices fails with position-dependent rotations, so position uses separate rotary dimensions.	bank level::senior ch::10_11_14
[Q193] Show that causal linear attention is an RNN.	Replace exp(q·k) with φ(q)·φ(k); the running sums of φ(k)vᵀ and φ(k) form a fixed-size state updated per token.	bank level::intermediate ch::10_11_14
[Q194] What is the weakness of SSMs and linear attention, and how do hybrids fix it?	Fixed-size state limits precise recall from long context; hybrids keep a minority of full-attention layers.	bank level::senior ch::10_11_14
[Q195] What does the delta rule change in linear attention?	It overwrites the value stored for a key instead of adding to it, reducing interference.	bank level::senior ch::10_11_14
[Q196] What is multi-token prediction for?	Extra training signal and built-in draft heads for speculative decoding.	bank level::intermediate ch::10_11_14
[Q197] How do position interpolation, NTK scaling and YaRN extend RoPE context?	PI scales all positions down; NTK raises the base, stretching low frequencies; YaRN interpolates only low-frequency dimensions with an attention temperature fix.	bank level::senior ch::10_11_14
[Q198] How do VLMs turn images into tokens, and what limits resolution?	Patch features from a vision encoder, projected into the LLM; token count grows with the square of image side, managed by tiling, native resolution and token merging.	bank level::intermediate ch::10_11_14
[Q199] Why mask prompt tokens in the SFT loss?	The model should learn to produce responses, not to predict user and system text.	bank level::basic ch::18_20
[Q200] What goes wrong with naive sequence packing?	Examples attend to each other and positions continue across boundaries; reset position IDs and use block-diagonal masks.	bank level::intermediate ch::18_20
[Q201] Write the Bradley–Terry reward-model loss.	−log σ(r(x, y_chosen) − r(x, y_rejected)).	bank level::intermediate ch::18_20
[Q202] What limits reward-model accuracy?	Human label noise (inter-annotator agreement often 60–70%), distribution shift to policy outputs, spurious features such as length.	bank level::senior ch::18_20
[Q203] Explain GAE.	δ_t = r_t + γV(s_(t+1)) − V(s_t), A_t = δ_t + γλA_(t+1): a λ-weighted mix of multi-step advantage estimates trading bias for variance.	bank level::intermediate ch::18_20
[Q204] Why does PPO clip the ratio?	To stop updates from moving the policy far from the one that sampled the data, where advantage estimates are invalid.	bank level::intermediate ch::18_20
[Q205] Compare the k1, k2 and k3 KL estimators.	Sample y from the policy π_θ and let r = π_ref(y)/π_θ(y), per token in practice; then KL(π_θ‖π_ref) = E[−log r]. k1 = −log r is unbiased but high-variance and negative on many single samples; k2 = (log r)²/2 has low variance but is biased, matching the KL only to second order when π_θ ≈ π_ref; k3 = (r − 1) − log r is unbiased because E[r] = 1 under π_θ, non-negative because log r ≤ r − 1, and lower-variance than k1. In a three-outcome example with true KL 0.040, their standard deviations are about 0.26, 0.07 and 0.09, and k1 goes as low as −0.69. GRPO puts k3 in the loss. Follow-up: "Is its gradient the KL's gradient?" No: differentiating k3 through r gives (1 − r)∇log π_θ, whose expectation is the gradient of the forward KL(π_ref‖π_θ), not the reverse KL being estimated; the two agree near the reference, which is why it works in practice.	bank level::senior ch::18_20
[Q206] Derive DPO.	Start from the KL-regularised objective max_π E[r(x, y)] − β·KL(π‖π_ref). Its optimum is π*(y|x) = π_ref(y|x)·exp(r(x, y)/β)/Z(x). Solve for the reward: r(x, y) = β·log(π*(y|x)/π_ref(y|x)) + β·log Z(x). Substitute into Bradley–Terry, P(y_w ≻ y_l) = σ(r(x, y_w) − r(x, y_l)): both responses share the prompt, so β·log Z(x) cancels. Replace π* with the trainable π_θ and minimise the negative log-likelihood: L_DPO = −E log σ(β·[log(π_θ(y_w|x)/π_ref(y_w|x)) − log(π_θ(y_l|x)/π_ref(y_l|x))]). The gradient weights each pair by σ(−β × margin), so pairs already ranked correctly contribute little: at β = 0.1, a 20-nat margin gives weight 0.12. Trade-off: no reward model or sampling, but the data are fixed and off-policy, and β is both the KL strength and the loss temperature. Follow-up: likelihood displacement (Q208) and the gap to PPO (Q207); section H grades this answer.	bank level::senior ch::18_20
[Q207] Why does offline DPO underperform PPO in practice?	Fixed off-policy data, no exploration, likelihood displacement; on-policy iterative DPO narrows the gap. PPO samples from the current policy, so its reward model scores the model's own mistakes and the update targets them; DPO learns only from pairs someone else generated, often by another model, and cannot ask "what if I answered differently?". The reward model in PPO also generalises: it can score responses never seen in the preference data. DPO's advantages are real, though: no reward model, no sampling loop, and about the cost of SFT. The usual compromise is iterative or online DPO: generate pairs from the current policy each round, label them with a reward model or judge, and retrain. Follow-up: "When would you still choose offline DPO?" A first alignment pass on a small budget, or style and format preferences where exploration matters little.	bank level::senior ch::18_20
[Q208] What is likelihood displacement?	In DPO, chosen and rejected log-probabilities both fall while their gap grows, moving mass to other responses. It is a property of the margin-only objective rather than of offline data as such: the loss rewards the gap, not the chosen likelihood, and the effect is strongest when chosen and rejected responses are similar, because their gradients share directions (Razin et al., 2024; Chapter 18). Fixes add a term that keeps the chosen likelihood up (an SFT term on chosen responses, as in RPO-style losses) or filter near-identical pairs.	bank level::intermediate ch::18_20
[Q209] When would you use KTO, IPO or SimPO?	KTO for unpaired binary feedback; IPO for near-deterministic preferences; SimPO to remove length bias and the reference model.	bank level::intermediate ch::18_20
[Q210] Describe reward over-optimisation and its defences.	Proxy reward keeps rising while gold quality peaks and falls; KL budgets, early stopping, bigger or ensembled reward models, length control, gold evaluations.	bank level::senior ch::18_20
[Q211] What is the KL of best-of-n sampling from the base policy?	At most log n − (n − 1)/n: about 0.64 nats at n = 4 and 3.2 at n = 64. The formula is an upper bound, not the exact value (Beirami et al., 2024): it is reached only when no single response carries much probability and rewards have no ties. With 4 equally likely answers and distinct rewards the true KL at n = 4 is 0.59; with a binary verifier that passes 30% of samples it is 0.45. Use it as a KL budget when comparing RL policies with best-of-n at equal KL: best-of-16 spends at most about 1.8 nats.	bank level::intermediate ch::18_20
[Q212] How does model merging work?	Average fine-tunes of the same base, or add scaled task vectors (fine-tuned minus base), with TIES or DARE to reduce interference.	bank level::intermediate ch::18_20
[Q213] Explain GRPO.	Sample a group per prompt; advantage = reward minus group mean (often divided by its std); PPO-style clipped token-level updates; no value model.	bank level::intermediate ch::19_13
[Q214] What is GRPO's length bias?	Averaging the loss within each response gives tokens weight A/|o|, so long wrong answers are penalised less per token; use a token-level mean.	bank level::senior ch::19_13
[Q215] What does DAPO change?	Clip-higher, dynamic sampling of groups with mixed rewards, token-level loss, overlong shaping, no KL term.	bank level::senior ch::19_13
[Q216] Why filter prompts by difficulty in RLVR?	Groups that are all right or all wrong have zero advantage and teach nothing.	bank level::intermediate ch::19_13
[Q217] Does RL create new reasoning or sharpen existing ability?	pass@1 rises but pass@k at large k often falls relative to the base model, suggesting sharpening and narrowing; longer, exploratory RL and distillation may extend the frontier. Measure pass@k curves. Yue et al. (2025) found base models catching up at k = 256 on maths and code. Chapter 19's toy run shows the mechanism: pass@1 rises from 0.34 to 0.47 while pass@64 falls from 0.91 to 0.76, because a strategy the base model picked 1% of the time is squeezed out as entropy collapses. The decisive experiment: compare pass@k curves of base and RL models to large k on problems the base model never solves within k samples; if RL solves some, it added coverage. My view: current RLVR mostly sharpens, and extending the frontier needs exploration kept alive (clip-higher, temperature, diverse prompts) and long training. Follow-up: "Why does it matter?" It decides whether more RL compute or a better base model is the next investment.	bank level::senior ch::19_13
[Q218] PRM versus ORM?	Process reward models score steps (denser signal, better for search and reranking, harder to label, hackable); outcome models or verifiers score final answers.	bank level::intermediate ch::19_13
[Q219] When does self-consistency fail?	When a wrong answer is more probable than the right one, voting amplifies it.	bank level::intermediate ch::19_13
[Q220] What is budget forcing?	Controlling thinking length at inference by forcing the end of thinking or appending "Wait" to extend it.	bank level::intermediate ch::19_13
[Q221] Why distil reasoning into small models rather than run RL on them?	Distillation from a stronger model's verified traces is cheaper and often better for small models.	bank level::basic ch::19_13
[Q222] What is the training–inference mismatch problem in RL systems?	The inference engine and trainer compute slightly different probabilities, corrupting importance ratios; recompute log-probabilities or apply importance corrections.	bank level::senior ch::19_13
[Q223] What is the standard error of a 500-question benchmark at 70% accuracy?	√(0.7 × 0.3 / 500) ≈ 2 points.	bank level::intermediate ch::23
[Q224] Why use paired comparisons between models?	Shared question difficulty cancels, making the standard error of the difference smaller.	bank level::intermediate ch::23
[Q225] How many questions detect a 2-point improvement?	Let d be the total disagreement rate, the share of questions where exactly one model is right (10% flipping each way is d = 20%). The per-question difference has variance d − δ² ≈ d, so for a paired test n = (z_α/2 + z_β)²·d/δ², and (1.96 + 0.84)² ≈ 7.84 at α = 0.05 and 80% power. For δ = 2 points: 7.84 × 0.10/0.02² ≈ 2,000 at d = 10%, and about 3,900 at d = 20%; Chain 3's 1-point gain at d = 20% needs about 15,700. At temperature above 0, part of d is sampling noise: averaging several generations per question shrinks it, the cheapest lever. Follow-up: the formula assumes independent questions; several items per passage need a clustered standard error.	bank level::senior ch::23
[Q226] Why compare models in bits per byte rather than per-token perplexity?	Tokenizers differ in token length; bits per byte normalises by text.	bank level::intermediate ch::23
[Q227] How would you detect contamination in a closed model?	Perturbation gaps with significance tests, guided completion, likelihood tests such as Min-K% Prob, time-split items.	bank level::senior ch::23
[Q228] What biases affect LLM judges and how do you control them?	Position (swap and average), length (length control), self-preference (different family, several judges); validate against humans.	bank level::intermediate ch::23
[Q229] What are arena leaderboards' known problems?	Style and length bias, selection from testing private variants, unequal sampling, prompt distribution mismatch, overlapping intervals.	bank level::senior ch::23
[Q230] What is IRT used for in LLM evaluation?	Modelling item difficulty and discrimination to select informative subsets, find bad items and compare models on a common scale.	bank level::intermediate ch::23
[Q231] What is an induction head?	A head that attends from token A to the token following an earlier A and copies it, composed with a previous-token head.	bank level::intermediate ch::26_27
[Q232] How does activation patching work?	Run a corrupted input, replace one component's activation with its clean-run value, and see whether the behaviour returns.	bank level::intermediate ch::26_27
[Q233] What is superposition, and how do SAEs address it?	More sparse features than dimensions stored as overlapping directions; SAEs learn an over-complete sparse dictionary to recover them.	bank level::senior ch::26_27
[Q234] How do you evaluate an SAE?	Sparsity, reconstruction, downstream loss with the SAE spliced in, interpretability of features, usefulness against simple baselines.	bank level::senior ch::26_27
[Q235] What is a steering vector?	A direction (for example a mean activation difference between contrasting prompts) added to the residual stream to change behaviour.	bank level::intermediate ch::26_27
[Q236] How would you measure sycophancy?	Answer flips under pushback, with flip asymmetry tested by McNemar, plus opinion-tailoring and feedback-bias probes.	bank level::intermediate ch::26_27
[Q237] What is weak-to-strong generalisation and PGR?	Training a strong model on a weak supervisor's labels; PGR is the fraction of the weak-to-ceiling gap recovered.	bank level::senior ch::26_27
[Q238] Why is chain-of-thought monitoring useful and fragile?	Reasoning often reveals intent, so monitors catch misbehaviour; optimisation pressure on the reasoning can teach concealment.	bank level::senior ch::26_27
[Q239] Zero of 100 dangerous-capability attempts succeeded. What can you claim?	The per-attempt rate is below about 3% at 95% confidence under that elicitation, not that the capability is absent.	bank level::intermediate ch::26_27
[Q240] What would convince you that a safety mitigation works?	Measured on held-out attacks or environments, against adaptive adversaries, with intervals, without having trained on the evaluation, and robust when the model is optimised against it.	bank level::senior ch::26_27
[Q241] Write the REINFORCE estimator. Why doesn't the reward need to be differentiable?	∇J ≈ (1/N) Σ_i (R_i − b) ∇log π(y_i). The gradient flows through log π of the sampled response, not through R, so a unit-test runner or string match works as the reward.	bank level::basic ch::7_11_18_19
[Q242] Why does a baseline leave the policy gradient unbiased, and which baselines do PPO, GRPO and RLOO use?	E[b ∇log π(y)] = b ∇Σ π(y) = 0. PPO: a learned value per token; GRPO: the group mean (shrinks the gradient by (N − 1)/N); RLOO: the mean of the other samples (exactly unbiased).	bank level::intermediate ch::7_11_18_19
[Q243] Why are full-sequence importance weights impractical for reasoning-length responses?	The weight is a product of per-token ratios; with per-token log-ratio std σ its variance is exp(Tσ²) − 1. At σ = 0.03 and 4,000 tokens that is about 36, an effective sample size near 3%.	bank level::intermediate ch::7_11_18_19
[Q244] What is the training–inference mismatch in RL, and how do you handle it?	The rollout engine and the trainer give different probabilities for the same tokens, so on-policy data is quietly off-policy. Recompute log-probabilities in the trainer, apply truncated importance weights, keep the output head in fp32, monitor the per-token gap, or use batch-invariant kernels.	bank level::senior ch::7_11_18_19
[Q245] How do GSPO and CISPO differ from GRPO?	GSPO replaces per-token ratios with one length-normalised sequence ratio and clips whole responses (stable for MoE, tolerant of numerical noise). CISPO clips the importance weight but keeps every token's gradient, so rare high-ratio tokens still learn.	bank level::senior ch::7_11_18_19
[Q246] What is on-policy distillation, and why is it cheaper than RL?	Sample from the student; the teacher scores every token (reverse KL as a dense reward). Many bits of signal per episode instead of one; Qwen3 reported better AIME results than RL at about a tenth of the GPU hours.	bank level::intermediate ch::7_11_18_19
[Q247] How would you run RL on a task with no verifier?	Per-prompt weighted rubrics scored by an LLM judge, or a generative reward model; validate the judge against experts, audit high-reward samples, control for length, and keep a held-out human evaluation.	bank level::senior ch::7_11_18_19
[Q248] How should you compare two RL recipes?	Same model, prompts, verifier and budget; fit reward against compute with a saturating (sigmoid) curve and compare the asymptote and efficiency. Many details change speed, not the ceiling, and the early leader can plateau lower.	bank level::senior ch::7_11_18_19
[Q249] What does QK-Clip do, and why not just use QK-norm?	After each update it rescales a head's query and key weights when its maximum attention logit exceeds a threshold, preventing logit blow-up under Muon. QK-norm needs materialised keys, which MLA avoids at inference.	bank level::intermediate ch::7_11_18_19
[Q250] How do you turn a dense-attention model into a sparse-attention one?	DeepSeek-V3.2: add a small fp8 indexer, train it to match the dense attention distribution (KL) with the model frozen, then switch to top-k sparse attention and continue training all weights.	bank level::intermediate ch::7_11_18_19
[Q251] What is test-time compute scaling, and how would you spend a fixed inference budget?	Accuracy on reasoning tasks rises with inference compute, spent sequentially (longer thinking, self-revision) or in parallel (many samples combined by voting, a verifier or a reward model). Parallel sampling raises coverage fast, pass@k = 1 − (1 − p)^k, so 0.83 at p = 0.2 and k = 8, but only a verifier turns coverage into accuracy. Majority voting helps only when the right answer is the single most likely one: over 16 samples it gives 0.52 when the right answer has probability 0.40 against wrong ones at 0.35 and 0.25, but 0.20 when it has 0.30 against a 0.45 wrong answer. The best allocation depends on difficulty: easier prompts gain most from sequential revision, harder ones from broader search, and an adaptive per-prompt budget beats fixed best-of-N at equal compute (Snell et al., 2024). Trade-off: latency and cost per query. Follow-up: "When is a bigger model better than more thinking?" On the hardest prompts, where the small model's pass@k stays low however large k gets.	bank level::senior ch::10_21_23_26_27_33
[Q252] Explain the roofline model. How do you tell whether a kernel is memory- or compute-bound?	Attainable FLOP/s = min(peak FLOP/s, arithmetic intensity × memory bandwidth), where arithmetic intensity is FLOPs per byte moved from HBM. The ridge point, peak ÷ bandwidth, is about 989 TFLOP/s ÷ 3.35 TB/s ≈ 295 FLOP/byte for dense bf16 on an H100. Below it a kernel is memory-bound and faster arithmetic does not help; above it, compute-bound. A bf16 product of a B × d activation with a d × d weight does 2Bd² FLOPs on about 2d² bytes of weights, so its intensity is about B for B ≪ d: about 1 for decoding at batch 1, thousands for a long prefill. Elementwise operations (activations, norms, residual adds) sit below 1 FLOP/byte, which is why kernel fusion and FlashAttention pay: they cut bytes moved, not FLOPs. Follow-up: "Why does MFU fall at small batch?" The matmuls drop below the ridge point; raise the batch or the tokens per step.	bank level::intermediate ch::10_21_23_26_27_33
[Q253] How do you evaluate long-context ability, and what is wrong with needle-in-a-haystack tests?	A needle test hides one fact in filler text and asks for it back. Frontier models score near 100%, because it tests lexical retrieval of a single item, which attention does easily, while real use needs multi-hop reasoning, aggregation and resistance to distractors. RULER (Hsieh et al., 2024) adds multiple needles and keys, variable tracking across the context and aggregation tasks, and finds that many models' effective context, where scores stay above a threshold, is far shorter than the advertised window. Tests that remove the word overlap between question and needle show steeper drops with length. Good practice: plot score against context length, vary the needle's position, use tasks from your own use (long documents, repositories, agent transcripts), and compare with a retrieval baseline at equal cost. Follow-up: "Why do models degrade with length?" Attention spread over many distractors, positions beyond the lengths seen in training, and little genuinely long training data.	bank level::intermediate ch::10_21_23_26_27_33
[Q254] What is Constitutional AI, and how does RLAIF differ from RLHF?	Constitutional AI (Bai et al., 2022) replaces most human harmlessness labels with a written list of principles. In a supervised stage the model critiques and revises its own responses against sampled principles and is fine-tuned on the revisions. In the RL stage an AI model judges which of two responses better follows the principles; those AI preferences train the preference model, so the RL step is RLAIF rather than RLHF. Gains: labels are cheap and fast, and the values are explicit and editable. Risks: the labeller's biases and blind spots are copied in, principles can be applied inconsistently or gamed, and you still need human evaluation of the result. The same idea underlies rubric-based judges, model specs and deliberative alignment, where the model is trained to reason over the written policy. Follow-up: "How do you validate AI labels?" Agreement with expert labels on a held-out set, compared with human–human agreement on the same items.	bank level::intermediate ch::10_21_23_26_27_33
[Q255] What are evaluation awareness and sandbagging, and why do they matter?	Evaluation awareness: the model recognises that it is being tested, from artificial scenarios, formats or cues, and may behave differently than in deployment. Sandbagging: underperforming on purpose on an evaluation, such as a dangerous-capability test, whether the model was instructed to or learned to. Both break the assumption behind safety evaluations, that behaviour under test predicts behaviour in use. Ways to measure and reduce them: read reasoning traces for verbalised awareness; compare behaviour on realistic and obviously synthetic versions of the same scenario; elicit capability by light fine-tuning on a few demonstrations, where a large jump means the original score understated the capability; use held-out, deployment-like environments; and report results as "under this elicitation", never as "the model cannot". Follow-up: "Can interpretability help?" A linear probe can detect a test-like-context direction in activations; steering along it and watching behaviour change tests whether behaviour depends on it.	bank level::senior ch::10_21_23_26_27_33
[Q256] Linear probe or sparse autoencoder: which would you use to detect a concept in activations?	If you know the concept and can label examples, start with a linear probe: logistic regression on residual-stream activations at a chosen layer, trained on a few hundred to a few thousand labelled examples. Several 2025 comparisons found that classifiers built on SAE features did not beat plain probes at detection tasks such as harmful intent, out of distribution included. SAEs earn their cost in unsupervised discovery: they surface features you did not know to look for and give an inspectable dictionary that helps explain what a probe picks up. Risks with probes: they can latch onto a correlate (length, format, topic), so test under distribution shift and against a control task; and decodable information is not necessarily used by the model, so test causality with patching or steering. Follow-up: "Why linear?" Many concepts appear as directions (the linear representation hypothesis), and a powerful non-linear probe can find information the model itself never reads.	bank level::senior ch::10_21_23_26_27_33
[Q257] What are transcoders and attribution graphs?	An SAE reconstructs an activation from itself; a transcoder reads the MLP's input and predicts the MLP's output through a wide, sparse layer, so it replaces the MLP with interpretable features, and a cross-layer transcoder lets each feature write to all later layers. Replace every MLP this way, freeze the attention patterns and normalisation scales for one prompt, and add error terms for what the transcoders miss: the model becomes linear between features. An attribution graph (Anthropic's circuit-tracing work, 2025) records each active feature's direct effect on later features and on the output logit; pruning it leaves a small circuit for that prompt, for example a two-hop path from "Dallas" to "Texas" to "Austin" for "the capital of the state containing Dallas". Check it by intervening on those features in the real model. Limits: one prompt at a time, attention's QK computation is left unexplained, and the error terms can carry much of the effect. Follow-up: "Why not just patch activations?" Patching tests components you chose; an attribution graph proposes which components to test.	bank level::senior ch::10_21_23_26_27_33
[Q258] How does reward hacking show up in agentic coding RL, and how do you harden the environment?	The reward is usually "the tests pass in a sandbox", and the policy finds ways to pass without solving the task: editing or deleting tests, special-casing the test inputs, exiting with code 0 before the assertions run, monkey-patching the test framework, reading hidden tests or the reference solution from the file system or git history, or satisfying an end-state check that accepts an over-broad change. It typically shows as a sudden reward jump with no gain on held-out tasks. Hardening: grade outside the agent's sandbox with tests it never sees; make test files read-only and fail any episode that touches them; strip git history and reference files from the image; combine independent checks (hidden tests, limits on diff size, a monitor for test tampering); and read a sample of the highest-reward trajectories every run. Follow-up: "Why not just penalise detected hacks?" A penalty on detected hacks can train undetected ones; fix the environment so the hack does not pay, and keep a held-out evaluation graded differently.	bank level::senior ch::10_21_23_26_27_33
[Q259] How many training FLOPs per token does a transformer need, including attention?	About 6N for the weight matmuls (2N forward; 4N backward, for gradients with respect to activations and to weights), plus an attention term that 6N leaves out. Per layer, QKᵀ and the attention-weighted sum of V each cost about 2·T·d FLOPs per token at context length T and width d, so with the backward pass the PaLM paper's MFU count is 6N + 12·L·d·T per token for L layers; it does not subtract the causal mask's saving, which would halve the term. With N ≈ 12·L·d², the attention term equals 6N when T ≈ 6d. For an 8B model (L = 32, d = 4096) it adds 27% at T = 8k, matches 6N near 32k and is over 4× 6N at 128k. Activation recomputation adds another forward pass (8N), and for an MoE N counts active, not total, parameters. Follow-up: "Then how is long-context training affordable?" FlashAttention keeps memory linear, and most tokens are trained at short length before a short long-context stage.	bank level::intermediate ch::10_21_23_26_27_33
[Q260] In multi-turn agentic RL, which tokens get the loss, and how is credit assigned?	A trajectory interleaves the model's tokens with tool outputs and environment observations. Only the model's own tokens get the policy-gradient loss; tool and environment tokens are masked out, because the policy did not produce them, and raising their likelihood would teach it to imitate the environment. The common credit scheme gives every model token the same trajectory-level advantage (final reward minus a group baseline), which is simple but noisy over a 50-turn episode; turn-level rewards or process judges give a denser signal but are easier to hack. Systems issues dominate: rollouts last minutes and vary widely in length, so training is often asynchronous, with rollouts from a policy a few steps old, which needs importance correction and a cap on staleness, and environment throughput (sandboxes started per second) often limits the run more than the GPUs do. Follow-up: "Why keep token IDs from the rollout?" Re-tokenising earlier turns' text can change token boundaries, so the trainer would score different tokens from those sampled.	bank level::senior ch::10_21_23_26_27_33
[Ch 1] [B] Cosine similarity vs Euclidean distance for embeddings?	Cosine ignores length and measures direction; on normalised vectors both give the same ranking, because ‖a − b‖² = 2 − 2cos(a, b) for unit vectors, so a cosine of 0.6 is a squared distance of 0.8. Use cosine when length is an artefact of the encoder (text length, token frequency), which is why vector databases normalise. When not to: if the norm was trained to mean something, as with item popularity in an unnormalised two-tower recommender or the sharpness of a query in attention, the raw dot product is the right score. Follow-up: "is a cosine of 0.3 high?" For random 768-dimensional vectors the cosine has standard deviation 1/√768 ≈ 0.036, so 0.3 would be about eight standard deviations; but trained embeddings are anisotropic (unrelated texts often score 0.6–0.8), so thresholds must be calibrated per model, and mean-centring helps.	chapter ch::1
[Ch 1] [B] What is the SVD and its main use?	A = UΣVᵀ: rotate, stretch each axis by a singular value, rotate again. Truncating to the top k singular values gives the best rank-k approximation (Eckart–Young), which is the basis of PCA, matrix factorisation, compression and LoRA's intuition. A number: a rank-16 factorisation of a 4,096 × 4,096 matrix has 131,072 parameters instead of 16.8 million, 0.78%. Follow-up: "how does it relate to eigendecomposition?" The singular values are the square roots of the eigenvalues of AᵀA (for [[3, 0], [4, 5]], √45 = 6.708 and √5 = 2.236), and the SVD exists for any rectangular matrix while an eigendecomposition needs a square one and need not be orthogonal.	chapter ch::1
[Ch 1] [I] A test is 95% sensitive with a 5% false positive rate; prevalence is 1%. What share of positives are real?	About 16%: 0.95 × 0.01 / (0.95 × 0.01 + 0.05 × 0.99). In natural frequencies, of 10,000 cases 100 are positive; the test catches 95 and falsely flags 495 of the 9,900 negatives, so 95 of 590 flags are real. The lever that matters is the false positive rate, not recall: halving it to 2.5% lifts precision to 28%, while raising recall to 99% only reaches 17%. Follow-up: "what do you do about it in production?" Report precision at the operating threshold rather than accuracy, choose the threshold from the costs of each error, and add a second, more specific stage (a stronger model or human review) for the flagged cases.	chapter ch::1
[Ch 1] [I] Relate L2 regularisation to Bayesian inference.	It is MAP estimation with a zero-mean Gaussian prior on the weights. With Gaussian noise of variance σ² and a prior of variance τ², the negative log posterior is the squared error over 2σ² plus ‖w‖²/(2τ²); multiplying by 2σ² gives ridge regression with λ = σ²/τ². So noisy data or a tight prior means more regularisation: with σ² = 1 and τ² = 0.5, λ = 2, and in the one-weight example of 1.4 the estimate shrinks from 0.929 to 0.8125. A Laplace prior gives L1, whose kink at zero produces exact zeros. Follow-up: "is weight decay in Adam the same as L2?" No: an L2 term added to the gradient gets divided by Adam's √v, so heavily updated weights are barely regularised; AdamW applies the decay directly to the weights (Chapter 7.1).	chapter ch::1
[Ch 1] [I] Why is cross-entropy preferred to squared error for classification?	It is the negative log-likelihood of the labels, so minimising it is maximum likelihood, and it penalises confident mistakes without bound. The mechanism is in the gradient: with a sigmoid output σ(z), squared error gives (σ − y)σ′(z), and σ′ vanishes when the model is confidently wrong, while cross-entropy cancels the σ′ and gives σ − y.
