At z = −4 with true label 1, σ = 0.018 and σ′ = 0.0177, so the squared-error gradient is −0.017 against −0.982 for cross-entropy, about 57 times smaller exactly when learning is needed. Cross-entropy is also convex in the weights for logistic regression; sigmoid plus squared error is not. When not to: unbounded penalties make cross-entropy sensitive to mislabelled examples, so with noisy labels use label smoothing or a robust loss. Follow-up: "what does label smoothing do to the gradient?" The target becomes (1 − ε)y + ε/K, so the gradient s − y_smooth is zero once the model reaches that target, and logits stop growing.	chapter ch::1
[Ch 1] [I] What does KL divergence measure, and where does it appear in LLM training?	The extra expected log-loss from using q instead of p. In RLHF it is the penalty β · KL(π ‖ π_ref) that keeps the policy close to the reference: a reverse KL, estimated on samples from the policy, so it is mode-seeking, and β is the exchange rate between reward and drift (large β, little reward hacking but little gain). The KL-regularised objective has the closed-form optimum π* ∝ π_ref · exp(r/β), which is the starting point of DPO (Chapter 18.6). Implementations differ in where the KL goes: subtracted from the reward per token (PPO in the InstructGPT style) or added to the loss (GRPO). And in which estimator: on the example of 1.9, k1 = −log r has per-sample variance 0.153 and k3 = r − 1 − log r only 0.0058, with the same mean. It also appears in distillation, forward KL from teacher to student offline and reverse KL for on-policy distillation. Follow-up: "why reverse and not forward KL for the penalty?" Forward KL would need samples from the reference and would reward covering everything the reference does; the penalty is meant to stop the policy going where the reference would not, which is what reverse KL punishes.	chapter ch::1
[Ch 1] [S] Why does LoRA work, in linear-algebra terms?	The weight change needed for a task has low effective rank, so it is well approximated by a product of two thin matrices, ΔW = BA, cutting trainable parameters from d·k to r(d + k): 131,072 instead of 16.8 million for a 4,096 × 4,096 layer at rank 16. The evidence is that fine-tuning can be done in a surprisingly low-dimensional random subspace (Aghajanyan et al., 2020, on intrinsic dimension) and that measured updates have rapidly decaying spectra. B starts at zero so the model starts exactly at the base weights. When not to: tasks that need a lot of new knowledge or a large distribution shift, such as continued pretraining on code or a new language, where LoRA lags full fine-tuning; the flip side, reported in "LoRA Learns Less and Forgets Less" (Biderman et al., 2024), is that it also forgets less of the base model. Follow-up: "how would you check the low-rank assumption?" Take a full fine-tune, compute the SVD of W_ft − W_base per layer, and measure how much of the squared Frobenius norm the top r singular values hold (Chapter 20.3).	chapter ch::1
[Ch 1] [S] How would you smooth click-through rates for items (restaurants, products, ads) with few impressions?	Use a Beta prior fitted to the distribution of rates across all items (empirical Bayes) and rank by the posterior mean (clicks + α)/(impressions + α + β), which shrinks small samples towards the global rate. With a Beta(2, 38) prior, 3 clicks in 10 impressions becomes 0.10 rather than 0.30, while 300 in 1,000 stays at 0.29. Fit α and β by the method of moments: with mean m and variance v of the rates, α + β = m(1 − m)/v − 1, after removing the binomial noise from v, or by maximising the beta-binomial likelihood. Trade-off: one global prior shrinks niche items towards the wrong mean, so use a prior per category when there is enough data. Follow-up: "is the posterior mean the right thing to rank new items by?" Not for exploration: it never gives a new item the chance to prove itself, so sample from the Beta posterior (Thompson sampling) or add an upper confidence bonus (Chapter 43).	chapter ch::1
[Ch 1] [I] Derive the gradient of softmax followed by cross-entropy.	The softmax Jacobian is ∂sᵢ/∂zⱼ = sᵢ(δᵢⱼ − sⱼ), and L = −Σᵢ yᵢ log sᵢ. By the chain rule ∂L/∂zⱼ = −Σᵢ yᵢ(δᵢⱼ − sⱼ) = sⱼ − yⱼ, because the 1/sᵢ from the log cancels and Σyᵢ = 1. For logits (2, 1, 0) with the second class true, s = (0.665, 0.245, 0.090) and the gradient is (0.665, −0.755, 0.090). Practical point: compute log-softmax directly (log-sum-exp with the max shift) rather than log(softmax), which underflows to log 0 for very negative logits. Follow-up: "what if y is a soft target, as in distillation?" The same derivation holds for any y that sums to 1, so the gradient is still s − y.	chapter ch::1
[Ch 1] [I] Why can gradient descent not use a learning rate above 2/λ_max?	On a quadratic with curvature λ, a step multiplies the distance to the minimum by (1 − ηλ), which shrinks only if |1 − ηλ| < 1, i.e. η < 2/λ; in many dimensions the stiffest direction, the largest Hessian eigenvalue, sets the limit. With λ = 10 the limit is 0.2: at 0.19 the error oscillates and shrinks, at 0.21 it grows by 10% a step. The flattest direction then converges at rate 1 − ηλ_min, so the number of steps scales with the condition number λ_max/λ_min, which is what momentum and adaptive optimisers fight. Follow-up: "how do you measure λ_max for a large network?" Power iteration with Hessian–vector products, each costing about two backward passes; doing so shows the edge-of-stability effect, where sharpness rises to about 2/η during training (Chapter 7.7).	chapter ch::1
[Ch 1] [S] Derive the policy gradient, and explain why a baseline helps.	Write ∇θ E_{p_θ}[R] = Σ R ∇p_θ = Σ p_θ R ∇log p_θ = E_{p_θ}[R ∇log p_θ], using ∇p = p∇log p, so you can sample responses, score them and weight the gradient of their log-probability (REINFORCE). Because E[∇log p_θ] = ∇Σp_θ = 0, subtracting any baseline b from R leaves the expectation unchanged but can collapse the variance. On a two-outcome example with rewards 10 and 9 the per-sample variance is 22.6 without a baseline and 0 with b = 9.5, against a true gradient of 0.25; with ten actions it falls from 26.8 to 0.066. PPO uses a learned value function as the baseline, GRPO the group mean and RLOO the leave-one-out mean. Follow-up: "is the group mean an unbiased baseline when it includes the sample itself?" Not exactly: rᵢ minus the mean over G samples equals (G − 1)/G times rᵢ minus the mean of the other G − 1, so it is the unbiased leave-one-out advantage scaled by a constant, which only rescales the gradient.	chapter ch::1
[Ch 1] [S] What is importance sampling, and why does PPO clip its ratio?	E_p[f] = E_q[(p/q) f]: samples from q, reweighted by p/q, estimate expectations under p. PPO's ratio π_θ/π_old is exactly this correction, because the batch was generated by the policy before the update. The cost is variance, measured by E_q[w²] = Σp²/q: reweighting from q = (0.5, 0.3, 0.2) to p = (0.7, 0.2, 0.1) leaves an effective sample size of about 86 per 100, but a q that gives the third outcome only 1% drops it to 52, because those rare samples carry weight 10. For a sequence the ratio is a product of per-token ratios, so the variance grows with length; clipping bounds each update's dependence on any one sample at the cost of some bias. Follow-up: "where else does this bite in LLM RL?" When the inference engine and the trainer compute slightly different probabilities for the same tokens, the samples are off-policy even at step zero, and truncated importance weights are used to correct it (Chapter 19.10).	chapter ch::1
[Ch 1] [I] You are comparing two language models with different tokenizers. Which loss do you compare?	Bits per byte, BPB = total loss in nats / (ln 2 × bytes of text), on the same text. Per-token loss and perplexity favour whichever tokenizer makes more, easier predictions. Example: model A at 2.0 nats per token with 4.0 bytes per token is 0.721 bits per byte, and model B at 2.3 nats per token with 4.8 bytes per token is 0.691, so B is better despite its higher per-token loss. Follow-up: "is bits per byte fully fair across languages?" Not quite: UTF-8 spends two to four bytes on many non-Latin characters, so compare models on the same text rather than comparing languages with it.	chapter ch::1
[Ch 1] [S] What is the ELBO, and what is the gap between it and the log-likelihood?	For a latent-variable model, log p(x) = log E_q[p(x, z)/q(z)] ≥ E_q[log p(x, z)/q(z)] by Jensen's inequality, for any q; that lower bound is the ELBO, and the gap is exactly KL(q(z) ‖ p(z | x)). In the two-state example of 1.10, log p(x) = −0.693, the ELBO with a uniform q is −0.916, and the gap of 0.223 nats equals that KL; with q equal to the posterior the bound is tight. VAEs, diffusion models and EM all optimise it. Follow-up: "why do VAEs use the reparameterisation trick rather than the score-function estimator?" z is continuous and the decoder is differentiable, so writing z = μ + σε lets gradients flow through the sample with much lower variance than REINFORCE.	chapter ch::1
[Ch 1] [B] Why is PCA computed from the SVD of the centred data matrix?	If X is centred and X = UΣVᵀ, then the covariance XᵀX/(n − 1) = VΣ²Vᵀ/(n − 1): the right singular vectors are the principal components and σᵢ²/(n − 1) are the variances along them. Using the SVD avoids forming XᵀX, which squares the condition number and loses precision. Forgetting to centre makes the first component point at the mean of the data. Follow-up: "how many components do you keep?" Enough to explain a chosen share of variance (say 90–95%), or the elbow of the scree plot, checked against the downstream task.	chapter ch::1
[Ch 2] What is a p-value?	The probability of observing a result at least as extreme as this one if there were no real effect. It is not the probability that the null is true, not the probability that the result is a fluke, not the size or importance of the effect, and p > 0.05 does not mean "no effect". Example: 5.0% against 5.4% conversion with 20,000 users per arm gives z = 1.80 and p = 0.07: noise alone produces a gap that large about one experiment in fourteen. If the question people actually want answered is "how likely is it that the treatment helps?", that is a Bayesian quantity, a posterior probability that needs a prior; with a flat prior it is roughly 1 − p/2 for the sign of the effect. Follow-up: "is p = 0.049 really different from p = 0.051?" No; report the effect with its interval and make the decision on costs (2.12), not on which side of 0.05 it fell.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] What is statistical power and why does it matter?	The probability of detecting an effect of a given size if it exists, usually targeted at 80%. Underpowered tests miss real effects and, when they do find something, exaggerate its size: with 17% power, the significant positive estimates average 2.5 times the true effect, and about 1% of significant results have the wrong sign. That is why effects found in small tests shrink in follow-ups. Follow-up: "can you compute power after the test from the observed effect?" Not usefully: "observed power" is a one-to-one function of the p-value and adds nothing; compute power for the minimum effect you decided in advance was worth detecting.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] How do you compute the sample size for an A/B test?	From the baseline rate or variance, the minimum detectable effect, α and power: n ≈ (z₁₋α/₂ + z₁₋β)² × variance terms / MDE² per arm, where (1.96 + 0.84)² ≈ 7.85. At a 5% baseline, detecting a +5% relative lift needs about 122,000 users per arm and +2% about 753,000: halving the MDE quadruples n. Then divide by daily traffic to get a duration, rounded up to whole weeks. Follow-up: "122,000 per arm is six weeks of traffic; what now?" Raise the MDE if a smaller effect would not change the decision, reduce variance with CUPED (ρ = 0.5 saves 25% of the sample), analyse only triggered users, choose a more sensitive metric, or decide the test is not worth running.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] What are guardrail metrics?	Metrics you commit not to harm, such as latency, cancellations, delivery time, revenue, or for an LLM feature cost per conversation and refusal rate; a launch needs the primary metric up and no meaningful guardrail degradation. "No significant degradation" is weak evidence when the test is underpowered for the guardrail, so the careful version is a non-inferiority test: decide a margin in advance (say latency may not rise by more than 2%) and require the upper end of the 95% interval on the degradation to sit below it. Follow-up: "the primary metric is up but a guardrail is down; do you ship?" That trade-off should have been priced in the overall evaluation criterion before the test; if it was not, escalate with both effects and their intervals rather than deciding alone.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] How do you build a confidence interval for a median?	Bootstrap: resample the independent units with replacement, recompute the median, take the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles. On 2,000 log-normal order values the median is 20.3 with a bootstrap interval of (19.7, 20.9). Resample users, not orders, if users have several orders. When not to: with very small samples or heavily discrete data the percentile bootstrap of a median is unreliable, and a different interval (an order-statistic interval, or a smoothed bootstrap) is better. Follow-up: "how do you compare medians between two arms?" Bootstrap each arm independently and take percentiles of the difference, or use quantile regression; do not use Mann–Whitney, which tests something else (2.2).	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] What is CUPED and why does it work?	Adjust each user's outcome by a pre-experiment covariate, Y − θ(X − X̄) with θ = cov(Y, X)/var(X); because X is measured before randomisation, the estimate stays unbiased, and the variance falls by the factor 1 − ρ². That follows from minimising Var(Y − θX) = Var(Y) − 2θCov + θ²Var(X) over θ. With ρ = 0.82, as in 2.5, variance drops 67%, the same as running the test with three times the users. When not to: a covariate measured after the treatment starts can be affected by it and biases the estimate; new users have no history. Follow-up: "what if the pre-period metric is a weak predictor?" Use a model's prediction from many pre-treatment features as the covariate (CUPAC), or regression adjustment with a treatment × covariate interaction (Lin's estimator), which is never less precise asymptotically.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] Your test was significant on day 3 of a planned 14. Do you stop?	Not to declare a win: stopping at the first significant look inflates false positives (about 14% with five looks instead of 5%), and early effects often include novelty. Continue to the planned horizon, or use a sequential method designed for interim looks: with O'Brien–Fleming boundaries over five looks, an early stop needs |z| above 4.56 at the first look. Two exceptions. You can and should stop early for harm, when a guardrail is clearly breached. And the business framing matters: the cost of waiting (a delayed gain, blocked traffic) against the cost of a false ship, which is low for an easily reversed change. Follow-up: "the product manager insists on shipping now" Offer a staged rollout to a small share with a holdout, so the decision can be revisited, and propose a sequential design for the next test.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] What is sample ratio mismatch?	The observed split differs significantly from the planned one, signalling broken assignment or logging; results are untrustworthy until it is explained. A planned 50/50 split that comes out 50,500 vs 49,500 gives a chi-square p of about 0.0016. Common causes: bots filtered differently in each arm, redirects or crashes that lose users from one arm, a trigger condition that depends on the treatment. Debug by slicing the ratio by day, platform and browser to see where it breaks. Follow-up: "one of fifty tests has SRM with p = 0.04; is that alarming?" On its own, no, one in twenty will by chance; platforms usually alert on a much stricter threshold such as p < 0.001.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] How do you handle 15 secondary metrics?	Pre-register one primary metric; treat secondaries as supporting evidence; control the false discovery rate with Benjamini–Hochberg, or the family-wise error with Holm when the metrics gate a decision; label segment findings exploratory and confirm them in follow-up tests. With 15 metrics and no real effects, the chance that at least one is significant at 5% is 1 − 0.95¹⁵ = 54%. Follow-up: "Bonferroni or Holm?" Holm, always: it gives the same guarantee and rejects at least as much.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] Why is interleaving more sensitive than A/B testing for rankers?	Each user sees both rankers merged in one list, so preference is measured within users, removing the between-user variance that dominates an A/B test of a ranker; industry reports put the traffic saving at one to two orders of magnitude. The trade-off: it measures which ranker users prefer, not the business impact of shipping it, so use it to screen candidates and confirm the winner with an A/B test. Follow-up: "when can interleaving mislead?" When the outcome is a page-level metric (total orders, session length) rather than per-item clicks, or when the rankers differ in properties such as diversity that only show at the level of the whole list.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] What is difference-in-differences and its key assumption?	The change in the treated group minus the change in the control group: if the treated city goes from 10 to 13 orders per user and the control city from 8 to 10, the estimate is 3 − 2 = 1. It assumes parallel trends without treatment, which you check on several pre-period points, not one. Use clustered standard errors at the level of the treated unit (city), and remember that few clusters means wide intervals. Follow-up: "what if cities adopted the treatment at different times?" The standard two-way fixed-effects regression can then be biased when effects vary over time, because already-treated units act as controls; use an estimator designed for staggered adoption, or compare each cohort only with not-yet-treated units.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] How would you test a new courier dispatch algorithm?	User-level randomisation is invalid because arms share couriers, and the measured difference can even have the wrong sign at full rollout. Use a switchback design (city × time slots of 30–60 minutes) or geo randomisation, with slot-level analysis, clustered errors, buffers for carryover, and metrics such as delivery time, courier utilisation, cancellations and orders per hour. Power comes from the number of slots, not orders: 3 cities × 14 days × 24 hourly slots is about 1,000 units, so size the test on slot-level variance. Follow-up: "what goes wrong with switchbacks?" Carryover (orders assigned in one slot are delivered in the next) and time trends; discard or buffer the boundaries and balance the assignment across hours of the day.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] The A/B test shows +3% orders but the effect disappears after full rollout. Why?	Novelty; interference (treatment took supply from control, inflating the difference); a winner's-curse overestimate from an underpowered test (at 17% power significant estimates average 2.5 times the truth); seasonality; or a changed mix of users at full rollout. Diagnose with the effect by day since exposure (novelty decays), a long-term holdout (measures the effect at 100%), the original test's power, and whether the change touched shared supply. Follow-up: "how do you prevent it next time?" Pre-compute power, shrink estimates towards a prior from past experiments when forecasting impact, and keep a holdout after launch.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] How would you measure the effect of a loyalty programme that was launched without an experiment?	Define the estimand first, usually the effect on members (ATT). Then use difference-in-differences against comparable cities or users not yet eligible, or matched, propensity-weighted or doubly robust (AIPW) comparisons on observed confounders such as pre-period order frequency, checking pre-trends and overlap. Do not adjust for anything the programme itself changes, such as post-launch activity, which is a collider-style error. In the chapter's simulation the naive difference was 3.1 against a true effect of 1.0, and IPW recovered 1.03. Follow-up: "what about unobserved confounding?" Run a sensitivity analysis (how strong would a hidden confounder need to be to explain the effect away?), look for an instrument or a threshold rule, and above all randomise the next expansion.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] How do you target vouchers to maximise incremental orders?	Uplift modelling on randomised voucher data (T- or X-learner, causal forests, or a doubly robust learner), ranking users by predicted incremental effect rather than predicted ordering probability: the users most likely to order are often "sure things" who would have ordered anyway, and a propensity model spends the budget on them. Evaluate with Qini curves on held-out randomised data and confirm with a holdout test. Follow-up: "you only have observational voucher data" Then propensity-adjusted learners under ignorability, with the warning that the targeting will be only as good as the confounders you measured; budget for a randomised test to calibrate it.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] Your metric is clicks per session and you randomised by user. How do you get its standard error?	The session is not the independent unit, so the naive Bernoulli formula over sessions is wrong. Use the delta method on user-level totals, Var(Ȳ/X̄) ≈ [Var(Ȳ) − 2R·Cov(Ȳ, X̄) + R²Var(X̄)]/μ_X², or bootstrap users. In the 2.1 example the naive SE is 0.170 points, the delta method 0.198 and the user bootstrap 0.197: the naive one is 14% too small, so its tests reject too often. Follow-up: "how would you know the naive formula is wrong without the derivation?" Run A/A tests: with the naive SE, more than 5% of them come out significant.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] A Mann–Whitney test is significant but the medians of the two arms are equal. Is that a contradiction?	No. Mann–Whitney tests whether P(X_treatment > X_control) differs from 0.5, stochastic dominance, not medians; arms with equal medians but different spread or skew can differ on it. It becomes a test of medians only under a pure location-shift assumption. U/(n₁n₂) is the ROC-AUC between the two samples, so the effect size to report is that probability, for example "a random treated user spends more than a random control user 53% of the time". Follow-up: "what would you use for medians?" A bootstrap of the difference in medians, or quantile regression.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] What is an A/A test, and what do you conclude if 21 of 200 A/A tests are significant at 5%?	Both arms get the same experience, so about 5% (10 of 200) should be significant and the p-values should be uniform. Getting 21 or more by chance has probability about 0.1%, so the pipeline is understating variance or the split is broken: check for analysis at a finer unit than randomisation (sessions instead of users), missing clustering, SRM, or metrics with heavy outliers. Follow-up: "how do you use A/A tests routinely?" Replay historical data through many random splits to validate every new metric and variance formula before it is used in a real decision.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] How do you A/B test an LLM feature?	Randomise users or conversations, never individual responses, and fix the model version, prompt and decoding settings for the test. Expect extra variance from stochastic outputs and correlation between turns: with 5 turns per conversation and intra-conversation correlation 0.3, the design effect 1 + (m − 1)ρ = 2.2 means a per-turn analysis understates the variance by more than half. Quality metrics are often LLM-judge scores, which must be calibrated against human labels and held fixed; cost per conversation, latency percentiles and refusal or escalation rates are guardrails. An offline evaluation on a fixed set comes first, and the online test answers whether the offline gain reaches users. Follow-up: "the new model wins on judge scores but users' resolution rate is flat" Suspect the judge (verbosity or style bias), check whether the feature was triggered for the users who matter, and treat the online behavioural metric as primary.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] A pricing change shows +2% revenue with p = 0.06. Do you ship?	Probably, and say why with numbers. p = 0.06 means z = 1.88 and an SE of 1.06 points, so the 95% interval is −0.1% to +4.1%; with a flat prior the probability the effect is positive is 0.97, the chance it is worse than −1% is 0.2%, and the expected loss from shipping is about 0.01 points against an expected gain of 2. A sceptical prior (most changes are small) shrinks the expected gain to about 0.9 points with P(positive) ≈ 0.90, so put the smaller number in the business case. When not to: if the change is hard to reverse (prices customers notice) or a guardrail moved, run longer or stage the rollout with a holdout. Follow-up: "isn't this p-hacking?" No, provided the metric and the decision rule were fixed in advance; reasoning about expected loss is a different, explicit decision rule, not a way to rescue a result after the fact.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 2] Model B beats model A by 1.5 points on a 1,000-question benchmark. Is it real?	Not from that number alone; analyse it paired. If the models disagree on 12% of questions, the paired SE is √((0.12 − 0.015²)/1000) = 1.09 points, z = 1.37 and p = 0.17: not distinguishable from noise. Detecting a true 1.5-point gain with 80% power at that disagreement rate needs about 4,200 questions. Also ask whether the gain exceeds the baseline's seed-to-seed spread (one training run each is a sample of one), whether questions are clustered, whether the evaluation sampled at temperature above zero with one sample per question, and whether B was the best of many checkpoints (selection makes the gain optimistic). Follow-up: "how many seeds would you need?" n ≈ 2 × 7.85 × σ²/δ² per arm: with a seed spread of 0.4 points and a 0.3-point gain, 28 per arm.	chapter ch::2
[Ch 3] Explain the bias-variance trade-off.	Expected error = bias² + variance + noise. Simple models underfit (high bias), flexible models overfit (high variance). Regularisation, more data and model choice move the balance; learning curves diagnose which side you are on. A strong answer defines the expectation: over training sets D drawn from the same source, at a fixed x, with bias = f̄(x) − f(x) and variance = E_D[(f̂_D − f̄)²]; the cross terms vanish because the noise has mean zero and E_D[f̂_D − f̄] = 0. Give a number: estimating a mean from 4 points with σ² = 1, shrinking ȳ to 0.9ȳ when μ = 2 trades 0.04 of bias² for 0.0475 less variance, so the biased estimator wins. Expect the follow-up "does this apply to LLMs?": past the interpolation threshold, test error can fall again (double descent), and single-epoch pretraining is mostly bias-limited, so scaling laws replace the U-curve (3.2).	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] L1 vs L2 regularisation?	L2 shrinks all weights smoothly and handles correlated features well; L1 drives some weights to exactly zero, performing feature selection, because its constraint region has corners on the axes. The rigorous version is the one-dimensional problem ½(w − z)² + λ|w|, whose solution is soft-thresholding, sign(z)·max(|z| − λ, 0): any weight whose data pull |z| is below λ sits exactly at zero, whereas L2 gives z/(1 + λ), never zero. With λ = 0.5 and z = 0.3, L1 gives 0 and L2 gives 0.2. Trade-off: with correlated features L1 picks one arbitrarily and can flip between them across resamples, so use elastic net when you need both sparsity and stability. Follow-up: "is weight decay the same as L2?" Under SGD yes, under Adam no (question 18).	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] Why do we need a validation set and a test set?	Validation is used for decisions (hyperparameters, features, thresholds), which makes it optimistic; the test set, used once, gives an unbiased estimate. The optimism is real and measurable: choosing the best of 50 equally good configurations inflates the winner by about two standard errors, purely from selection. When data is small, nested cross-validation (tuning inside each outer fold) gives an honest estimate of the whole procedure at k_outer × k_inner times the cost. Follow-up: "what if you have already looked at the test set many times?" Then it has become a validation set; say so in the report and collect or hold out a fresh one.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] What is data leakage? Give examples.	Information unavailable at prediction time entering training: target-derived features, future data in aggregates, preprocessing fitted on all data, the same entity in train and test, duplicates across splits. Name the test that catches each: ask of every feature "is it known at the prediction moment?", split by time and by entity, fit preprocessing inside folds, deduplicate before splitting. In the chapter's simulation a timestamp feature on drifting data gives AUC 0.633 under a random split and 0.521 under a time split. The LLM version is benchmark contamination: test items in the pretraining corpus.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] How would you handle class imbalance?	Right metrics (PR-AUC, precision/recall at threshold), class weights, threshold tuning by cost, resampling only on training folds if needed, probability correction afterwards. The senior point is that a well-specified model trained with log loss already ranks correctly, so "do nothing, choose the threshold from costs" is often the best answer: in the chapter's simulation, class weights change test AUC from 0.8737 to 0.8740 but inflate the mean prediction from 0.028 to 0.295. If you do reweight by k, divide the odds by k afterwards. Follow-up: "when does reweighting genuinely help?" When the model is too constrained, or the class too rare, for the optimiser to fit it at all.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] Explain Adam.	Momentum (running mean of gradients) plus per-parameter adaptive step sizes (running mean of squared gradients), with bias correction for the early steps. AdamW applies weight decay separately from the gradient update. Why bias correction: both averages start at zero, so after step 1 with β₁ = 0.9 the first moment is 0.1g and with β₂ = 0.999 the second is 0.001g²; dividing by 1 − βᵗ restores g and g², so the first step is ±η for every parameter, which is also why Adam needs warm-up. ε (about 1e-8) only guards against division by zero. Cost: two extra fp32 states, 8 bytes per parameter. When not to: well-tuned SGD with momentum still often generalises slightly better on long vision runs, and convex problems are better served by L-BFGS. Follow-up: "why AdamW?" An L2 term inside Adam is divided by √v, so frequently-updated weights are barely regularised.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] When would you use MAE, MSE or a quantile loss?	MSE when large errors are disproportionately costly (predicts the mean); MAE when robust to outliers (predicts the median); quantile when you need a buffer, such as an ETA you will meet 90% of the time. Derive the first: E[(Y − p)²] = Var(Y) + (E[Y] − p)², minimised at the mean; for the pinball loss, the balance point has a fraction q of the mass below it. Consequence worth saying: an ETA trained with MSE is late about half the time. Follow-up: "how do you evaluate a quantile model?" Pinball loss at the same q, plus coverage: the fraction of outcomes below the prediction should be close to q.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] How do you validate a model that predicts next week's demand?	Walk-forward validation with expanding or rolling windows, a gap equal to the forecast horizon, features computed point-in-time, metrics per segment, and comparison with seasonal-naive baselines. Use several folds, not one, because a single week can be unusual (a holiday), and report the spread across folds. Use WAPE rather than MAPE when some series are near zero (Chapter 4.5). Follow-up: "the model beats the baseline on average but not in December": seasonal regimes need either their own features or a separate evaluation slice.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] How does target encoding work and what can go wrong?	Replace a category with the (smoothed) mean target of that category. Computed naively, each row's own label leaks into its feature; use out-of-fold encoding with smoothing, and past-only data for time series. Smoothing shrinks small categories to the prior: with prior 0.10, smoothing 20 and 3 positives in 5 rows, the encoding is (3 + 2)/(5 + 20) = 0.20, not 0.60. The leak is largest exactly for rare categories, where one row's label moves the mean most, so naive encoding looks great on validation and fails on new data. Follow-up: "what do you use at serving time?" The encoding fitted on all training data, refreshed on the same schedule as the model.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] Your model has 0.99 AUC offline. What do you do?	Suspect leakage: check feature importances for a dominant feature, review feature timestamps against the prediction moment, check splits by time and entity, look for duplicates, and compare with simple baselines. A fast test: retrain without the top feature; if AUC collapses to a plausible level, that feature is the leak. Also ask what a human expert or the previous system achieves; a result far above every reference is the warning sign. Only after these checks consider that the problem is genuinely easy, and then ask whether it needed ML at all.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] What does early stopping do and why does it regularise?	Stops training when validation loss stops improving; it limits how far parameters move from their initial values, restricting effective model capacity. For a quadratic loss, gradient descent from zero for t steps with learning rate η behaves approximately like L2 regularisation with λ ≈ 1/(ηt): directions with high curvature are fitted early, low-curvature (noise-dominated) directions late. Trade-off: it costs nothing and needs no tuning beyond patience, but it ties the regularisation strength to the learning rate and schedule. Follow-up: "why does it help with noisy labels?" Networks fit clean patterns first and memorise mislabelled examples late.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] How do you frame "reduce late deliveries" as an ML problem?	Clarify the decision (promise times, dispatching, restaurant prep alerts), then choose a framing whose output that decision uses: quantile ETA regression for promises, a late-risk classifier for proactive alerts. Define the label (minutes late vs promise), the prediction moment, features available then, error costs, and the baseline. Make the costs concrete: if a late order costs about five times as much goodwill as a slightly pessimistic promise costs conversion, the promise should sit near the 1 − 1/(1 + 5) ≈ 0.83 quantile, which is why a 0.8–0.9 pinball loss is the natural target. Name the failure mode: promises affect behaviour (customers cancel long promises), so the offline metric must be checked with an A/B test.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] Your validation metric improves but production does not. What could be wrong with your training setup?	Leakage, splits that do not mirror production (random vs time, entity overlap), a training distribution different from serving (selection bias in logged data), train-serve skew in features, a proxy label misaligned with the business metric. Order the investigation by cost: log the serving features and diff them against the training features for the same rows (skew is the cheapest to confirm), re-run the offline evaluation with a time split, then fit a domain classifier between training and recent production inputs; an AUC well above 0.5 says the populations differ, and its top features say where. Follow-up: "everything checks out": then the proxy label is wrong, or the online test is underpowered.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] When should you not use ML?	When rules achieve the goal, when there is no reliable label or feedback loop, when errors are unacceptable and unexplainable, when data is too small, or when nobody will act on the prediction. A concrete threshold helps: with 50 positives a year, a model's precision estimate at any threshold has a standard error of several points, so a rule plus human review is more honest. ML also carries ongoing costs (monitoring, retraining, on-call), so a heuristic delivering 80% of the value often wins. Follow-up: "how do you decide later whether to add ML?" Ship the rule, log its decisions and outcomes, and use that log as the training data and baseline.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] Does the bias-variance trade-off apply to large neural networks and LLMs?	The decomposition always holds; the U-shaped curve does not. Past the interpolation threshold (parameters ≈ examples), gradient descent picks the minimum-norm interpolating solution, which gets smoother as parameters grow, so test error falls again: double descent. In the chapter's random-features run, test MSE peaks at 135 at p = n = 100 and falls to 0.49 at p = 4,000, while a ridge penalty of 10 removes the peak (0.13). LLM pretraining sees most tokens once, so it is bias-limited, and loss falls as a power law in scale (Chapter 13). Variance returns when data is repeated or in small fine-tunes. Follow-up: "is overfitting impossible then?" No: multi-epoch training on limited data, fine-tuning and benchmark tuning all overfit in the classical way.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] Explain covariate, label and concept shift, and how you would detect and correct each.	Covariate shift changes p(x) with p(y | x) fixed: correct with importance weights p_test(x)/p_train(x), estimated by a domain classifier. Label shift changes p(y) with p(x | y) fixed: rankings survive, and you correct probabilities by multiplying the odds by the ratio of new to old prior odds (10% to 2% prevalence turns a 0.5 into 0.155). Concept shift changes p(y | x): only fresh labels reveal it. Trade-off: weights cost variance; the effective sample size is n·exp(−δ²) for a δ-standard-deviation Gaussian shift, 37% at δ = 1. Follow-up: "how do you monitor?" Input drift (PSI, domain-classifier AUC) for the first two, and a trickle of fresh labels for the third.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] What is benchmark contamination and how would you detect it?	It is target leakage for LLMs: test items, or close paraphrases, appear in the pretraining or fine-tuning data, so the score measures recall rather than ability. Detect it with n-gram or embedding overlap between benchmark and corpus, canary strings, comparing accuracy on items released before versus after the training cut-off, and checking whether rephrased or perturbed items score much lower. Mitigate with decontamination filters, private held-out sets, and fresh items written after the cut-off (Chapter 23.6). Trade-off: overlap filters miss paraphrases and over-filter common text. Follow-up: "a model scores 15 points higher on old items than on new ones of the same difficulty": strong evidence of contamination, but check that difficulty is truly matched.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 3] Is L2 regularisation the same as weight decay?	For plain SGD, yes: adding λw to the gradient and shrinking the weights by (1 − ηλ) each step give the same update. Under Adam, no: the L2 gradient term is divided by √v along with everything else, so parameters with large or frequent gradients are hardly regularised and rare ones are over-regularised. AdamW decays the weights directly, outside the adaptive rescaling, which is why it generalises better and why transformer recipes use it with decay around 0.1 on matrices but not on norms or biases. Follow-up: "why not decay norms and biases?" They have few parameters, do not cause overfitting, and decaying a norm's gain fights the scale the network needs.	chapter ch::3
[Ch 4] Define precision and recall, and give a case where each matters more.	Precision: of the items flagged, the share that are truly positive. Recall: of the true positives, the share flagged. Precision matters more when false alarms are costly (blocking good customers' orders); recall matters more when misses are costly (food-safety complaints, account takeovers). Add the number that makes it concrete: with 1% positives, a classifier with 90% recall and a 5% false positive rate flags 0.009 + 0.0495 of all items, so its precision is only 15%. Follow-up: "how do you pick between them?" Put a cost on each error and pick the threshold that minimises total cost (4.1).	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] Why is accuracy a bad metric for fraud detection?	With 1% fraud, predicting "not fraud" for everything is 99% accurate. Accuracy is dominated by the majority class; precision, recall and PR-AUC focus on the rare class you care about. Accuracy also hides which error was made, and the two errors have very different costs in fraud. When is accuracy fine? Balanced classes with roughly symmetric error costs, such as many multiple-choice benchmarks, though even there it should come with a confidence interval.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] What does ROC-AUC measure?	The probability that a randomly chosen positive is scored above a randomly chosen negative. It measures ranking quality across all thresholds and ignores calibration. It equals U/(n₁n₀), the Mann–Whitney statistic, which is why it can be computed from ranks in O(n log n), and ties count as half a correct pair (average ranks). It is invariant to prevalence and to any monotone transform of the scores. Its weakness is that it averages over thresholds you will never use: for a screen that may flag at most 1% of items, report partial AUC up to FPR 0.01 or recall at that rate. Follow-up: "is AUC a decision metric?" No: it does not tell you what happens at your threshold, so pair it with precision and recall at the operating point.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] MAE or RMSE?	MAE is in the target's units, robust to outliers, and is minimised by the median. RMSE punishes large errors more and is minimised by the mean. Choose by the cost of errors: if one 30-minute ETA miss is much worse than three 10-minute misses, RMSE (or a quantile loss) fits better. A quick number: one 20-minute miss adds as much squared error as a hundred 2-minute misses. RMSE ≥ MAE always; a large gap between them signals a few big misses, worth inspecting before choosing.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] What is F1 and when would you use Fβ instead?	The harmonic mean of precision and recall, high only when both are high. Fβ with β > 1 when recall matters more, β < 1 when precision matters more. Better still: choose the threshold with explicit costs. Example: P = 0.9, R = 0.3 gives F1 = 0.45, much lower than the arithmetic mean of 0.6, which is the point of the harmonic mean. F1 also ignores true negatives entirely, so it is unsuitable when correctly leaving negatives alone matters.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] ROC-AUC or PR-AUC for a problem with 0.5% positives?	PR-AUC, or precision and recall at the operating point. Under heavy imbalance, the false positive rate stays tiny even with many false positives, so ROC-AUC looks great while precision is poor. The PR-AUC baseline is the positive rate (0.005), which makes progress visible. Two caveats a strong answer adds: PR-AUC depends on prevalence (the same scores give AP 0.12 at 1% positives and 0.48 at 10%), so it is not comparable across datasets or months with different base rates; and compute average precision, not a trapezoidal area, which is optimistic with coarse scores (0.31 against 0.08 in the chapter's example).	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] What is calibration and when does it matter?	Predicted probabilities match observed frequencies. It matters whenever the probability is used as a number: multiplying p(order) by basket value, comparing p(click) × bid across ads, setting expected-cost thresholds, or combining heads of a multi-task model. Measure with a reliability diagram and ECE; fix with Platt, isotonic or temperature scaling on held-out data. Add that ECE depends on binning (prefer equal-mass bins), is biased upwards on small samples and hides the direction of the error, so report log loss or Brier with it. Family matters: deep networks tend to be over-confident, random forests under-confident at the extremes. Follow-up: "does calibration change AUC?" Not with a monotone recalibration.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] You trained on 10% of negatives. What happens to the predictions and how do you fix them?	They are inflated, because the model saw positives ten times more often than reality. Correct with q = p / (p + (1 − p) / w) with w = 0.1. Ranking (AUC) is unaffected, because the correction is monotone. The derivation: the model's odds estimate positives over negatives at each x, and keeping a fraction w of negatives divides the true odds by w, so p/(1 − p) = (1/w)·q/(1 − q); solve for q. A raw 0.5 becomes 0.091. Follow-up: "and with class weights of 10 on positives?" Identical correction, since weighting positives by 10 is equivalent in expectation to keeping 10% of negatives.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] Micro, macro or weighted F1 for a 300-class product taxonomy?	Macro (and per-class) if the long tail matters, because micro is dominated by the frequent classes. Report micro too as an overall volume view, plus a confusion analysis of the most confused class pairs. In the chapter's ten-item example micro-F1 is 0.70 and macro 0.65, and only macro shows the rare classes failing. Trade-off: per-class F1 on classes with a handful of test items is extremely noisy, so pool rare classes for reporting or show intervals. For a hierarchy, also report accuracy at each level.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] How do you choose a classification threshold?	From the business: minimise expected cost with explicit FP and FN costs; or maximise recall subject to a precision floor; or respect a capacity limit (top N per day for manual review). Tune on validation data, and revisit when base rates change. With calibrated probabilities, the cost-minimising threshold has a closed form: act when p × cost_FN > (1 − p) × cost_FP, so with a miss costing 200 and a false alarm 30, the threshold is 30/230 ≈ 0.13. That closed form is a reason to calibrate.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] Why is MAPE dangerous for demand forecasting?	It divides by the actual value, so small actuals (night hours, small zones) explode the error, and it is undefined at zero. WAPE (total absolute error over total actual) is stable and weights errors by volume. In the chapter's four-point example a single target of 0.5 pushes MAPE to 0.83 while WAPE is 0.12. MAPE is also asymmetric: an over-forecast can exceed 100% error while an under-forecast cannot, so optimising it biases forecasts low.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] Model B beats model A by 0.003 AUC. Do you ship it?	Not on that alone. Get a confidence interval on the difference with a paired bootstrap over users. Check slices, calibration and latency cost. Then ask whether offline AUC gains have historically translated into online gains for this system; if yes and the difference is significant, run an A/B test. A strong answer sets a minimum effect worth acting on before looking: if past experiments show online gains only for AUC improvements above about 0.005, a significant 0.003 is real but not worth B's extra complexity, latency or serving cost. Significance answers "is it real?"; the minimum effect answers "does it matter?". Follow-up: "the interval is [0.001, 0.005]": real but small; ship only if B costs nothing extra, or bundle it with other changes in one A/B test.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] Your offline metric improved but the A/B test shows no change. What are possible reasons?	Proxy mismatch (clicks vs orders), offline candidate set different from production, leakage or train-serve skew, feedback-loop bias in offline labels, an effect too small for the test's power, or segments moving in opposite directions. Investigate each with data, not opinions. Order by cost: compute the test's minimum detectable effect first (if it is larger than the effect you expected, the test could not have seen it), then diff logged serving features against training features, then check per-segment results. Follow-up: "how would you prevent this next time?" Track, across past launches, how well offline deltas predicted online deltas.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] How do you build the metric set for a new ML feature?	Start from the business goal (north star), choose an online primary metric that reflects it and is sensitive enough to move in a test, add guardrails and counter-metrics, then pick an offline metric and validate that it predicts the online one. Write them down before building the model. Sensitivity is quantitative: the primary metric must move detectably within your traffic, so prefer a per-session conversion over a monthly retention number that would need months of data. Counter-metrics catch gaming: optimising clicks, watch orders per click.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] How would you compute a confidence interval for NDCG@10 on a search test set?	NDCG is computed per query and averaged, so bootstrap over queries (or over users, if one user issues many queries): resample them with replacement, recompute the mean NDCG each time, take percentiles. For comparing two rankers, bootstrap the per-query difference. Because NDCG@10 is a mean of per-query values, the normal approximation also works: the standard error is the standard deviation of per-query NDCG over √(number of queries), so 1,000 queries with a per-query standard deviation of 0.3 give ±1.96 × 0.3/√1000 = ±0.019. The paired difference has a much smaller spread than either ranker alone.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] Explain the unbiased pass@k estimator and why the naive one is wrong.	Sample n ≥ k solutions, count c correct, and estimate pass@k = 1 − C(n − c, k)/C(n, k): the probability that a random k-subset of your samples contains at least one correct one. Its expectation equals the true pass@k. The naive 1 − (1 − c/n)^k is biased low, because (1 − ĉ)^k is convex in ĉ and Jensen's inequality pushes its average above (1 − p)^k. With n = 10, c = 3, k = 5 the estimates are 0.917 and 0.832. Follow-up: "how large should n be?" Several times k, because the estimator's variance grows as k approaches n; and report pass@k only with the n used.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] How many test items do you need to measure 80% accuracy to within ±1 point?	n = 1.96² × 0.8 × 0.2/0.01² ≈ 6,150, because the 95% half-width is 1.96√(p(1 − p)/n); ±2 points needs about 1,540, so halving the error bar quadruples the cost. To compare two models, pair them on the same items: if they disagree on 10% of 1,000 items, the standard error of the difference is about 1.0 point, against 1.8 for separate test sets. Then test with McNemar's (b − c)²/(b + c). Follow-up: "the items come in clusters of related questions": the effective sample size is smaller, so use a clustered standard error or bootstrap over clusters.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] How would you evaluate a reward model, and an LLM's calibration?	A reward model is judged by pairwise accuracy on held-out preference pairs, which is AUC over matched pairs. Compare it with the human agreement rate, which is the realistic ceiling (around 73% for the InstructGPT labellers), not with 100%, and slice by how clear-cut the human preference was. For calibration, choose the confidence signal (answer-token probability, verbalised confidence, or agreement across samples), then draw a reliability diagram with equal-mass bins and report ECE alongside Brier. Expect post-trained models to be over-confident relative to their base models, and fix token probabilities with temperature scaling on a held-out set. Follow-up: "reward-model accuracy went up but RL got worse": accuracy is not robustness under optimisation pressure; check for reward hacking on the policy's own outputs.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 4] Your PR-AUC fell from 0.40 to 0.31 month on month with no model change. What happened?	Probably the base rate fell. PR-AUC depends on prevalence: with the same score distributions, average precision is 0.48 at 10% positives and 0.12 at 1%. Check the positive rate first, then a prevalence-invariant metric such as ROC-AUC or recall at a fixed false positive rate on both months. If those are stable, the model is unchanged and the precision at your threshold has fallen because positives are rarer, which may still warrant moving the threshold. If ROC-AUC fell too, look for drift in inputs or labels. Follow-up: "what do you put on the dashboard?" PR-AUC next to the prevalence, and precision and recall at the operating threshold.	chapter ch::4
[Ch 5] [B] Why do we need non-linear activations?	Without them, any stack of layers is a single linear function: W₂(W₁x + b₁) + b₂ = (W₂W₁)x + (W₂b₁ + b₂), one matrix and one bias, so depth buys nothing and the model can only draw straight decision boundaries. With a non-linearity between layers, a one-hidden-layer network can approximate any continuous function on a bounded domain given enough units, and XOR needs only two ReLU units (5.1). Follow-up: "then why go deep?" Depth composes features, representing many functions with exponentially fewer units than one wide layer would need.	chapter ch::5
[Ch 5] [B] Why did ReLU replace sigmoid in hidden layers?	Sigmoid saturates and its gradient is at most 0.25, so gradients vanish through many layers; ReLU has gradient 1 for positive inputs and is cheap. The number: ten sigmoid layers can shrink a gradient by 0.25¹⁰ ≈ 10⁻⁶. ReLU's weakness is dead units: a unit whose input is negative for every example passes no gradient and never recovers, typically after a too-large learning rate (on the chapter's spiral MLP, all units die at learning rate 10). Leaky ReLU fixes that; GELU and SiLU are smooth variants that give slightly better results in transformers, and modern LLMs use the gated SwiGLU. Follow-up: "where is sigmoid still used?" Output layers for binary probabilities and gates (LSTMs, SwiGLU's SiLU).	chapter ch::5
[Ch 5] [I] Derive the gradient of softmax with cross-entropy with respect to the logits.	p − y (divided by n for a batch mean): the softmax Jacobian and the log combine into this simple form. Explicitly: ∂pᵢ/∂zⱼ = pᵢ(δᵢⱼ − pⱼ); then ∂L/∂zⱼ = −Σᵢ (yᵢ/pᵢ)·pᵢ(δᵢⱼ − pⱼ) = −yⱼ + pⱼΣᵢyᵢ = pⱼ − yⱼ, using Σyᵢ = 1. Check with logits [2, 1, 0] and true class 0: p = [0.665, 0.245, 0.090], gradient [−0.335, 0.245, 0.090]. The gradient is bounded in [−1, 1] and never saturates, unlike softmax with squared error. Follow-up: "how do you compute it stably?" Subtract the max logit before exponentiating, and compute log-softmax directly.	chapter ch::5
[Ch 5] [I] What problem do Xavier and He initialisation solve?	Keeping activation and gradient variance roughly constant across layers so signals neither vanish nor explode; He accounts for ReLU zeroing half the inputs. Derivation: a unit's pre-activation variance is fan_in × Var(w) × E[x²], so the forward pass needs Var(w) = 1/fan_in; the backward pass gives the same with fan_out; Xavier compromises with 2/(fan_in + fan_out), and He uses 2/fan_in for ReLU. The number: with std 0.01 at width 256, each ReLU layer multiplies the signal's scale by about 0.11, so 20 layers leave 10⁻¹⁹ to 10⁻²⁰. Follow-up: "what about residual networks?" Scale each block's output projection by 1/√(2L), as GPT-2 does, or zero-initialise it.	chapter ch::5
[Ch 5] [I] Batch norm vs layer norm, and why transformers use layer norm.	Batch norm normalises across the batch per feature; layer norm across features per example. Layer norm does not depend on batch size or sequence length and behaves the same in training and inference. Batch norm uses batch statistics in training and running averages at evaluation, so small batches (with 4 examples the batch mean has half a standard deviation of noise), padding tokens counted in the statistics and distribution shift all create a train–eval gap; autoregressive generation, one token at a time, has no meaningful batch at all. Most LLMs use RMSNorm, which drops the mean subtraction and the bias and is cheaper with no loss of quality. Follow-up: "why does BN help at all?" Its smoothing of the loss landscape allows larger learning rates; the original "internal covariate shift" explanation was challenged by Santurkar et al. (2018).	chapter ch::5
[Ch 5] [I] How does dropout work at train and test time?	Train: zero each unit with probability p and scale the rest by 1/(1 − p). Test: use all units unchanged. Why the scaling: a kept-or-dropped unit has expectation (1 − p)h, so dividing by (1 − p) makes the expected training activation equal h, matching test time; with p = 0.3 a kept unit becomes 1.43h. It regularises by training an implicit ensemble of thinned networks that share weights. When not to use it: LLM pretraining mostly uses none, because a single pass over the data gives little to overfit; and it interacts badly with batch norm, whose statistics are collected under dropout's extra variance. Follow-up: "Monte Carlo dropout?" Keep dropout on at test time and average several passes for an uncertainty estimate.	chapter ch::5
[Ch 5] [I] Why use learning-rate warm-up?	Early gradients and Adam's moment estimates are unreliable; large early steps can push training into bad regions. Warm-up ramps the rate up gently. The sharp version: Adam's first update moves every coordinate by about the learning rate whatever its gradient, because the second-moment estimate comes from a single gradient, so at step one the update is large in every direction at once. Warm-up over a few hundred to a few thousand steps (often about 1% of training) lets the estimates settle. Follow-up: "does post-norm need more warm-up than pre-norm?" Yes, its gradients at initialisation are poorly scaled across layers (Chapter 10.5).	chapter ch::5
[Ch 5] [S] Your network's training loss does not decrease. Walk through debugging.	Check data and labels; initial loss vs ln(k); overfit one batch; gradient flow; learning-rate range test; train/eval modes; then capacity and regularisation. Make each step concrete: for 10 balanced classes the initial loss should be ln 10 ≈ 2.30, and a much larger value means the output layer starts over-confident; a correct model and loop drive the loss on 20 examples to nearly zero within a few hundred steps; per-layer gradient norms of exactly zero point to a detach, a frozen parameter or dead ReLUs. The order matters because each check is cheaper than the next and rules out a whole class of bug. Follow-up: "loss goes down but validation does not": now it is a generalisation question (Chapter 3), not a bug.	chapter ch::5
[Ch 5] [S] Why is bf16 preferred over fp16 for training large models?	Same exponent range as fp32, so it avoids overflow and underflow without loss scaling, at slightly lower mantissa precision that training tolerates. The bit layouts: bf16 is 1 sign, 8 exponent and 7 mantissa bits (max about 3.4 × 10³⁸, epsilon 0.0078); fp16 is 1, 5 and 10 (max 65,504, smallest normal 6.1 × 10⁻⁵). Large activations or attention logits overflow fp16, and small gradients underflow it, which loss scaling only partly fixes. The cost of bf16's 7 mantissa bits is that tiny updates vanish (1 + 1/300 rounds to 1), so master weights and optimiser states stay in fp32. When fp16 is still fine: inference, and small models with dynamic loss scaling, especially on hardware without fast bf16. Follow-up: "and fp8?" It needs per-tensor or per-block scaling factors (Chapter 7.6).	chapter ch::5
[Ch 5] [I] What do residual connections do, and why are they needed?	A residual block computes x + F(x), so its Jacobian is I + ∂F/∂x: the gradient always has a direct path to every layer, and each block only learns a correction to the running representation. Without them, the gradient through L layers is a product of L factors and vanishes unless every factor is near 1 (0.5²⁰ ≈ 10⁻⁶). The catch is the opposite failure: if each branch is not small, (1 + a)ᴸ explodes (1.5²⁰ ≈ 3,300), so branches are scaled by about 1/√(2L), zero-initialised, or fed normalised inputs (pre-norm). Follow-up: "what does deleting one block of a trained ResNet do?" Very little, because the network behaves like an ensemble of many mostly short paths (Veit et al., 2016).	chapter ch::5
[Ch 5] [S] Why does training need so much more memory than inference, and what can you do about it?	Reverse-mode differentiation computes every parameter's gradient in one backward pass, but each layer's backward step needs the activations from its forward step, so all of them must be kept: activation memory grows with depth × batch × width (× sequence length for transformers), and for large models it exceeds the weights. On top come gradients and optimiser states: with Adam in mixed precision, about 16 bytes per parameter against 2 for bf16 inference. Remedies, by cost: smaller micro-batches with gradient accumulation (throughput only), activation checkpointing (about one extra forward pass, roughly 33% more compute, for most of the activation memory), and sharding optimiser states and weights across GPUs (communication). Follow-up: "why not forward-mode?" It needs one pass per input direction, millions of passes.	chapter ch::5
[Ch 5] [S] Should you use dropout and batch norm in a modern LLM?	Generally neither. LLMs use layer normalisation, nowadays RMSNorm in a pre-norm position, because it is independent of the batch, identical at training and inference, and works one token at a time during generation, where batch statistics are meaningless. Dropout is usually off in pretraining: each token is seen about once, so there is little overfitting to prevent, and dropout would cost useful signal per step. Both choices reverse in other regimes: batch norm remains strong in convolutional vision models with large batches, and dropout (or other regularisers) returns when fine-tuning on a few thousand examples for several epochs. Follow-up: "how do you regularise a large pretraining run, then?" Mainly with more data, weight decay around 0.1 and early stopping of the schedule.	chapter ch::5
[Ch 6] Write a training loop for binary classification from memory. What does each line do?	The loop in 6.4: model.train(); for each batch, move it to the device, opt.zero_grad(set_to_none=True) to clear the last step's gradients, compute logits, loss = F.binary_cross_entropy_with_logits(logits, y), loss.backward() to fill .grad, optionally clip_grad_norm_, opt.step() to update, sched.step() if the schedule is per step. Then model.eval() and torch.no_grad() for validation, and save model, optimiser and scheduler state when validation improves. Sanity number: at step 0 a balanced binary loss should be about ln 2 = 0.693; much higher means the initial logits are too large. The follow-up is usually "where does clipping go?": after backward and before step, and after scaler.unscale_ if you use fp16.	chapter ch::6
[Ch 6] Why do we call zero_grad() ? What happens if we don't?	Because backward() adds into .grad rather than overwriting it. Without zero_grad, step t updates with g₁ + … + gₜ, so the effective step grows roughly linearly with t (like momentum with no decay) and training usually diverges within a few hundred steps. The same accumulation is what makes gradient accumulation free (6.8): you deliberately skip zeroing for K micro-batches. set_to_none=True (the default in recent versions) sets .grad to None instead of filling zeros, which saves a memory write and lets optimisers skip parameters that received no gradient. Follow-up: "when would you not zero every step?" During gradient accumulation, and that is the only legitimate case.	chapter ch::6
[Ch 6] What is the difference between model.eval() and torch.no_grad() ? Do you need both?	They are orthogonal, and for validation you need both. model.eval() changes module behaviour: dropout is turned off and BatchNorm uses its running statistics instead of the batch's. torch.no_grad() changes autograd: no graph is recorded, so no activations are saved and memory drops to roughly that of inference. With eval but no no_grad you get correct numbers and wasted memory; with no_grad but no eval you get noisy, wrong numbers (with dropout 0.1, a random 10% of activations are zeroed and the rest scaled by 1/0.9 = 1.11 on every pass). Follow-up: torch.inference_mode() is a stricter, slightly faster no_grad; its tensors can never enter autograd later, so use it for serving and pure evaluation, and no_grad when outputs may be reused in a graph. The opposite mistake, forgetting model.train() after validation, leaves dropout off for the rest of training.	chapter ch::6
[Ch 6] Why return logits and use BCEWithLogitsLoss rather than sigmoid + BCELoss ?	Numerical stability. The fused loss computes log σ(x) as −softplus(−x), which is exact for any x, while sigmoid followed by log first rounds σ(x) to zero for very negative x and then takes log 0. PyTorch's BCELoss clamps the log at −100 to avoid infinities, so for a logit of −120 with label 1 it reports a loss of 100 instead of 120 and a gradient of exactly zero, while BCEWithLogitsLoss reports 120 and a gradient of −1. The model therefore cannot learn its way out of a confident mistake. The same holds for CrossEntropyLoss, which fuses log-softmax. Follow-up: "where do you apply the sigmoid then?" Only at inference, when you need a probability, and in calibration code.	chapter ch::6
[Ch 6] Your fine-tuning run OOMs on a 24 GB GPU. Walk through the options in the order you would try them, with the trade-off of each.	First compute: full fine-tuning with AdamW costs 16 bytes per parameter (6.5), so a 1.5B model already needs 24 GB before activations and cannot fit however small the batch. In order: (1) halve the micro-batch and add gradient accumulation, which keeps the optimisation identical (with token-sum normalisation) and costs throughput only; (2) bf16 autocast, which halves activation memory and is nearly free on recent GPUs; (3) gradient checkpointing, about a third more compute for most of the activation memory; (4) a shorter maximum length, which changes the task; (5) 8-bit Adam, saving 6 of the 16 bytes (the 1.5B model drops to 15 GB) for a small quality risk; (6) LoRA or QLoRA, which removes gradient and optimiser state for frozen weights at some quality cost on hard tasks; (7) FSDP across GPUs or a bigger card. Profile first with torch.cuda.max_memory_allocated() to know whether weights, optimiser state or activations dominate, because that decides which lever pays. Follow-up: "why not just use batch size 1?" Activations are not the problem when the 16 bytes per parameter alone exceed the card.	chapter ch::6
[Ch 6] Explain mixed precision. Why does fp16 need a loss scaler and bf16 not?	Mixed precision runs matrix multiplications in 16 bits (twice the throughput and half the memory traffic of fp32) while keeping fp32 master weights, optimiser state and sensitive ops such as softmax, norms and losses in fp32. fp16 has 5 exponent bits: its smallest normal value is 6.1 × 10⁻⁵ and anything below about 6 × 10⁻⁸ becomes zero, and many gradients of a large model are that small. Scaling the loss by 65,536 multiplies every gradient by the same factor, so a gradient of 10⁻⁸ becomes 6.6 × 10⁻⁴ and survives; the scaler divides it back before the update, skips steps that overflow and adjusts the scale dynamically. bf16 keeps fp32's 8 exponent bits (same range) and gives up mantissa bits instead, so nothing underflows and no scaler is needed. Follow-up: "then why keep fp32 master weights with bf16?" Because bf16 has only 8 bits of precision: 1 + 10⁻³ rounds to 1, so small updates would vanish (Chapter 7). When not to use fp16: any large-model training on hardware with bf16.	chapter ch::6
[Ch 6] How would you resume a training job that crashed at epoch 7 so the result matches an uninterrupted run as closely as possible?	Checkpoint, and restore, everything that determines the future of the run: model, optimiser (Adam's m and v, or the first steps after resume are unwarmed and oversized), scheduler (or the learning rate jumps back to its peak), the GradScaler's scale, the step and epoch counters, all RNG states (torch.get_rng_state, torch.cuda.get_rng_state_all, NumPy, Python's random), and the data position: the sampler's generator state plus the number of batches already consumed this epoch, or a StatefulDataLoader that saves this for you. In DDP each rank restores its own RNG state and the DistributedSampler gets set_epoch(7). Then verify: resume from a checkpoint 100 steps back and compare the loss curve with the original run over those 100 steps; with deterministic kernels it should match exactly, otherwise to within noise. Follow-up: "is bit-for-bit worth it?" Rarely at scale, since non-deterministic kernels already make two uninterrupted runs differ in the last bits; same data order and same optimiser state are what matter.	chapter ch::6
[Ch 6] Training loss is flat from step 0. Give your debugging sequence.	Go from cheap and certain to expensive. (1) Check the initial loss: about ln C for C balanced classes (0.693 for binary); a different value points at the head, the labels or the loss. (2) Overfit one batch of 8–32 examples: the loss should go to near zero within a few hundred steps; if it cannot, the bug is in the model, loss or optimiser, not the data or the capacity. (3) Log per-layer gradient norms (hooks, 6.1): all zeros means a detached graph (.item(), .detach(), no_grad), frozen parameters, or parameters missing from the optimiser (check opt.param_groups). (4) Check the loss inputs: logits, not probabilities, for BCEWithLogitsLoss; long class indices for cross_entropy; shapes equal (the (B,1) vs (B,) broadcast). (5) Check the data: are inputs varied, labels aligned with inputs after shuffling, normalisation sane? (6) Only then sweep the learning rate over 1e-5 to 1e-2. Follow-up: "it overfits one batch but the full run is still flat": the learning rate, the data pipeline or the label noise, not the model code.	chapter ch::6
[Ch 6] When would you choose DDP vs FSDP?	DDP replicates the whole model, gradients and optimiser state on every GPU and all-reduces gradients; it is simplest and fastest when everything fits. FSDP (ZeRO stage 3) shards parameters, gradients and optimiser state across the N GPUs and all-gathers each layer's weights just before it runs. Numbers for a 7B model on 8 GPUs: DDP needs 16 × 7 = 112 GB per GPU and does not fit on an 80 GB card; full sharding needs 112 / 8 = 14 GB per GPU plus activations. The price is communication: DDP's all-reduce moves about 2× the gradient size per step, while FSDP all-gathers parameters in the forward pass, again in the backward pass, and reduce-scatters gradients, about 3×, so roughly 1.5× DDP's volume (much of it overlapped with compute). Intermediate options: ZeRO-1 shards only optimiser state (12 of the 16 bytes) at DDP's communication cost; hybrid sharding shards within a node and replicates across nodes. Use FSDP even when the model fits if the freed memory buys a larger micro-batch or longer sequences. Follow-up: "why 1.5×?" All-reduce = reduce-scatter + all-gather; FSDP adds one more all-gather for the backward pass.	chapter ch::6
[Ch 6] You fine-tune an LLM with gradient accumulation over 8 micro-batches of chat examples of very different lengths. What can go wrong with the loss normalisation, and how do you fix it?	If each micro-batch's loss is a mean over its own target tokens and you divide by 8, every micro-batch gets equal weight, so tokens in short examples get more weight than tokens in long ones. With one micro-batch of 10 tokens at loss 2.0 and one of 90 at 1.0, the naive loss is 1.5 against a true per-token mean of 1.1: the short micro-batch's tokens weigh 9 times more. The gradient is then not the full-batch gradient, and results change with the accumulation setting. Fix: sum the token losses in each micro-batch and divide by the total number of target tokens in the whole window (all-reduced across data-parallel ranks), as in 6.8. Also wrap the first 7 backward calls in no_sync(), flush the leftover window at the end of an epoch, and remember the same issue appears whenever padding or loss masks vary per batch. Follow-up: "is per-sequence averaging ever what you want?" Sometimes, to stop very long examples dominating; but that should be a deliberate weighting, not a side effect of the accumulation count.	chapter ch::6
[Ch 7] Write the AdamW update. Why is weight decay "decoupled"?	See 7.1: m and v are exponential averages of g and g², bias-corrected by 1 − βᵗ, and θ ← θ − η(m̂/(√v̂ + ε) + λθ). With Adam, an L2 term in the gradient is divided by √v, so parameters with large gradients get almost no regularisation; AdamW subtracts lr·wd·p directly, independent of the gradient statistics. A number to show you understand the normalisation: on the first step every coordinate moves by ≈ η whatever its gradient (0.001 for both a 1e-4 and a 1e2 gradient at η = 1e-3). Follow-up: "what does bias correction do?" Without it m₁ = 0.1g and v₁ = 0.05g² (β₂ = 0.95), so early updates would be the wrong size; dividing by 1 − βᵗ undoes the zero initialisation.	chapter ch::7
[Ch 7] Why do transformers need warm-up?	Three reasons, from the most mechanical to the deepest. First, Adam's first steps have a poorly estimated second moment, so every coordinate moves by about the full learning rate in a random-looking direction; with large rates this throws the model into a bad region or blows up attention logits. Second, in post-LN transformers the gradients of the last layers are large at initialisation (Xiong et al., 2020), so a full-size step there diverges. Third, the sharpness view: at initialisation the loss surface is sharp, and a learning rate above about 2/sharpness is unstable (the edge of stability, 7.7); a gradual ramp lets training move into flatter regions where the target rate is stable, so warm-up's real job is to make a high peak rate reachable (Kalra and Barkeshli, 2024). With a few thousand warm-up steps out of hundreds of thousands, the cost is negligible. When it matters less: pre-LN models at small learning rates, and fine-tuning from a trained checkpoint, which already sits in a flat region (a short warm-up is still cheap insurance). Follow-up: "how long?" Long enough that the loss curve has no early spike; typically 0.5–2% of steps, longer when the batch or peak rate is raised.	chapter ch::7
[Ch 7] What is the WSD schedule and why do teams like it?	Warm-up, a long constant phase, then a short decay over the last 10–20% of steps. Any checkpoint in the constant phase can be decayed into a good model, so you can extend runs, run cheap scaling-law and data experiments from branches, and avoid fixing the total length in advance. Number: with five model sizes and four token budgets, cosine needs 20 separate runs; WSD needs five constant-rate runs plus 20 short decays of about 20% each. Trade-off: during the stable phase the loss sits above a cosine run's, so mid-run comparisons are meaningless; compare decayed checkpoints. Follow-up: "what shape should the decay have?" Decay to (near) zero rather than to 10%, and a 1 − sqrt shape was reported slightly better than linear (7.2).	chapter ch::7
[Ch 7] What is the critical batch size?	The batch size beyond which larger batches give diminishing reductions in the number of steps. It is roughly the gradient noise scale tr(Σ)/|G|², grows as the loss falls, and is larger for bigger, better-trained models. The derivation: with batch B the best step achieves a fraction 1/(1 + B_noise/B) of the noiseless improvement, so steps scale as 1 + B_noise/B and examples as 1 + B/B_noise; at B = B_crit you pay 2× in both, at B = 4 B_crit you need 1.25× the minimum steps but 5× the minimum data. In the chapter's logistic regression the noise scale grows from 35.6 examples at initialisation to about 145,000 near the optimum, which is why LLM runs ramp the batch size up. Follow-up: "how would you measure it in a real run?" Compare gradient norms at two batch sizes (small and large) and solve for tr(Σ) and |G|², as McCandlish et al. do, instead of computing per-example gradients.	chapter ch::7
[Ch 7] How does Muon differ from Adam?	Adam normalises each coordinate separately. Muon orthogonalises the whole momentum matrix of a hidden layer (via five Newton–Schulz iterations, which map every singular value s through an odd polynomial and push it into a band near 1), equalising the size of the update in all directions; it is steepest descent under the spectral norm. Updates are scaled by 0.2√max(m, n) so their RMS matches AdamW's (an orthogonal 64 × 32 matrix has RMS 1/8). It applies to 2-D hidden matrices only; embeddings, heads and norm parameters stay on AdamW. Memory: one momentum buffer instead of two, so 4 rather than 8 bytes of state per parameter. Trade-off: extra matrix multiplications per step (a few percent of step time at scale) and the need to control attention-logit growth (Kimi K2's QK-Clip). Follow-up: "why not exact SVD?" Too slow and poorly suited to bf16 on GPUs; the approximate polar factor is good enough because the exact singular values do not matter.	chapter ch::7
[Ch 7] What problem does µP solve? State its main rules for Adam.	Hyperparameters, especially the learning rate, stop transferring across widths under the standard parametrisation. µP scales hidden learning rates as 1/width, initialises the output layer with variance 1/width² and scales attention by 1/d, so the per-step change in each layer's output is width-independent and tuned settings transfer from small proxies. In the chapter's experiment the output change from one Adam step grows from 0.110 to 5.64 as width goes from 64 to 1,024 under SP (about 50×) and stays at 0.03 under µP. Limits: µP covers width only; depth needs residual-branch scaling (depth-µP, CompleteP), and the best learning rate still moves with batch size and run length. Follow-up: "why does the change grow linearly, not with √width?" Adam's update has the same sign pattern as the gradient, so the n contributions to each output add coherently rather than as a random walk.	chapter ch::7
[Ch 7] Why bf16 rather than fp16 for training? Why keep fp32 master weights?	bf16 has fp32's exponent range (8 bits, up to 3.4 × 10³⁸), so gradients do not underflow and no loss scaling is needed; fp16 tops out at 65,504 and flushes values below about 6 × 10⁻⁸. Its 8-bit precision cannot represent small updates to weights of order 1 (1 + 1e-3 rounds to 1, since bf16's spacing near 1 is 0.0078), so updates are accumulated in an fp32 copy. Cost: 4 extra bytes per parameter of the 16 in mixed-precision training. Follow-up: "could you drop the master copy?" With stochastic rounding, which makes the expected rounded value equal the true one, some recipes do; with round-to-nearest, small updates systematically vanish.	chapter ch::7
[Ch 7] What makes fp8 training hard?	Tiny dynamic range: e4m3 tops out at 448 and flushes values below about 0.002 (without scaling, 1e-4 to 5e-4 all become 0), so tensors need fine-grained scaling factors per tile or block. Some operations and states must stay in higher precision (embeddings, output head, norms, softmax, optimiser state, master weights), and matmul accumulation must be in higher precision. Errors can accumulate silently and only show late in a run. The pay-off is roughly 2× matmul throughput over bf16. Follow-up: "which format for gradients?" e5m2 historically (more range); fine-grained scaling has made e4m3 usable for more tensors. Always compare against a bf16 baseline at the same token budget.	chapter ch::7
[Ch 7] Your loss spiked at step 60k and did not recover. Walk through what you do.	Triage, then localise, then fix, then record. Restart from a checkpoint well before the spike (a few hundred to a few thousand steps, since the cause usually builds up).
