Localise with logged signals: the global gradient norm before clipping; per-layer gradient and update norms to find where it started (embeddings, a particular attention block, the output layer); the update-to-weight ratio per layer (a jump from ~1e-3 to 1e-2 is the signature); the max attention logit and output log Z in the steps before. Check the data: replay the same batches after a restart and see whether the spike recurs at the same step (data-driven) or not (numerical, stochastic); inspect those batches for corrupted or extremely repetitive text. Fixes, cheapest first: skip the offending batches, lower the peak rate or β₂, then architectural guards (QK-norm, z-loss) for this or future runs. Decide whether to reset optimiser state: a reset re-creates the stale-moment problem, so keep the state unless it contains NaNs or the spike polluted v. Follow-up: "the spike recurs without the bad data": then it is logit or weight growth, and the per-layer norms will show which.	chapter ch::7
[Ch 7] What are z-loss and QK-norm for?	z-loss penalises log² of the softmax normaliser to stop output logits drifting; QK-norm normalises queries and keys so attention logits stay bounded. Both prevent the logit growth that causes late-training instability. The underlying reason is that softmax is shift-invariant, so the cross-entropy gradient (p − y) sums to zero and nothing restrains the mean logit; in bf16, logits near 300 are spaced 2 apart, so the differences that carry the probabilities are rounded away. With coefficient 1e-4 and log Z = 12 the z-loss adds only about 0.014 to the loss but provides the missing restoring force. Follow-up: "why can't Kimi K2 use QK-norm?" With MLA the keys are not fully materialised at inference, so they used QK-Clip, rescaling query and key weights after each step instead.	chapter ch::7
[Ch 7] What is the edge of stability?	Full-batch GD increases sharpness (the top Hessian eigenvalue) until it hovers at 2/η, where classical analysis of a quadratic predicts divergence, while the loss still falls non-monotonically. It shows that the learning rate shapes which minima are reachable: with η = 0.01, training is confined to regions of sharpness about 200 or less. Adam shows the same on its preconditioned sharpness. Implication: a higher learning rate biases training towards flatter regions, and warm-up is partly a way to reach them gradually. Follow-up: "does it hold with mini-batches?" Roughly, with a lower threshold because noise adds instability; it is a regime, not a precise law.	chapter ch::7
[Ch 7] A paper claims a new optimiser trains 2× faster. What do you check?	That the Adam baseline was tuned as hard (learning rate, β₂, warm-up, weight decay) at the same batch size and schedule; that the comparison is at matched tokens and final decayed loss, across several scales and seeds; and that the gain holds on downstream evaluation, not only on loss. Also check that "2× faster" means fewer tokens to a target loss and not just lower loss at one point mid-run, that wall-clock overhead per step is counted, and that the gain does not shrink with scale (many do). A quick sanity number: a 1.3× token-efficiency gain at 100M parameters that falls to 1.1× at 1B is a different claim from a constant 2×. Follow-up: "what single experiment would convince you?" A learning-rate sweep for both optimisers at two or three model sizes, reported as the loss of the best rate per size, with seed error bars.	chapter ch::7
[Ch 7] You double the length of a pretraining run. How should the weight decay change, and why?	View AdamW's weights as an exponential average of past updates with timescale τ = 1/(ηλ) steps (7.1). At η = 3e-4 and λ = 0.1 that is 33,333 steps. If the run length doubles and λ stays fixed, τ becomes a smaller fraction of the run, so the final weights average over a relatively shorter window; the rule that has worked in practice (Wang and Aitchison, 2024) is to keep τ a fixed fraction of the total steps, so halve λ (or η) when you double the steps. For layers followed by normalisation the equilibrium angular update is √(2ηλ), so ηλ, not η alone, sets their effective learning rate: raising η and lowering λ by the same factor keeps it constant. Trade-off: very small λ lets weight norms grow and effective learning rates decay; very large λ turns the weights into a short-memory average and raises the loss floor. Follow-up: "and if you change the batch size?" The number of steps changes, so τ as a fraction of the run changes too; recompute it.	chapter ch::7
[Ch 7] Derive why the gradient noise scale B = tr(Σ)/‖G‖² is a batch size.	For a step −εg_B on a locally quadratic loss with gradient G, Hessian H and per-example gradient covariance Σ, the expected decrease is ε‖G‖² − ½ε²(GᵀHG + tr(HΣ)/B). Optimising ε gives a best decrease of ΔL_max(∞)/(1 + B_noise/B) with B_noise = tr(HΣ)/(GᵀHG), which reduces to tr(Σ)/‖G‖² when H is proportional to the identity. So B_noise is exactly the batch at which noise halves the progress per step: below it, doubling the batch nearly doubles progress; above it, gains vanish. Check: on a noisy quadratic with B_noise = 9, measured gains are 0.050, 0.252 and 0.450 at B = 1, 9 and 81 against 0.050, 0.250 and 0.450 predicted. Caveat: the quadratic model and H ∝ I are approximations; in practice B_simple is a guide to the order of magnitude. Follow-up: "why does it grow during training?" ‖G‖ shrinks near a minimum while the per-example disagreement tr(Σ) does not.	chapter ch::7
[Ch 8] Explain skip-gram with negative sampling. Why sample noise words from frequency to the 0.75?	Skip-gram learns word vectors by predicting the words around each centre word; the exact objective is a V-way softmax p(o | c) ∝ exp(u_oᵀv_c), which costs a pass over the whole vocabulary per pair. SGNS replaces it with k + 1 binary classifications: maximise log σ(u_oᵀv_c) for the true context word and log σ(−u_nᵀv_c) for k sampled noise words, about 8,000 times cheaper for V = 50,000 and k = 5. The 0.75 power flattens the distribution so rare words appear as negatives more often than their raw frequency would allow: a word 100 times rarer than "the" is sampled only 100^0.75 ≈ 32 times less often. Follow-up: "what does SGNS actually learn?" At the optimum u_oᵀv_c ≈ PMI(o, c) − log k, an implicit factorisation of a shifted PMI matrix (Levy and Goldberg); unlike NCE, it does not recover normalised probabilities.	chapter ch::8
[Ch 8] What problem does fastText solve that word2vec does not?	Out-of-vocabulary and rare words. word2vec has one vector per word seen in training, so a typo, a new product name or an unseen inflection gets no vector at all, and rare words get poorly trained ones. fastText represents a word as the sum of vectors for its character n-grams (3 to 6 characters, with boundary markers, plus the whole word), so "shawerma" shares most of its n-grams with "shawarma" and lands near it, and morphological variants share sub-pieces. Trade-off: many more vectors to store (n-gram buckets are hashed, typically into about 2 million rows), and n-gram overlap can make words that look alike but differ in meaning look similar. Follow-up: fastText is also a fast linear text classifier over averaged n-gram embeddings, a strong 10-minute baseline.	chapter ch::8
[Ch 8] Why do vanilla RNNs struggle with long sequences? How do LSTMs help?	Backpropagation through time multiplies one Jacobian diag(1 − h²)W_h per step, so the gradient from T − t steps back is bounded by ‖W_h‖^(T−t): it vanishes geometrically if the largest singular value is below 1 and can explode if it is above. In the chapter's measurement, after 20 steps an RNN's gradient is 4.6 × 10⁻⁸ of its last-step value (a factor 0.43 per step). The LSTM adds a cell state updated additively, c_t = f_t ⊙ c_{t−1} + i_t ⊙ g_t, whose direct derivative is just the forget gate, so gradients along the cell are scaled by learned gates near 1 rather than a fixed matrix; with forget bias 1 the gradient after 20 steps is about 10⁻². Exploding gradients are handled separately, by clipping. Follow-up: "does the LSTM solve long-range dependencies?" It extends memory to hundreds of steps, not thousands; attention removed the distance problem entirely.	chapter ch::8
[Ch 8] What is the difference between an LSTM and a GRU?	The GRU has two gates instead of three and no separate cell state. Its update gate z interpolates between the old state and a candidate, h_t = (1 − z_t) ⊙ h_{t−1} + z_t ⊙ h̃_t, so one gate does the job of the LSTM's forget and input gates; its reset gate r controls how much of the old state the candidate h̃_t = tanh(W[r_t ⊙ h_{t−1}, x_t]) sees. With three weight blocks instead of four it has 25% fewer parameters (394,752 vs 526,336 at size 256 in PyTorch) and is a little faster. Accuracy is usually similar; LSTMs sometimes win on tasks needing precise counting or long memory because their output gate and separate cell decouple what is stored from what is exposed. Follow-up: "which would you pick?" GRU for small or on-device models, LSTM when you can afford to tune both.	chapter ch::8
[Ch 8] Explain the seq2seq bottleneck and how attention fixes it. How does Bahdanau attention relate to transformer attention?	The encoder compresses everything into one vector; attention lets every decoder step look at all encoder states, weighted by relevance. The transformer keeps the query-key-value weighting, makes it multi-head and scaled dot-product, and applies it within the same sequence instead of recurrence. Concretely, Bahdanau scores each encoder state h_i with a small network vᵀtanh(W_q s + W_k h_i), softmaxes the scores into weights α and feeds Σα_i h_i to the decoder; the cost is O(T_in) per output step instead of a fixed-size bottleneck, and translation quality stopped falling with sentence length. Luong replaced the scoring network with a dot product; the transformer divides it by √d (at d = 64 the raw score's standard deviation is 8, which saturates the softmax), uses separate learned projections for queries, keys and values, runs many heads, and adds self-attention so the encoder and decoder need no recurrence at all, which makes training parallel over positions. Follow-up: "what did the transformer give up?" Recurrence's constant memory per step: attention's cost and KV cache grow with length, which is why linear-recurrent models are back (8.6).	chapter ch::8
[Ch 8] You have 2 million order sessions and need item embeddings for a "you may also like" carousel tomorrow. What do you build first and how do you evaluate it?	Item2vec on sessions (items as words, sessions as sentences), with popular items subsampled; evaluate offline with next-item hit rate@k against a co-occurrence baseline, then an A/B test on carousel CTR and orders. The details that make it work: treat the whole basket as the context window, since basket order is meaningless; keep the 0.75 noise exponent and subsample the head items, or every neighbour list becomes the bestsellers; handle cold-start items (new items have no sessions) with a fallback such as content embeddings or category popularity; and serve with an approximate nearest-neighbour index refreshed daily as the catalogue changes. Evaluation must beat a popularity baseline, not just random, because popularity alone often reaches a respectable hit rate; on 50,000 held-out sessions a hit rate near 10% has a standard error of about 0.13 points, so differences of half a point are real. Also report coverage (fraction of the catalogue ever recommended), since item2vec can collapse onto the head. Follow-up: "when would you move beyond item2vec?" When you need personalisation by user history or context, a two-tower or sequential recommender (Chapter 43).	chapter ch::8
[Ch 8] What is exposure bias and how would you reduce it?	With teacher forcing the decoder is always conditioned on true prefixes during training, but at inference it conditions on its own outputs, so an early mistake puts it in states it never saw and errors compound: at a 1% per-token error rate, a 100-token output is error-free only 37% of the time. Fixes: scheduled sampling (feed the model its own predictions with increasing probability during training; cheap but biased, since the target is not updated after the prefix drifts); sequence-level training that optimises a reward of whole sampled outputs with policy gradients (MIXER, minimum-risk training), which trains on the model's own distribution by construction; and decoding choices that avoid bad prefixes. The senior link is LLM post-training: SFT is pure teacher forcing on demonstrations and inherits exposure bias, while RLHF and RL with verifiable rewards sample whole responses from the policy and score them, so the model learns to recover from its own mistakes (Chapters 18 and 19). Follow-up: "why not just do RL from the start?" Without a teacher-forced warm start the samples are too poor for a reward to give any signal, and RL is far more expensive per token.	chapter ch::8
[Ch 8] When would you still choose an RNN over a transformer in production?	Streaming with constant memory per step, tiny on-device models, very long signals where quadratic attention is too costly (though state-space models now compete there). The reason is the state: an RNN's per-step cost and memory are independent of how much it has read, while a transformer's KV cache grows with every token. A GRU with hidden size 128 holds 128 numbers of state per sequence whatever the length; a transformer at the same position may hold thousands of cached keys and values. Typical cases: keyword spotting and sensor or event streams on a device, real-time anomaly detection over long telemetry, and low-latency features over user event sequences. Trade-off: RNNs train sequentially and are weaker at precise long-range recall. Follow-up: "what about Mamba or linear attention?" They keep the fixed-size state but train in parallel and recall better, so for new long-sequence work they are the first thing to try (Chapter 11).	chapter ch::8
[Ch 8] Why does word2vec keep two embedding tables, and how does that relate to weight tying in language models?	With one table, a word's score as its own context would be ‖u_w‖², large for every word with a large vector, so the model would predict words next to themselves; separate centre (v) and context (u) tables avoid that. In a transformer LM the input embedding plays the role of v and the output (unembedding) matrix the role of u. Weight tying shares them, saving V × d parameters (263M for a 128,256-token vocabulary at d = 2,048, about a fifth of a 1.2B model) and giving rare tokens' input vectors extra gradient from the output side. Small models usually tie; large models usually untie, because the saving is a small fraction of the total and input and output roles benefit from separate vectors. Follow-up: "what goes wrong if you tie and then add tokens?" The new rows serve as both input and output vectors, so their initialisation affects output probabilities immediately; initialise them near the mean embedding.	chapter ch::8
[Ch 9] Why do modern models use subword tokenisation?	Words give short sequences but an open-ended vocabulary: every new name, inflection and typo is out of vocabulary, and rare words get poorly trained embeddings. Characters or bytes cover everything but make sequences about 4 times longer, which costs roughly 4× the compute (the quadratic attention term matters only at long lengths) and forces the model to learn spelling before meaning. Subwords keep frequent words whole and split rare ones into reusable pieces, and with a byte-level base alphabet nothing is ever out of vocabulary. Number: on this book's text a 32k byte-level BPE averages about 4 characters per token. Follow-up: "are tokenizer-free models the future?" Byte-level models with learned patching (for example the Byte Latent Transformer) match tokenized models at scale with better robustness, but most production models still use subwords.	chapter ch::9
[Ch 9] Walk through how BPE is trained and how it encodes a new word.	Training: split the corpus into words, split each word into characters (or bytes) plus an end marker, count every adjacent pair weighted by word frequency, merge the most frequent pair into a new symbol, update the counts, and repeat until the vocabulary reaches its target size; the ordered merge list is the tokenizer. In 9.3's corpus "e" + "r" wins first with 55 occurrences, then "d" + "er" with 45, and "order" is assembled in four merges. Encoding a new word: split it into characters and apply learned merges, in production by repeatedly merging the lowest-rank pair present, which gives the same result as replaying the list. "reordering" was never seen but encodes as re + orderin + g. Follow-up: "what is the complexity?" The naive version is O(merges × corpus); real implementations keep a priority queue of pair counts and update only the words a merge touches.	chapter ch::9
[Ch 9] What is the difference between BPE and WordPiece? What does SentencePiece add?	Both build a vocabulary by merging, but BPE picks the most frequent pair while WordPiece picks the pair that most increases the training likelihood, roughly count(ab)/(count(a)·count(b)), so it prefers pairs that are surprisingly often together over pairs that are merely common. WordPiece encodes with greedy longest-match and marks continuations with "##". SentencePiece is a library, not an algorithm: it runs BPE or Unigram directly on raw text, treating the space as an ordinary symbol "▁", so it needs no language-specific pre-tokeniser (important for Chinese, Japanese and Thai, which have no spaces) and decoding is exactly lossless. Unigram, its other algorithm, prunes a large vocabulary by likelihood and can sample segmentations for subword regularisation. Follow-up: "which do current LLMs use?" Mostly byte-level BPE (tiktoken-style, as in GPT-4 and Llama 3); SentencePiece BPE in Llama 1–2 and many multilingual models.	chapter ch::9
[Ch 9] Why can LLMs struggle to count letters in a word?	The model never sees the letters. "strawberry" reaches it as two or three token ids, and whatever it knows about their spelling is a memorised association per token, not something it can read off the input; counting the r's requires recalling each token's spelling and adding across tokens, two steps that are easy to get wrong. The fix is to change the representation: ask the model to spell the word out first, which produces roughly one token per letter, and then count, which is what reasoning models do on their own. Number: spelling a 10-letter word costs about 10–20 extra tokens, trivial next to a wrong answer. Follow-up: "is this a reasoning failure?" Mostly a perception failure; the same model counts correctly when the letters are separated by spaces.	chapter ch::9
[Ch 9] Your Arabic queries cost far more per request than English ones. Why, and what can you do?	The tokenizer was trained on mostly English text, so Arabic splits into more tokens. Each Arabic letter is also 2 UTF-8 bytes against 1 for Latin, so a byte-level tokenizer with few Arabic merges starts from a longer sequence and compresses it less. Measure it first: run 200 real queries per language through the model's own tokenizer. If, for example, English averages 1.3 tokens per word and Arabic 3.0, a 50-word Arabic request costs 150 tokens against 65, 2.3 times more, and also fills the context faster and often gets worse answers. Levers: compare models whose tokenizers have larger multilingual vocabularies (the cheaper price per token can be the more expensive per request); shorten and cache the shared prompt prefix, which is usually in English anyway; keep outputs short and structured; for high-volume narrow tasks, a smaller fine-tuned model. Trade-off: switching models changes quality as well as cost, so evaluate per language. Follow-up: "would you translate Arabic to English first?" Only if a quality test shows no loss; the translation call costs tokens too and loses dialect and nuance.	chapter ch::9
[Ch 9] You need to classify search queries that mix Arabic, English and Arabizi. Design the text-processing part.	Six stages, in order: NFKC normalisation (folds ligatures and full-width forms); per-token script detection to tag Arabic, Latin and Arabizi (Latin letters mixed with the digits 2, 3, 5, 7 and 9 that stand for Arabic sounds); for retrieval and classical features only, Arabic normalisation (strip diacritics and tatweel, unify alef forms, ta marbuta and alef maqsura) as in 9.6; then a model robust to spelling variants, either fastText-style character n-grams as a fast baseline or a multilingual embedding model fine-tuned on labelled queries; and a transliteration dictionary for the few hundred most frequent Arabizi terms, mined from logs, which covers a large share of traffic. Evaluate with a test set split by script and code-switching pattern, and report per-slice accuracy with confidence intervals, so a good English score cannot hide a poor Arabizi one. Trade-off: heavy normalisation helps matching but hurts generation, so keep the raw text alongside. Follow-up: "how do you get labels for Arabizi?" Sample from logs stratified by script and label a few thousand; use an LLM for pre-labelling with human review.	chapter ch::9
[Ch 9] Estimate the monthly cost of summarising every restaurant review with an LLM, and say how you would cut it in half.	Reviews per month × average tokens in and out × prices; halve it with a batch API, a smaller model for short reviews, summarising only reviews that are shown, and caching instructions. Worked with placeholder prices ($0.50 per million input, $0.05 cached input, $1.50 output): 3 million reviews a month, a 250-token instruction prefix, 150 tokens of review and 60 of summary cost 3 × 10⁶ × (400 × 0.50 + 60 × 1.50) / 10⁶ = $870. Caching the prefix brings it to $532.50, and the batch API (50% off, fine for an offline job) to $266, which already more than halves it. Summarising only reviews that are actually displayed, or skipping reviews under 20 words, cuts N further. The warning sign is a reasoning model: 500 thinking tokens per review would raise the bill to about $2,800, so turn reasoning off for this task. Trade-off: a cheaper model must pass the same quality check on a labelled sample. Follow-up: "how do you know the token counts?" Count them with the model's own tokenizer on a sample of real reviews, per language.	chapter ch::9
[Ch 9] What are the trade-offs of a larger vocabulary?	Shorter sequences (cheaper compute, more text per context window, better coverage of many languages) against larger embedding and output matrices, a costlier output softmax, and rare tokens that get few updates. Numbers: Llama 3's 128,256-token vocabulary at d = 4,096 costs 0.525B parameters per matrix, about 1.05B of an 8B model untied, and the output layer adds 2Vd ≈ 1.05 GFLOP per token, about 6.6% of the forward pass. Compression has diminishing returns: on this book's text, characters per token rise from 1.32 at 300 entries to 3.21 at 4,000 but only 4.02 at 32,000. Scaling studies (Tao et al., 2024) suggest the compute-optimal vocabulary grows with model size, so large multilingual models moved to 128k–256k while small models tie embeddings to afford one. Follow-up: "how would you choose it?" Train a few tokenizers, train small models on each at equal compute, and compare bits per byte and downstream accuracy, not per-token loss.	chapter ch::9
[Ch 9] Model A has lower perplexity than model B on the same test set, but they use different tokenizers. Which is better?	You cannot tell from perplexity, because a token is a different amount of text for each tokenizer: a larger vocabulary makes each prediction harder and raises per-token loss even for a better model. Convert to bits per byte: BPB = (N_tok / N_bytes) · L_tok / ln 2. Example: A at 1.8 nats/token with 3.2 bytes per token has perplexity 6.05 and 0.812 bits per byte; B at 2.0 nats/token with 4.0 bytes per token has perplexity 7.39 but 0.721 bits per byte, so B is the better model of the text by about 11%. Measure bytes on exactly the scored text, after the same normalisation. Downstream task accuracy needs no correction. Follow-up: "where does this bite in research?" Vocabulary ablations and scaling-law fits across model families, where per-token loss silently changes units.	chapter ch::9
[Ch 9] Your SFT model learns to start answers with a stray space, and RL log-probabilities disagree with the inference engine's. What tokenisation bug would you look for?	A seam: prompt and completion were tokenised separately and concatenated. encode(a) + encode(b) differs from encode(a + b) whenever a merge crosses the boundary or a trailing space becomes its own token: in the chapter's example "…is " + "Paris" gives [..., "Ġis", "Ġ", "Paris"] separately but [..., "Ġis", "ĠParis"] jointly. Training then teaches ids the model never sees at inference, the loss mask is off by one at the boundary, and log-probabilities are computed on a different sequence from the one the engine sampled. Fix: render the whole conversation with the chat template, tokenise once, derive the mask from character offsets, and assert in the pipeline that the prompt's ids are a prefix of the full ids; in RL, score exactly the token ids the sampler produced rather than re-tokenising text. Follow-up: "what about special tokens in user text?" Encode untrusted text with special-token parsing off, or a user can forge <|im_start|>system.	chapter ch::9
[Ch 10] Explain self-attention.	Each position computes a query, key and value by linear projections; attention weights are the softmax of scaled query-key dot products; each output is the weighted sum of values. It lets every token gather information from relevant tokens anywhere in the (allowed) context, with routing that is learned and differentiable: a soft dictionary lookup. For T tokens it builds a T × T weight matrix per head, which is where the quadratic cost comes from. The follow-up is usually "what do Q, K and V each do?": the query says what a token is looking for, the key what it offers to be matched on, and the value what it hands over once matched.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] Why divide by √dₖ?	Dot products of dₖ-dimensional vectors with unit-variance entries have variance dₖ, so for dₖ = 128 their standard deviation is about 11. Scores that large saturate the softmax: nearly all weight goes to one key, and the Jacobian diag(p) − ppᵀ goes to zero, so gradients vanish. Dividing by √dₖ brings the scores back to unit scale whatever the head size; in 10-fig-1, an unscaled 128-dimensional head puts 86% of its weight on one key, against 11% when scaled. The follow-up is "is √dₖ sacred?": no. QK-norm and µP-style parameterisations use other scales, and what matters is controlling the score scale at initialisation and during training.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] Why multiple heads?	Each head can attend to different relations (syntax, coreference, position) in its own subspace. Concatenating them is more expressive than one head of the same total size, at the same cost, because one softmax yields one weighting per position, while h heads yield h independent weightings. Trained models show specialised heads, such as previous-token and induction heads (10.3). The follow-up is "can you remove heads?": often yes. Many heads can be pruned with little loss, which is part of why MQA and GQA lose so little quality.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] What is the causal mask?	A mask setting scores for future positions to −∞ before the softmax, so each token only attends to itself and earlier tokens; exp(−∞) = 0 gives them zero weight. It is what makes teacher forcing honest: one forward pass yields a valid next-token prediction at every position, so a T-token sequence gives T − 1 training examples at once. It is required for next-token training and for generation with a KV cache. The follow-up is the bug question: mask with −∞ or a large negative number, never 0, and handle rows where every position is masked, which give NaN.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] BERT vs GPT?	BERT is an encoder with bidirectional attention and a masked-token objective, best for understanding tasks and embeddings. GPT is a decoder with causal attention and a next-token objective, best for generation. BERT learns from the 15% of positions it masks; GPT gets a training signal at every position. The follow-up is "which would you use for classification at 20k requests per second?": a fine-tuned encoder, since BERT-base at 110M parameters costs about 70 times fewer FLOPs per request than an 8B decoder (Chapter 12).	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] Why do transformers need positional encodings, and how does RoPE work?	Attention is permutation-invariant, so without position information "dog bites man" and "man bites dog" look the same. RoPE rotates each 2-D pair of query and key dimensions by an angle proportional to position, with a different frequency per pair. Because R(a)ᵀR(b) = R(b − a), the score depends only on the distance n − m, and it works naturally with caching, since a key's rotation never changes once computed. With base 10,000 and head dimension 128, wavelengths run from about 6 to about 54,000 tokens. Running beyond the trained length still needs scaling tricks (position interpolation, NTK, YaRN) and some long-context fine-tuning, because the slow pairs reach angles never seen in training. The follow-up is "why not ALiBi?": it extrapolates stably but uses distant context weakly, and RoPE plus YaRN won in practice.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] Pre-norm vs post-norm?	Pre-norm, x + f(Norm(x)), keeps the residual path an identity, so signals and gradients pass through a chain of additions untouched, and deep models train stably with short warm-ups. Post-norm, Norm(x + f(x)), normalises the stream after each block; Xiong et al. (2020) showed its gradients near the output are large at initialisation, so it needs long warm-ups and careful learning rates. The trade-off is that pre-norm's residual stream grows with depth, which weakens the later layers. Modern recipes add sandwich or output norms (Gemma 2/3, OLMo 2), scaled residual initialisation and QK-norm. The follow-up is "is post-norm ever better?": at moderate depth with careful tuning it can reach slightly better quality, and DeepNorm makes it trainable at great depth.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] What is the KV cache and how big is it?	It stores the keys and values of past tokens for every layer, so each decoding step computes only the new token's projections and attends over the cache instead of recomputing the prefix. Size = 2 × layers × KV heads × head dim × tokens × bytes per value. For a 32-layer model with 32 KV heads of 128 at 8k tokens in fp16 that is 4.3 GB per sequence; with GQA-8 it is 1.1 GB, and at 128k tokens 17 GB, more than the 8B model's 16 GB of weights. The trade-off is memory for compute: the cache turns quadratic recomputation into linear reads, but it caps batch size and context. The follow-up is "how do you shrink it?": GQA or MLA, 8-bit or 4-bit cache quantisation, sliding windows, paging (vLLM) and prefix sharing.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] What do MQA and GQA change?	They share key/value heads across query heads: one KV head for all queries in MQA, groups (for example 32 queries on 8 KV heads) in GQA. That shrinks the KV cache and the memory read per decoding step by the sharing factor (4× for 32/8), allowing larger batches and longer contexts. GQA keeps quality close to full multi-head attention, while MQA loses a little more. The follow-up is "can you convert an existing model?": yes. Ainslie et al. mean-pool the K and V heads of each group and "uptrain" for about 5% of the original pretraining compute.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] What does FlashAttention do?	It computes exact attention in tiles that fit in on-chip SRAM, with an online softmax, never materialising the T × T matrix in HBM. The trap: its FLOPs are still O(T²d), the same as standard attention. What falls is HBM traffic, from Θ(Td + T²) to Θ(T²d²/M) for SRAM size M, so it is faster and memory-linear in T. The backward pass recomputes the score tiles from the saved softmax statistics instead of storing the T² matrix, which costs extra FLOPs but saves time. FlashAttention-2 improved parallelism over the sequence and the split of work between warps; FlashAttention-3 uses Hopper features (asynchronous loads, warp specialisation, fp8). The follow-up is "does it help decode?": less, because decode has one query per sequence. Its value is in training and prefill, and decode uses split-KV variants.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] What is the computational complexity of a transformer layer?	Per token per layer, the weight matmuls (QKV/O projections plus FFN) cost about 24d² FLOPs and attention over T keys costs 4Td. So a layer over T tokens is O(T·d²) for the matmuls and O(T²·d) for attention. The two are equal at T = 6d, about 24.6k keys for d = 4,096, or about 26.6k for the exact Llama-3-8B shape. Below that the FFN and projections dominate, which covers most pretraining lengths; above it attention does. In causal training the average key count is T/2, so the crossover in sequence length is about 12d. The follow-up is "so is long context expensive because of FLOPs or memory?": at inference mostly memory, because the KV cache must be read every step.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] Why did decoder-only models win for general-purpose LLMs?	One self-supervised objective works on any text, and every position is a training example. Scaling with data and compute is smooth, every task can be expressed as continuation, inference is simple and efficient with KV caching, and instruction tuning goes on top. A strong answer adds the counter-arguments. At equal training FLOPs, encoder-decoders with mixed denoising objectives (UL2, and T5Gemma's adaptation of a decoder into an encoder-decoder) are competitive or better on input-heavy tasks such as summarisation. A prefix LM gets bidirectional attention over the prompt at little cost. Bidirectional encoders still win per FLOP for embeddings and classification. Decoder-only also won on engineering: one stack, one cache and one serving path, which the whole tooling ecosystem now assumes. The follow-up is "would you train an encoder-decoder today?": for a narrow input-to-output task at small scale, possibly; for a general assistant, no, because the ecosystem advantage outweighs the modest efficiency gain.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] How do you extend a model's context length?	Use RoPE scaling (position interpolation, NTK-aware, or YaRN, which stretches only the low-frequency pairs), then fine-tune on genuinely long documents for a few billion tokens with a larger RoPE base. Context parallelism (Chapter 17) handles training memory. For inference memory, use GQA or MLA and cache quantisation: an 8B GQA-8 model needs 17 GB of KV cache per 128k-token sequence in bf16. Then evaluate on realistic long-context tasks (RULER-style multi-needle, variable tracking, aggregation) and not only single needles, which models pass long before they can use the context. The trade-off is that over-stretching hurts short-context quality, so mix short data into the long-context phase. The follow-up is "why YaRN over plain interpolation?": interpolation also compresses the high-frequency pairs that encode local order, so short-range quality drops.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] Why is decoding memory-bound while prefill is compute-bound?	Prefill processes many tokens per weight load (large matrix multiplications); decode processes one token per sequence per step, so each step reads all weights and the cache for little compute. Concretely, the weight matmuls have arithmetic intensity of about B FLOPs per byte for a batch of B sequences in bf16. A GPU with 989 TFLOP/s and 3.35 TB/s balances at about 295 FLOPs per byte, so you need about 300 concurrent sequences to become compute-bound. The senior point is that the KV-cache read does not amortise across the batch, because each sequence has its own cache. Its intensity is the GQA group size (4 for 32 query heads on 8 KV heads), whatever the batch, so long-context decode stays memory-bound. Batching more sequences raises intensity for the weights, which is why continuous batching matters, and cache compression is the lever for long contexts. The follow-up is "what does speculative decoding do to this?": it verifies several draft tokens per weight read, raising intensity without more users.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] Walk me through what happens when an LLM generates one token.	The new token's id selects a row of the embedding matrix. In each layer, the vector is normalised and projected to Q, K and V; RoPE rotates Q and K; K and V are appended to the cache; attention is computed over the cache and added to the residual stream; then the FFN output is added. After the last layer, a final norm and the output projection give logits over the vocabulary, and sampling (temperature, top-p) picks the token. For an 8B model that is about 16 GFLOPs plus attention, and about 16 GB of weights read, so at 3.35 TB/s a single sequence needs about 5 ms per token at best. The follow-up is "where does the time go?": reading weights at small batch, reading the cache at long context, and the sampling step and kernel launches at very small models.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] How many parameters does a decoder have, roughly, and where are they?	Per layer about 4d² for attention (less with GQA) and 8d² for a SwiGLU FFN of matched size, so about 12d² per layer, plus 2 × vocabulary × d for untied embeddings. For d = 4,096, 32 layers and a 128k vocabulary: about 8B, 70% in the FFNs and about 13% in the two embedding matrices. The follow-up is "why does the FFN get the parameters?": attention moves information, while the FFN stores and transforms it, acting as a key-value memory (10.6). Widening the FFN is the cheapest way to add capacity per FLOP.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] Explain the online softmax behind FlashAttention.	Keep a running maximum m, normaliser l and weighted value sum per query. When a tile raises the maximum, rescale the accumulated sums by exp(m_old − m_new), then add the new tile's contributions, and divide by l at the end. The result is exact, because rescaling by exp(m_old − m_new) is just changing the reference point of a softmax, and only one tile of scores exists at a time. The trade-off is a little extra arithmetic for the rescaling, against far less memory traffic. The follow-up is "how is the backward pass done?": store the per-row log-sum-exp, and recompute the score and probability tiles during the backward pass.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] Why is training parallel but generation sequential?	In training the whole target text is known, so the model is fed the true tokens (teacher forcing) and the causal mask lets one forward pass compute the next-token prediction at every position simultaneously. A 4,096-token sequence gives 4,095 losses from one pass. In generation, the input at step t + 1 is the model's own sample from step t, which does not exist until step t finishes, so the tokens must come one at a time. Prefill of the prompt is parallel like training; only new tokens are sequential. The trade-off of teacher forcing is exposure bias: the model never trained on its own errors, which RL-style post-training partly addresses. The follow-up is "how do you generate faster, then?": speculative decoding or multi-token prediction, which guess several tokens and verify them in one parallel pass (Chapter 21).	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] Where does C ≈ 6ND come from, and what does it leave out?	For a linear layer Y = XW, the forward pass costs 2 FLOPs per weight per token. The backward pass computes both ∂L/∂X = (∂L/∂Y)Wᵀ and ∂L/∂W = Xᵀ(∂L/∂Y), each the same size, so it costs 4. Six in total. An 8B model on 15T tokens needs about 7.2 × 10²³ FLOPs. It omits attention FLOPs, about 6·T·d per layer per token in causal training (13% extra for the 8B shape at 8k context), activation recomputation, which adds a forward pass (8ND of hardware FLOPs), and for MoE the fact that N is the active, not total, count. The follow-up is "how do you turn this into days?": divide by number of GPUs × peak FLOP/s × MFU. At 40% MFU on 1,000 GPUs of 989 TFLOP/s, 7.2 × 10²³ FLOPs takes about 21 days.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] Why do trained models put so much attention on the first token, and what do you do about it?	Softmax weights must sum to 1, so a head that finds nothing relevant must still put its weight somewhere. Models learn to dump it on the first token, which every position can see, and build massive activations to make that token win. These "attention sinks" matter in practice: streaming or sliding-window inference that evicts the first tokens collapses, and the massive activations make quantisation harder. Fixes give heads a way to output nothing: an extra 1 in the softmax denominator, a learned sink logit per head (gpt-oss), or an output gate per head (Qwen3-Next). With four zero-score tokens and a sink logit of 2, the sink takes 65% of the weight. The follow-up is "is the sink a bug?": no, it is a learned no-op. The fixes help because they provide the no-op explicitly instead of through a side effect.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 10] What would you change in the 2017 transformer if you trained an LLM today?	Pre-norm with RMSNorm instead of post-LayerNorm, for stability. RoPE instead of sinusoidal or learned absolute positions, for relative positions and context extension. SwiGLU instead of ReLU, for lower loss at equal compute. GQA, or MLA at large scale, to shrink the KV cache. No biases and no dropout in pretraining. QK-norm or soft-capping and z-loss against logit growth. Depth-scaled or µP initialisation. Untied embeddings at large scale. At large scale, also MoE FFNs (Chapter 11). Each is a measured improvement at fixed compute, and together they are the "modern baseline" any new architecture must beat (Chapter 11, 11.8). The follow-up is "which matters most?": for stability, the normalisation choices; for serving cost, GQA or MLA; for quality per FLOP, SwiGLU and MoE.	chapter ch::10
[Ch 11] How does a top-k MoE layer work, and what does it change in compute and memory?	A router scores experts per token; the token goes through the top k experts and their outputs are mixed with the (renormalised) router weights. Compute per token scales with k, memory with the total number of experts; training and serving need expert parallelism and all-to-all communication. For the fine-grained layer in 11.2 (128 experts of width 1,024 plus one shared, top 8, d = 4,096), that is 1,623M parameters in memory but only 113M (7%) used per token. The follow-up is "so is it 15× cheaper to serve?": in FLOPs yes, but at serving batch sizes almost every expert is read each step, so the memory and communication costs follow the total.	chapter ch::11
[Ch 11] Why do MoE models need load balancing? Give two approaches.	Routers collapse onto a few experts: an expert that gets more tokens trains faster and gets chosen more. The Switch/GShard auxiliary loss E·Σ f_e P_e pushes towards uniform load. It is 1.0 when balanced, and it uses f (the hard, non-differentiable load) as a weight on P (the differentiable mean probability). Auxiliary-loss-free balancing adjusts a per-expert selection bias up or down by load, avoiding gradient interference; in the check script it takes loads from 0.009–0.350 to 0.123–0.127. The trade-off: too large an auxiliary weight forces routing to ignore content and costs quality. The follow-up is "why not just a capacity limit?": capacity drops tokens (37% at CF 1.0 for the skewed router in 11.2) rather than fixing the router.	chapter ch::11
[Ch 11] What are fine-grained and shared experts?	Many small experts with more of them active, giving more combinations, and one or more always-on experts holding common knowledge so routed ones specialise. Introduced in DeepSeekMoE and now standard: DeepSeek-V3 has 256 routed experts plus 1 shared, with 8 routed active. Splitting each of 16 experts into 4 smaller ones and activating 4× as many keeps FLOPs constant but raises the number of possible expert combinations enormously, from C(16, 2) = 120 to C(64, 8) ≈ 4.4 × 10⁹. The cost is more routing overhead and smaller matmuls, which are less efficient on GPUs. The follow-up is "how small can experts get?": until the matmuls become too small to run efficiently and routing communication dominates.	chapter ch::11
[Ch 11] Explain MLA. Why does it need decoupled RoPE?	Keys and values are reconstructed from a cached low-rank latent per token, cutting the cache by tens of times: 1,152 bytes per token per layer against 65,536 for full MHA with 128 heads of 128, 57× smaller. The up-projections are absorbed into the query and output matrices at inference, which only works if no position-dependent rotation sits between them. RoPE's rotation depends on the position, so it cannot be folded into a fixed matrix, and position goes through a small extra set of 64 rotary dimensions shared across heads. The follow-up is "MLA vs GQA at equal cache size?": DeepSeek reports MLA matching or beating MHA quality, while GQA at the same cache size loses some; the price is more complex kernels.	chapter ch::11
[Ch 11] Show that causal linear attention is an RNN.	With φ(q)·φ(k) instead of exp(q·k), the output for query t is φ(q_t)ᵀ Σ_{s≤t} φ(k_s)v_sᵀ divided by φ(q_t)ᵀ Σ_{s≤t} φ(k_s). The two sums, S_t and z_t, do not depend on the query, so they can be accumulated step by step: S_t = S_{t−1} + φ(k_t)v_tᵀ, z_t = z_{t−1} + φ(k_t). Each output then needs only the current state. Memory is d_k × d_v per head regardless of length, against a KV cache that grows linearly. The follow-up is "how do you train it fast?": the chunked form, quadratic inside chunks of 64–256 tokens and recurrent between them, which uses tensor cores.	chapter ch::11
[Ch 11] What is the main weakness of SSMs and linear attention? How do hybrids address it?	A fixed-size state cannot store everything, so precise recall from long context degrades: copying, retrieving one fact, multi-hop lookups. Perplexity hides it, and associative-recall benchmarks expose it. Hybrids keep a few full-attention layers for retrieval and make the rest linear-time. With one attention layer in four, an 8B-shaped model's cache at a million tokens falls from 137 GB to about 34 GB. The follow-up is "how do you choose the ratio?": sweep it at small scale and evaluate recall at the target length, at matched training tokens.	chapter ch::11
[Ch 11] What does the delta rule add to linear attention?	It subtracts the state's current prediction for a key before writing the new value, so it overwrites associations instead of summing them; combined with a gate it can forget. It is one step of online gradient descent on ½‖Sᵀk − v‖² with learning rate β: the gradient is k(Sᵀk − v)ᵀ, so S ← S + βk(v − Sᵀk)ᵀ. With a unit key and β = 1, the state returns v exactly afterwards. In the check script's recall test, the additive rule returns a mixture of old and new values ([0, 3, 3, 0]), and the delta rule returns only the new one ([0, 3, 0, 0]). The follow-up is "what is test-time training, then?": replacing the linear memory with a small network trained the same way, as in TTT and Titans.	chapter ch::11
[Ch 11] What is multi-token prediction good for?	Extra training signal from predicting several tokens ahead, and built-in draft heads for speculative decoding. DeepSeek-V3's sequential MTP module keeps the causal chain by feeding the true next token's embedding into each depth, and its second-token prediction is accepted 85–90% of the time, giving about 1.8× decode throughput. The training-signal benefit is mixed and scale-dependent, so the inference speedup is the reliable win. The follow-up is "why not parallel heads?": independent heads predict t + 2 without knowing t + 1, so their drafts are less coherent and accepted less often.	chapter ch::11
[Ch 11] A paper says its linear-time model beats a transformer at 350M parameters. What do you ask?	Same data and tokens? A modern, tuned baseline (RMSNorm, SwiGLU, RoPE, GQA, a tuned learning rate), not the 2017 recipe? Does it hold at larger scale, with a scaling-law fit across three or more sizes? Recall and long-context evaluations, not only perplexity? Real throughput and memory with optimised kernels? Seeds and confidence intervals? A strong answer prioritises: matched tokens and a tuned baseline first, because they explain most claimed wins, then recall, because that is where linear models hide their weakness. One number: at 350M parameters the seed-to-seed spread of downstream accuracy can be a point or more on small benchmarks, so a 1-point win with one seed is not evidence. The follow-up is "what experiment would convince you?": a sweep at three or more sizes with matched data, both arms tuned, recall and long-context evaluations at the target length, and wall-clock measurements.	chapter ch::11
[Ch 11] When would you choose a dense model over an MoE?	Small deployments where memory, not compute, is the constraint; on-device; heavy fine-tuning by teams without MoE expertise; very low or bursty traffic where experts are under-used. The numbers: memory follows total parameters, so a 30B-A3B MoE needs about 60 GB in bf16, like a 30B dense model, while computing like a 3B one. At batch 1, decode reads only the active experts, but tokens route independently, so a batch of 64 touches about 126 of 128 experts, and at serving batch sizes the step reads nearly all weights. Expert parallelism adds all-to-all communication on every MoE layer, which needs fast interconnect, and fine-tuning or RL is less stable because routing shifts. The follow-up is "so when is MoE clearly better?": large-scale serving with high, steady traffic over many GPUs, and training, where a fixed FLOP budget buys a lower loss.	chapter ch::11
[Ch 11] Compare Native Sparse Attention and DeepSeek Sparse Attention. Why is the DSA indexer trained by distillation?	Both make each query attend to a selected subset of earlier tokens. NSA, trained from scratch, combines three branches with learned gates: attention over compressed block summaries, attention over the top-scoring blocks chosen with the compressed scores, and a sliding window. Selection is per block so that memory accesses are contiguous and the kernels are fast. DSA, as used in DeepSeek-V3.2, adds a small fp8 "lightning indexer" to an already trained MLA model. The indexer scores every earlier token, and each query attends to its top 2,048 tokens individually. The main attention becomes O(L·k), while the indexer stays O(L²) but is cheap. The indexer is distilled because top-k selection gives it no useful gradient from the language-model loss, and the dense model's own attention distribution is a ready-made target: a KL loss trains the indexer to predict where the dense model would look, before switching to sparse training. The trade-off: NSA pays a full pretraining run, while DSA retrofits an existing model for about 100B tokens. The follow-up is "what can go wrong?": on tasks needing more than k relevant tokens spread across the context, any fixed top-k loses information.	chapter ch::11
[Ch 11] You are designing a hybrid model. How do you choose the ratio and placement of attention layers, and how do you evaluate it?	Start from the cost target: with one attention layer in four, the KV cache is a quarter of a dense model's, and with one in eight an eighth. Then sweep the ratio at small scale (1:3, 1:7, and pure recurrent as a control) with matched data, and evaluate associative recall, multi-needle retrieval and real long-document tasks at the target length, not only perplexity. Spread the attention layers evenly through the depth rather than stacking them at the bottom or top, and test NoPE in the global layers against RoPE for length generalisation. One number: an 8B-shaped model at 256k tokens needs 34 GB of KV cache per sequence with GQA-8, and a 1:3 hybrid about 8.6 GB. That decides whether sixteen users fit on a GPU (11.9). The follow-up is "how do you know the result transfers to scale?": fit the gap between hybrid and dense across sizes and check that it is not growing, and re-check recall at the largest size before committing.	chapter ch::11
[Ch 11] Explain Mamba in five minutes.	Start from a continuous state space, h' = Ah + Bx, y = Ch, discretised with a step Δ into h_t = Āh_{t−1} + B̄x_t with Ā = exp(ΔA). A large Δ means forget and write; a small Δ means keep and ignore. Mamba makes Δ, B and C functions of the current token ("selective"), so the model decides per token what to store. In the 1-D example in 11.4, a selective step keeps a key value at about 1.85 four tokens later, while a fixed step lets it fade to 0.26. Selectivity breaks the convolution trick of S4, so training uses a parallel associative scan in a fused kernel. Mamba-2 restricts A to a scalar times the identity, which turns the model into linear attention with a decay mask, y = (L ∘ CBᵀ)x, and allows matmul-based training. The weakness is the same as any fixed state: exact recall from long context. The follow-up is "how is that different from an LSTM?": the recurrence is linear in h, so it can be trained in parallel, and the gating is input-dependent but has no nonlinearity between steps.	chapter ch::11
[Ch 12] Compare BERT, GPT and T5: architecture, objective and best use.	BERT is an encoder with bidirectional attention trained by masked language modelling: every position sees both sides, which makes it strong for classification, extraction, reranking and (after contrastive fine-tuning) embeddings, but it cannot generate left to right. GPT is a decoder with a causal mask trained on next-token prediction: every position is a training example and generation is native, which is why all general LLMs are decoders. T5 is an encoder-decoder trained by span corruption, framing every task as text-to-text; it suits input-to-output tasks such as translation and summarisation. One number: BERT-base at 110M parameters costs about 14 GFLOPs per 64-token request against about 1,000 for an 8B decoder, a 73-fold difference that decides high-volume classification. The follow-up is "then why not always use BERT for classification?": because a decoder handles new labels, few-shot settings and long instructions without retraining, so it wins when labels change often or volume is low.	chapter ch::12
[Ch 12] Why is a vanilla BERT [CLS] vector a poor sentence embedding, and what fixes it?	Pretraining never asked [CLS] to summarise meaning so that cosine similarity is meaningful: next-sentence prediction was a weak signal, and RoBERTa dropped it. Raw BERT vectors are also anisotropic, crowded into a narrow cone, so all cosines are high and uninformative. The fix is contrastive fine-tuning on pairs (Sentence-BERT, then E5, GTE, BGE), usually with masked mean pooling, which pulls paraphrases together and pushes in-batch negatives apart. The follow-up is "what about decoder LLMs?": use last-token pooling or make attention bidirectional, add task instructions to queries, then train contrastively (12.2). Do not mean-pool a causal model's raw states, because early positions have seen only a prefix of the text.	chapter ch::12
[Ch 12] What is a mixture of experts and why is it attractive?	An MoE layer replaces the feed-forward block with many expert FFNs and a router that sends each token to the top k of them, so total parameters grow with the number of experts while compute per token grows only with k. At a fixed training budget it reaches lower loss than a dense model with the same active parameters, because it stores more knowledge per FLOP. One number: DeepSeek-V3 has 671B total and 37B active parameters, so each token costs the FLOPs of a 37B dense model (about 74 GFLOPs) but serving needs memory for all 671B. When not to use one: a single-GPU or low-traffic deployment, where memory binds and experts sit idle. The expected follow-up is "how do you stop the router collapsing?": an auxiliary load-balancing loss or a per-expert selection bias (Chapter 11, 11.2).	chapter ch::12
[Ch 12] How does CLIP learn a shared image-text space?	It trains an image encoder and a text encoder together on image-caption pairs with a symmetric InfoNCE loss. In a batch of N pairs, the N×N matrix of cosine similarities divided by a temperature goes through a cross-entropy whose correct class is the diagonal, applied over rows (image to text) and columns (text to image). Every other caption in the batch is a negative, so large batches help: CLIP used 32,768. The temperature is learned, initialised at 0.07 and with the logit scale clipped at 100. The follow-up is "how does SigLIP differ?": it replaces the batch softmax with an independent sigmoid per pair, which removes the batch-wide normalisation and works well at smaller batches (Chapter 14, 14.2).	chapter ch::12
[Ch 12] State the Chinchilla result. Why do labs now train small models far beyond it?	For a fixed training budget C ≈ 6ND, loss is minimised by growing parameters and tokens in equal proportion, about 20 tokens per parameter; Chinchilla (70B, 1.4T tokens) beat Gopher (280B, 300B tokens) at the same compute. But Chinchilla minimises training compute only, and a deployed model pays about 2N FLOPs for every token it serves. When lifetime inference tokens exceed training tokens, a smaller model trained longer reaches the same loss at a lower total cost, which is why Llama 3 8B saw about 15T tokens (about 1,900 per parameter). Over-training has limits: returns diminish, high-quality data runs out (Chapter 13, 13.6), and heavily over-trained models degrade more under low-bit quantisation. The follow-up is "is 20 universal?": no. It is specific to that data, tokenizer and recipe, and must be refitted for your own setup.	chapter ch::12
[Ch 12] You have a budget of 1e23 FLOPs. How big a model and how many tokens? What if it will serve 1B requests a month?	With C = 6ND and D = 20N, C = 120N², so N = √(10²³/120) ≈ 29B parameters on about 580B tokens. The Besiroglu re-fit of Chinchilla's loss gives the same, 29.4B on 566B tokens; Chapter 13's 14.6B comes from the flawed approach-3 fit (12.5, and the "Three answers for one budget" passage in 13.3). For serving, assume 1B requests of 1,000 tokens each, so 10¹² tokens a month, and a year of service. The 29B model then spends 2 × 29.4B × 1.2 × 10¹³ ≈ 7.1 × 10²³ FLOPs on inference, seven times its training compute. Under the re-fitted loss, a 10.9B model needs 2.7T tokens (1.8 × 10²³ FLOPs) to reach the same loss but only 2.6 × 10²³ FLOPs of inference. That totals 4.4 × 10²³ against 8.1 × 10²³, 46% less (code/ch12_check.py). So train roughly a 10B model on several trillion tokens. Since inference FLOPs run at lower utilisation than training FLOPs, the real optimum is smaller still. The follow-up is "what stops you going to 3B?": the token count needed explodes, because the data term falls slowly, and data supply becomes the limit.	chapter ch::12
[Ch 12] Design the model choice for tagging 50M catalogue items with 300 attributes, in English and a second, lower-resource language.	Start with arithmetic. At 200 tokens per item, 50M items is 10¹⁰ tokens; a large API model at $1 per million input tokens costs about $10,000 per pass before output tokens, and every relabel repeats it. A fine-tuned encoder runs the same pass on a few GPUs for under a day. So: label 5,000–10,000 items with a large LLM, with human review of a stratified sample per language. Fine-tune a multilingual encoder (XLM-R or mDeBERTa) with a 300-way multi-label sigmoid head, or a small LLM if attributes need reasoning over long text. Use a VLM only for items whose information is mainly in images. Route low-confidence items to the large model and then to humans, and report per-language, per-attribute F1 with confidence intervals, since rare attributes and the low-resource language are where it fails. The trade-off: the encoder must be retrained when attributes change, while the LLM path adapts by prompt. The follow-up is "how do you set the confidence threshold?": on held-out data, by plotting precision of the kept items against the escalation rate (12.6).	chapter ch::12
[Ch 12] Are emergent abilities real?	Wei et al. (2022) reported abilities that are near chance below some scale and then jump. Schaeffer et al. (2023) showed that much of this comes from the metric. If per-token accuracy p rises smoothly with scale, exact match on a 10-token answer is p¹⁰, which stays near zero and then climbs steeply. In the toy curve in code/ch12_check.py, per-token accuracy goes 0.50 → 0.65 → 0.77 between 10^9.5 and 10^10.5 parameters while exact match goes 0.001 → 0.013 → 0.072, which looks like a sudden jump. With log-likelihood or token-level metrics, progress is smooth and predictable. Du et al. (2024) reconcile the views: across model sizes and token counts, many abilities appear once pretraining loss falls below a threshold, so they are sudden as a function of loss but predictable, because loss is. My answer: the underlying competence improves smoothly, but some user-visible abilities really are thresholded, because a task fails if any step fails. The follow-up is "so can you forecast them?": partly, by predicting loss from a scaling law, then mapping loss to accuracy with a fitted sigmoid (Chapter 13, 13.8).	chapter ch::12
[Ch 12] A model card says "235B-A22B, 128k context, Apache 2.0". What does it cost to serve, and when would you prefer a dense 32B?	235B total parameters need 470 GB in bf16 or 235 GB at 8 bits, so at least four 80 GB GPUs at 8 bits before any KV cache. Each token does the work of 22B active parameters, about 44 GFLOPs, so per-token compute is close to a dense 22B model. At small batch it also decodes almost as fast as one, because only the active experts' weights are read. Apache 2.0 allows commercial use and fine-tuning. Prefer a dense 32B (64 GB in bf16, one or two GPUs) when traffic is low or bursty, when you will fine-tune heavily (MoE fine-tuning is less stable and needs expert-parallel tooling), or when you serve on one GPU. The follow-up is "why does the MoE's advantage shrink at high batch?": with 128 experts and top-8 routing, 64 tokens in a batch touch about 126 experts, so a step reads nearly all weights and all-to-all traffic dominates (Chapter 11, 11.2).	chapter ch::12
[Ch 12] Your team wants reasoning for a customer-facing assistant. Separate reasoning model, hybrid thinking mode, or an effort setting?	The choice is about cost control and operational simplicity, since quality differences between the packagings are smaller than differences between model families. A hybrid or effort-controlled model needs one deployment and lets you route easy requests to non-thinking mode and hard ones to thinking. A separate reasoning model is easier to evaluate and pin, but doubles hosting. The number to compute: if 20% of requests think for 4,000 tokens before a 300-token answer, average decode length rises from 300 to 1,100 tokens, about 3.7 times the decode cost and TPOT-bound latency. When not to use reasoning: latency-critical paths with simple intents, where a router plus a non-thinking model wins. The follow-up is "how do you decide which requests get thinking?": train or prompt a cheap classifier on logged requests labelled by whether thinking changed the outcome, and evaluate the routed system end to end.	chapter ch::12
[Ch 13] Derive the compute-optimal model size from L = E + A/N^α + B/D^β and C = 6ND.	Substitute D = C/6N so the data term becomes B(6N/C)^β, differentiate with respect to N and set to zero: αA/N^(α+1) = βB(6/C)^β N^(β−1), which gives N_opt = G(C/6)^(β/(α+β)) with G = (αA/βB)^(1/(α+β)), and D_opt ∝ C^(α/(α+β)). Equal exponents mean equal growth; with the Besiroglu re-fit (α = 0.348, β = 0.366) the exponents are 0.51 and 0.49, so at 1e23 FLOPs you get about 29B parameters on 570B tokens, 19 tokens per parameter. The follow-up is "so should we train 29B?": no, that minimises training compute only; if the model will serve many tokens, the inference-aware optimum (13.5) is several times smaller and trained much longer.	chapter ch::13
[Ch 13] Why did Kaplan and Chinchilla disagree?	Kaplan read intermediate checkpoints of runs with a fixed long schedule, which under-estimates short, fully-decayed runs and biases towards bigger models; they also excluded embedding parameters and used smaller models. Chinchilla matched schedules to run length. The current answer (Pearce and Song 2024; Porian et al. 2024) is that the non-embedding parameter count, the uncounted last-layer FLOPs, an over-long warm-up and untuned AdamW β₂ and batch size at small scale together explain most of the gap: correct them and Kaplan's exponent of 0.73 moves to Chinchilla's 0.5. The trade-off to mention is that small runs are cheap but are where measurement errors have most leverage on an extrapolated exponent. Follow-up: "what does this mean for your own fits?" Count parameters and FLOPs consistently, tune per scale or use µP, and fully decay every run.	chapter ch::13
[Ch 13] Why do labs train 8B models on 15T tokens?	Inference cost: minimising training plus lifetime serving cost favours smaller models trained much longer than Chinchilla-optimal. With the re-fit constants and a target equal to the compute-optimal 1e23 loss, 1e14 lifetime tokens moves the best size from 29.5B to 7.5B on 9.5T tokens (1,265 tokens per parameter): 4.2× the training compute, but about a third of the total (1.9e24 against 6.0e24 FLOPs). The counter-pressures are that unique data runs out (13.6), over-trained models degrade more when quantised (Kumar et al. 2024), each extra token buys less loss, and distillation from a larger teacher is an alternative route to a strong small model. Follow-up: "when would you not over-train?" When the model is a research artefact or a teacher that will serve few tokens itself, or when you cannot get more unique data without many repeats.	chapter ch::13
[Ch 13] How would you fit a scaling law for a new architecture?	IsoFLOP profiles over 5+ budgets spanning two orders of magnitude, fully decayed runs, tuned or µP-transferred learning rates, several seeds at small scale, fit the optimum's power law, validate on held-out larger runs, compare slopes and offsets with a tuned transformer baseline. Fit the parametric form with the LSE parameterisation and a Huber loss from a grid of starts, and bootstrap over runs for an interval on N_opt and on the predicted loss. Cost check: five budgets with seven sizes each is 35 runs per architecture; if the largest budget is 1e20 FLOPs and the budgets are spaced 3× apart, the whole sweep is about 7 × (1 + 1/3 + 1/9 + …) × 1e20 ≈ 1e21. The trade-off: equal effort on the baseline's hyperparameters, or you measure tuning, not architecture. Follow-up: "the new architecture wins at small scale but has a shallower slope": report the crossover compute, and do not adopt it above that.	chapter ch::13
[Ch 13] What is the value of repeating data?	Roughly as good as new data up to about 4 epochs, rapidly diminishing beyond about 16 (Muennighoff et al.); repeat high-quality sources preferentially. The formula is D′ = U + U·R*(1 − e^(−R/R*)) with R = epochs − 1 and R* ≈ 15.4: 4 epochs over 100B tokens is worth 373B, 16 epochs 1,059B, and the ceiling is about 16.4U. When NOT to rely on it: the fit is for natural web text at moderate scales; highly duplicated or tiny high-quality sources can over-fit sooner, so watch held-out loss on that domain. Follow-up: "data runs out, more epochs or bigger model?" More epochs first, up to a few, because the fitted decay for excess parameters (R_N* ≈ 5.3) is faster than for repeated data.	chapter ch::13
[Ch 13] Are emergent abilities real?	Many apparent jumps come from discontinuous metrics like exact match; with continuous metrics the same tasks improve smoothly. A 10-token exact-match answer scores p^10: 0.20 at per-token accuracy 0.85 and 0.43 at 0.92, a "jump" from a smooth per-token gain. Some multi-step abilities still appear abruptly, because a task can need a capability that only appears below some loss, so treat forecasts with wide intervals. The quantised-skills view (13.1) reconciles both: smooth average loss, individually abrupt skills. Follow-up: "how would you test it on a new task?" Re-score with log-likelihood of the gold answer across a model family; if that is smooth, the jump is the metric.	chapter ch::13
[Ch 13] How can you predict a benchmark score before a big run?	Train a ladder of small models, fit accuracy as a function of loss (sigmoid), fit loss as a function of compute, chain the two. With a sigmoid of midpoint 2.10 and width 0.05, ladder models at loss 2.30 and 2.20 score 0.26 and 0.34, and the target's predicted loss of 2.035 maps to 0.84. The weakness is that the sigmoid's midpoint is only identified if some ladder models are already on the steep part; otherwise you are extrapolating from chance. Follow-up: "which benchmarks forecast well?" Ones with many items, continuous or cloze formats, and low seed variance; multi-step agentic tasks forecast badly.	chapter ch::13
[Ch 13] Explain the unbiased pass@k estimator and why you need it.	With n samples and c correct, pass@k = 1 − C(n−c,k)/C(n,k): the probability that a random subset of k of the n samples contains no correct one, subtracted from 1. Plugging the sample rate into 1 − (1−p)^k is biased for small n, because it is a convex function of the estimated p and Jensen's inequality pushes its expectation away from the truth. With 200 samples and 13 correct, pass@10 = 0.498. Follow-up: "how large should n be?" Several times k, and report n with the number.	chapter ch::13
[Ch 13] What is test-time compute scaling?	Accuracy improving with inference compute (more samples, search, longer reasoning). For hard problems it can substitute for model size, if there is a good way to verify or select answers. With per-sample accuracy 0.05, pass@64 is 0.96, but majority voting or a reward model will select the right answer far less often than that unless the verifier is good. Follow-up: "when is a bigger model better than more samples?" When the base accuracy is near zero (sampling cannot find what the model cannot produce) or when no reliable selector exists (Snell et al.).	chapter ch::13
[Ch 13] A colleague's scaling curve bends at the largest size. Possible causes?	The learning rate or batch size not re-tuned for that size, a schedule mismatch, data running out (repetition), numerical instability, approaching the irreducible loss, or a bug in that run. Check hyperparameters first: a curve that bends because the LR is now too high usually shows loss spikes or a worse early curve, which you can see in the logs in minutes. A useful number: if the fitted exponent drops from 0.30 to 0.20 over the last half-decade, the predicted loss at 10× more compute moves by about 10^(−0.2) versus 10^(−0.3) of the reducible part, a 26% difference in the remaining gap, enough to change a go/no-go. When NOT to "fix" it: if E is close, bending is expected and the parametric form already captures it. Follow-up: "how do you decide?" Rerun the largest point with a short LR sweep; if one setting rejoins the line, it was tuning.	chapter ch::13
[Ch 13] What does 6ND leave out, and when does it matter?	6ND counts 2 FLOPs per parameter per token forward and 4 backward (the input gradient and the weight gradient, each the same size as the forward matmul). It omits attention-score FLOPs, about 6·n_layers·T·d per trained token, which is 13% of 6N for an 8B model at 8k context and 54% at 32k; activation recomputation, which raises hardware FLOPs to about 8ND; and for MoE you must use active parameters (DeepSeek-V3: 6 × 37B × 14.8T ≈ 3.3e24, not 6e25). It matters for long-context runs, for MFU accounting (MFU uses 6ND by convention so recomputation does not count as useful work) and for comparing dense and sparse models. Follow-up: "why is backward 2× forward?" Because for every weight matrix you need both ∂L/∂x (to keep back-propagating) and ∂L/∂W (to update), each a matmul of the same size as the forward one.	chapter ch::13
[Ch 13] A new method lowers the loss at 1e23 FLOPs by 0.02. How much compute is that worth?	Invert the compute-optimal frontier L(C) = E + K·C^(−γ) with γ = αβ/(α+β): the multiplier is ((L − E)/(L − E − ΔL))^(1/γ). With the re-fit constants, L − E = 0.215 and γ = 0.178, so 0.02 is worth (0.215/0.195)^5.6 ≈ 1.73× compute; with the approach-3 constants it is 1.53×. The trade-off is that the answer depends on E, which is the least well identified constant, so give an interval, and that the multiplier for a fixed ΔL grows with scale as the reducible loss shrinks. Follow-up: "is a 1.7× compute gain worth adopting?" Only if it holds at more than one scale on the ladder, survives held-out validation, and costs less than 70% extra in engineering or inference.	chapter ch::13
[Ch 13] Why should loss follow a power law at all?	No theorem says it must, but two models predict it. Quantised skills: if needed skills have Zipf-distributed frequencies p_k ∝ k^(−(a+1)) and a model learns the n most common, the remaining loss is the tail mass, which shrinks as n^(−a) (with a = 0.5, each 10× more skills divides it by 3.2). The data manifold: approximating a function on a manifold of intrinsic dimension d with N pieces gives error ∝ N^(−c/d), so small exponents mean high intrinsic dimension. Both say exponents belong to the data, which is why architectures usually shift the line rather than tilt it. Follow-up: "what would break the power law?" Running out of distinct data, hitting the entropy floor E, or a recipe change partway up the ladder.	chapter ch::13
[Ch 14] How does an image become tokens in a ViT?	Cut it into p × p patches, flatten and linearly project each, add positions. 224² with 16-pixel patches gives 196 tokens. Each patch is 16 × 16 × 3 = 768 raw values, and the linear projection to the model width is equivalent to a convolution with kernel and stride p. Tokens grow with the square of the side: with 14-pixel patches a 448 image is 1,024 tokens and an 896 image 4,096, which is why resolution is the main cost lever. Follow-up: "how do you handle a different resolution at inference?" Interpolate learned position embeddings, or use 2-D RoPE, which extends naturally.	chapter ch::14
[Ch 14] Compare CLIP and SigLIP losses.	CLIP: softmax cross-entropy over the batch in both directions. SigLIP: an independent sigmoid per image–text pair with a learned bias. SigLIP needs no batch-wide normalisation, so it scales to large batches cheaply and works better at small ones. In CLIP every logit's gradient depends on the whole row through the softmax, and the loss needs all-gathered embeddings across devices; in SigLIP each of the B² pairs is a separate binary decision, and because B − 1 of every B pairs are negatives, the learned bias starts strongly negative (−10 in the original) to avoid a large initial loss. Both learn the temperature. Follow-up: "what do both fail at?" Fine detail and spatial relations, because captions rarely mention them.	chapter ch::14
[Ch 14] Describe the LLaVA architecture and training.	Frozen vision encoder, MLP projector into the LLM's embedding space, image tokens spliced into the sequence. Stage 1 trains the projector on captions; stage 2 instruction-tunes with the LLM unfrozen. In LLaVA-1.5 that is about 558k caption pairs and then a 665k-example instruction mixture, with 576 image tokens per 336-pixel image. Modern recipes (Qwen2.5-VL, Molmo) differ in three ways: they unfreeze the vision encoder after alignment, at a lower learning rate than the LLM; they add a large multimodal pretraining or mid-training stage with interleaved documents, OCR and grounding; and they use native resolution. Follow-up: "why not unfreeze everything from the start?" A random projector sends noisy gradients into a good encoder and can destroy its features; align first.	chapter ch::14
[Ch 14] How do VLMs handle high-resolution documents?	Tiling with an overview image, or native-resolution encoding with 2-D RoPE; then token merging (pixel shuffle) or a resampler to fit the token budget. A 1,344-pixel page is 9,216 patches, 2,304 tokens after 2 × 2 merging, so a 20-page document is 46k tokens and 6 GB of KV cache on an 8B GQA model. Trade-off: merging loses detail that small print needs; OCR into text tokens is far cheaper for text-only pages. Follow-up: "how do you choose resolution?" Per page, by content type, validated with a resolution sweep on real documents.	chapter ch::14
[Ch 14] Cross-attention versus projector-in-sequence designs?	Cross-attention leaves the LLM's text path untouched and keeps image features out of the context; projector designs are simpler, reuse the LLM fully and handle interleaving naturally, at the cost of context length. In numbers: a 1,024-token image in sequence costs 1,024 positions of context, 134 MB of KV cache on an 8B GQA-8 model and 1.6 × 10¹³ prefill FLOPs, every time it is attended. With cross-attention the image features are keys and values only for the cross-attention layers, so if one layer in four is a cross-attention layer the cache cost is about a quarter and no context positions are used. Llama 3.2 Vision chose cross-attention with frozen text weights so its text-only behaviour is exactly that of the text model. Follow-up: "why is in-sequence more common?" Simpler training, interleaving of many images, and the LLM's full attention over image tokens is better for fine-grained reasoning.	chapter ch::14
[Ch 14] What is early fusion, and why is it attractive?	Images become discrete tokens in the same vocabulary, so one model reads and generates both. Costs: the tokenizer loses detail and training is harder to stabilise. Chameleon reported needing QK-norm and other changes to keep mixed-modal training stable, because image and text tokens have very different statistics. Scaling comparisons find early fusion at least competitive with late fusion at equal compute, so the trade-off is mainly cost of training from scratch versus reuse of an existing LLM. Follow-up: "continuous or discrete?" Continuous patches keep more detail for understanding; discrete tokens make generation a next-token problem.	chapter ch::14
[Ch 14] How would you detect that a VLM relies on language priors?	Blind baselines (image removed), counterfactual images that contradict the prior, hallucination probes for absent objects. The number to report is the visual contribution, real-image accuracy minus blind accuracy, with a paired confidence interval: on 1,000 questions, a difference of 5 points with 30% of answers changing between the two conditions has a standard error of about 1.7 points. Follow-up: "the blind score is 60% on a four-choice benchmark, what now?" The benchmark mostly measures language priors; report the image-dependent subset separately.	chapter ch::14
[Ch 14] Explain classifier-free guidance.	Train with the condition randomly dropped; at sampling, push the prediction away from the unconditional one: ε_u + w(ε_c − ε_u). Because predicted noise is a scaled score, this adds (w − 1) times the gradient of log p(condition | x), an implicit classifier trained for free. w around 3–7 is typical; higher w follows the prompt more closely and loses diversity and, at extremes, saturates colours. It doubles the network evaluations per step unless distilled. Follow-up: "why not train a separate classifier?" Classifier guidance needs a classifier robust to noisy inputs; CFG needs only condition dropout.	chapter ch::14
