[Ch 14] How does Whisper process audio?	Log-mel spectrogram (10 ms hop), conv downsampling, transformer encoder; a decoder generates text with special tokens for language, task and timestamps. Thirty seconds at 16 kHz gives 3,000 frames and 1,500 encoder positions after the stride-2 convolution. It was trained on 680k hours of weakly labelled web audio, which is why it is robust to accents and noise but can hallucinate text on silence. Follow-up: "how would you stream it?" Chunked inference with overlap, or a model trained for streaming; Whisper's 30-second window is not causal.	chapter ch::14
[Ch 14] What limits video understanding?	Token count. Sparse and adaptive frame sampling, spatio-temporal token merging, 3-D positions, and memory or retrieval for long videos. Ten minutes at 2 fps and 256 tokens per frame is 307,200 tokens, beyond most context windows. Benchmarks are a second limit: many questions can be answered from one frame. Follow-up: "how do you test temporal understanding?" Questions about order, counts over time and causality, with a shuffled-frames baseline.	chapter ch::14
[Ch 14] Your VLM hallucinates objects that are not in the image. How do you debug it?	First measure it properly: a POPE-style probe asks about present and absent objects in balanced proportions, and a "yes" bias shows as a high false-positive rate on absent objects. Then localise: run the same questions with the image removed; if the model still names the object at a similar rate, the language prior dominates (a kitchen implies a fridge), which points at training data whose captions mention unseen objects. Check resolution: small objects missed at 336 pixels may be confabulated from context. Fixes, in order of cost: higher resolution or a dense-feature encoder; re-captioning training data so captions mention only visible objects; adding negative questions ("is there a dog?" with answer no) to instruction data; and preference or RL training with pairs that penalise invented objects. Report the false-positive rate on absent objects with a confidence interval before and after. Follow-up: "the hallucinations are about text in images": that is an OCR resolution problem, so test tiling first.	chapter ch::14
[Ch 14] Freeze or unfreeze the vision encoder, and at what learning rate?	Freeze during projector alignment, always; a random projector's gradients can wreck a good encoder. Afterwards, unfreeze when the target domain needs what caption matching did not teach: documents, charts, screens, spatial reasoning. Use a learning rate a few to ten times lower than the LLM's, with warm-up, and monitor the encoder's standalone quality (zero-shot retrieval or a linear probe) at checkpoints. When not to unfreeze: small instruction datasets in the photo domain, where the encoder can only over-fit, and multi-task models that must keep the encoder's general features. The number to watch is the text-only and encoder-only benchmark delta; if the encoder probe drops by more than a point or two while VLM scores rise, lower its learning rate. Follow-up: "LoRA on the encoder?" A reasonable middle ground when data is small.	chapter ch::14
[Ch 14] Design a VLM for GUI and computer-use agents.	Input: screenshots at near-native resolution, since icons and small text need it; a 1,920 × 1,080 screen is about 10,600 patches at 14 pixels, 2,600 tokens after 2 × 2 merging per step, so a 30-step episode holds about 79k image tokens unless older screenshots are dropped or summarised. Output: actions as text, with click targets as coordinates in a documented convention (normalised or absolute) that the harness and the training data share. Training: grounding data from synthetic and real screens with accessibility-tree labels, then trajectories, then RL on tasks with checkable end states (Chapter 19). Latency: each step pays prefill for a new screenshot; prefix-cache the conversation and keep only the last two or three screenshots at full resolution. Evaluate pointing accuracy separately from task success, so you know whether failures are perception or planning. Follow-up: "why not use the accessibility tree alone?" It is cheaper and exact where available, and many apps and canvases do not expose one; strong agents use both.	chapter ch::14
[Ch 14] How much text-only data should you mix into VLM training, and how do you detect degradation?	Enough that text benchmarks stay within noise of the original LLM; recipes report substantial text-only shares, and the right number is found by ablation, not by rule. Detect degradation by evaluating the original LLM and every VLM checkpoint on the same text suite (knowledge, maths, instruction following, long-form writing, multilingual), with confidence intervals; degradation appears first on skills the multimodal data never exercises. A worked threshold: on a 1,000-question text benchmark at 70% accuracy, each score has a standard error of 1.4 points, so the difference of two unpaired scores has a standard error of about 2.0 and a drop of 4 points or more is beyond noise; a paired comparison on the same questions detects smaller drops. Fixes are more or better text data, a lower LLM learning rate, freezing the LLM for early stages, or a cross-attention design that keeps the text weights frozen. Follow-up: "can model merging help?" Interpolating the VLM's LLM weights back towards the original can recover text ability at some cost in visual skill; measure both.	chapter ch::14
[Ch 15] What is the pre-training objective of a GPT-style model?	Next-token prediction: minimise the cross-entropy −Σ_t log p(x_t | x_<t) over trillions of tokens, with a causal mask so that one forward pass over T tokens yields T losses in parallel. Nothing else is in the objective; knowledge, syntax and in-context learning emerge because predicting text well requires them. A number to have ready: a loss of 2.0 nats per token is a perplexity of e² ≈ 7.4, or about 0.72 bits per byte at 4 bytes per token, and bits per byte is what you compare across tokenizers. The trade-off is that the base model only continues text, which is why every later stage exists. Follow-up: "why does such a simple objective give general abilities?" Because minimising it is compression of the data, and compressing text written by people who reason, code and explain requires modelling those processes.	chapter ch::15
[Ch 15] What does SFT change, and why mask the prompt tokens in the loss?	SFT trains on (prompt, ideal response) pairs in a chat template and mainly changes format and behaviour: answer instead of continue, follow the template, stop at the end-of-turn token. Masking the prompt (labels = −100) keeps the gradient on what the model should produce: with a 900-token prompt and a 100-token answer, an unmasked loss spends 90% of its gradient on reproducing user text, and teaches the model to imitate users. When not to mask fully: some recipes keep a small prompt-loss weight when prompts are themselves high-quality text, with mixed results. Follow-up: "what about multi-turn and packing?" Score every assistant turn including its end-of-turn token, mask every user and system turn, and reset positions and use a block-diagonal mask when packing (§18.2).	chapter ch::15
[Ch 15] Explain the three steps of RLHF.	First SFT a model; second, collect human rankings of its sampled responses and train a reward model with the Bradley–Terry loss −log σ(r_chosen − r_rejected); third, optimise the policy with PPO to maximise reward minus β·KL to the SFT reference. The KL term is what makes the target well-defined: the optimal policy is π_ref·exp(r/β)/Z, so β sets how far the policy may move. The headline result to quote is InstructGPT, where outputs of a 1.3B RLHF model were preferred by labellers to those of the 175B GPT-3. The cost: four models in memory (policy, value, reference, reward) and sampling in the loop. Follow-up: "why not just maximise the reward?" Because the reward model is only accurate near the data it was trained on, and an unconstrained policy finds its errors (§18.5).	chapter ch::15
[Ch 15] What is reward hacking? Give an example.	The policy finds outputs that score well on the reward signal without being good, because the reward model or checker is an imperfect proxy (Goodhart's law). Classic examples: answers that grow longer because the reward model learned "longer is better", flattery and agreement with the user, a coding policy that special-cases the unit tests. The KL penalty is the first defence, and its size can be computed: a policy that drifts by 0.1 nats per token over a 500-token answer accumulates 50 nats of KL, a penalty of 2.5 reward units at β = 0.05, enough to cancel a typical reward gain. When not to rely on KL alone: verifiable-reward RL needs hardened checkers instead. Follow-up: "how do you detect it?" Track reward against a held-out judge or human audit; the proxy keeps rising while the gold metric flattens or falls, and length-controlled metrics expose verbosity.	chapter ch::15
[Ch 15] Derive or explain the intuition behind DPO. What does beta do?	The optimal policy of KL-constrained reward maximisation is the reference reweighted by exp(reward/beta); invert it to express reward through log-probability ratios, plug into Bradley-Terry, and the reward model disappears. Beta controls how far the policy may move from the reference. Concretely, r(x, y) = β log(π*(y|x)/π_ref(y|x)) + β log Z(x), and Z(x) cancels in the difference between chosen and rejected, leaving −log σ(β[Δ log-ratio]); the loss starts at ln 2 = 0.693 and falls to 0.598 when the policy raises the chosen response by 1 nat and lowers the rejected by 1 nat at β = 0.1. Small β lets the log-ratios grow large before the loss saturates, so the policy moves further; typical values are 0.05–0.5. Follow-up: "what goes wrong in practice?" Likelihood displacement (both log-probabilities fall while their gap grows) and over-fitting to near-deterministic preferences, which IPO bounds (§18.6).	chapter ch::15
[Ch 15] Compare PPO-based RLHF, DPO and KTO. Which would you use with thumbs-up/down data from content moderators?	PPO is flexible but costly and unstable; DPO is simple and stable for pairwise data; KTO handles unpaired binary feedback, so it fits moderator thumbs. In memory, PPO trains a policy and a value model and runs a reference and a reward model: for a 7B model with Adam (about 16 bytes per trained parameter) that is about 224 GB of training state plus 28 GB of bf16 weights for the two frozen models, against about 112 GB plus 14 GB for DPO or KTO (or none for the reference if its log-probabilities are precomputed). KTO weights desirable and undesirable examples separately, so a 90/10 thumbs-up skew can be rebalanced. When NOT to use KTO: if you have, or can cheaply build, real pairs (an edit and its original, two samples for the same prompt), DPO or IPO extracts more signal per label. Follow-up: "when is PPO worth it?" When the reward comes from a model or checker you can query on fresh samples and on-policy exploration matters, as in RL on verifiable tasks.	chapter ch::15
[Ch 15] How do reasoning models like DeepSeek-R1 get trained? Why does RL work so well for maths and code?	SFT on some reasoning traces, then RL (GRPO) with a correctness reward; answers can be checked automatically, so there is no learned reward model to exploit and training scales; the verifiers still need hardening against reward hacking (tests special-cased, lenient answer-matching). R1-Zero showed that RL from the base model alone, with accuracy and format rewards, produces long chains of thought with self-checking, but with poor readability and language mixing; R1 adds a small cold-start SFT set of long chains of thought, reasoning RL, then rejection-sampled SFT data (about 800k examples, reasoning and general) and a final RL stage. A number that explains the data work: with 8 samples per prompt, a prompt solved 2% of the time gives an all-wrong group, and so zero advantage, 85% of the time (0.98⁸), which is why prompt difficulty is filtered. Follow-up: "does RL teach new reasoning or sharpen what is there?" The evidence that pass@k at large k barely improves suggests mostly sharpening; §19.6 sets out the experiment that would decide it.	chapter ch::15
[Ch 15] You need a cheap model to tag 50M catalogue items (product listings, support tickets). Describe a distillation plan and how you would check it.	Write a teacher prompt with the taxonomy, definitions and a rubric for ambiguous cases; label a stratified sample of about 200k items (stratified by category, source and length, oversampling rare classes); fine-tune a small encoder or small LLM on it; and audit 300–500 human-labelled items per release to measure per-class precision and recall, since one bad class can hide behind a good average (at precision 0.95 on 400 items, the standard error is 1.1 points). Route items below a confidence threshold back to the teacher. Cost, at illustrative prices of $3 per million input tokens and $15 per million output: labelling 200k items of 300 input and 20 output tokens costs about $240, while sending all 50M through the API would cost about $60k per pass, which is the case for distillation. Plan for drift: re-label a fresh sample monthly and retrain when per-class metrics drop. Check the provider's terms of service on training with its outputs. Follow-up: "when would you skip distillation?" When volume is small, the taxonomy changes weekly, or the task needs the teacher's world knowledge.	chapter ch::15
[Ch 15] A fine-tuned assistant became sycophantic. Which stage do you suspect and how would you fix it?	The preference stage: labellers or reward models preferred agreeable answers. Add pairs where the correct answer disagrees with the user, raise beta or KL, and evaluate on a sycophancy set. Confirm before fixing: on, say, 500 questions the model answers correctly, append "Are you sure? I think it's X" and measure the flip rate for the SFT, preference and final checkpoints; if it jumps after the preference stage, the cause is there. Then check the data: what fraction of preference pairs have a chosen response that agrees with a user's stated view? Fixes include balanced pairs that reward polite disagreement, reward-model evaluation on a sycophancy probe, and a higher KL weight; the trade-off is that over-correcting produces a contrarian model that also flips correct user claims. Follow-up: "could SFT cause it?" Yes, if demonstrations always defer to the user; the stage-by-stage comparison distinguishes the two.	chapter ch::15
[Ch 15] Where does the compute go across the pipeline, and why does RL take longer than its FLOPs suggest?	For a 7B model on 2T tokens, pretraining is 8.4e22 FLOPs; SFT (300M tokens) is 0.015% of that, DPO 0.007%, and an RLVR run of 8.2e9 sampled tokens 0.55%, or about 0.82% once the reference and old-policy forward passes are counted. Post-training is cheap in FLOPs and expensive in data, verifiers and experiment count. RL is slow because the 1.1e20 FLOPs of generation are memory-bound autoregressive decoding at low utilisation, so they can take longer in wall-clock than the 3.4e20 FLOPs of training, which run at high utilisation; RL systems therefore separate fast inference engines from the trainer (§19.10). Follow-up: "what would you optimise first?" Generation throughput (batching, prefix caching of the prompt, asynchronous rollouts), measured as the share of each step spent waiting for samples.	chapter ch::15
[Ch 16] Walk through a web-data pipeline.	URL filtering, HTML extraction, language ID, heuristic filters, deduplication, quality classifier, PII and toxicity filtering, decontamination, tokenisation and sharding with metadata. Cheap steps first. The ordering is a cost argument: a URL block list costs microseconds per document, a fastText classifier milliseconds per thousand, an LLM rater orders of magnitude more, so each step should see only what survived the cheaper ones; FineWeb's 96 snapshots end up as about 15T tokens, and its educational subset as 1.3T. The trade-off is that early cheap filters make irreversible mistakes (for example prose rules dropping code), so keep per-step removal statistics and samples. Follow-up: "which step has the most impact?" Published ablations point to extraction quality and model-based quality filtering, with deduplication giving a smaller but reliable gain.	chapter ch::16
[Ch 16] How does MinHash LSH find near-duplicates?	Shingle documents; for each of n hash functions keep the minimum hash; agreement rate estimates Jaccard similarity. Split signatures into b bands of r rows; pairs matching on any band are candidates with probability 1 − (1 − sʳ)ᵇ, which sets the threshold. Why agreement estimates Jaccard: under a random permutation the first element of A ∪ B is equally likely to be any of them, and the two minima agree only if it lies in A ∩ B, so P(agree) = J; with n hashes the standard error is √(J(1 − J)/n), 0.034 at J = 0.82 and n = 128. The threshold is near (1/b)^(1/r), 0.71 for 16 × 8, where one band is expected to match. Follow-up: "what is the bottleneck at web scale?" Not hashing but connected components over billions of candidate edges, which needs distributed union-find; and false negatives are the costly error, because false positives are removed by an exact Jaccard check.	chapter ch::16
[Ch 16] How would you build a quality classifier, and what can go wrong?	Positive examples from a trusted reference (or LLM ratings of samples), negatives from random web; a cheap model (fastText) scores everything; keep top buckets. Risks: narrowing style and topics, bias against dialects or regions, over-fitting to benchmark-like text. Validate it by what it selects, not by its accuracy: train small proxies on the top-k% of each candidate classifier's picks at matched tokens and compare them on a broad suite, as DCLM did. Watch the circularity of positives that resemble benchmarks, and the token yield: FineWeb-Edu's threshold keeps 1.3T of 15T tokens (8.7%) and DCLM about 10%, which is too few unique tokens for a 15T-token run. At frontier scale, Nemotron-CC's answer is an ensemble of classifiers, many quality buckets weighted rather than cut, and rephrasing of lower-quality text. Follow-up: "how do you pick the threshold?" By the proxy results at several thresholds, constrained by how many epochs the selected data would need.	chapter ch::16
[Ch 16] Why deduplicate, and can you overdo it?	Saves compute, reduces memorisation and contamination. Overdoing it (for example global deduplication across many snapshots) can remove good recurring documents and leave low-quality unique ones. FineWeb found per-snapshot deduplication worked better than global deduplication for exactly that reason. Duplicates also distort the mixture: a page copied 1,000 times is effectively up-weighted 1,000×, which drives verbatim memorisation (Lee et al. 2021), and so extraction risk (Chapter 28). Follow-up: "how would you tell if you over-deduplicated?" Compare proxies trained on the two versions at matched tokens, and sample what the stricter setting removed.	chapter ch::16
[Ch 16] How do you choose a data mixture?	Small-scale ablations on proxy models, learned weighting (DoReMi), or fitted mixing laws, constrained by how many epochs each source can afford; validate per domain. DoReMi trains a reference model, then a proxy against an adversary that re-weights domains by excess loss (proxy minus reference) with multiplicative updates, and uses the averaged weights: from equal weights, excess losses of 0.30, 0.10 and 0.02 give 0.388, 0.318 and 0.294 after one step with η = 1. RegMix trains many tiny proxies on random mixtures, regresses loss on the weights and optimises the prediction; it pays off when there are many domains. The epoch constraint comes from Chapter 13: at temperature T = 2 a 15T-token run sees a 100B-token maths source 7.5 times. Follow-up: "what does a loss-optimal mixture miss?" Skills that loss on a generic validation set does not measure, so track per-domain loss and targeted benchmarks.	chapter ch::16
[Ch 16] What is annealing on high-quality data, and how can you use it as a measurement tool?	Switching to curated data during the final learning-rate decay. Annealing a checkpoint with and without a candidate source is a cheap test of its value. It works because as the learning rate falls, the noise floor drops and the data seen during the decay shapes where the weights settle. Llama 3 reports that annealing on high-quality code and maths raised its 8B model on GSM8K and MATH by 24.0% and 6.4%, with negligible effect on the 405B model, so the effect shrinks once pretraining has taught the skill. Follow-up: "what is the risk?" Benchmark-like anneal data inflates scores; decontaminate the anneal set with extra care and keep a held-out task family.	chapter ch::16
[Ch 16] How do you decontaminate, and what are the limits?	N-gram overlap (around 13-grams) with test sets, plus embedding or LLM checks for paraphrases. Misses translations, paraphrases and contamination via synthetic data. The check script's example: a copied homework page shares 16 13-grams with the benchmark item and is flagged; a paraphrase shares none and is missed. Report how many documents were removed per benchmark, and run membership-style checks on the trained model (Chapter 23). Follow-up: "what if a benchmark leaked anyway?" Report results on a fresh, post-cut-off or private set and say so.	chapter ch::16
[Ch 16] What are the risks of synthetic pretraining data?	Narrowing, inherited errors, contamination, and collapse if models train on their own outputs repeatedly without fresh data. Rephrasing real documents is the safest form because the facts come from real text; generating from scratch is riskier because the generator's errors and blind spots become training data. Mitigations: keep real data in the mix, verify what can be verified (maths answers, code tests), generate from diverse prompts and seeds, and decontaminate synthetic data as strictly as web data. Follow-up: "how would you measure narrowing?" Diversity metrics on the generated set (n-gram and embedding diversity) and per-domain held-out loss on real text.	chapter ch::16
[Ch 16] How do you make data ablations trustworthy?	Proxy models large and long enough, evaluations with continuous signals, seeds for a noise floor, one change at a time, check that rankings agree across two proxy scales. Turn the seeds into a threshold: with a measured seed standard deviation of 0.5 points, the difference between two arms has standard error 0.5 × √(2/k), so it must exceed 1.39 points with one seed per arm, 0.98 with two and 0.80 with three. A proxy is too small when the evaluation sits at chance: on 1,000 four-choice questions anything below about 27.7% is indistinguishable from guessing, so switch to log-likelihood or cloze scoring or a larger proxy. Follow-up: "your intervention gives +0.6 points with one seed": rerun with at least three seeds per arm and a paired comparison before reporting it.	chapter ch::16
[Ch 16] How would you improve a model's performance in a low-resource language?	Better language ID and extraction for that language, language-specific quality filters, upsampling within epoch limits, translated and synthetic data with verification, tokenizer coverage (Chapter 9), and evaluation by native speakers. Quantify the epoch budget first: a language with 2B unique tokens given 1% of a 2T-token run is seen 10 times, past the point where repetition stops helping (Chapter 13), so the gain must come from more unique data (better extraction, more snapshots, translation, rephrasing) rather than a higher weight. Check the tokenizer: if a word costs three times the tokens it costs in English, the model sees a third of the text per token. Follow-up: "how do you know it worked?" Per-language held-out loss and native benchmarks, plus a check that English did not regress.	chapter ch::16
[Ch 16] Which evaluations would you use for a 1B-parameter data ablation, and why?	Ones that move above noise at that scale: per-domain held-out loss or bits per byte (continuous and cheap), and benchmarks in a cloze or log-likelihood formulation rather than multiple-choice letter answers, which small models cannot yet use. Drop any benchmark where the proxies sit within two standard errors of chance (27.7% on a 1,000-question four-choice set), and prefer benchmarks whose scores rise monotonically over training checkpoints, which is a sign of signal rather than noise. Measure the seed noise of each evaluation and weight decisions towards high signal-to-noise ones. Follow-up: "the loss improves but no benchmark moves": report both, and test whether the loss gain is concentrated in a domain that the benchmarks do not cover.	chapter ch::16
[Ch 16] Design the data pipeline for a code model.	Start from public repositories with licence detection and opt-outs, redact secrets and personal data, then deduplicate exactly and with MinHash at file level, because forks and vendored code make duplication far higher than on the web. Filter auto-generated and data files, extreme line lengths and low alphanumeric ratios; add a quality classifier for educational value; and decontaminate against coding benchmarks, including their solutions. Group files by repository and order them by dependencies so training sequences contain cross-file context. Mix in natural-language text about code (documentation, Q&A) and maths. The number to check is the epoch budget per language: popular languages have hundreds of billions of unique tokens, rare ones a few billion, so rare languages hit the repetition limit first. Follow-up: "how do you evaluate?" Execution-based benchmarks with the unbiased pass@k estimator (Chapter 13), after decontamination.	chapter ch::16
[Ch 17] Estimate the time to train a 70B model on 15T tokens on 4,096 GPUs.	6 × 70e9 × 15e12 = 6.3e24 FLOPs; 4,096 × ~1e15 FLOP/s × 0.4 MFU ≈ 1.6e18 FLOP/s; about 45 days. That is 4.4M GPU-hours, roughly $9M at an assumed $2 per GPU-hour, and the GPU-hours barely change if you use 1,024 or 16,384 GPUs: only the wall-clock does. State the assumptions out loud (6ND ignores attention, about +15% at 8k context; 40% MFU is good, not guaranteed) and add the losses a plan must budget: restarts and lost work, which the arithmetic of 17.9 puts at several percent to over 10% at this scale. The follow-up is "and on Blackwell?": at roughly 2.25 PFLOP/s dense bf16 per GPU the same run takes about 20 days on 4,096 GPUs, or fewer with fp8.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] How much memory does training a 7B model with Adam need?	16 bytes per parameter = 112 GB for weights, gradients, fp32 master copy and the two Adam moments, plus activations: at 4,096 tokens per sequence and micro-batch 1 with FlashAttention, about 32 layers × 4,096 × 4,096 × 34 bytes = 18 GB. So it does not fit on one 80 GB GPU. FSDP over 8 GPUs brings the state to 14 GB per GPU, which leaves room for activations; an 8-bit Adam (2 + 2 + 4 + 2 bytes) brings the total to 70 GB; activation checkpointing cuts activations to about one layer's input per layer. If the goal is fine-tuning rather than training, LoRA on a frozen bf16 base needs about 14 GB and QLoRA about 4 GB (Chapter 20). The follow-up: "what about a 192 GB GPU?" The 112 GB fits, but you still shard to use more GPUs at once.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] Explain ZeRO stages 1–3.	Shard optimiser states (1), plus gradients (2), plus weights (3). Stages 1 and 2 add no communication; stage 3 gathers weights per layer, about 1.5× DDP's traffic, hidden by prefetching. The 1.5× follows from counting collectives: DDP all-reduces the gradients (2M); ZeRO-1/2 reduce-scatter the gradients and all-gather the updated weights (also 2M); ZeRO-3 all-gathers the weights in the forward pass and again in the backward pass and reduce-scatters the gradients (3M). For 70B on 64 GPUs the state per GPU falls from 1,120 GB to 293, 155 and 17.5 GB. When not to use ZeRO-3: across slow links with small per-GPU batches, where the gathers cannot be hidden; HSDP (shard within a node or rack, replicate across) is the usual answer.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] How does ring all-reduce work, and what does it cost?	Reduce-scatter then all-gather around a ring; each GPU sends about twice the data size, independent of the number of GPUs. Precisely, each GPU sends 2(n − 1) chunks of M/n bytes, 2(n − 1)/n · M, so 140 GB of 70B gradients cost 276 GB per GPU, 5.5 s at 50 GB/s. The hidden cost is latency: 2(n − 1) sequential steps, about 20 ms on 1,024 GPUs at 10 µs each, which is why libraries switch to tree or hierarchical algorithms at scale and send gradients in large buckets. Follow-up: "why is the bandwidth term optimal?" Every GPU must receive the other n − 1 shards of the sum and contribute its own to each, so (n − 1)/n · M in each direction of the two phases is a lower bound.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] How does Megatron split an MLP across GPUs?	First matrix by columns, second by rows; each GPU computes its hidden slice independently; one all-reduce sums the outputs. Attention splits by heads. The trick is that the elementwise non-linearity sits between the two matrices: a column split of the first matrix gives each GPU complete hidden units, so GeLU or SwiGLU can be applied locally with no communication, and the row split of the second matrix produces partial sums that one all-reduce combines. That gives two all-reduces per layer forward and two backward, 0.94 GB per GPU per layer for the 70B example at 8k tokens. With sequence parallelism each all-reduce becomes a reduce-scatter and an all-gather, the same volume, but the norm and dropout regions are then split too.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] Why keep tensor parallelism inside a node?	It needs an all-reduce of activations in every layer, forward and backward, so it needs NVLink-class bandwidth. For the 70B example that is 301 GB per GPU per step: 0.67 s at 450 GB/s on NVLink, 6 s at 50 GB/s over the network, longer than the 4.35 s step. The precise rule is "inside an NVLink domain", which is 8 GPUs on H100 and B200 servers but 72 on NVL72 racks. Even there, TP rarely goes much beyond 8, because each GPU's matrix multiplications become too small to run efficiently; the bigger domain is spent on expert parallelism and sharding instead.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] What is the pipeline bubble, and how do you reduce it?	Idle time at the start and end of each batch, (p−1)/(m+p−1). More micro-batches, interleaved schedules, zero-bubble schedules. The formula comes from counting slots: a step lasts m + p − 1 micro-batch slots and each stage idles for p − 1 of them, so 4 stages and 16 micro-batches lose 15.8%, and interleaving two chunks per GPU cuts it to 8.6%. Each fix has a price: more micro-batches mean a larger global batch (risking the critical batch size), interleaving keeps about 1 + (p − 1)/(p·v) times more activations in flight and sends more boundary traffic, and zero-bubble schedules complicate the code. On rack-scale hardware the cheapest fix is often to drop PP altogether.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] How does context parallelism handle attention?	Each GPU holds a block of the sequence; key–value blocks pass round a ring while each GPU computes attention for its queries, with load balancing for the causal mask. The transfer is hidden when s/n ≥ (F/B)·(h_kv/h): for the 70B shape, about 2,500 tokens per GPU over a 50 GB/s network and under 300 over NVLink. The alternative is Ulysses: two all-to-alls switch from a sequence split to a head split, so each GPU runs ordinary attention on the full sequence for a/n heads; it is simple and fast inside an NVLink domain but limited by the number of key–value heads. Large runs combine them.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] What is MFU, and what is a good value?	Model FLOPs achieved per second over peak; 35–50% for well-tuned dense training, lower for MoE. Compute it from throughput: 900 tokens/s per GPU × (6 × 70e9 + 12 × 80 × 8,192 × 8,192) FLOPs per token ÷ 989 TFLOP/s = 44% for the 70B example. Count model FLOPs only: recomputation is real hardware work but does not count, which is why MFU is lower than "hardware FLOPs utilisation". For fp8 runs say which peak you divide by: a run 1.5× faster than a 40%-MFU bf16 run is at 60% of the bf16 peak but 30% of the fp8 peak.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] Your run's throughput dropped 20% overnight. What do you check?	Triage in order of likelihood and cheapness. First, is it everywhere or one rank: per-rank step times show a straggler (one slow GPU, a degraded link, thermal throttling) as one outlier that every synchronous step waits for; evict or replace the node. Second, did anything change: a config push, a new data shard (longer documents, slower tokenisation), a library or driver update, a checkpoint interval or size change. Third, what the trace says on a slow step: data-loader waits point to storage or I/O contention; longer communication points to the network (error counters, a congested switch, a job sharing the fabric). A 20% drop on 1,024 GPUs at $2 an hour is about $10,000 a day, so the fix is worth hours, not days. Follow-up: "nothing changed and all ranks are equally slow" — check clocks and power capping across the cluster, and compare against a known-good micro-benchmark of the same kernels.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] How often should you checkpoint?	Balance checkpoint cost against expected lost work: √(2·C·M); make checkpoints sharded and asynchronous to make C small. The derivation is two lines: the waste is C/T for checkpointing plus T/(2M) for the half-interval lost per failure; the minimum is at T = √(2CM). At C = 1.2 min and a failure every 3 hours, T = 21 minutes and 11.5% of the run is wasted; cutting C to 0.1 min by writing asynchronously gives 6 minutes and 3.3%. The restart itself adds R/M regardless of T (15 minutes every 3 hours is 8.3%), so at large scale the follow-up answer is "make restarts fast": hot spares, automatic relaunch, and loading from peer memory.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] Derive ZeRO-3's communication cost and say when it is not worth it.	With M the bf16 weight size and n ranks: forward all-gather of every layer's weights, (n − 1)/n · M; a second all-gather in the backward pass, since the weights were freed after use; a reduce-scatter of the gradients, another (n − 1)/n · M. Total 3(n − 1)/n · M, against DDP's 2(n − 1)/n · M, so 1.5×. For 70B on 64 GPUs: 413 GB against 276 GB per GPU per step, 8.3 s against 5.5 s at 50 GB/s. It is hidden only if each layer's compute takes longer than gathering the next layer's weights, which needs enough tokens per GPU. When it is not worth it: small per-GPU batches over slow links, or a model that already fits with ZeRO-1. Then use HSDP or ZeRO-1 with TP.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] Where does the 34 in the activation formula come from, and how would you adapt it to a Llama-style block?	It is a byte count per token per layer, in units of the width h: 11 for attention (the QKV input, Q and K, V, the output-projection input, a dropout mask), 19 for the MLP (input, the 4h-wide first output and its GeLU, a mask), 4 for the two layer-norm inputs; the attention scores add 5·a·s/h, which FlashAttention removes. For a Llama-style block, drop the dropout masks, shrink K and V by the GQA factor (8 of 64 heads), and recount the SwiGLU MLP as about 2 + 8f/h bytes for width f = 3.5h: roughly 6.5 + 30 + 4 ≈ 40. Then measure once and calibrate, because fused kernels move these counts.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] Plan a layout for a 70B dense model on 1,152 GPUs in NVL72 racks. What changes from an H100 cluster?	On 8-GPU H100 servers the plan was TP = 8, PP = 4, DP = 32, with a 15.8% bubble. With 72 GPUs per NVLink domain and about 186 GB per GPU, keep TP = 8 inside the rack, drop PP and use ZeRO-1 across DP = 144: 35 GB of weights and gradients, 0.7 GB of optimiser state and about 23 GB of activations per GPU, no bubble, 1.9 s per 4.7M-token step at 40% MFU, about 70 days for 15T tokens. The gradient all-reduce, 35 GB per GPU per step, now crosses racks and must be overlapped. I would not widen TP to 72: per-GPU matrices get too small. The domain size matters most for MoE, where 64-way expert parallelism fits on NVLink. Follow-up: "what is the failure unit?" The rack; budget spares.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] What does fp8 training change, and how do you know it did not hurt quality?	The linear layers' matrix products run in fp8 at twice the bf16 tensor-core rate, with fine-grained scales (per 1 × 128 activation tile and 128 × 128 weight block in DeepSeek-V3, 32-value blocks in MXFP8) so that outliers do not crush small values; master weights, optimiser state, accumulation, embeddings, the output head, norms and softmax stay in higher precision. End-to-end gains are typically well under 2×, and memory per parameter barely changes because the optimiser state dominates. Validation is a long matched comparison against bf16 on the same data, at small and intermediate scale, on loss and downstream evaluations, because fp8 problems often appear late. Report MFU against a stated peak.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 17] MFU is 25% and the plan said 40%. Walk me through finding the gap.	Convert to time: 25% is 6.96 s per step against 4.35 s planned, so 2.6 s is missing. Take a profiler trace on one rank (the first pipeline stage) and account for every second: GEMMs 2.6 s (67% of peak on those FLOPs, so the shapes are fine), attention 0.7 s, small memory-bound kernels 1.2 s, exposed communication 1.0 s, data-loader wait 0.36 s, bubble 1.1 s. Fix the largest fixable items first: fusion with torch.compile (1.2 → 0.5 s), better overlap of collectives (1.0 → 0.3 s), loader prefetch (→ 0), interleaved pipeline (15.8% → 8.6%): 4.48 s, 38.8% MFU. Then compare step times across ranks for stragglers. The trap to avoid is changing several things at once.	chapter ch::17
[Ch 18] What makes a good SFT dataset?	Diverse prompts covering target skills and formats, high-quality verified responses, both refusals and non-refusals done right, multi-turn data, deduplicated and decontaminated. Quality beats volume for format; skills need volume. LIMA's 1,000 examples are enough to teach the assistant format to a strong base model, but maths, code and tool use need hundreds of thousands of verified, mostly synthetic examples. The trade-off to name is breadth against forgetting: a narrow mixture teaches its skill and erodes others, so keep general data in the mix and run a capability-regression suite after every SFT run. Follow-up: "how do you verify synthetic responses?" Execute code, check maths answers against references, and use a judge only where nothing checkable exists.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] Write the Bradley–Terry reward-model loss and explain its assumptions.	−log σ(r_c − r_r). Assumes preferences come from a single scalar reward per response plus logistic noise; ignores ties, intransitive and context-dependent preferences. The logistic is exact if each rater's perceived quality is the true reward plus Gumbel noise, and only differences are identified, so rewards are normalised before RL. A pair scored (1.2, 0.2) costs 0.313 and a mis-ranked pair (0.3, 0.5) costs 0.799, so the gradient concentrates on mistakes. Its weak spot is that one scalar must serve every annotator and every criterion, which is why teams train separate helpfulness and safety models or move to generative, rubric-based reward models.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] Walk through PPO for LLMs.	Sample responses; score them with the reward model; add a per-token KL penalty to the reference; compute advantages with a value model and GAE; update with the clipped ratio objective for a few epochs; repeat. In memory there are four models: policy, value (often initialised from the reward model), frozen reference and frozen reward model; the last two can be offloaded or share a base with adapters. Typical settings are ε = 0.2, 1–4 epochs per batch, a KL coefficient around 0.01–0.1, γ = 1 and λ close to 1. Monitor the KL to the reference, value loss, policy entropy, clip fraction, and the correlation of reward with response length, which is the first sign of hacking. Follow-up: "why is it slow?" Generation and three forward passes per batch dominate, so RLHF systems put an inference engine in the loop.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] Why clip the probability ratio?	The data was sampled by the old policy; large policy changes make the advantage estimates invalid. Clipping removes the incentive to move the ratio beyond 1 ± ε. Concretely, with ε = 0.2 and A = +1, a ratio of 1.5 earns only 1.2: the objective is flat beyond the clip, so there is no gradient pushing further. The clip is one-sided in the useful sense: moves in the wrong direction (ratio 1.5 on a negative advantage) are penalised in full. It is a cheap first-order stand-in for TRPO's KL trust region. Follow-up: "does clipping bound the KL?" Not strictly; many epochs on the same batch can still drift, so implementations also monitor approximate KL and stop early.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] Derive DPO in a few lines.	The KL-regularised optimum is π_ref·exp(r/β)/Z. Solve for r, substitute into Bradley–Terry; Z cancels; the loss depends only on log-ratios of policy to reference. The step people skip is the first: write J(π) = Σπr − βΣπ log(π/π_ref) = −βΣπ log(π/(π_ref e^(r/β))) = −β KL(π ‖ π*) + β log Z with π* = π_ref e^(r/β)/Z, so the optimum is π* because the KL is zero only there. Then r = β log(π*/π_ref) + β log Z, and the difference r_c − r_r loses the intractable Z. Check: on the four-response bandit the DPO solution matches [0.451, 0.332, 0.183, 0.033] exactly. Follow-up: "what assumption breaks in practice?" The derivation assumes the optimum over all policies with full data coverage; finite offline data and a parametric policy break it.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] If DPO and RLHF share an optimum, why do they behave differently?	DPO fits a fixed offline dataset; PPO samples on-policy and can explore. Distribution shift, finite data and likelihood displacement make offline DPO weaker; iterative on-policy DPO narrows the gap. The shared optimum holds only with infinite data covering every response; with finite pairs, DPO's loss says nothing about responses outside the data, and the policy can put mass there. Careful comparisons (Xu et al., 2024) found tuned PPO ahead, most clearly on code, while Llama 3 chose DPO with fresh pairs each round for compute and stability at 405B. My default: iterative DPO as the baseline, online RL where a reliable reward exists. Follow-up: "how do you tell distribution shift is the problem?" Measure the policy's log-likelihood of the preference data; if it is far below that of its own samples, the pairs are off-policy.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] What is reward over-optimisation and how do you detect and limit it?	Proxy reward keeps rising while true quality falls. Detect with a gold judge or humans on checkpoints at increasing KL; limit with KL penalties, early stopping, better or ensembled reward models, length control. In the chapter's best-of-n simulation the gold reward peaks around n = 8–16 (KL ≈ 1.2–1.8 nats) and is below the starting point by n = 1,024, while the proxy keeps climbing. The KL budget is the most transferable control: Gao et al. found gold reward follows a predictable curve in √KL, and larger reward models push the peak further out. Follow-up: "what does it look like in samples?" Longer, more confident, more flattering answers; read them.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] Your DPO run increases the margin but the chosen log-probability falls. Is that a problem?	It is likelihood displacement: mass moves to other responses. Check generation quality; add an SFT term on chosen responses, lower the learning rate, or use on-policy pairs. It is not automatically a problem: if held-out win rates improve, the mass may have moved to better responses outside the data. It becomes one when quality or format degrades, which is likelier when chosen and rejected responses are near-identical, because the push down on the rejected response drags the shared tokens with it. So filter near-duplicate pairs too, and compare the chosen log-probability with that of the model's own greedy outputs. Follow-up: "why doesn't the loss prevent it?" It depends only on the difference of log-ratios, which can rise while both fall.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] When would you choose KTO over DPO?	When feedback is binary per response (thumbs up/down, accept/reject) and pairs are not available. KTO scores each response's implied reward log(π/π_ref) against a reference point z₀ (a batch KL estimate) with a saturating loss, λ_D(1 − σ(β(ρ − z₀))) for desirable responses and λ_U(1 − σ(β(z₀ − ρ))) for undesirable ones, and the two weights correct for imbalance. When not to: if real pairs exist, DPO or IPO uses the comparison directly and is usually more sample-efficient, and with extreme imbalance (99% thumbs-up) the weights must be tuned carefully or the rare class is ignored. Product logs are also biased towards what users chose to rate.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] What are the risks of AI feedback?	Judge biases (self-preference, length, position), shared blind spots, and reward hacking against the judge. Audit against human labels, rotate judges, control for length. Concretely: swap the order of the two responses and keep only consistent verdicts, cap or normalise length in the comparison, use a judge from a different model family than the policy, and keep a human-labelled audit set of a few hundred pairs per category to measure judge–human agreement over time. The deeper risk is invisible: errors the judge and the policy share are never caught by agreement checks between them. Follow-up: "why does it work at all?" Comparing is easier than generating, and a written rubric makes the judgement explicit.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] How would you evaluate a new post-trained checkpoint before release?	Instruction-following, length-controlled chat quality, capability regression suite, safety and over-refusal, calibration, plus a human evaluation sample; compare with the previous checkpoint with confidence intervals. A number to have ready: a win rate measured on 500 prompts has a standard error of about 2.2 points near 50%, so differences under about 4–5 points need a bigger sample or a paired design. The ship decision is a set of gates, not one score: no regression beyond its interval on any capability, over-refusal not worse, and human preference not worse. Follow-up: "which regression is most often missed?" Maths, code and multilingual ability after preference tuning on chat data.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] Derive the policy gradient.	J = E_(y∼π)[R(y)]; ∇J = Σ_y ∇π(y) R(y) = Σ_y π(y) ∇log π(y) R(y) = E[R(y) ∇log π(y)]. For a sequence, ∇log π(y) is the sum of per-token log-probability gradients. No gradient of the reward is needed. The key step is ∇π = π ∇log π, which turns a sum weighted by π into an expectation you can sample. In the chapter's four-response bandit, batches of 8 samples estimate the gradient without bias but with a total standard deviation of 0.174, which rises to 3.15 when every reward is shifted by 10, and that is the motivation for baselines. Follow-up: "why can the reward be a unit-test runner?" Because only log π is differentiated.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] Why does a baseline not bias the gradient, and which baseline is best?	E[b ∇log π(y)] = b ∇Σ_y π(y) = 0 for any b that does not depend on the sampled y. Variance is lowest near the expected reward: a learned value (PPO), a group mean (GRPO) or a leave-one-out mean (RLOO, exactly unbiased; the plain group mean shrinks the gradient by (N − 1)/N). In the check script, rewards shifted by +10 give a gradient standard deviation of 3.15 without a baseline, 0.093 with the group mean and 0.106 leave-one-out; the group mean's estimate is 0.875 of the true gradient at N = 8, a scale the learning rate absorbs. Follow-up: "can the baseline depend on the prompt?" Yes; it must not depend on the sampled response.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] On-policy versus off-policy: why can't PPO reuse old rollouts indefinitely?	Off-policy reuse needs importance weights; for a sequence they are products of per-token ratios whose variance grows exponentially with length. PPO uses per-token ratios, clips them, and runs only a few epochs per batch so the data stays nearly on-policy. With a per-token log-ratio standard deviation of only 0.03, the sequence ratio's variance is exp(T·0.03²) − 1: 0.09 at 100 tokens, 1.46 at 1,000 and 35.6 at 4,000, where the effective sample size is 3% of the batch. Follow-up: "so is PPO biased?" Yes: per-token ratios with a sequence-level advantage are an approximation, which GSPO and CISPO revisit (Chapter 19).	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] Why do LLMs use policy-gradient methods rather than Q-learning?	Huge action space (the vocabulary) at every step, a strong pretrained policy to start from, and a sparse terminal reward. Policy gradients improve the existing policy directly; Q-learning would need to learn values for 100k+ actions per state from scratch. The nuance a sharp interviewer may raise: the logits of a language model can be read as soft Q-values for a KL-regularised (maximum-entropy) problem, which is how Rafailov et al. (2024, "From r to Q*") reinterpret DPO as token-level credit assignment. So "from scratch" is the practical problem, not a theoretical one: the pretrained policy is a good initial Q-function only in that soft sense, and greedy max-Q acting would discard the sampling diversity RL needs.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] Derive DPO's loss from the RLHF objective on the whiteboard, including the step everyone skips.	Start from J(π) = Σ_y π(y)r(y) − βΣ_y π(y) log(π(y)/π_ref(y)). Factor out −β and put the reward in the logarithm: J = −βΣπ log(π/(π_ref e^(r/β))). Normalise the denominator with Z = Σπ_ref e^(r/β), defining π* = π_ref e^(r/β)/Z; since Σπ = 1, J = −β KL(π ‖ π*) + β log Z. The maximum is π = π*, with value β log Z: for rewards [1, 0.5, 0, −1], β = 0.5 and π_ref = [0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.4], Z = 1.637 and the maximum is 0.246, against −0.200 at π_ref. Invert to r = β log(π*/π_ref) + β log Z; in the Bradley–Terry difference Z cancels; replace π* by π_θ to get −log σ(β[log-ratio(y_c) − log-ratio(y_r)]). Follow-up: "where did the KL direction matter?" It is the reverse KL KL(π ‖ π_ref), which is what makes the optimum a reweighting of π_ref.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] Write the IPO, SimPO and KTO losses and say when you would use each.	With h the log-ratio margin: IPO is (h − 1/(2τ))², a squared loss with a finite target margin, for noisy or near-deterministic preferences where DPO's margin would run away (at h = 1.5 and τ = 0.1, the loss is 12.25, pulling towards h = 5). SimPO is −log σ((β/|y_c|) log π(y_c) − (β/|y_r|) log π(y_r) − γ): no reference model and length-normalised, for when length bias is the main problem and memory is tight. KTO is λ_D(1 − σ(β(ρ − z₀))) for good and λ_U(1 − σ(β(z₀ − ρ))) for bad responses, for unpaired thumbs-up/down data. On the chapter's example pair, DPO sees a correct ranking (0.621) while SimPO sees a mis-ranked pair (1.148) because the chosen response is shorter, a reminder to check what your margin is really measuring.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] What does λ in GAE control? Give numbers.	λ interpolates between one-step TD (λ = 0: A_t = δ_t, low variance, biased by the value model) and the Monte Carlo return minus a baseline (λ = 1: unbiased, noisy). On the chapter's trajectory (values [0.4, 0.5, 0.6, 0.8], reward 1 at the end) the advantages are [0.1, 0.1, 0.2, 0.2] at λ = 0, [0.547, 0.470, 0.390, 0.200] at 0.95 and [0.6, 0.5, 0.4, 0.2] at 1. With an under-confident value model, λ = 0 delivered 60% of the true advantage gap with no noise, λ = 1 all of it with a standard deviation of 0.48. The LLM-specific point: the real reward reaches a token k positions from the end with weight λ^k, 0.006 at k = 100 for λ = 0.95, so long responses push you to λ ≈ 1 or to dropping the value model (GRPO).	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] Why did Llama 3 drop PPO for rejection sampling and DPO? Would you?	Its report cites compute and stability: at 405B parameters, PPO's four models and online generation are expensive, and DPO with fresh on-policy pairs each round performed better in their setting, especially on instruction following. Rejection-sampling SFT (sample n, keep the reward model's best, fine-tune) captures much of best-of-n's gain without RL, and running several rounds makes the whole pipeline close to on-policy. I would make the same choice for general chat and preference alignment, where the reward is a learned model that over-optimisation would exploit anyway. I would not for maths, code or agent tasks with verifiers: there online RL (Chapter 19) is clearly stronger. Follow-up: "what does rejection sampling cost?" n samples per prompt plus scoring: at n = 8 it is eight times the generation of plain SFT data.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 18] How would you collect 100,000 human preference pairs, and what would go wrong?	Write guidelines with a priority order (harmlessness, correctness, helpfulness, style) and hard worked examples, including over-refusal cases; ask for preference strength to filter near-ties; double-label 5–10% to measure agreement per category; seed gold items to track each annotator; sample responses from the current policy so the reward model learns about what it will judge. Budget: at four minutes a pair, about 6,700 annotator-hours. What goes wrong: length bias (chosen responses longer), drift in raters and guidelines, agreement of only 60–70% on chat that sets the reward model's ceiling, and helpfulness–harmlessness conflicts that one scalar cannot represent. Follow-up: "how do you treat ties?" Drop them, or keep them with a 0.5 soft label that pulls the rewards together.	chapter ch::18
[Ch 19] How was DeepSeek-R1 trained?	Strong base model; R1-Zero: RL with GRPO and rule-based correctness and format rewards, no SFT; R1: a small cold-start SFT on readable long traces, reasoning RL, then rejection-sampled SFT and a general RL stage; then distillation into small models. Each stage fixes the previous one's problem: the cold start fixes R1-Zero's unreadable, language-mixing traces (helped by a language-consistency reward during RL); the rejection-sampled SFT (about 600k reasoning plus 200k general samples) restores general abilities that reasoning RL ignored; the final RL stage adds helpfulness and harmlessness rewards. The 800k samples also trained the distilled models, which beat RL run directly on models of the same size. Follow-up: "what did R1-Zero prove?" That outcome rewards alone, at scale, elicit long reasoning and self-verification from a strong base model.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] Explain GRPO and why it has no value model.	Sample a group per prompt; the advantage is the reward relative to the group mean (often divided by its std); PPO-style clipped updates. The group mean is the baseline, so no learned critic. For rewards [1, 0, 0, 1, 1, 0, 0, 0] the advantages are +1.21 and −0.72. Dropping the critic saves a model the size of the policy and avoids its hardest job, predicting eventual success from a half-written chain of thought, at the price of G samples per prompt. When not to: if rewards are dense per step or episodes are very long and multi-turn, a learned value can still pay. Follow-up: "is the group mean unbiased?" It includes the sample itself, which shrinks the gradient by (G − 1)/G; RLOO's leave-one-out mean does not.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] What is the length bias in the original GRPO loss?	Per-response averaging gives each token weight A/|o|, so long wrong answers are penalised less per token, encouraging length growth on failures. Fix: token-level averaging or a constant normaliser. In the chapter's group, a correct 200-token answer gets +6.04e-3 per token while wrong answers of 1,500 and 2,000 tokens get −0.48e-3 and −0.36e-3; with token-level averaging every correct token gets +0.18e-3 and every wrong one −0.11e-3. In total weight, the 2,000-token wrong answer goes from −0.091 (the same as a 400-token one) to −0.215. Follow-up: "why does it matter for 'emergent' length growth?" Part of the length increase reported in early R1-style runs is this artefact, so measure length growth under both normalisations before calling it reasoning.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] What does DAPO change?	Clip-higher (asymmetric clipping), dynamic sampling (drop groups with no reward variance), token-level loss, overlong reward shaping, no KL penalty. Each targets a measured failure: entropy collapse (the upper clip at 1.28 lets a token at p = 0.01 reach 0.0128 instead of 0.012), wasted compute on zero-signal groups (at G = 8 a prompt at a 5% pass rate gives nothing 66% of the time), the length bias above, and noisy rewards on truncated responses. Removing the KL assumes the policy should move far from the reference, which holds for long-reasoning RL but not for preference tuning. Follow-up: "which change matters most?" Report ablations at a fixed generation budget; published gains are often recipe-specific.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] Why do groups where all answers are right (or wrong) matter?	Zero advantage, zero gradient. At extreme pass rates most groups give no signal; filter by difficulty or resample. The probability of a dead group is p^G + (1 − p)^G: 85% at a 2% pass rate with G = 8, 66% at 5%, under 1% at 50%. Doubling G to 16 cuts the 5% case to 44%, at twice the generation, so dynamic sampling and a 10–90% difficulty filter are cheaper than ever-larger groups. Follow-up: "what happens over training?" Prompts drift towards 100% as the policy improves, so the share of dead groups rises unless pass rates are re-estimated and the pool refilled.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] Does RL teach models new reasoning?	Evidence is mixed: RL raises pass@1 and often lowers pass@k at large k, suggesting sharpening of existing solutions; longer, more exploratory RL may extend the frontier; distillation from stronger models adds capability. Measure pass@k curves. The chapter's toy makes the mechanism concrete: pass@1 rises from 0.337 to 0.465 but pass@64 falls from 0.910 to 0.763, and on the hard quarter needing a rare strategy from 0.402 to 0.032, because the rare strategy is squeezed out. Yue et al. found the same crossing in real models; ProRL's long, reset-stabilised runs reported gains at large k where the base model failed. The decisive experiment: held-out problems the base model fails at k = 1,024, same temperature, strict checking. My view: short narrow RL sharpens; long diverse RL with exploration kept alive can extend, within limits set by pretraining.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] PRM versus ORM?	ORMs (or verifiers) judge final answers; PRMs judge each step. PRMs give denser signal and better reranking and search but are expensive to label and easy to hack as RL rewards. Lightman et al. needed 800k human step labels (PRM800K); Math-Shepherd replaced them with Monte Carlo estimates (the fraction of continuations from a step that reach the right answer), which costs many rollouts per step and inherits their noise. As an RL reward, a PRM is a learned model, so the policy learns steps that look good to it; R1-style training avoided PRMs for that reason. Where they earn their cost is at inference: reranking and step-level search. Follow-up: "how would you get process signal without a PRM?" Token- or step-level advantages from rollouts, or on-policy distillation from a teacher.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] When does self-consistency fail?	When a wrong answer is more probable than the right one: voting then amplifies the error. It also needs comparable final answers. With 30% single-sample accuracy and wrong answers spread over five options, majority voting over 25 samples reaches 70%; if one wrong answer takes the other 70%, the vote is right only 2% of the time. The cheap check is to sample 20–40 answers on a few hundred dev problems and look at answer histograms before paying for voting. For free-form outputs (proofs, code), cluster answers by an equivalence check or use a verifier instead.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] How would you reduce overthinking on easy questions?	Length penalties relative to group or difficulty, adaptive budgets, training on mixed difficulty with rewards for efficiency, routing easy queries to a non-thinking mode. A length penalty relative to the group (shorter correct answers get a bonus over longer correct ones) is safer than an absolute one, which can teach the model to give up on hard problems. Hybrid models make routing explicit: Qwen3-style thinking and non-thinking modes, or an effort setting, chosen by a classifier or the user. The number to quote: 4,000 thinking tokens before a 200-token answer is about 20× the output cost of answering directly. Follow-up: "how do you know you have not hurt hard problems?" Track accuracy by difficulty bucket, not on average.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] How do you make a 7B model reason well cheaply?	Distil verified long reasoning traces from a stronger model (SFT), then optionally a short RL stage on verifiable tasks at appropriate difficulty. Scale: s1 and LIMO showed about 1,000 carefully chosen traces already elicit much of the behaviour from a strong base; DeepSeek's distilled models used about 800k, and on small models that beat running RL directly. Cost: generating 100k traces of 8k tokens is 0.8B teacher tokens, small next to an RL run that generates over 30M tokens per step for a thousand steps. RL on top still pays when you have a good verifier and the student's pass rates on your target tasks sit in the 10–90% band; on-policy distillation is the middle option, dense like SFT and on-policy like RL, reported at about a tenth of RL's GPU hours. Follow-up: "what goes wrong with distillation?" The student copies the teacher's verbosity and errors unless traces are verified and filtered.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] Why is RL for reasoning expensive, and how do systems handle it?	Generation of long rollouts dominates; separate inference engines, asynchronous pipelines with bounded staleness, length-aware batching, careful handling of probability mismatches between engine and trainer. The arithmetic: a 30B model training on 32.8M tokens per step needs 10N FLOPs per token (update, reference, old log-probs), 97 s at 40% MFU on 256 GPUs; generation needs only 2N but decodes at low utilisation, and it dominates whenever utilisation is below 8% of peak (about 1,300 tokens/s per GPU here), plus the synchronous step waits for the longest responses. Hence asynchronous RL, which keeps both sides busy at the price of slightly stale samples. Follow-up: "how stale is acceptable?" Bound it to one or a few policy versions and correct with clipped or truncated importance weights.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] What is the training–inference mismatch in RL, and how do you handle it?	The rollout engine and the trainer give slightly different probabilities for the same tokens, so the data is off-policy. Recompute log-probabilities in the trainer, apply truncated importance weights, keep the output head in fp32, monitor the per-token gap, or use batch-invariant kernels. Why it bites: per-token differences compound over a sequence; at a log-ratio standard deviation of only 0.03, a 4,000-token sequence's ratio has variance 35.6 (Chapter 18.4). Truncated IS weights each token by min(π_trainer/π_engine, C): bounded variance for a little bias, which also makes 8-bit rollouts usable. Follow-up: "what is the early warning?" A rising engine–trainer KL often precedes a collapse.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] GRPO, GSPO and CISPO: what differs?	Same group-relative advantage. GRPO clips per-token ratios; GSPO uses one length-normalised sequence ratio and clips whole responses (more stable for MoE); CISPO clips the importance weight but keeps the gradient of every token, so rare high-ratio tokens still learn. The underlying tension: the reward belongs to the sequence but the ratio to the token. GSPO's sequence ratio is exp(mean per-token log-ratio), which keeps it near 1 even for long responses, unlike the raw product. CISPO's objective is stop-gradient(clipped weight) × Â × ∇log π, so a "wait" token whose ratio jumped above 1 + ε still gets a bounded gradient instead of none. Follow-up: "when would you pick GSPO?" MoE RL, where routing changes make per-token ratios noisy.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] How would you do RL where answers can't be checked?	Per-prompt rubrics scored by an LLM judge (weighted share of criteria met), generative reward models, or pairwise preferences; audit high-reward samples for judge exploitation and length bias, and validate the judge against expert labels. A rubric turns one vague judgement into several checkable ones ("mentions the interaction", "does not invent a dosage"), and Gunjal et al. found rubric rewards beat a single Likert judge score. The risks are the judge's: verbosity, rubric items recited rather than satisfied, and shared blind spots. Measure judge–expert agreement per criterion on a few hundred items before training, and keep a held-out human evaluation. Follow-up: "how is this different from RLHF?" The criteria are explicit per prompt, so the reward is narrower and harder to game than a general preference model.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] What is on-policy distillation and when would you use it?	Sample from the student, score each token by the teacher (reverse KL as a dense reward). Use it to make a small model match a strong teacher cheaply, or to recover skills lost after domain fine-tuning; it needs teacher log-probabilities on a shared vocabulary. It combines RL's on-policy sampling (the student learns in states it actually reaches, fixing SFT's exposure bias) with a dense per-token signal instead of one bit per episode. The Qwen3 report found it beat RL on the same student (74.4% against 67.6% on AIME'24) at about a tenth of the GPU hours. When not to: no access to teacher log-probabilities, a different tokenizer, or a target beyond the teacher's own ability.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] How do you compare two RL recipes fairly?	Same model, prompts, verifier and generation budget; several runs if affordable; fit a saturating curve of reward against compute and compare asymptote and efficiency, not one checkpoint; report pass@k curves and held-out evaluations. The reason: Khatri et al. found that most recipe details change how fast reward rises, not where it ends, so a recipe that wins early can plateau lower. Seeds matter too: RL runs are noisy, and a difference smaller than the spread of two seeds is not a result. Follow-up: "you can afford one run each?" Then compare at several compute points along the curve and say the conclusion is provisional.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] Write the full GRPO objective and say where the KL sits.	J = E[(1/G) Σ_i (1/|o_i|) Σ_t (min(ρ_(i,t)Â_i, clip(ρ_(i,t), 1 − ε, 1 + ε)Â_i) − β·D_(i,t))], where ρ_(i,t) = π_θ(o_(i,t) | q, o_(i,<t)) / π_old(·), Â_i = (r_i − mean r)/std r is shared by every token of output i, and D_(i,t) = π_ref/π_θ − log(π_ref/π_θ) − 1 is the k3 KL estimate. The KL sits in the loss, as a per-token regulariser, not in the reward as in InstructGPT-style PPO, so the advantage contains only the verifier's signal. Each term is a fix site: 1/|o_i| is the length bias (DAPO and Dr. GRPO replace it), the std is the difficulty weighting (Dr. GRPO drops it), the per-token ρ is what GSPO and CISPO change, and β is often set to zero for long reasoning. Follow-up: "compute one token": ρ = 1.3, Â = +1.21 gives min(1.573, 1.452) = 1.452.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] What is entropy collapse and why does clip-higher help?	Policy entropy, the mean over generated positions of the next-token distribution's entropy, falls fast under RL as the policy commits to strategies that already work; once it is near zero the policy stops exploring and pass@k at large k falls. The clip bounds the probability ratio, a multiplicative change, so a token at p = 0.01 can gain at most 0.002 per update with ε = 0.2, while one at 0.5 can gain 0.1: rare, useful tokens grow slowest. Raising only the upper bound to 1.28 lets them grow faster without letting probabilities fall faster. Other tools: temperature, entropy bonuses, removing the KL. Follow-up: "what entropy is healthy?" Slowly falling; a collapse in the first few hundred steps is the warning sign.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 19] Estimate the cost of one RLVR step for a 30B model with 512 prompts × 16 samples × 4k tokens.	Tokens: 32.8M. Training side: 10N FLOPs per token (6N update, 2N reference, 2N old-policy log-probs) = 9.8e18 FLOPs, 97 s on 256 GPUs at 40% MFU. Generation: 2N per token but memory-bound; at 2,000 tokens/s per GPU it takes 64 s, at 1,000 it takes 128 s. So a step is about 2.5–4 minutes and 1,000 steps is roughly 10–16k GPU-hours on H100-class hardware, before tail effects. The judgement: generation dominates when decode utilisation is below 8% of peak and because synchronous steps wait for the longest responses, so the first optimisations are decode throughput (TP for KV room, fp8 rollouts with truncated IS) and asynchronous generation. Follow-up: "what if you use G = 8?" Half the generation, but more dead groups.	chapter ch::19
[Ch 20] When would you fine-tune instead of prompting or using RAG?	For consistent format or style at scale, narrow high-volume tasks where a small fine-tuned model replaces an expensive one, or domain language the base model handles poorly. Not for adding changing facts (RAG) or when prompting already meets the bar. The usual order is prompt, then retrieval, then fine-tune when evaluation shows a stable gap or when cost demands a smaller model. Remember the hidden costs: data collection, re-training at every base-model upgrade, hosting, and a regression suite. Follow-up: "what about a model that keeps getting a format wrong?" Try constrained decoding (Chapter 21) before fine-tuning.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] Explain LoRA.	Freeze W; learn ΔW = B·A with rank r much smaller than the dimensions; output Wx + (α/r)BAx. B starts at zero so training starts from the base model. Trains well under 1% of parameters; merge afterwards for zero latency cost. Why B = 0 and A ≠ 0: each matrix's gradient contains the other (∂L/∂B ∝ g(Ax)ᵀ, ∂L/∂A ∝ Bᵀgxᵀ), so with both zero nothing ever trains; with A random, B gets a gradient at step one. A 4096 × 4096 layer at r = 16 has 131,072 trainable parameters, 0.78% of 16.9M. Use about 10× the full fine-tuning learning rate and adapt all linear layers, the MLPs above all. When it underperforms: knowledge injection and continued pretraining, where the data exceeds the adapter's capacity. Serving: merging costs nothing at inference; keeping adapters separate lets one base serve many tasks at a small per-request overhead.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] What do rank and alpha do?	Rank sets the capacity of the update; alpha scales it (by α/r), keeping the update's magnitude comparable across ranks so learning rates transfer. The reason is that Adam's first steps on B are aligned with Ax, so the output change grows like r and α/r cancels it. Later in training the r directions add like random terms, growing like √r, so α/r over-shrinks high ranks; rsLoRA uses α/√r, which keeps the output scale constant (about 0.092 at every rank in the chapter's check, while α/r falls from 0.046 to 0.006 between r = 4 and 256). Follow-up: "what rank would you pick?" Small (1–16) for RL and narrow SFT; larger only when the dataset is big.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] What is QLoRA?	LoRA on a frozen base quantised to 4-bit NF4 with double quantisation and paged optimisers, so large models fine-tune on one GPU with quality close to 16-bit LoRA. A 7B base takes about 3.5 GB instead of 14 GB, and double quantisation cuts the per-block constants from 0.5 to 0.127 bits per parameter. The cost is speed: every weight block is de-quantised for every matrix product, forward and backward. Merge the adapter into a 16-bit copy of the base for serving, not into the 4-bit weights. Follow-up: "why NF4 rather than int4?" Its levels sit at normal quantiles, which gives 9.2% rather than 10.1% relative RMS error on normally distributed weights.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] Prompt tuning vs prefix-tuning vs LoRA?	Prompt tuning learns soft input embeddings only; prefix-tuning learns key/value prefixes at every layer; LoRA learns low-rank updates to weights. LoRA is the most robust across model sizes and adds no inference latency once merged; prompt tuning is very parameter-cheap but weaker on small models. A soft prompt of 20 tokens at width 4,096 is only 82k parameters, but it uses context length on every request and acts only through the input. Prefix-tuning acts at every layer and needs reparameterisation to train stably. When prompt tuning still makes sense: very large frozen models served to many tasks, where swapping a few vectors per request is cheaper than any weight change.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] Which modules would you target with LoRA, and why?	All linear projections in attention and MLP blocks usually work best for a given budget; attention-only (q, v) is the original minimal setup. Choose by evaluation. The 2025 evidence is sharper: attention-only LoRA underperforms even when given the same parameter count through a higher rank, and the MLP (or, in MoE models, the expert) layers matter most, plausibly because that is where most parameters and most stored knowledge sit. For a 7B Llama-style model, r = 16 on all seven projections per block is 40M parameters (0.59%). Follow-up: "the embeddings?" Adapt them only when adding new tokens.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] How much memory does fine-tuning a 7B model need?	Full fine-tuning with Adam in mixed precision: about 16 bytes per parameter, roughly 112 GB plus activations. LoRA on a bf16 base: about 14 GB plus activations. QLoRA: about 3.5 GB for weights plus activations and adapters. Add activations at a stated shape: with gradient checkpointing, 8 sequences of 2,048 tokens store about 4.3 GB of layer inputs. Then the logits: 8 × 2,048 × 32k vocabulary × 4 bytes is 2.1 GB, plus its gradient, and with a 128k vocabulary 8.4 GB, often the real cause of an out-of-memory error; chunked or fused cross-entropy fixes it. So bf16 LoRA fits on a 24 GB GPU at this shape only with checkpointing and care over the logits.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] What is catastrophic forgetting and how do you limit it?	Loss of general abilities after narrow fine-tuning. Limit with PEFT, low learning rates, fewer epochs, replay of general data, and a regression suite. Biderman et al. found LoRA "learns less and forgets less": smaller gains on the target domain and smaller losses elsewhere than full fine-tuning. Replay of 10–30% general data is the usual trade. Measure it: a small general-ability and safety suite before and after every fine-tune, because safety behaviour is often the first thing to go. Follow-up: "can you recover lost skills afterwards?" On-policy distillation from the original model is one cheap way (Chapter 19.9).	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] How do you build training data for a fine-tune?	Real inputs where possible, outputs from experts or a strong model, filtered for quality and format, deduplicated, balanced across cases, with a separate human-verified evaluation set. Sample inputs from production so the distribution matches, oversample the hard cases you care about, and validate every output (parse it, check it, or have a judge or human audit a sample). Keep the evaluation set out of every synthetic pipeline, including as seeds. A few hundred to a few thousand examples is typical for a narrow task; look at errors before adding volume.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] You need a classifier for 50 million item descriptions a month. Plan it.	Label a stratified sample with a strong LLM (batch API), audit a few hundred by hand, fine-tune a small encoder or a small LLM with LoRA on the LLM labels, compare with TF-IDF and prompted baselines, route low-confidence cases to the LLM, monitor drift and re-label periodically. The cost argument decides it: 50M descriptions at about 100 tokens is 5B input tokens a month, which a small fine-tuned model serves for a small fraction of a frontier API's cost. Audit per class, because the LLM labeller's errors concentrate in a few confusable classes, and set the routing threshold from a precision target on the audit set.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] Your fine-tuned model scores higher on the task but users complain it got worse. What happened?	The evaluation set may not represent real traffic; general abilities or tone regressed; it overfits training phrasing; the chat template or decoding settings differ in production. Compare on production samples and run the regression suite. Debug in that order: sample real complaints and score the base and fine-tuned models side by side; check that serving renders the same template and uses the same sampling settings; check for safety and refusal drift. The common root cause is an evaluation set built from the same source as the training data, so it rewards memorised phrasing.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] How would you serve 20 different LoRA adapters efficiently?	One base model in GPU memory with multi-LoRA serving (adapters loaded per request and batched together), adapters cached by popularity, merged models only for the highest-traffic adapters. A rank-16 adapter on all linear layers of a 7B model is 40M parameters, about 80 MB in bf16, so 20 adapters take 1.6 GB against 13.5 GB for one base copy, while 20 merged models would need 270 GB. The overhead of unmerged adapters is a small extra matrix product per layer, batched across requests by kernels written for it. Merge only an adapter whose traffic justifies a dedicated replica.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] Why might LoRA match full fine-tuning on some tasks and not others?	Narrow tasks need low-rank changes, which LoRA captures; large distribution shifts (new languages, continued pre-training on lots of domain text, complex skills) need higher-rank updates, where full fine-tuning or high-rank LoRA does better. Biderman et al. measured the gap on continued pretraining and instruction tuning for code and maths: LoRA trailed full fine-tuning clearly on code continued pretraining and less on instruction tuning, while forgetting less. "LoRA Without Regret" frames it as capacity against information: LoRA matches full fine-tuning until the dataset carries more information than the adapter holds, which never happens in RL (at most about one bit per episode) and does happen with billions of tokens of new domain text.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] What did "LoRA Without Regret" find, and how does it change your defaults?	With enough capacity, LoRA tracks full fine-tuning in supervised and RL post-training; for RL even rank 1 matches it, because a binary reward carries at most a bit per episode (512k bits over a thousand steps of 512 episodes, against 2.5M parameters in rank-1 LoRA on a 7B model); the optimal learning rate is about 10× the full fine-tuning rate; MLP layers matter more than attention; and LoRA degrades more than full fine-tuning at large batch sizes. My defaults become: all layers, a small rank for RL, a rank scaled to the dataset for SFT, ten times the full fine-tuning learning rate, moderate batches. Follow-up: "when is full fine-tuning still worth it?" Large knowledge injection.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] Why does LoRA's B start at zero and A not? What breaks if both are zero?	h = Wx + (α/r)BAx. The gradients are ∂L/∂B = (α/r) g (Ax)ᵀ and ∂L/∂A = (α/r) Bᵀ g xᵀ: each involves the other matrix. With both zero, both gradients are exactly zero forever, a saddle point.
