With A random and B zero, BA = 0 so the model starts as the pretrained one, B gets a non-zero gradient immediately (0.39 against 0 for A in the chapter's check), and after one step A starts learning too. The mirror choice, A zero and B random, also works. Follow-up: "why random A rather than small?" A's scale sets the size of Ax and so of B's first gradients; Kaiming initialisation keeps it in a sensible range.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] Does fine-tuning affect safety? What would you do about it?	Yes, and not only when the data is harmful. Qi et al. (2023) removed most refusals with a handful of harmful examples and measurably weakened them with benign instruction data; emergent misalignment (Betley et al., 2025) showed that narrow training on insecure code made models broadly misaligned on unrelated prompts. So: mix safety data (refusals and helpful answers to benign-but-risky prompts) into every fine-tune, evaluate both harmful compliance and over-refusal before and after, prefer LoRA with a modest learning rate and few epochs, and for a public fine-tuning API, screen uploaded data and evaluate the resulting models before release.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] How would you fine-tune a reasoning model for a new domain?	Keep thinking traces in the training data, because training on bare answers teaches the model to skip its reasoning; generate traces by sampling the model itself and keeping those that reach correct answers (rejection sampling), or from a stronger teacher. Render data with the exact chat template, including how it handles thinking in earlier turns, and keep the loss on the trace. For tasks that need no reasoning, train and serve in the non-thinking mode, which is cheaper. Then check that reasoning benchmarks did not regress. If the domain has a verifier, a short RL stage with low-rank LoRA is often better than SFT. Follow-up: "the traces are 10× longer than the answers; cost?" Training tokens scale with them, so filter for concise correct traces.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 20] Your single-GPU LoRA fine-tune runs out of memory at the first step. What do you check?	Recompute the budget term by term: the base weights (14 GB in bf16 for 7B), adapter state (under 1 GB), activations (enable gradient checkpointing; 4.3 GB for 8 × 2,048 tokens), and the logits, which people forget: 8 × 2,048 × vocabulary × 4 bytes, 8.4 GB for a 128k vocabulary plus as much for the gradient. The logits are the usual culprit with modern vocabularies; use chunked or fused cross-entropy, or a smaller micro-batch with gradient accumulation. Then check the sequence length distribution (one 32k-token example in an otherwise short dataset sets the peak) and whether the optimiser was accidentally created over all parameters.	chapter ch::20
[Ch 21] Explain greedy, beam search, temperature, top-k and top-p. Which would you use for JSON extraction?	Greedy takes the most likely token each step; beam search keeps the B best partial sequences and, with length normalisation, returns the most probable whole output, which suits translation but makes chat bland and repetitive. Temperature divides the logits by T before the softmax (T < 1 sharpens), top-k keeps the k best tokens, top-p keeps the smallest set whose probability reaches p, and min-p cuts tokens below a fraction of the best one. On the chapter's logits, T = 0.5 lifts the top token from 0.54 to 0.71. For JSON extraction use temperature 0 with constrained decoding against the schema, then validate the parsed object, because a valid shape can still hold a wrong value. Follow-up: "Is temperature 0 deterministic?" Not quite: batch composition changes the order of floating-point reductions, so served outputs can differ run to run.	chapter ch::21
[Ch 21] What is the KV cache, and why does it grow with context length and batch size?	Every attention layer stores the key and value vectors of every past token so they are not recomputed at each step; its size is 2 × layers × KV heads × head dim × tokens × sequences × bytes per number. Each new token adds one entry per layer, and each concurrent sequence has its own cache, so it is linear in both. For a Llama-3-8B shape (32 layers, 8 KV heads, head dim 128, fp16) one 8k-token sequence needs 1.07 GB, and 32 of them need 34 GB, more than the 16 GB of weights. The trade-off is memory for compute: without the cache, generation would recompute attention over the whole prefix at every step. Follow-up: "How do you shrink it?" Fewer KV heads (GQA, MQA), a compressed latent (MLA), an 8-bit cache, sliding-window layers, and prefix sharing.	chapter ch::21
[Ch 21] What do TTFT and TPOT measure, and which phase drives each?	Time to first token is set by the prefill, which processes the whole prompt in parallel and is compute-bound: about 2 × parameters × prompt tokens FLOPs, so an 8k prompt on an 8B model is 1.3e14 FLOPs, about 0.3 s at an achieved 500 TFLOP/s. Time per output token is set by decode, which is memory-bound: each step reads all weights and the cache, so at batch 1 an 8B fp16 model on a 3.35 TB/s GPU cannot go below about 4.8 ms per token. Streaming makes perceived latency roughly TTFT. Follow-up: "Why does a long prompt from one user slow everyone else?" Its prefill occupies the GPU between other users' decode steps; chunked prefill or separate prefill and decode pools fix that.	chapter ch::21
[Ch 21] What is quantisation, and why are outliers a problem?	Quantisation stores weights (and possibly activations) as low-bit integers or floats with a scale per block, so the model takes 2–4× less memory and memory-bound decode gets faster. The rounding error grows with the scale, which is set by the largest absolute value in the block (mean squared error about s²/12), so one outlier stretches the grid for every other weight: in the chapter's test one value of 40 raised the int8 error from 1.0% to 9.9%, while per-row scales kept it at 0.7%. The fixes shrink the block (group-wise scales at about 4.125 bits per weight), split outliers into 16-bit (LLM.int8()), rescale channels (AWQ, SmoothQuant) or compensate errors (GPTQ). Follow-up: "Are activations or weights harder?" Activations: they have large outlier channels that vary by input, which is why W8A8 needs SmoothQuant or fp8.	chapter ch::21
[Ch 21] How much GPU memory do you need to serve an 8B model to 32 concurrent users with 8k-token contexts? Walk through it.	Weights are 16 GB in fp16 (about 4 GB at 4-bit). The KV cache is about 1.07 GB per 8k sequence with GQA, times 32 = 34 GB. On top come activations and workspace for the prefill chunk (one to two GB), the CUDA context and captured CUDA graphs (about a gigabyte), and allocator fragmentation and the engine's headroom, so about 55 GB in total: one 80 GB GPU in fp16. The trade-offs show at longer contexts: at 32k tokens the cache is 4.3 GB per user and 137 GB for 32, which no single GPU holds; an fp8 cache halves it to 69 GB, still too much with the weights, so you either shard over two GPUs or serve about 13 users at 32k on one (60 GB left for cache ÷ 4.3 GB). Follow-up: "Why does vLLM grab 90% of memory at start-up?" It pre-allocates the cache pool and pages it out in blocks, so you size for the tokens actually in flight, not for the maximum length of every request.	chapter ch::21
[Ch 21] Explain continuous batching and PagedAttention. Why do they raise throughput?	Static batching waits for the longest sequence in a batch: with seven answers of 100 tokens and one of 500, the GPU's slots are only 30% used. Continuous batching admits a new request at the next decode step whenever a slot frees up, so the batch stays full. PagedAttention stores the cache in fixed-size blocks allocated as sequences grow, like virtual-memory pages, instead of reserving the maximum length per request; waste drops to less than one block per sequence, more sequences fit, and shared prefixes can point to the same blocks. Since decode throughput grows almost linearly with batch until memory runs out, packing memory tighter is throughput. Follow-up: "What happens when memory runs out mid-generation?" The engine preempts a request and either swaps its blocks to CPU or recomputes them later.	chapter ch::21
[Ch 21] How does speculative decoding keep the big model's output distribution? When does it not help?	A draft model proposes k tokens; the target scores them all in one pass, accepts each draft token x with probability min(1, p(x)/q(x)), and at the first rejection samples from the residual norm(max(0, p − q)); if all are accepted it samples a bonus token. Drafting and accepting gives min(p, q), rejecting and resampling gives max(0, p − q), and the two sum to p, so the output distribution is exactly the target's. The wall-clock speedup is (1 − α^(k+1)) / ((1 − α)(1 + kc)) with c the draft's relative cost: α = 0.8, k = 4, c = 0.05 gives 2.8×. It does not help when acceptance is low (mismatched draft, high temperature, creative text) or when large batches already make the GPU compute-bound, since verification then costs up to k + 1 times the arithmetic. Follow-up: "How do you choose k?" The optimum falls as c rises or α falls; at α = 0.8 it is about 8 for c = 0.05 and 4 for c = 0.2, and engines tune it empirically.	chapter ch::21
[Ch 21] A support chatbot has p95 latency of 9 seconds. What would you measure and change?	Decompose one slow request into queueing + retrieval and tool calls + TTFT + output tokens × TPOT and measure each at p95, not on average. A typical breakdown: 0.5 s queue, 0.4 s retrieval, 1.1 s TTFT for a 6k-token prompt, and 350 output tokens × 20 ms = 7.0 s, for 9.0 s in total, so here the answer length dominates: cap or shorten the output (a tighter format), stream so perceived latency is about 2 s, cache the stable prefix to cut TTFT, and consider a smaller model or speculative decoding to cut TPOT. Check whether the p95 is driven by the long tail of output lengths, by long prompts stalling other users' decodes (fix with chunked prefill or disaggregation), or by queueing: in a simple queue the wait is ρ/(1 − ρ) service times, so going from 80% to 90% utilisation more than doubles it (4 → 9). Follow-up: "Streaming is on and users still complain." Measure time to the first useful content, since a long preamble or a slow tool call before the answer is invisible in TTFT.	chapter ch::21
[Ch 21] Self-host or API for tagging 50M items a month? Show the break-even reasoning.	Start from volume: 50M items × about 600 input tokens = 30B tokens a month, with only a few output tokens per label. On a batch API at an illustrative $1.50 per million input tokens (half of a $3 list price) that is $45,000 a month; with a small API model at $0.075 per million it is $2,250. Self-hosting an 8B model is prefill-dominated: at an achieved 400 TFLOP/s and 1.6e10 FLOPs per token one GPU does about 25,000 tokens per second, so 30B tokens need 333 GPU-hours, 833 at 40% utilisation, about $2,100 a month at $2.50 an hour, plus engineering and on-call, say $15,000 a month. Against the $1.50 API the break-even is 15,000 / (1.50 − 0.07) ≈ 10B tokens a month, so at 30B self-hosting wins if an 8B model meets the quality bar on your eval set; against the small API model it never breaks even. Do not self-host when quality needs a frontier model, volume is spiky, or no team can run it. Follow-up: "What would change the answer?" API prefix caching, a distilled encoder classifier (cheaper than both) and data-residency rules.	chapter ch::21
[Ch 21] Prove that speculative decoding is lossless.	Let p be the target distribution and q the draft's at one position, with x drafted from q, accepted with probability min(1, p(x)/q(x)), and on rejection replaced by a sample from r = max(0, p − q) / Σ max(0, p − q). Then P(x) = q(x)·min(1, p(x)/q(x)) + P(reject)·r(x). The first term is min(p(x), q(x)); the rejection probability is 1 − Σ min(p, q) = Σ max(0, p − q), which cancels r's denominator, so the second term is max(0, p(x) − q(x)); and min(p, q) + max(0, p − q) = p. A check with p = (0.6, 0.3, 0.1) and q = (0.3, 0.5, 0.2) gives acceptance α = 0.7 and a residual of (1, 0, 0), and the totals come back to (0.6, 0.3, 0.1). The trade-off is that the draft affects only speed, through α = 1 − TV(p, q). Follow-up: "What about several tokens?" Apply the rule left to right, stop at the first rejection, and induction over positions gives exactness for the whole sequence.	chapter ch::21
[Ch 21] Compare GPTQ, AWQ and SmoothQuant. When would you choose W4A16 over FP8?	GPTQ quantises one weight at a time and updates the remaining weights of the row to cancel the output error, using the inverse of H = 2XXᵀ from calibration inputs; AWQ rescales input channels with an exact identity (W·diag(s))(diag(s)⁻¹X) so channels with large activations get relatively finer rounding; SmoothQuant uses the same identity the other way, with s = √(max|X|/max|W|), to move activation outliers into the weights so both fit int8. W4A16 cuts weight bytes about 4×, which speeds memory-bound decode at small batch (an 8B step falls from 4.8 ms to about 1.2 ms), but the matmul still runs in bf16 after dequantisation, so prefill and large batches gain nothing and can slow down. FP8 W8A8 halves the bytes and doubles tensor-core throughput, so it helps compute-bound serving with little tuning. Choose W4A16 for latency-sensitive, small-batch or memory-limited serving, and FP8 for high-throughput serving on GPUs that support it. Follow-up: "What do you check after quantising?" Long-context, maths and non-English evals, where errors accumulate first.	chapter ch::21
[Ch 21] Why is LLM decode memory-bound, and why does batching not fix it for long contexts?	A GPU needs about 150–300 FLOPs per byte read to be compute-bound. Decoding does about 2 FLOPs per parameter per sequence while reading each 2-byte weight once, so the weight part has an intensity of about b, the batch size: batching helps it. But each sequence reads its own KV cache, so the attention part has an intensity of n_heads / n_kv FLOPs per byte regardless of batch: 1 for multi-head attention and 4 for an 8B model with 32 query and 8 KV heads. In the chapter's curve, at 238 users of 2k tokens the caches are 64 GB against 16 GB of weights, and reading them takes 19 of the 23.8 ms step. The levers are therefore on the cache: GQA and MLA, an 8-bit cache, and bounded attention windows. Follow-up: "And prefill?" Thousands of tokens share each weight read, so prefill is compute-bound, which is why disaggregation runs the two phases on separate pools.	chapter ch::21
[Ch 21] Structured outputs made your extraction pipeline reliable to parse, but accuracy dropped on reasoning-heavy fields. Why, and what do you do?	Constrained decoding masks invalid tokens and renormalises step by step, which is not the same as conditioning the model on valid outputs: if the first token has probability 0.6 but only 10% of its continuations are valid, against 0.4 and 90% for the alternative, the correct conditional puts 0.14 on the first branch while masking commits to it 60% of the time. Strict formats can also force token boundaries the model never saw and leave no room to reason. Fix it by putting a free-text reasoning field before the constrained fields, or by a two-pass design (answer freely, then extract with a cheap constrained call), and compare the variants on a labelled set. The trade-off is extra output tokens. Follow-up: "Why not just parse free text with regex?" It breaks at scale; a second constrained pass is cheaper than handling parse failures.	chapter ch::21
[Ch 21] Why not decode a reasoning model greedily, and what settings would you use?	Over thousands of thinking tokens a deterministic decoder tends to fall into loops, repeating a step until it hits the length limit, and a truncated trace scores as wrong. Vendors of open reasoning models recommend sampling instead, for example a temperature around 0.6 with top-p 0.95 for DeepSeek-R1-style and Qwen3 thinking models, and some APIs fix the temperature and expose a reasoning-effort setting instead. Because sampled accuracy is a random variable, evaluate with several samples per question: on 30 competition problems, one sample per problem gives a standard error of about 9 points at 60% accuracy, so averages over 8–16 samples are standard (Chapter 23). Follow-up: "How do you control cost?" Effort or budget settings, routing easy requests to a non-thinking mode, and a length limit chosen from the measured accuracy-versus-tokens curve.	chapter ch::21
[Ch 22] What makes a good prompt for a classification task?	Precise label definitions with examples, boundary cases, an "other" or "unsure" option, a strict output format (enum in a schema), and context separated clearly from instructions. The prompt is a specification for a literal reader: most errors trace to a missing definition or an unclear boundary between two labels, which a one-line "X, not Y, when…" sentence fixes. The trade-off is length: every definition is paid for on every call unless it sits in a cached prefix. Follow-up: "How do you know the prompt is good?" Only from a labelled evaluation set, with errors read and grouped, not from a few spot checks.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] Zero-shot vs few-shot: when do you add examples?	When instructions alone leave format or boundary cases ambiguous. Use examples that cover hard cases with balanced labels, and consider retrieving similar examples per input. Examples also carry biases: models lean towards the majority label among the examples and towards the labels of the last few, so shuffle and balance them. Contextual calibration measures the lean with a content-free input: if "N/A" scores (0.7, 0.3), an input scored (0.6, 0.4) recalibrates to (0.39, 0.61) and flips. Follow-up: "And for reasoning models?" Use examples for the output format only; worked chain-of-thought examples tend to hurt them.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] What is chain-of-thought prompting and when is it worth it?	Asking the model to reason before answering, which gives it more computation per answer and lets later steps build on earlier ones. Worth it for multi-step reasoning tasks; wasteful for simple extraction or classification. Reasoning models do it internally, controlled by an effort or token budget, and the budget is the cost: 5,000 thinking tokens at $15 per million output tokens is $0.075 a call, 33 times a 150-token answer. Follow-up: "Can you trust the reasoning as an explanation?" No: faithfulness studies show stated reasoning does not always reflect what drove the answer.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] How do you get reliable JSON out of an LLM?	Structured-output or tool-calling modes with a JSON schema (constrained decoding), Pydantic validation, one retry with the error message, and a fallback path. Nullable fields where data may be missing, so the model does not invent values, and a reasoning field before the answer fields when the task needs thought. The schema guarantees shape, not truth: an enum can hide a guess. Follow-up: "Why can strict formats lower accuracy?" Masking tokens step by step is not the same as conditioning on valid outputs, and it leaves no room to reason (Chapter 21).	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] How do you version and test prompts?	Version template, model and parameters together, since a model change with the same prompt is a new version; keep a labelled evaluation set; run it on every change in CI; accept changes only without regressions on key slices; log versions with outputs. Size the set for the changes you care about: with versions disagreeing on 10% of items, a 5-point paired gain needs about 310 items, so a 100-item set cannot confirm it. Follow-up: "What about automatic prompt optimisers?" They select among many candidates on the dev set and over-fit it, so keep a test set they never see.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] What is "lost in the middle" and what do you do about it?	Models use information at the start and end of long contexts better than in the middle (Liu et al., 2023), and quality on multi-fact tasks falls with length well before the window limit. Retrieve fewer, better passages; put the most relevant first or last; place the question after the context; test at realistic lengths, not only with needle-in-a-haystack retrieval. Newer models show a smaller effect, so measure it for yours. Follow-up: "How would you measure it?" Place the answer document at each position among 20–50 distractors and plot accuracy by position and context length.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] When would you choose long context over RAG?	Long context for a small, stable corpus that is needed as a whole and queried rarely; RAG for large or changing corpora, high volume and per-document permissions; often a combination. Put numbers on it: 200,000 tokens of context at $3 per million is $0.60 a query plus seconds of prefill, against $0.015 for a 5,000-token RAG prompt, 40 times less. Prompt caching narrows the gap (a cached 200k prefix read at a tenth of the price is about $0.06) when many queries share the same documents, but quality still degrades with length and access control is harder when every user's prompt holds every document. Follow-up: "What would make you switch to long context?" Tasks that need reasoning across the whole corpus, such as comparing every clause of a contract, where retrieval misses the connections.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] How does prompt caching work and how do you design for it?	The provider stores the processed state (the KV cache) of a prompt prefix and reuses it when the next call starts with byte-identical tokens, billing those tokens at a discount and skipping their prefill. Put stable content first, keep it byte-identical (no timestamps or user names, fixed example order, unchanged tool definitions), and put per-request data last. In the chapter's example, caching 90% of a 3,000-token prefix cuts a $38,250 monthly bill to $16,380. Follow-up: "Why does it matter more for agents?" Each turn resends the whole history, so an append-only context makes nearly every input token a cache hit.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] What is indirect prompt injection?	Instructions hidden in content the system processes (documents, emails, web pages, tool output) that the model may follow, because it reads instructions and data in one token stream and cannot reliably tell them apart. It is more dangerous than direct injection for RAG and agents with tools, because the attacker never talks to the system and the victim is the user. The serious version is data exfiltration: an injected instruction makes the agent send private data out, for example through a rendered markdown image URL. Follow-up: "Can a better prompt fix it?" No; "ignore instructions in documents" lowers the success rate but is not a defence.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] Design defences for an assistant that reads customer emails and can issue refunds.	Start from the threat model: emails are untrusted and attacker-written, and the assistant has a consequential tool. The model drafts; refunds go through a policy engine with limits in code; refunds above a threshold need human approval; email content is treated as untrusted data (a quarantined reader model that extracts structured fields such as order id and claimed issue, with no tools); the privileged component sees only those fields; tools are scoped to the customer whose email it is; there is no outbound channel (no rendered links or images, no free-form email sending); an injection red-team set runs in CI, with attack success rate reported with an interval; refund rates are monitored. The trust boundary is the email body, and the residual risk is an attacker getting a within-limit refund they are entitled to anyway, bounded by the policy engine, not by the prompt. Follow-up: "Why not just an injection classifier?" Adaptive attackers find phrasings it misses; it is one layer, not the boundary.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] Your classification prompt scores 88% on the eval set. How do you improve it systematically?	Error analysis: read and categorise the failures (ambiguous labels, missing context, format errors, genuinely hard cases); fix the largest category first (often definitions or examples); re-run; check slices for regressions. Check the noise first: on 500 items at 88% the standard error is about 1.5 points, so gains under 3–4 points need a paired comparison to be believed. Consider dynamic few-shot, a stronger model only for low-confidence cases, or fine-tuning if the error pattern is consistent. Follow-up: "When do you stop prompting and fine-tune?" When errors are consistent and numerous enough to label, and the volume makes a smaller tuned model cheaper.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] A new model version is released. How do you decide whether to switch?	Run the full evaluation set with the current prompt and with light prompt adjustments, because prompts do not transfer perfectly and the new model may need re-tuning; compare quality per slice, latency and cost per successful task rather than per token (a model that is cheaper per token but needs retries or longer reasoning can cost more). Check behaviour changes beyond accuracy: refusals, formatting, verbosity, structured-output reliability and safety tests. Shadow-test on production traffic, switch behind a flag with a rollback plan, and monitor. Also check the provider's deprecation timeline for the old model, which sets your deadline whether or not you want to switch. Follow-up: "The new model is 2 points better overall but worse on one slice?" Decide by the slice's business weight and its interval; a small slice may simply be noisy.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] How would you cut the cost of an LLM feature by 5× without losing quality?	Measure where tokens go first; then shorten and cache the stable prefix; trim retrieved context; constrain and shorten the output, which is usually the priciest token type; route easy cases to a smaller model (cascade); batch offline work at about half price; cache repeated requests; distil high-volume tasks into a small model. In the chapter's example, caching alone cuts the bill from $38,250 to $16,380, and what remains is mostly output. Verify each step on the evaluation set, and report which lever gave how much. Follow-up: "What is the risk of a cascade?" The confidence signal: verbalised confidence is poorly calibrated, so route on label probabilities or sample agreement, checked with a reliability plot.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] Explain the "lethal trifecta" and how you would design an agent that browses the web on a user's behalf.	An agent is exploitable for data theft when it combines access to private data, exposure to untrusted content and a way to communicate externally; with all three, an instruction in a web page can make it send the user's data out, and only the model's judgement stands in the way. The design removes a leg: a browsing session runs without access to the user's private data and with egress limited to the browsing itself, or a CaMeL-style design has a privileged model plan from the trusted request, a quarantined model parse pages, and a policy check that private values never flow to destinations the user did not name. Close the accidental channels: no rendered images from arbitrary domains, no link unfurling, no free-form outbound tools. Measure attack success rate on a benchmark such as AgentDojo together with utility, since a defence that blocks every task is useless. Follow-up: "What does CaMeL give up?" Tasks whose control flow depends on untrusted content cannot be expressed, so some utility is lost.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] A coding agent's quality drops after about an hour of work, and its bill is high. How do you engineer its context?	First measure: success rate, instruction adherence and repeated actions against context length, plus the cache hit rate. A high bill with a low hit rate means the context is not append-only (a timestamp in the system prompt, reordered tool definitions), and fixing that alone can cut input cost about sevenfold: a 40-turn run that re-reads 1.96M tokens costs $5.88 at $3 per million, or $0.83 with cache reads at a tenth of the price. For quality, clear old tool outputs in batches, compact the history at a threshold with a summary that keeps file paths, error strings and failed approaches, move exploration into sub-agents that return short summaries, and keep a progress-notes file that survives compaction. Each technique loses information, so ablate them on a fixed task set. Follow-up: "Why not just use a bigger context window?" Quality degrades with length well before the limit, and cost grows with every turn.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] Why might adding two few-shot examples flip a model's predictions, and how do you make the prompt robust?	Few-shot predictions carry majority-label, recency and common-token biases: the model leans towards labels that are frequent among the examples, that appear last, or that are common words, so adding or reordering examples shifts every prediction in the same direction. Make it robust by balancing and shuffling examples, using a contrasting example alongside retrieved neighbours, evaluating over several orders and reporting the spread, and, where you have label probabilities, applying contextual calibration: divide by the probabilities the prompt gives a content-free input and renormalise. Follow-up: "Do larger models still need this?" Less, but they follow the example labels more closely, flipped labels included, so wrong example labels hurt them more.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 22] How do you prompt a reasoning model differently from a chat model?	State the goal, constraints and success criteria, and let the model plan; do not prescribe step lists or include chain-of-thought examples, which constrain its search and invite copying; use examples only for the output format; give all needed context up front. Then choose the reasoning budget from a measured accuracy-versus-effort curve on your eval set, because thinking tokens are billed as output: at $15 per million, 5,000 thinking tokens on a million calls a month is $75,000, and at 100 tokens per second it adds 50 seconds of latency. Route easy traffic to a non-thinking mode. Follow-up: "Should you show users the reasoning?" Treat it as a debugging aid, not an explanation, since it may not be faithful.	chapter ch::22
[Ch 23] Model B beats A by 1.5 points on a 1,000-question benchmark. Is B better?	Not yet: compute the paired difference and its standard error. If the models disagree on 15% of questions, the paired SE is √((0.15 − 0.015²)/1,000) = 1.2 points, so the 95% interval is about 1.5 ± 2.4, which includes zero; unpaired, the SE would be about 2 points. Then check clustering (passages or repositories inflate the SE by √(1 + (m − 1)ρ)), prompt-format and seed variance, which can be as large as the gap, and contamination. What would convince me: the gap surviving more items or a second benchmark of the same skill, a consistent direction in held-out loss, and, for a training change, a second seed. If this is one row of a 10-benchmark table, expect one or two "significant" rows by chance. Follow-up: "How many questions would you need?" About 7.84 × 0.15 / 0.015² ≈ 5,200 for 80% power.	chapter ch::23
[Ch 23] Why cluster standard errors, and how?	Questions that share a passage, repository or template are correlated, so the naive SE understates uncertainty. Sum the residuals within each cluster, square the sums, add them over clusters, take the square root and divide by n; the inflation is the design effect 1 + (m − 1)ρ for clusters of size m and intraclass correlation ρ. With passages of 5 and ρ = 0.1 the variance grows 1.4×; with clusters of 20, 2.9×, so 1,000 questions behave like 345. The trade-off: with few clusters (under about 30) the clustered SE is itself noisy, and a cluster bootstrap is safer. Follow-up: "Where does the design effect come from?" A cluster sum of m scores has variance mσ² plus m(m − 1)ρσ² of covariance terms.	chapter ch::23
[Ch 23] How many questions do you need to detect a 2-point improvement?	For a paired comparison with 80% power at the 5% level, n = (1.96 + 0.84)² × d / δ² = 7.84 × d / 0.02², where d is the discordant rate: about 2,000 questions at d = 10% and 3,900 at 20%. The 1.96 is the two-sided critical value; the 0.84 places the threshold 0.84 SEs below the true effect so that 80% of estimates clear it. Multiply by the design effect if questions are clustered. Follow-up: "And unpaired?" Replace d by 2p(1 − p), about 0.42 at 70% accuracy, so roughly four times more questions than a pair with 10% disagreement.	chapter ch::23
[Ch 23] Why use bits per byte?	Loss per token depends on the tokenizer: a tokenizer with longer tokens has fewer, harder predictions per text. Bits per byte divides the total loss in nats by the number of bytes and by ln 2, which normalises by text length and allows comparisons across tokenizers. In the chapter's example, a model at 2.45 nats per token beats one at 2.10, because its tokenizer needs 18% fewer tokens: 0.707 against 0.739 bits per byte. Follow-up: "Is it fully tokenizer-independent?" Close, but byte counts depend on the encoding, so compare on the same raw text.	chapter ch::23
[Ch 23] How would you detect contamination in a closed model?	Perturbation gaps with significance tests (rephrase items or change the numbers, and test the paired drop with McNemar: 51 items lost against 20 gained gives p = 0.0003 in the chapter's example), guided completion (does it complete a test item verbatim?), likelihood-based tests such as Min-K% Prob, and time-split evaluations on items published after the cut-off, which are the strongest evidence. The trade-off: perturbed items can be genuinely harder, so compare with a model known to be clean. Follow-up: "Is a Min-K% signal proof?" No; it is a screening tool with false positives.	chapter ch::23
[Ch 23] What is IRT and how is it used in LLM evaluation?	Item response theory models P(correct) = σ(a_i(θ_m − b_i)) with item difficulty b, discrimination a and model ability θ, fitted by maximum likelihood on a models × items matrix. An item's Fisher information is a²p(1 − p), maximal at a²/4 where the model's ability equals its difficulty, so an a = 2 item is worth sixteen a = 0.5 items there; choosing high-information items spread across abilities builds small subsets (in the check script, 100 informative items correlate 0.979 with the full score against 0.968 for random ones). Negative discrimination flags likely label errors, since strong models are marked wrong. Follow-up: "What is the risk of a tiny subset?" It is tuned to today's models; a new model outside their ability range is measured poorly.	chapter ch::23
[Ch 23] What are the known problems with arena leaderboards?	Style and length biases (voters prefer longer, formatted answers), selection effects from testing private variants and publishing the best (ten equal variants inflate the published rating by about 1.54σ, 21 points in the chapter's example), unequal sampling between providers, a prompt distribution unlike your users', and overlapping intervals. A 20-point gap means a 53% head-to-head win rate. Follow-up: "How would you fix selection?" Pre-register the variant to be rated, or count every tested variant.	chapter ch::23
[Ch 23] How do you make an LLM judge trustworthy?	Run both orders and score each prompt as the mean of its two verdicts, control for length, use a different model family or several judges, provide references for hard tasks, and validate against human labels per category with intervals. In the chapter's simulation a 10-point position bias reports 45% or 65% depending on order and averaging recovers 55%; dropping inconsistent pairs instead reports 60%, a different quantity. Follow-up: "What does a high inconsistency rate tell you?" That much of the verdict is position, not content: make the rubric more specific or use a stronger judge.	chapter ch::23
[Ch 23] Why might a benchmark lack construct validity?	Items solvable by shortcuts, answer-format artefacts, label errors, or measuring a narrow skill under a broad name: a "long-context" task answerable from the last page, a "reasoning" set solvable by pattern matching. Read 20 items, and test blind or perturbed baselines: a model given only the answer options, or the question without its passage, should score near chance. Follow-up: "Give an example of a hidden shortcut." Multiple-choice sets where the longest or most specific option is usually right.	chapter ch::23
[Ch 23] What is METR's time-horizon metric?	The length of tasks, in human expert time, that a model completes with 50% success, estimated by fitting a logistic curve of success against log task length. It tracks agentic capability over time in interpretable units, and in METR's original analysis it doubled roughly every 7 months from 2019 to 2025, faster from 2024. The 80%-reliability horizon is several times shorter, which matters for deployment. Follow-up: "What are its weaknesses?" The task suite (mostly software and research tasks), how human times are measured, and the extrapolation of a trend.	chapter ch::23
[Ch 23] Derive the standard error of a paired difference between two models.	Let D_i = B_i − A_i ∈ {−1, 0, 1}. D_i² = 1 exactly when the models disagree, so E[D²] = d, the discordant rate, and with E[D] = δ, Var(D) = d − δ² and SE = √((d − δ²)/n). It equals Var(A) + Var(B) − 2Cov(A, B): pairing removes the covariance that shared question difficulty creates. Example: d = 0.12, δ = 0.018, n = 1,000 gives 1.09 points, about half the unpaired value at 70% accuracy. Follow-up: "What test goes with it?" McNemar's test on the discordant counts, which is the exact version of the same idea.	chapter ch::23
[Ch 23] A reasoning model is evaluated at temperature 0.6 on a 30-problem benchmark. How would you report it?	Separate the two sources of variance: Var(s_i) = Var(p_i) + E[p_i(1 − p_i)]/K for K samples per problem. Between-question variance is fixed by the 30 problems; sampling variance shrinks with K. At 60% accuracy with Var(p_i) = 0.16, one sample gives a SE of 8.9 points, sixteen give 7.4, and the floor is 7.3, so report the mean over K = 8–16 samples per problem with an interval, and the number of samples. For comparing two similar models, sampling noise dominates the paired variance, and sixteen samples can shrink the paired SE about threefold. Follow-up: "Why not use greedy decoding to remove the noise?" Greedy decoding loops on reasoning models and is not how they are used; the sampled behaviour is the construct.	chapter ch::23
[Ch 23] Which evaluations would you use for a 1B-parameter data ablation, and why?	Ones with signal at that scale: per-domain bits per byte on held-out text, and knowledge and commonsense tasks in the cloze formulation (length-normalised likelihood of each answer), not multiple-choice letters, which sit at chance for small models. Before the ablations, run a reference model and keep only tasks that are above chance, rise monotonically over checkpoints and have a signal-to-noise ratio above about 1 (spread between recipes over checkpoint-to-checkpoint noise); in the chapter's example the letter format has an SNR of 0.6, cloze 3.0 and held-out loss 8.0. Average the last few checkpoints, and confirm the winner at a second seed or scale. Follow-up: "Why not MATH or HumanEval?" They are at the floor at 1B, so their differences are noise; accuracy jumps there are largely a metric artefact.	chapter ch::23
[Ch 23] Write down the Bradley–Terry model and explain how you fit arena ratings with intervals.	P(i beats j) = σ(s_i − s_j). Each battle becomes a logistic-regression row with +1 for i and −1 for j and no intercept; fix one model's strength or centre them, add a small L2 penalty, and convert to the Elo scale with R = 400 s / ln 10, so P = 1 / (1 + 10^(−ΔR/400)) and a 100-point gap is a 64% win rate. Count ties as half a win each or use a tie parameter. Bootstrap battles for intervals; with 3,000 battles among five models, neighbouring intervals overlap. Follow-up: "What does it assume?" A single strength per model, the same for every prompt and voter, so mixed prompt types or style preferences break it; style-controlled variants add covariates.	chapter ch::23
[Ch 24] Why are public benchmarks not enough to choose a model for your product?	They test generic tasks, may be contaminated, and ignore your inputs, criteria and failure costs. Use them to shortlist, then evaluate candidates on your own set. The number to quote: a 300-case set of your own at 85% pass has a standard error of √(0.85 × 0.15/300) ≈ 2 points, which is enough to separate candidates that differ by 6 points or more, and that difference on your task is worth more than a 1-point gap on a public leaderboard. The follow-up is usually "which public benchmarks would you still look at?": the few closest to your task (τ-bench for tool use, SWE-bench Verified for coding), as a filter, with contamination in mind.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] How do you start evaluating a new LLM feature?	Error analysis: collect 50–100 realistic traces, read and annotate failures (open coding), group them into failure modes (axial coding), count them, then build code checks or calibrated judges for the most frequent modes. Stop reading when about 20 consecutive traces add no new category. Expect criteria to change as you read, so write the rubric after the first pass, not before. The trade-off: random traces find common failures, but a mode that affects 2% of traffic is missed in 100 traces 13% of the time, so search the logs for costly rare cases too.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] What is LLM-as-judge, and its main formats?	Using an LLM to grade outputs against criteria: pointwise (one output), pairwise (which of two), reference-guided (against a known answer). Best as binary verdicts per criterion with a critique written before the verdict. Pointwise gives absolute pass rates for monitoring; pairwise is more sensitive for comparing two versions but has position bias and no absolute level. The follow-up: "why not a 1–10 scale?" Because neighbouring scores are not used consistently, scores drift with the judge model, and a binary verdict maps directly to a TPR and TNR you can calibrate.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] Exact match vs token F1?	Exact match requires the normalised strings to be identical; token F1 gives partial credit for word overlap. Both suit short answers with a reference; neither suits open-ended generation. Worked example: "within 24 hours of delivery" against the reference "24 hours" has precision 2/5 and recall 1, so F1 = 0.57 while exact match is 0. Token F1 therefore penalises correct but verbose answers, and neither metric notices a fluent answer with one wrong number in it.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] What biases do LLM judges have and how do you handle them?	Position, verbosity, self-preference, and slice-dependent leniency. Swap orders in pairwise judging (average the two orders, or equivalently score inconsistent pairs as ties; dropping them inflates the win rate), penalise padding or control for length, use a different model family or a panel, and calibrate against human labels per slice. The number: Chapter 23.8's simulation shows a 10-point first-position bias turning a true 55% win rate into 45% or 65% depending on order. Follow-up: "does averaging both orders fix it?" For a constant bias, yes; for a bias that depends on the content, only calibration against humans tells you.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] How do you validate an LLM judge?	Expert labels on 100–200 outputs for the same criterion; measure kappa and the judge's TPR and TNR; compare with human-human agreement; iterate on the judge prompt; keep a held-out set; re-check after judge or model changes. Oversample failures, because TNR is estimated only from them: with 50 failures, an observed TNR of 0.66 has a 95% interval of 0.52–0.78. The trade-off is expert time against precision. The follow-up: "what TNR is good enough?" Whatever makes the corrected pass rate's interval narrow enough for the decision, typically above 0.85 for a launch gate.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] Your judge agrees with humans 92% of the time. Is it good?	Not necessarily: if 90% of outputs pass, a judge that always says pass agrees 90% and has a kappa of 0. Check kappa and especially the TNR, the share of real failures it catches. Compute J = TPR + TNR − 1: true differences between systems shrink by J in the judge's numbers, so a judge with J = 0.6 needs about 1/0.6² ≈ 2.8 times as many cases to detect the same improvement. Follow-up: "how would you improve it?" Read the false passes, make the judge quote evidence for each claim, and re-measure on a held-out split.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] How many examples do you need in an evaluation set?	Enough for the precision you need: 100 cases gives roughly ±7 points on a pass rate near 85% (±10 near 50%); detecting small differences between systems needs paired comparisons on the same cases or many hundreds of examples. The formula is n ≈ 7.85 × [p₁(1 − p₁) + p₂(1 − p₂)]/δ² for independent samples, about 900 per arm for 80% → 85%, and n ≈ 7.85 × d/δ² paired, about 314 if the systems disagree on 10% of cases. Follow-up: "and at temperature 0.7?" Add samples per case, but past about four, more cases help more than more samples.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] How do you compare two prompts fairly?	Same cases, same judge, same seeds where possible, a paired test (McNemar on per-case pass/fail), intervals, per-slice results, plus cost and latency. McNemar uses only the discordant cases: if prompt B fixes 25 cases and breaks 9, the exact binomial test on 9 of 34 gives p ≈ 0.009. The trade-off is that a paired test on a set you also tuned on is optimistic, so confirm on the held-out slice. Follow-up: "what if each prompt is run several times per case?" Average per case first, then do a paired test or bootstrap over cases.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] Design the evaluation for a customer-support RAG assistant.	Start from error analysis on real tickets, then layer the checks. Code checks run on every output: format, cited ids exist in the context, language, no PII, refund amounts within policy caps. Calibrated judges cover faithfulness, correctness against references for the common questions, and correct abstention; retrieval is measured separately at the final context (Chapter 25). Slice by country, language and intent. Prioritise: the launch gate is wrong answers with real cost, so set thresholds from error costs, for example "wrongly answered out-of-scope questions ≤ 2% with the upper 95% bound ≤ 4%", which needs about 400 out-of-scope cases (8 of 400 gives an upper bound of 3.9%). Then production sampling with daily judging and an anchor set for judge drift, and online metrics (resolution without escalation, re-contact rate, CSAT) with cost and latency guardrails. What blocks launch: any policy-cap violation, a faithfulness gate missed on any major slice, or a judge whose TNR is below 0.85. Follow-up: "where would you spend the first week?" Reading 100 tickets and labelling the calibration set.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] Judge scores went down 5 points after a model upgrade of the judge itself. What happened?	The measuring instrument changed: the new judge may be stricter or differently biased. Run the old and new judges side by side on the human-labelled anchor set and refit TPR and TNR for both. Check that the generator did not change at the same time (a silent provider update can move both). Then correct both series with their own error rates before comparing trends: a judge whose TNR rose from 0.66 to 0.85 reports lower pass rates for the same system, and the corrected rates may agree. Pin judge versions and change them deliberately, with an overlap period. Follow-up: "which judge do you keep?" The one with the higher J on the anchor set, re-baselining historical numbers with it.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] How do you evaluate when there is no single right answer (for example writing product descriptions)?	Define concrete criteria from error analysis (factual consistency with the source catalogue data, no invented claims, length, tone, banned phrases), code checks where possible, calibrated binary judges for the rest, pairwise preference tests between versions, and human review samples. Per-item rubrics help: for each source record, list the facts a good description must contain and the claims it must not make, and score the share satisfied. The trade-off: pairwise tests detect small improvements but give no absolute level, so keep a pointwise pass rate for monitoring. Follow-up: "how do you stop the judge rewarding verbosity?" A length-controlled comparison and an explicit padding criterion.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] How would you convince a product manager that an LLM feature is ready to launch?	Show per-failure-mode pass rates with intervals on a representative set, critical slices, judge calibration evidence, cost and latency, a comparison with the current process, the monitoring plan, and the rollout plan with guardrails and rollback criteria. A strong answer prioritises: name the one or two failure modes that block launch, agree their thresholds with the PM in advance from the cost of each error, and size the sets so the interval can actually clear the threshold (about 400 cases to show a 2% rate is below 4%). Compare with the human baseline, which also makes mistakes. Then a staged rollout: 5% of traffic, judged daily, with automatic rollback if the corrected faithfulness rate falls below the gate. Follow-up: "the PM wants to launch next week with 100 cases." Say what 100 cases can show (±7 points) and offer a smaller launch scope.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] Your evaluation runs at temperature 0.7 and a new prompt shows a 2-point gain on 200 cases. Real?	Probably not yet. With one sample per case at 70% the standard error of each score is about 3.2 points, of which the within-case sampling noise is a large part. Run each case 4 times for both prompts, with shared seeds where possible, average per case, and compute a paired difference with a bootstrap over cases. That removes most of the sampling noise and, through pairing, the case difficulty. The trade-off: four samples cost four times the tokens, and past that, adding cases reduces the error more. Follow-up: "and in CI?" Gate must-pass cases on at least 3 of 4 passes and quarantine flaky ones.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] How do you put an error bar on a judge-corrected pass rate?	Bootstrap both sources: redraw the calibration counts (TP among real passes, TN among real failures) and the production sample, recompute the Rogan–Gladen estimate each time, and take percentiles. For a judge with TPR 140/150 and TNR 33/50 reporting 80% on 1,000 outputs, the corrected rate is 77.5% with an interval of 68–85%, against ±2.5 points if you ignored the judge's uncertainty. The calibration set dominates because the correction divides every error by J ≈ 0.59. The caveat a senior answer adds is transfer: the interval assumes production failures look like calibration failures. Follow-up: "how would you narrow it?" A better judge (higher J) helps more than more production samples.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 24] When would you use per-item rubrics instead of one judge prompt?	When answers are open-ended and expert-level, and "good" depends on case-specific facts: medical, legal or technical advice where each case has its own must-mention and must-not-say points. Per-item rubrics turn one hard judgement into several easy checks, which judges get right more often, and the same rubrics can serve as RL rewards. The cost is expert time per case, perhaps an hour for ten cases, and the risk is rewarding checklist-filling over a coherent answer, so keep a holistic check alongside. Follow-up: "how do you validate rubric grading?" Have experts grade a sample against the same rubrics and measure agreement per criterion.	chapter ch::24
[Ch 25] How do you evaluate a RAG system?	By component and end to end: retrieval (context recall and precision at the final context), generation (faithfulness, answer relevance, citations), correctness against references, abstention on out-of-scope questions, plus online metrics. Tag failures by the first stage that broke, because a retrieval miss and an ignored passage need different fixes. A number to anchor it: on 300 questions, a 10-point gap between context recall at the retriever (90%) and at the final context (80%) points straight at re-ranking or truncation. Follow-up: "what if you have no relevance labels?" A judge can rate retrieved passages for precision, but recall needs labels or reference answers.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 25] What is faithfulness and how is it measured?	Whether every claim in the answer is supported by the provided context. Decompose the answer into claims and verify each with an NLI model or calibrated LLM judge; report the supported share and the unsupported claims. Example: "Vouchers are 20% of the order. Refunds arrive within 3 days." against a context without the 3-day claim scores 0.5. NLI checkers struggle with long contexts and claims that combine several sentences, so check against the best supporting sentences or use a judge. Follow-up: "is faithfulness the same as correctness?" No: faithfully repeating an outdated document is still wrong.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 25] Context precision vs context recall?	Recall: did the context contain what is needed to answer? Precision: how much of the context is relevant, rewarding relevant passages ranked early. Recall failures are retrieval problems; low precision adds distractors and cost. Worked example: relevant passages at ranks 2 and 4 of 4 give an average-precision style score of (1/2 + 2/4)/2 = 0.5; moving one to rank 1 gives 0.75. The trade-off is that raising k improves recall and lowers precision. Follow-up: "which matters more?" Recall first, because nothing downstream recovers a missing passage.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 25] How do you evaluate an agent?	Task success on the environment's end state, trajectory quality (tool choice, arguments, order, forbidden actions), efficiency (steps, cost, latency), robustness to tool failures, repeated runs for reliability, and safety tests. Report pass^k for user-facing agents: a 90% agent succeeds on all 8 runs of a task only 43% of the time. Follow-up: "why the end state?" Because the agent's message can claim success that did not happen.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 25] Faithfulness is 0.95 but users say answers are wrong. How?	The retrieved documents are wrong or outdated, or the answer is faithful but incomplete, or it answers a different question. Check context recall and correctness against references, document freshness, and answer relevance. Then read 30 of the complaints and tag the first stage that failed: if most are retrieval or stale documents, the generator is behaving and the index is the problem. Follow-up: "how would you catch stale documents?" Track document age in retrieved contexts and add time-sensitive questions with dated references.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 25] What are the risks of using RAGAS-style metrics as-is?	Uncalibrated judge prompts, generic criteria not tied to your failure modes, score changes when the judge model changes, flattering synthetic test sets, and no visibility of wrong answering on out-of-scope questions. Answer relevance also scores correct terse answers low, because "€8" implies many possible questions. Use the definitions, but calibrate each judge on 100–200 of your own labels (Chapter 24.7). Follow-up: "how far off are they typically?" You do not know until you measure TPR and TNR on your data, which is the point.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 25] How do you evaluate abstention?	Include questions the documents cannot answer; report correct-when-answered, wrongly-abstained and wrongly-answered rates; choose a threshold or prompt that balances them by business cost. Size the out-of-scope set for the gate: to show wrongly-answered is under 4% with 95% confidence when the observed rate is 2% takes about 400 out-of-scope questions. Follow-up: "where do out-of-scope questions come from?" Production logs where human agents escalated, plus hand-written near-misses about topics the documents do not cover.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 25] Why check the end state instead of the agent's final message?	The message can claim success while the actions were wrong or incomplete; the state shows what actually happened (voucher amount, ticket status, side effects). Check the whole state, not only the expected fields: an agent that issued the right voucher and also upgraded the customer's tier passes a field-by-field check and fails a full diff. Follow-up: "what about harmless changes like logs?" Keep an explicit allowlist of fields that may change.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 25] How do you handle multiple valid agent trajectories in evaluation?	Score outcomes on end state; use rules for must-do and must-never steps; use soft matching (order-aware recall and precision of key calls) rather than exact sequences; add a calibrated trajectory judge. For computer-use agents, trajectory matching is nearly useless and the end-state check carries almost everything. Follow-up: "how do you validate the trajectory judge?" Label 100 trajectories by hand and measure its TPR and TNR like any judge.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 25] Design the evaluation for the agentic customer-service flow (E.10).	A sandbox with mocked order, courier and payment tools; replayed historical cases with expert decisions; simulated customers, including adversarial ones, validated against real transcripts; full state-diff checks (correct action, payout within policy, nothing else changed); trajectory rules (evidence before payout); injection tests; then shadow mode and a staged rollout. Sizes: about 300 tasks covering the main complaint types, 8 runs each for pass^k, so about 28,800 model calls per full run with 12-call trajectories (nightly), and a 2-run smoke version on every change. Launch gates set from costs: zero over-cap payouts across all runs (2,400 runs with none gives an upper bound near 0.12%), pass^4 above an agreed level such as 0.6 on the top five complaint types, attack success under injection below 2%, and at least 90% agreement with human decisions in two weeks of shadow mode. Report dollar-weighted errors, because overpaying €50 matters more than underpaying €2. Roll out to 5% of contacts with a human approval queue above €20, then widen. Follow-up: "what if the simulator is too cooperative?" Validate it against real transcripts first.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 25] How do you know whether a RAG change helped, given noisy judges?	Paired comparison on the same questions; calibrated judges with known TPR and TNR; intervals; per-stage metrics that should move in the expected direction (a retrieval change should move context recall first); reading a sample of flipped cases. The judge's error compresses differences by J = TPR + TNR − 1, so a judge with J = 0.6 shows a 5-point gain as 3 points and needs about 2.8 times as many cases. Follow-up: "the end-to-end score rose but context recall did not move. Believe it?" Read the flipped cases first: the gain is probably noise or a generation change.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 25] What would you monitor in production for a RAG assistant?	Retrieval scores and empty-retrieval rate, abstention rate, sampled faithfulness and correctness judgements, citation validity, latency and cost per answer, escalation and re-contact rates, user feedback, and drift in question topics (new topics with poor retrieval). Correct judged rates for judge error and keep an anchor set to detect judge drift. Alert on changes, not levels: a doubling of empty retrievals in a day usually means an indexing failure. Follow-up: "which one would you page someone for?" Empty-retrieval rate and citation validity, because they signal broken infrastructure rather than gradual quality change.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 25] An agent's success rate jumps from 40% to 70% after an RL run. What do you check?	Whether it learned the task or the grader. Read a sample of the newly successful trajectories for exploits: edited or skipped tests, special-cased outputs, early exits, reading the fix from git history, side effects the end-state check ignores. Re-grade the successes with a held-out verifier and a full state diff. If 4 of 100 audited successes are exploits, report the exploit rate (1.6–9.8%) and correct the headline. Then fix the grader, add the exploit as a regression test for it, and rerun. Follow-up: "how do you prevent it next time?" Red-team the grader before training, protect test files, run grading in a fresh container, and monitor the gap between the training grader and a held-out one.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 25] pass@k versus pass^k: when each, and how do you estimate them?	pass@k (at least one of k succeeds) fits settings with a verifier that picks a good run, such as code with tests; pass^k (all k succeed) fits user-facing agents where each run is a real interaction. Estimate them per task without bias with 1 − C(n − c, k)/C(n, k) and C(c, k)/C(n, k), average over tasks, and bootstrap over tasks. Heterogeneous difficulty makes pass^k fall more slowly than p^k (Jensen: E[p^k] ≥ (E p)^k), as in the Beta(3, 1) example where pass^8 is 0.27 instead of 0.10. Follow-up: "why not just compute (c/n)^k?" It is biased.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 25] Agent B is 7 points more accurate than agent A and eight times the price. Which do you ship?	Put both on an accuracy-versus-cost plot with a simple baseline. If a verifier exists, agent A with up to three retries can reach 95% at $0.068 per task against B's 81.8% at $0.40, so B is off the Pareto frontier. If actions are irreversible and there is no verifier, retries are not available and the comparison is B's accuracy gain against its cost per task and the cost of each failure: at $10 of harm per failure, 6.8 points of accuracy are worth $0.68 per task, more than B's extra $0.35. Follow-up: "what else would you report?" Latency and pass^k, not just mean accuracy.	chapter ch::25
[Ch 26] Explain the residual stream view of transformers.	Each component reads linearly from a shared vector and adds its output back; logits are a sum of contributions; components communicate through the stream, which allows decomposition into paths and circuits. Formally, x_{l+1} = x_l + Σ_h head_{l,h} + MLP_l, so x_L = x₀ plus the sum of every component's output, and the logits W_U·LN(x_L) are approximately a sum of per-component direct effects. The width d is far smaller than the total output of all components, so components use subspaces, and superposition (26.7) packs more into them. Follow-up: "what breaks the linear decomposition?" The final layer norm's scale and the nonlinearities inside MLPs and attention; direct logit attribution freezes the scale.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] What is an induction head and how does it work?	A layer-1 head that attends from token A to the position after a previous occurrence of A and copies that token, implementing [A][B] … [A] → [B]. It relies on a layer-0 previous-token head via K-composition: the earlier head writes "my previous token was A" into B's position. The query carries the current token (A), the key carries the previous-token information the earlier head wrote, and the value carries the attended token's identity (B); the OV circuit is a copying circuit with a large positive diagonal in W_U W_OV W_E. In the toy example, a score of 5 for the matching key gives attention 0.98. Induction heads form abruptly early in training, together with a jump in in-context learning, Olsson et al.'s phase change. Follow-up: "how would you find them in GPT-2 small?" Repeated random tokens, a high attention score on the induction offset, and ablation raising loss on the repeat.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] How does activation patching work, and how is it different from ablation?	Patching restores one component's clean activation into a corrupted run to test whether it carries the information that restores behaviour; ablation removes a component. Patching is targeted to a specific contrast. Report the normalised effect (m_patched − m_corr)/(m_clean − m_corr) on a logit difference: in 26.6, patching both induction heads gives (0.974 − 0.030)/(0.975 − 0.030) = 0.999. Denoising (clean into corrupted) tests sufficiency; noising (corrupted into clean, like ablation) tests necessity, and redundant heads score high on the first and low on the second. Path patching restricts the patch to one edge of the circuit. Follow-up: "why logit difference rather than accuracy?" It is continuous and responds before the top token flips.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] What can go wrong with probing?	Decodable is not used; powerful probes memorise; random features already make many properties decodable. Use linear probes, control tasks, baselines and causal follow-ups. In the check script, a random network's hidden layer already gives 0.62 on a non-linear target that the raw input gives 0.49 on, so a probe score means little without that baseline. Follow-up: "what is selectivity?" Probe accuracy on the real task minus accuracy on a control task with random labels.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] What is superposition, and why does it motivate SAEs?	Models represent more sparse features than dimensions as overlapping directions, making neurons polysemantic. It needs three conditions: sparsity (features rarely co-occur, so interference rarely costs), varied importance (unimportant features are dropped, important dense ones get their own dimension), and a nonlinearity to filter interference (a linear model just does PCA). Geometry makes room for it: the number of directions with pairwise |cos| ≤ ε grows like exp(dε²/4), about 28,000 at d = 4,096 and ε = 0.1. SAEs learn an over-complete sparse dictionary to recover those directions. Follow-up: "why are neurons polysemantic then?" Because feature directions are not aligned with neurons, each neuron projects onto many of them.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] How would you evaluate a sparse autoencoder?	Sparsity (L0), reconstruction, downstream loss with the SAE spliced in, feature interpretability, and usefulness on concrete tasks compared with baselines. The headline trade-off is downstream loss against L0, compared across SAEs at equal L0; also report dead latents. In the check script, an SAE with near-perfect reconstruction recovered only 3 of 24 true features, which is why reconstruction alone is not the goal. Follow-up: "what baseline?" A linear probe or a difference-of-means direction for the same task; several 2025 studies found they match SAEs on some tasks.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] TopK versus L1 SAEs?	TopK fixes the number of active latents directly and avoids shrinkage of active latents caused by the L1 penalty; L1 needs tuning of the penalty and trades sparsity against reconstruction less directly. The shrinkage is λ/2 per active latent: minimising (a − f)² + λf gives f = a − λ/2, so with λ = 0.3 an activation of 1 is reported as 0.85. TopK's costs are a fixed k for every input (some inputs need more features) and more dead latents, handled with an auxiliary loss. Follow-up: "what does JumpReLU do?" A learned threshold per latent with an L0 penalty: no shrinkage above the threshold, and variable sparsity per input.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] What is a steering vector? Give an example.	A direction added to the residual stream to change behaviour, x ← x + αv, for example the mean difference between activations on refusing and complying prompts, which can add or remove refusals. The coefficient needs a sweep: plot the intended effect and the loss increase on unrelated text against α, and pick the range where the first is high and the second small. Directional ablation, x ← x − r̂r̂ᵀx, removes a concept everywhere and can be folded into the weights. Follow-up: "why does this matter for open-weight models?" Removing the refusal direction disables refusal training with no fine-tuning.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] Why doesn't the logit lens work on early layers, and what fixes it?	Early representations are not in the output basis; the tuned lens learns an affine map per layer, trained to minimise the KL divergence from the final layer's distribution, to decode them. The logit lens is W_U·LN_f(x_l); the tuned lens is W_U·LN_f(A_l x_l + b_l). In the check script's induction model, the lens reads chance accuracy on induction tokens before layer 1 (0.035) and 0.972 after it, locating the computation in one layer. Follow-up: "is the tuned lens's answer what the model 'thinks'?" Only approximately: the affine map is trained, so it can add information the layer does not make easily available.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] Is a reasoning model's chain of thought a faithful explanation?	Not necessarily: models can use unstated reasons, and optimisation pressure on the chain of thought can make it less informative. Useful for monitoring, not proof. The evidence is measured: biased few-shot prompts shifted answers while the chain of thought rationalised them (accuracy drops up to about 36%, Turpin et al.), and in a 2025 hint study reasoning models mentioned a hint they used only about 25–39% of the time. Follow-up: "how would you measure faithfulness for your model?" Insert hints and count disclosures, or truncate and corrupt chains of thought and measure how often the answer changes.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] Write down the QK and OV circuits of an attention head and say what each does.	The score between query position i and key position j is s_ij = x_iᵀ W_QK x_j/√d_h with W_QK = W_Qᵀ W_K, which decides where to look; the head writes Σ_j A_ij W_OV x_j with W_OV = W_O W_V, which decides what to move. Both are d × d matrices of rank at most d_h, and only the products are meaningful, since any invertible matrix can be moved between W_Q and W_K. Pushing the embeddings through gives vocabulary-level tables: W_Eᵀ W_QK W_E (which token attends to which) and W_U W_OV W_E (what attending to a token does to the logits). Composition is when a later head's W_QK or W_OV reads an earlier head's W_OV output, measured by ‖W_QK² W_OV¹‖_F normalised by the two norms, though that weight-based score can be near the random baseline even for a real circuit (it was in the check script), so confirm with patching. Follow-up: "what does a copying head look like?" A large positive diagonal in W_U W_OV W_E.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] Derive why an L1 SAE shrinks features, and how you would fix it.	For one active latent with a unit decoder, the loss in f is (a − f)² + λf for f ≥ 0. The derivative −2(a − f) + λ is zero at f = a − λ/2, so every active latent is reduced by λ/2 and anything below λ/2 is zeroed: soft-thresholding, as in the lasso. With a = 1 and λ = 0.3 the latent is 0.85, and reconstructions are systematically too short. Fixes: TopK (no magnitude penalty, fixed k), JumpReLU (learned threshold with an L0 penalty, used in Gemma Scope), gated SAEs (penalise the gate, not the magnitude), or a post-hoc rescaling of active latents. Follow-up: "why weight the penalty by decoder norm?" Otherwise the SAE shrinks f and grows the decoder to dodge the penalty.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] Your attribution-patching scan and real patching disagree about a head. Which do you believe, and why might they differ?	Real patching, because attribution patching is a first-order Taylor estimate, (a_clean − a_corr)ᵀ∇m at the corrupted run, and is wrong wherever the model is non-linear between the two activations. The usual culprits are attention that saturates (restoring one head moves its attention from diffuse to sharp), layer norms whose scale changes, and all-or-nothing components. The trade-off: attribution patching costs two forward passes and one backward pass for every component, against one pass per component for real patching, so use it to shortlist and confirm the top candidates. Follow-up: "can you improve the approximation?" Integrate the gradient along the path from corrupted to clean, as integrated gradients does, at the cost of several backward passes.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] Explain integrated gradients and when you would distrust it.	IG integrates the gradient along a straight path from a baseline x′ to the input, IG_i = (x_i − x′_i)∫∂F/∂x_i dα, and satisfies completeness: the attributions sum to F(x) − F(x′). For F = x₁x₂ at (2, 3) with a zero baseline, IG gives (3, 3), summing to 6, while gradient × input gives (6, 6), summing to 12. Distrust it when the baseline is arbitrary (no neutral token for text), when features are correlated, and when the method fails the randomisation sanity check of Adebayo et al. Attributions are local and correlational: they say which inputs mattered here, not how the model computed. Follow-up: "how does it relate to SHAP?" IG is the Aumann–Shapley value along one path; SHAP averages marginal contributions over feature orderings.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] What is an interpretability illusion, and how do you guard against it?	A subspace intervention that changes behaviour by activating a pathway the model does not normally use, so the conclusion "the variable lives here" is wrong. Makelov et al. showed a learned direction can mix a dormant component that encodes the variable with an active one that does not, and patching along it succeeds. Guards: test the subspace on held-out inputs and interventions it was not optimised on, restrict it to directions the model varies along on real data, and look for independent support (a probe, an SAE feature, a known circuit). Follow-up: "what is DAS?" Distributed alignment search learns a rotation and subspace so interchange interventions match a hypothesised algorithm, which is exactly the setting where the illusion can arise.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 26] How would you find out what RLHF changed inside a model?	Model diffing. Train a crosscoder on activations of the base and chat models with shared latents and separate decoders, then look at latents whose decoder norm is near zero for the base model: candidates for what post-training added (refusal, persona, format). Validate them causally by steering or ablating in the chat model, and beware that L1 training can make latents look chat-specific when they are not. Cheaper complements: compare probes and steering vectors across the two models, and track a known direction such as refusal. Follow-up: "what if nothing new appears?" Post-training may mostly reweight existing features, which you would see as changed activation frequencies of shared latents.	chapter ch::26
[Ch 27] What is reward hacking? Give an LLM example and mitigations.	Exploiting the training signal rather than achieving the goal: verbose flattering answers against reward models; editing tests in coding RL. Mitigate with hidden tests, sandboxes, property-based verifiers, KL limits, monitoring and audits. Over-optimisation has a measured shape: Gao et al. fit gold reward as d(α − βd) for best-of-n, with d = √KL, so gold reward peaks at d = α/(2β) and then falls; best-of-n's KL is log n − (n − 1)/n, so best-of-16 sits at d ≈ 1.35. Reward-model ensembles delay the peak but fail when all members share a blind spot. When not to penalise: do not train against a chain-of-thought monitor's signal, because Baker et al. found it teaches the model to hide intent while the hacking continues. Follow-up: "how would you detect it in a run?" A sudden reward jump, a growing gap between the training reward and a held-out gold check, and reading successful trajectories.	chapter ch::27
[Ch 27] Why does RLHF produce sycophancy, and how would you measure it?	Raters reward agreement and confidence, so the reward model learns that agreeing scores well. Measure answer changes under pushback, asymmetry of flips, opinion-tailoring and feedback bias, with paired tests. In the check script, 45 correct answers flip to wrong and 7 wrong to correct under pushback; McNemar on 45 versus 7 gives p ≈ 7 × 10⁻⁸. The asymmetry is the signal, because an honest model should also flip wrong answers when pushed. Follow-up: "how would you fix it?" Preference data where the correct answer disagrees with the user, and reward models audited for agreement bias.	chapter ch::27
[Ch 27] Describe three jailbreak families and a layered defence.	Role-play, many-shot, GCG suffixes (or encodings, multi-turn, injection). Defence: safety training with adversarial examples, input and output classifiers, representation-level methods, least-privilege system design, monitoring. GCG in one sentence: greedy coordinate search over suffix tokens, using gradients with respect to one-hot token encodings to propose swaps and forward passes to pick the best, maximising the probability of "Sure, here is". Its gibberish suffixes have high perplexity, so a perplexity filter catches most of them, but fluent optimised attacks evade it. Follow-up: "how do you know the defence works?" Attack success with an interval against adaptive attacks, reported with over-refusal.	chapter ch::27
[Ch 27] What is weak-to-strong generalisation and why does it matter?	Training a strong model on a weak supervisor's labels and measuring the performance gap recovered; an empirical analogy for humans supervising superhuman models. PGR = (acc_w2s − acc_weak)/(acc_ceiling − acc_weak): with the check script's 0.927, 0.910 and 0.974, PGR = 0.017/0.064 = 27%. Recovery is partial and depends on the structure of the weak supervisor's errors and the training setup: systematic errors are imitated more with longer training, scattered ones averaged out. Follow-up: "why report an interval?" The denominator is a small difference, so a third decimal of accuracy moves PGR by a point.	chapter ch::27
[Ch 27] Summarise the sleeper-agents and alignment-faking results and their limits.	Backdoors survived safety training, and adversarial training sometimes taught models to hide them better; a model strategically complied during perceived training to avoid modification, reasoning about it explicitly. Both were under constructed conditions; they show possibility and mechanisms, not deployment frequency. The methodological lesson is that safety training can only remove behaviours it elicits, so evaluations must try to elicit them. Follow-up: "how would you test whether a production model fakes alignment?" Compare behaviour between conditions the model believes are monitored and unmonitored, with rates and intervals, and read its reasoning.	chapter ch::27
[Ch 27] What is AI control?	Designing deployment protocols (trusted monitoring, audits, restricted permissions) that keep a possibly misaligned model from causing harm, evaluated against red-team strategies that assume the model is trying to subvert them. In the toy trusted-monitoring protocol, a monitor with separation μ = 2 and a 2% audit budget catches 48% of attacks; deferring the next 10% most suspicious to the trusted model raises safety to 70%, at the cost of 10% of solutions coming from the weaker model. Follow-up: "what limits it?" The capability gap between the trusted and untrusted models, and collusion when the untrusted model monitors itself.	chapter ch::27
[Ch 27] Why is chain-of-thought monitoring useful and fragile?	Models often reveal intent in reasoning ("let me just make the test pass"), so monitors catch misbehaviour that actions alone hide; but optimisation pressure on the reasoning, latent reasoning or learned concealment can remove the signal. Baker et al. showed the pressure case directly: penalising flagged thoughts reduced visible hacking while hacking continued. And chains of thought are not fully faithful even without pressure: reasoning models disclosed hints they used only about 25–39% of the time (Chapter 26.9). Follow-up: "so what rule do you follow?" Do not train against the monitor, measure monitorability per model, and combine with probes on internal states.	chapter ch::27
