[Ch 27] How do you evaluate a dangerous capability responsibly?	Strong elicitation (fine-tuning, tools, scaffolds), expert-designed tasks, appropriate statistics (upper bounds when no successes), clear thresholds, and documented limits. Check for sandbagging: fine-tuning on a few correct demonstrations should not raise the score much if the model was trying. Distinguish knowledge (WMDP-style multiple choice) from end-to-end ability (an agentic task), and capability from propensity. Follow-up: "what if the score drops after unlearning?" Re-test after an attacker's fine-tuning budget, because unlearned knowledge often returns.	chapter ch::27
[Ch 27] Zero of 50 attempts at a dangerous task succeeded. What can you claim?	That the per-attempt success rate is below about 5.8% with 95% confidence (1 − 0.05^(1/50)), under this elicitation; not that the model cannot do it. A determined actor with 50 tries at 5% each succeeds at least once with probability 92%, so the per-attempt bound understates the risk. To claim below 1%, you need about 300 attempts with no success. Follow-up: "what else would you check?" Whether elicitation was strong enough, and whether the model was sandbagging.	chapter ch::27
[Ch 27] What is emergent misalignment?	Narrow fine-tuning (for example on insecure code written without telling the user) causing broadly misaligned behaviour on unrelated prompts, suggesting training shapes general dispositions or a "persona", not only narrow skills. The practical consequences: evaluate fine-tuned models broadly, not only on the fine-tuning task, and be careful with training data whose implicit character (deceiving the user) differs from its surface task. Follow-up: "how would you test for it?" A fixed set of open-ended probes for misaligned answers run before and after every fine-tune, with rates and intervals.	chapter ch::27
[Ch 27] Explain Constitutional AI and RLAIF.	Bai et al. trained harmlessness with no human harmlessness labels, using a written constitution. In the supervised phase the model critiques and revises its own answers to red-team prompts against principles, and is fine-tuned on the revisions. In the RL phase a feedback model picks the better of two answers for a sampled principle, its probabilities become soft preference labels, a preference model is trained on them plus human helpfulness labels, and the policy is trained with RL: RL from AI feedback. The benefits are cost and legibility, since the target is a document you can read and change. The risks are the judge's biases and blind spots becoming the policy's, so keep human audit sets. Follow-up: "how does it relate to Constitutional Classifiers?" The same idea of generating training data from a written constitution, applied to input and output classifiers rather than the policy.	chapter ch::27
[Ch 27] What is deliberative alignment, and how does it differ from RLHF?	OpenAI's method for reasoning models: teach the model the text of the safety spec and to reason about it in the chain of thought before answering. A model with the spec in context generates spec-citing reasoning and answers; a spec-aware judge filters them; the model is fine-tuned on them with the spec removed, so it learns to recall it; then RL uses the spec-aware judge as reward. RLHF teaches the rules only implicitly through rated examples. The reported benefit was better jailbreak robustness and less over-refusal at the same time, and better generalisation to new situations. Trade-offs: it relies on faithful reasoning, and training on chains of thought can weaken their monitoring value. Follow-up: "how would you evaluate it?" Per-rule compliance with intervals, both error directions, and adaptive attacks.	chapter ch::27
[Ch 27] What is a model spec and why does the instruction hierarchy matter?	A public document describing intended behaviour: objectives, hard rules, adjustable defaults and how conflicts resolve. It is the source for training data and grader rubrics, and the reference for evaluation. Its core structure is the instruction hierarchy: developer policies over the system prompt, the system prompt over the user, and the user over content the model reads, which carries no authority. Training on conflicts between levels (Wallace et al., 2024) made models much more robust to prompt injection. Follow-up: "how would you measure it?" Attack success rate for instructions injected at each level, and the rate of wrongly ignoring legitimate higher-level instructions.	chapter ch::27
[Ch 27] Design a control evaluation for a coding agent with repository access.	Threat model: the untrusted agent tries to insert a backdoor or exfiltrate a secret without being caught. Blue team: a trusted monitor scoring each action and diff, an audit budget (say 2% of actions reviewed by humans), defer-to-trusted or resample for the next band of suspicion, and least-privilege permissions. Red team: controls the agent, knows the protocol, chooses when and how to attack. Measure safety (no attack succeeds unaudited) and usefulness (tasks completed relative to the agent alone) across protocols. The number: with monitor separation μ = 2 and a 2% audit budget, auditing alone gives 48% safety and deferring the next 10% gives 70%, which shows the monitor's quality matters most. Follow-up: "what affordances do you give the red team?" Everything a scheming model plausibly has, including knowledge of the protocol; too few makes the protocol look safe.	chapter ch::27
[Ch 27] A mitigation cuts ASR from 12/200 to 3/200 on the same prompts. Is it real, and what else do you report?	Not by interval overlap: the intervals (3.5–10.2% and 0.5–4.3%) overlap, and overlap is the wrong test for paired data. Count discordant prompts: if 10 succeed only against the old model and 1 only against the new, McNemar's exact test gives p = 0.012, so yes. Then report what the number does not cover: adaptive attacks (an automated red-teamer and human experts), the attempt budget (50 tries at 1.5% each still succeed 53% of the time), and over-refusal on a benign borderline set, side by side. Follow-up: "what if over-refusal doubled?" Then the mitigation moved the problem; plot both rates across thresholds and choose with the product's costs.	chapter ch::27
[Ch 28] What is hallucination and how would you reduce it in a RAG system?	Fluent output not supported by the context or by facts: intrinsic (contradicts the context) or extrinsic (adds unverifiable claims). In RAG, most hallucinations start upstream, so first check whether the right passage was retrieved; then ground the prompt (answer only from the context, say "I don't know" otherwise), require citations per claim and verify them with an NLI model or judge, and add an abstain path. Measure it: on a 200-question set, a 10% unsupported-claim rate has a 95% interval of roughly ±4 points, so compare variants on the same set. Trade-off: stricter grounding raises abstention on answerable questions, so report coverage beside accuracy. Follow-up: "Would lowering temperature fix it?" Only sampling noise; it cannot supply knowledge the model lacks, and a wrong mode becomes a consistent wrong answer.	chapter ch::28
[Ch 28] What is the difference between a jailbreak and prompt injection?	In a jailbreak the user is the attacker and tries to make the model break the operator's or developer's rules (role-play, encodings, many-shot). In prompt injection the attacker is a third party whose text the model reads (a web page, an email, a tool result) and the victim is the user or operator. Jailbreaks are mitigated mostly by safety training and classifiers; injection needs architecture, because a model cannot reliably tell instructions from data: least privilege, a quarantined model for untrusted text, and gating outbound actions. Follow-up: "Which is worse for an agent?" Indirect injection: the attacker never talks to your system and can target every user whose agent reads the poisoned page.	chapter ch::28
[Ch 28] How would you detect and remove PII before sending text to an LLM API?	Layer a cheap regex pass for structured identifiers (emails, phone numbers per market, cards validated with Luhn so order ids survive) with an NER model or Presidio for names and addresses, and replace each with a typed placeholder that can be re-inserted after the call if needed. Evaluate it like a classifier on a labelled sample per language: a regex may reach 0.99 precision on emails but under 0.5 recall on names in non-Latin scripts. Trade-off: aggressive redaction hurts the task (an address-correction feature needs the address), so redact per use case. Follow-up: "And the logs?" Redact before logging too, set retention limits, and check the provider's data-processing terms.	chapter ch::28
[Ch 28] How do you choose a threshold for a moderation classifier?	From costs, not by eye: with calibrated probabilities, flag when p > c_FP/(c_FP + c_FN), so a miss nine times as costly as a false flag gives p* = 0.1. Calibrate per language and group first, because a model overconfident on one dialect has a different effective threshold there. Then add a second threshold for automatic action, send the band between to humans, and size the band from reviewer capacity: 860 items per 10,000 at 200,000 a day is 17,200 reviews, about 24 reviewers. Evaluate precision at the production prevalence, not on a balanced set. Follow-up: "Different thresholds per category?" Yes: child-safety and threats have far higher miss costs than profanity.	chapter ch::28
[Ch 28] Design the safety layer for a customer-support agent that can issue refunds.	Start with least privilege (read order, propose refund), approval rules by amount and customer history enforced in code, untrusted text (chat, order notes) delimited and never able to set the amount, an output schema, rate limits and anomaly alerts on refunds, a full audit log, and a red-team set including injection through chat and order notes. Check the lethal trifecta: the agent sees private order data and untrusted chat, so its outbound channels (email, links) must be gated. Put a number on exposure: if 0.5% of 100,000 daily chats attempt abuse and the cap is 20 euros without approval, the worst case is 10,000 euros a day, which sets the cap. Trade-off: every approval step adds handling time. Follow-up: "How do you know it works?" ASR and utility under attack on an injection suite, reported with intervals on every release.	chapter ch::28
[Ch 28] A user complains that the AI summary of reviews about their business is unfair and invented a hygiene complaint. Walk through your response, short term and long term.	Short term: take down or correct the summary, find the trace, and check the sources: did any review mention hygiene, was one misread, or was the claim invented? Respond to the complainant with what happened. Long term: add a faithfulness check that every claim cites a review and is entailed by it, a regression test for this case, a feedback path for businesses, and a measured hallucination rate per release. Size it: if 1% of 50,000 summaries contain an unsupported negative claim, that is 500 potentially defamatory statements, which justifies an entailment check on negative claims before publishing. Follow-up: "Is a summary like this legally risky?" Possibly defamation; involve legal and prefer quoting reviews over paraphrasing for serious allegations.	chapter ch::28
[Ch 28] How would you check that a moderation model is not biased against a dialect (for example Gulf Arabic versus Modern Standard Arabic)?	Build a labelled set with dialect and standard samples balanced by true label, compare false-positive rates (the harm is silencing) and recall, with intervals, then read the errors, since dialect words that resemble slurs or profanity are a common cause. Fix with dialect training data, a dialect-aware model or per-variety calibration, and monitor after release. Numbers: 500 benign items per variety give an FPR interval of about ±2 points at 5%, enough to detect 5% versus 10%. Trade-off: equalising FPR by threshold lowers recall for one group (the impossibility result in 28.2). Follow-up: "Where do you get labels?" Native-speaker annotators with agreement measured, not translations of English data.	chapter ch::28
[Ch 28] What does the EU AI Act mean for an ML team shipping LLM features in the EU (for example at a delivery company)?	Know which uses could be high-risk (worker management such as rider assignment and evaluation; credit; employment), meet transparency duties for chatbots and generated content, keep documentation and logs, follow group policy, and involve legal early. As a deployer of a general-purpose model, most obligations sit with the provider; you inherit them if you substantially modify it or use it in a high-risk area. Dates: prohibitions from February 2025, general-purpose model duties from August 2025, high-risk duties later and possibly delayed by the 2025 omnibus proposal. Follow-up: "And outside the EU?" Map the same process to the NIST AI RMF and ISO/IEC 42001.	chapter ch::28
[Ch 28] What fairness criteria do you know, and can you satisfy them all at once?	Demographic parity (equal selection rates), equal opportunity (equal TPR), equalised odds (equal TPR and FPR), predictive parity (equal PPV) and calibration within groups. With different base rates you cannot have equal PPV, FPR and FNR together: FPR = p/(1 − p) · (1 − PPV)/PPV · (1 − FNR), so equal PPV and FNR force FPRs apart. Example: TPR 0.8, FPR 0.1 in two groups with base rates 50% and 20% gives PPV 0.889 versus 0.667; equalising PPV would need group B's FPR at 0.025. So choose by harm (equal FPR for moderation, where false flags silence people), and report all rates per group with intervals. Follow-up: "Why not drop the group attribute?" Proxies (language, location) reconstruct it, and without it you cannot even measure the disparity.	chapter ch::28
[Ch 28] What does a differential-privacy ε mean, and what does DP-SGD cost?	(ε, δ)-DP says that adding or removing one example changes the probability of any trained model by at most a factor e^ε, plus δ. Directly, any membership attack has TPR ≤ e^ε · FPR + δ: at FPR 1%, ε = 1 caps TPR at 2.7%, while ε = 8 is vacuous in the worst case. DP-SGD clips each example's gradient to norm C and adds Gaussian noise σC before averaging; an accountant turns σ, sampling rate and steps into ε. The noise norm scales as σC√d/B, about 7.7 against a signal of at most 1 for a billion parameters at B = 4,096, so use huge batches, LoRA-sized trainable sets and public pretraining. Follow-up: "Who is hurt most?" Rare examples and small groups lose most accuracy.	chapter ch::28
[Ch 28] How would you test whether a fine-tuned LLM memorised customer data?	Two tests. Extraction: prompt with prefixes of training records (and generic prompts at scale), and check generations for verbatim or near-verbatim continuations, especially of canary strings you inserted on purpose: a canary reproduced from its prefix is direct evidence. Membership inference: compare the model's loss on training records with held-out records from the same distribution, and report TPR at a low FPR (0.1% or 1%), not AUC; a LiRA-style per-example calibration is stronger. Memorisation grows with duplication, model size and epochs, so deduplicate and redact before training. Follow-up: "Pretraining MIAs report AUC 0.7 on a dated split. Believe it?" No: if members and non-members differ in date, the attack detects distribution shift, not membership.	chapter ch::28
[Ch 28] A user asks you to remove their data from a deployed model. What do you do?	Remove it from all datasets and future training runs immediately, and from retrieval indexes and caches, where deletion is exact and easy (a strong argument for keeping personal data in retrieval rather than in weights). For the weights, the only exact answer is retraining without it; SISA-style sharding makes that cheap by retraining one shard. Approximate unlearning (gradient ascent, NPO-style objectives) is faster but evidence shows it often suppresses rather than removes, and knowledge returns after light fine-tuning, so verify with extraction and membership tests and do not call it deletion. Follow-up: "What if retraining costs millions?" Batch requests into the next scheduled retrain and gate outputs with filters meanwhile, with legal's agreement.	chapter ch::28
[Ch 28] Your team is building an email agent that reads the inbox and can send mail. How do you defend it against prompt injection?	Name the threat model: it has all three legs of the lethal trifecta (private data, untrusted inbound mail, an outbound channel), so a single injected email can exfiltrate data. Remove or gate a leg: drafts instead of sends, or confirmation for any recipient not already in the thread. Architecturally, use the dual-LLM or CaMeL pattern so the plan comes only from the user's request and untrusted email content flows as data with provenance, checked by policies ("content from email X may only go to X's sender"). Add an instruction-hierarchy-trained model and spotlighting as probabilistic layers. Measure ASR and utility under attack on an AgentDojo-style suite: 6 of 200 is 3% [1.4%, 6.4%]. Follow-up: "Why not a classifier?" Adaptive attackers get past classifiers; architecture bounds the damage when they do.	chapter ch::28
[Ch 28] How does LLM text watermarking work, and what are its limits?	The generator hashes the previous token to split the vocabulary into a green fraction γ and adds a bias to green logits; a detector with the key counts green tokens and computes z = (|s|_G − γT)/√(Tγ(1 − γ)). With γ = 0.25 and 100 green tokens out of 200, z = 8.2, far beyond chance. Limits: paraphrasing or translation by another model strips much of the signal, short or low-entropy texts (code) carry too little, only the key holder can detect, and the absence of a watermark proves nothing. For media, C2PA signed metadata complements it. Follow-up: "Use a classifier detector instead?" No for consequential decisions: unwatermarked detectors have high false-positive rates, notably on non-native writers.	chapter ch::28
[Ch 29] How are sentence embeddings produced from a transformer?	Pool token vectors (mean pooling over real tokens, CLS, or last token for decoder models) from an encoder that was fine-tuned contrastively so that cosine similarity reflects meaning. Raw masked-LM encoders are poor at this: their vectors sit in a narrow cone, with average cosines between unrelated sentences of 0.6 or more, so similarity is dominated by a frequency direction. Follow-up: "Why last-token pooling for decoders?" Under causal attention only the last position has seen the whole input.	chapter ch::29
[Ch 29] What is contrastive learning?	Training with pairs that should be close and negatives that should be far, typically with the InfoNCE loss: a softmax over similarities where the true pair is the target class. Geometrically it does two things: aligns positive pairs and spreads all embeddings uniformly over the sphere (Wang and Isola). Follow-up: "Where do the negatives come from?" The other items in the batch for free, plus mined hard negatives.	chapter ch::29
[Ch 29] Why does batch size matter for in-batch negatives?	Each query's negatives are the other items in the batch; larger batches give more and harder negatives and better gradient signal, and InfoNCE's mutual-information bound I ≥ log B − L cannot certify more than log B nats (5.5 at B = 256, 8.3 at B = 4,096). To grow B beyond memory, use GradCache (embed without activations, compute embedding gradients, re-run sub-batches) and gather embeddings across devices. Trade-off: false negatives grow with B; at a 10⁻⁴ chance per document of an unlabelled match, 34% of queries see at least one in a batch of 4,096. Follow-up: "What else gives hard negatives without a huge batch?" Mining them explicitly (29.3).	chapter ch::29
[Ch 29] Cosine similarity vs dot product?	Identical on L2-normalised vectors. Unnormalised dot product also rewards vector length, which some models use (for example to encode popularity or confidence); use what the model was trained with, and normalise consistently at indexing and query time. Follow-up: "Which does an ANN index need?" Inner-product indexes on normalised vectors give cosine; mixing normalised queries with unnormalised documents silently changes the ranking.	chapter ch::29
[Ch 29] What is the temperature in InfoNCE?	A divisor on similarities before the softmax. Since each negative's gradient is pᵢⱼ/τ, a low temperature concentrates the push on the hardest negatives: with a positive at 0.8, a hard negative at 0.7 and an easy one at 0.3, τ = 0.05 gives the easy one essentially zero gradient, while τ = 0.3 gives it a quarter of the hard one's. Typical values are 0.01–0.05 for text embedders; CLIP learns it, with the logit scale clamped at 100 (τ ≥ 0.01). Too low amplifies false negatives and makes training unstable (almost all gradient through one negative, scaled by 1/τ); too high learns only coarse separation. Follow-up: "Why clamp a learned τ?" Without it the model can sharpen the softmax without improving the geometry and training diverges.	chapter ch::29
[Ch 29] How do you mine hard negatives safely?	Retrieve top candidates with BM25 or the current model, skip the very top ranks, filter with a cross-encoder or margin to remove false negatives, and re-mine as the model improves. The danger is specific: a false negative has a high similarity, hence a high softmax probability, hence the largest push away. Rule of thumb: sample from ranks 10–50 and drop any candidate the cross-encoder scores within a margin of the positive. Follow-up: "Better than filtering?" Distil from the cross-encoder, which turns half-relevant negatives into graded targets.	chapter ch::29
[Ch 29] What are Matryoshka embeddings?	Embeddings trained with the loss applied to several prefix lengths, so truncated vectors remain useful, allowing a choice of size per stage (short for first-pass search, full for re-scoring). Numbers: 2M vectors at 1,024 float32 dimensions take 8.2 GB; at 256 int8 dimensions, 0.5 GB. Without Matryoshka training, truncating by coordinate loses badly; PCA-then-truncate is the fallback to compare against. Follow-up: "Why do prefixes carry the most information?" Each prefix has its own loss term, so the first dimensions must discriminate on their own.	chapter ch::29
[Ch 29] How would you reduce embedding storage 30× with little quality loss?	Binary or product quantisation for the first-stage search with full-precision re-scoring of a short list; int8 for a 4× reduction with very small loss; Matryoshka truncation if the model supports it. Binary is 32× (1 bit instead of 32); on the chapter's synthetic set it kept 0.37 recall@10 alone and 1.00 after re-scoring the top 100, and published results on real models report roughly 90–96% of quality kept with re-scoring. Measure recall at each step on your own embeddings. Follow-up: "Where do the float vectors live?" On disk or cheaper storage; only the shortlist is read per query.	chapter ch::29
[Ch 29] How do you choose an embedding model for a new product?	Shortlist from MTEB filtered by task and language, then evaluate on your own labelled queries with retrieval metrics, plus latency, dimension, cost and licence; consider fine-tuning the best small model. On 400 queries a recall@10 interval is about ±3–4 points, so smaller differences are ties. Follow-up: "Why not take the top of the leaderboard?" Averages mix tasks, some models trained near benchmark data, and none of it is your data.	chapter ch::29
[Ch 29] Design embeddings for item similarity at a marketplace (for example restaurants at a delivery company).	Define similarity by behaviour (co-ordering, substitution) as well as content; train a two-tower or item-item contrastive model on co-order pairs with content features (description embeddings, category, price) for cold start; logQ correction for popularity, because popular items appear as in-batch negatives far more often than chance; evaluate on held-out substitution and "also ordered" pairs; Matryoshka or PQ for cheap serving. Numbers: 500k items at 256 int8 dimensions is 128 MB, small enough to serve from memory everywhere. Trade-off: behaviour-only similarity cannot place new items. Follow-up: "How do you stop it recommending only the popular?" logQ correction in training and exposure constraints at serving (Chapter 43).	chapter ch::29
[Ch 29] Your fine-tuned embedding model beats the base model offline but RAG answers did not improve. Why?	The RAG bottleneck may be elsewhere (chunking, re-ranking, generation); the evaluation set may differ from real questions; gains may sit in queries that were already answered well; the index may not have been rebuilt consistently. Check end-to-end and per-stage metrics: if recall@5 rose from 0.80 to 0.85 but 70% of wrong answers had the right passage in context, the ceiling on end-to-end gain is small. Follow-up: "Which per-stage metric first?" Context recall at the number of passages the LLM sees.	chapter ch::29
[Ch 29] How would you build a multilingual retrieval system, for example for Arabic and English?	A multilingual embedding model plus language-specific BM25; per-language and cross-lingual evaluation sets; transliteration variants; fine-tuning on translated and code-switched pairs if needed; language-aware boosting; native-speaker review of failures. Report each slice separately, because an average dominated by English hides a weak second language. Follow-up: "Same-language mediocre matches outrank better cross-language ones. Fix?" Language-aware boosts or retrieve per language and fuse with RRF.	chapter ch::29
[Ch 29] Derive the gradient of InfoNCE with respect to a similarity, and say what it explains.	For query i, Lᵢ = −sᵢᵢ/τ + log Σₖ exp(sᵢₖ/τ), so ∂Lᵢ/∂sᵢⱼ = (pᵢⱼ − 1[j = i])/τ. The positive is pulled with (1 − pᵢᵢ)/τ and each negative pushed with pᵢⱼ/τ, its softmax probability. It explains four things at once: low τ is hard-negative mining (the push concentrates on high-similarity negatives), false negatives are the most damaging examples (highest similarity, largest push), easy in-batch negatives teach little (p ≈ 0), and the loss stops teaching once pᵢᵢ ≈ 1. Example: at τ = 0.05 with similarities 0.8, 0.7 and 0.3, the gradients are −2.38, +2.38 and 0.00. Follow-up: "Gradient with respect to the query vector?" (1/τ)(Σⱼ pᵢⱼdⱼ − dᵢ) for dot products.	chapter ch::29
[Ch 29] How are the strongest retrievers trained beyond hard negatives?	By distillation from a cross-encoder: the teacher reads query and passage together and gives graded scores, and the bi-encoder learns them with MarginMSE, the squared difference between student and teacher margins (s⁺ − s⁻), or with KL between softmaxes over a candidate list. It transfers graded relevance and neutralises false negatives, since a mislabelled negative the teacher likes is learnt as relevant. Cost: one teacher pass per (query, candidate) pair, done offline. Follow-up: "Why match margins, not scores?" The student's cosine scale and the teacher's logit scale differ; margins are comparable after one scale factor.	chapter ch::29
[Ch 29] How do you turn a decoder LLM into an embedding model?	Causal attention means only the last position has seen everything, so either pool the last token (as Qwen3-Embedding does) or remove the causal mask and adapt: LLM2Vec adds masked next-token prediction and unsupervised SimCSE before supervised contrastive training; NV-Embed adds a latent-attention pooling layer. Then train contrastively on large mixed data with instructions on the query side. Trade-off: a 7B embedder costs far more per token to encode than a 100M encoder, which dominates index-build cost (300M tokens is hours on one GPU for a base model, much more for 7B). Follow-up: "Why instructions only on queries?" So the document index is task-independent and need not be rebuilt per task.	chapter ch::29
[Ch 30] How does an inverted index work?	For each term, store a postings list of the documents that contain it and how often. A query only reads the postings of its own terms and accumulates scores (TF-IDF or BM25) per document, so it scales to millions of documents with millisecond latency. Cost is proportional to the postings touched, which is why very common terms are expensive and why engines skip or prune them. Follow-up: "How do updates work?" New documents go into small new segments that are merged in the background; deletes are marked and purged on merge.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] Sparse vs dense retrieval: strengths of each?	Sparse: exact matches on names, codes, rare terms; no training; explainable; cheap to update. Dense: matches meaning across different wording and languages. Sparse fails on paraphrase; dense fails on exact identifiers and can fail out of domain (BEIR showed many dense models losing to BM25 on new domains). Hybrid combines both and is the usual default. Follow-up: "Where does SPLADE sit?" Learned term weights with expansion in an inverted index: sparse efficiency with some semantic matching.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] What is a bi-encoder and why can it scale?	Two encoders map query and document to vectors independently; relevance is their dot product. Because document vectors do not depend on the query, they are precomputed and indexed for ANN search: 10 million documents become one 7.7-billion-multiply-add exact scan or a millisecond HNSW lookup. The price is that query and document never interact token by token, which caps precision. Follow-up: "How do you recover that precision?" Re-rank the top candidates with a cross-encoder.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] Why approximate nearest neighbour instead of exact search?	Exact search costs N × d per query, too slow at tens of millions of vectors and high traffic; it is also memory-bandwidth bound, since every query reads every vector (153.6 GB for 50M × 768 float32). ANN indexes (HNSW, IVF, PQ) search a small part of the collection and lose a little recall, which you measure against exact search on a sample and tune. Follow-up: "When is exact fine?" Under a few hundred thousand vectors, for ground truth, or batched on GPUs.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] What is reciprocal rank fusion?	Combine ranked lists by summing 1 / (k + rank) per document, with k around 60. It needs no score normalisation or training, so it is the standard way to merge BM25 and dense results. With k = 60, rank 1 is worth 0.0164 and rank 10 is worth 0.0143, so a document found by both lists at moderate ranks beats one found at the top of only one. Follow-up: "When is something better?" With a labelled set, a tuned convex combination of normalised scores usually wins (Bruch et al., 2023).	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] Explain HNSW and its main parameters.	A multi-layer proximity graph: sparse upper layers with long links, dense bottom layer with all points. Each node's top layer is drawn so that P(level ≥ l) = M^(−l), like a skip list, giving about log_M N layers (5 for 50M at M = 32). Search descends greedily from the top and finishes with a beam search of width efSearch on layer 0; neighbours are chosen by a diversity heuristic that keeps links to other clusters. M sets links per node (2M on layer 0: memory and recall), efConstruction sets build quality, efSearch sets candidates per query (the recall/latency knob at query time). Memory is about d × 4 + 2M × 4 bytes per vector: 3,328 bytes at 768 dimensions and M = 32. On the chapter's test, recall@10 rose from 0.88 at efSearch 8 to 0.999 at 64. Follow-up: "Weaknesses?" Memory, slow builds, deletes (tombstones and rebuilds) and selective filters that disconnect the graph.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] Explain product quantisation and when you would use it.	Split vectors into m sub-vectors, quantise each against 256 learned centroids, store one byte per sub-vector. Scoring is asymmetric: the query stays full precision, and a table of query-to-centroid similarities per sub-space turns each vector's score into m lookups. It cuts memory 10–30× at a recall cost, so it suits hundreds of millions of vectors, usually combined with IVF (encoding residuals from the cell centroid) and an exact re-rank of the short list. Numbers: 100M × 768 float32 is 307 GB; at 64-byte codes, 6.4 GB. Why recall drops: when the score error is comparable to the gaps between neighbours (0.034 against a 0.042 spread over the top 100 in the chapter's test), the top 10 is shuffled, but the true neighbours mostly stay in the top 100. Follow-up: "Beats HNSW when?" When the vectors do not fit in RAM at a cost you accept; OPQ and ScaNN's anisotropic loss reduce the error further.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] How do you train a dense retriever for your domain?	Start from a pretrained embedding model; collect (query, relevant passage) pairs from logs, experts or LLM generation; fine-tune with in-batch negatives (multiple negatives ranking loss) plus mined hard negatives filtered for false negatives, or distil from a cross-encoder; evaluate recall@k against BM25 and the base model on a held-out set. Follow-up: "Why filter negatives?" A false negative scores high and therefore gets the largest push away in the InfoNCE gradient (Chapter 29.2).	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] Cross-encoder vs ColBERT vs bi-encoder?	Bi-encoder: one vector per document, fastest, least precise. ColBERT: one vector per token with MaxSim scoring, near cross-encoder quality with offline document encoding, at a large storage cost (a 200-token passage stores 200 vectors instead of one, though ColBERTv2 compresses them to a few dozen bytes each). Cross-encoder: reads query and document together, most accurate, one pass per pair, so re-ranking only. Follow-up: "Re-rank depth?" Plot recall at the final k against depth on your evaluation set; 50–200 is typical.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] How do you handle metadata filters in vector search?	Post-filtering with oversampling is simple but returns too few results for selective filters: at selectivity 0.25 and 4× oversampling the expected number of hits is exactly 10, so 44% of queries come back short. Pre-filtering restricts search to allowed ids (filtered HNSW traversal, or exact search on small subsets, which a query planner switches to below a selectivity threshold). If a filter is always present (tenant, country, city), partition the index. Follow-up: "Why does filtered HNSW degrade?" At low selectivity the allowed subgraph disconnects, or the search must visit about k/s nodes.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] How would you evaluate the retrieval part of a RAG system?	A labelled set of real queries with relevant passages, extended with careful synthetic queries; recall@k at the context size the LLM gets, plus MRR or nDCG for ordering; compare BM25, dense, hybrid and re-ranked configurations, by segment, with bootstrap intervals; separately measure ANN recall and latency. Report judged@k when comparing a new retriever on an old pooled set. Follow-up: "Why recall over nDCG for RAG?" The LLM reads all k passages; what matters most is whether the answer is among them.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] RAG answers are wrong for 20% of questions. How do you find out whether retrieval is the cause?	For a sample of failures, check whether a relevant passage was in the retrieved set (manually or with an LLM judge). If not, it is a retrieval failure: look at query type, language, chunking, missing documents, filters. If yes, it is a generation or context-assembly failure. Quantify the split before fixing anything: with 100 sampled failures, a 60/40 split has an interval of about ±10 points, enough to decide where to start. Follow-up: "What if the passage was retrieved at rank 40 but only 5 reach the LLM?" That is a ranking failure: add or fine-tune a re-ranker.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] Design retrieval for a multilingual help centre with 50,000 articles (for example Arabic and English).	Hybrid: BM25 with language-specific analysers plus a multilingual embedding model; filter by country and audience; cross-encoder re-ranking of the top 50 with a multilingual re-ranker; conversational rewriting for follow-ups. At this size, HNSW in pgvector or a dedicated store is plenty: 50,000 articles at about five chunks each is 250,000 vectors, 0.8 GB in float32 at 768 dimensions. Evaluate per language with native-speaker labels; check cross-lingual retrieval (Arabic query, English article). Follow-up: "BM25 in Arabic?" Needs a proper analyser (normalisation of letter variants, light stemming); whitespace tokenisation underperforms.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] You must switch to a new embedding model. How do you do it safely?	Build a second index with the new model in parallel, evaluate it on the same set (recall, latency, size), shadow-test on live queries, then switch reads with a flag and keep the old index for rollback. Never mix vectors from two models in one index. Budget the re-embedding: 50M chunks at 20,000 a minute per GPU is about 42 GPU-hours. Follow-up: "Queries cached with old embeddings?" Version every cache and index by model name.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] How would you serve 100 million vectors with a p95 under 50 ms on a budget?	IVF-PQ (or HNSW over PQ-compressed vectors) to fit memory, with an exact re-rank of the top few hundred from full vectors on SSD; shard by a natural partition if filters allow; tune n_probe or efSearch to the required recall measured against exact search on a sample; cache frequent queries. Show the memory maths: 100M × 768 × 4 bytes is about 307 GB raw, about 6.4 GB at 64-byte codes; re-scoring 200 candidates reads 0.6 MB from NVMe per query. Follow-up: "Why not HNSW over floats?" It needs over 330 GB of RAM per replica with the graph.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] Derive BM25's IDF and explain why Lucene adds 1 inside the log.	IDF(t) = ln(1 + (N − df + 0.5)/(df + 0.5)). The ratio estimates the odds that a document lacks the term (the Robertson–Spärck Jones relevance weight with no relevance information), smoothed by 0.5; rare terms get large weights. The classic form ln((N − df + 0.5)/(df + 0.5)) is negative when df > N/2, so matching a very common word would lower a score; adding 1 keeps it positive. Example with N = 3: df = 1 gives 0.981, df = 2 gives 0.470 (classic: −0.511), df = 3 gives 0.134. Follow-up: "What do k₁ and b do?" k₁ sets how fast term frequency saturates (ceiling k₁ + 1); b sets how strongly long documents are penalised.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] Why does HNSW search take about log N steps, and what is the role of m_L = 1/ln M?	Each node's top level is exponential with P(level ≥ l) = M^(−l), so each layer is a 1/M sample of the one below, like a skip list, and there are about log_M N layers. On each layer a greedy walk takes roughly a constant number of hops before the next, M-times denser, layer takes over, so total work is about (hops × M) per layer times log_M N, plus efSearch × 2M at the bottom. Choosing m_L = 1/ln M matches the layer density ratio to the node degree, which the paper recommends. It is empirical: worst-case inputs have no such guarantee. Follow-up: "Why 2M links on layer 0?" All points live there and the final precise search happens there.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 30] A reviewer says your new dense retriever's nDCG gain on an old benchmark is unreliable. Why might they be right?	The benchmark's labels were pooled from older (often lexical) systems, so documents only your system retrieves are unjudged and count as irrelevant; that biases against you, but it can also hide failures, since judged@k differences make the numbers incomparable. Report judged@10 for both systems; if yours is much lower, judge the new top results (with a calibrated LLM judge if needed) and recompute. Also check for training-set overlap with the benchmark and report intervals over queries. Follow-up: "Graded or binary gains?" nDCG with 2^rel − 1 gains, so top-grade placement dominates.	chapter ch::30
[Ch 31] What problem does RAG solve compared with fine-tuning?	It gives the model current, private, citable knowledge at query time without retraining; updating knowledge means updating the index. Fine-tuning changes behaviour, style or format, and is a poor way to add facts that change: it is slow to update, cannot cite, and fine-tuning on new facts can even raise hallucination rates. Follow-up: "When combine them?" Fine-tune for the skill of using context (RAFT-style) or for format, and retrieve for the facts.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] Walk through a RAG pipeline.	Offline: load, parse, chunk with context, embed and index with metadata, keep in sync. Online: rewrite the query, hybrid retrieval with filters, re-rank, assemble context in a budget, generate with citations and an abstain option, verify grounding. Most failures are upstream of the LLM, so each stage gets its own metric. Follow-up: "Where would you look first when answers are wrong?" Whether the gold passage was retrieved at all.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] How do you choose a chunking strategy?	Start with structure-aware recursive chunking with headings prepended; try two or three sizes; compare retrieval recall and answer quality on an evaluation set at a fixed token budget (with 2,000 tokens, that is 10 chunks of 200 versus 5 of 400); consider small-to-big when answers need surrounding context. Follow-up: "What matters more than size?" Contextual headers, so that a chunk like "Requests must be made within 24 hours" says what it is about.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] What is HyDE?	Generate a hypothetical answer with an LLM and search with its embedding, because a fake answer resembles real documents more than a short question does. Combine with the original query, and renormalise the combined vector or fuse two searches with RRF; it can mislead when the guess is confidently wrong, and it adds an LLM call of latency. Follow-up: "When does it help most?" Short queries against long documents in a domain the LLM knows well.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] How do you make RAG answers trustworthy?	Answer only from retrieved documents, cite passage ids per claim, validate citations and support, abstain when documents do not answer, show sources to users, and monitor the share of unsupported claims. Calibrate the abstain signal rather than thresholding raw similarity, and report coverage against selective accuracy. Follow-up: "How do you check support cheaply?" Word overlap catches gross errors; an NLI model or judge checks properly on a sample.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] The right document is retrieved but answers are still wrong. What do you check?	Whether the relevant passage made it into the final context after assembly; its position; the number of distractors; whether the passage is self-contained; instruction clarity; whether a stronger model fixes it (a sign of reasoning difficulty rather than retrieval). Current frontier models pass simple needle tests, but accuracy still drops with long, distractor-heavy or multi-hop contexts (context rot), so cutting from 30 passages to 5 good ones often fixes it. Follow-up: "And if the model ignores the passage in favour of what it knows?" A context–memory conflict: instruct that documents are authoritative, test with counterfactual passages, and consider RAFT-style fine-tuning.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] How do you keep a RAG index up to date?	Incremental indexing by content hash; deletes for removed documents; effective dates and versions in metadata; re-embedding everything when the embedding model changes, built in parallel and switched after evaluation. Track freshness lag (time from a document change to it being searchable) as a metric. Follow-up: "Stale answers despite updates?" Old versions still indexed: dedupe by document id and keep only the current version unless history is asked for.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] When would you use GraphRAG?	For global or multi-hop questions over a relatively stable, entity-rich corpus, where the indexing cost is justified. Not for a help centre answering specific questions. Cost: an LLM extraction pass over every chunk, so a 50M-token corpus means at least 50M input tokens of LLM calls plus the extracted output and community summaries, redone as documents change. Follow-up: "A cheaper middle ground?" Extract entities as metadata and filter or join on them.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] What is agentic RAG and what does it cost?	The model decides whether, where and how often to retrieve, possibly combining documents with live tools. Better for complex or mixed questions; costs more calls, latency, and harder evaluation. Three retrieval rounds with a 2,000-token context each roughly triple the input tokens and add two model round-trips of latency. Follow-up: "How do you evaluate it?" On trajectories as well as answers (Chapter 25): did it search for the right things, and did it stop.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] Design a RAG assistant for workforce policies (for example courier or rider policies) in 10 countries and 3 languages.	Ingestion with country, language, audience and effective-date metadata; structure-aware chunks with headings; hybrid retrieval filtered by the user's country and language from the session; multilingual embeddings and re-ranker; citations and abstain path; per-country, per-language evaluation sets; freshness checks when policies change; escalation to support for low-confidence answers. Size the evaluation: 30 slices (country × language) at 50 questions each is 1,500 labelled questions, with about ±14 points per slice at 50%, so report slices for large markets and pooled numbers for the rest. Follow-up: "Mixing rules across countries?" A hard country filter from the session, never from the prompt.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] Your RAG demo scored well, but users complain after launch. What happened, and what do you do?	The demo questions were not real questions: users ask vaguer, multi-part, out-of-scope and follow-up questions. Collect real queries and failures, label them, build an evaluation set from them, and run error analysis by stage (retrieval, assembly, generation). Expect the out-of-scope share to be much higher than in the demo set, which makes the abstain path the first fix. Follow-up: "How many to label first?" 100–200 failures are enough to rank the failure categories.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] How do you decide between a bigger context window and better retrieval?	Compare on the evaluation set: answer quality, cost per question and latency for "top 5 passages" vs "top 50" vs whole documents. At 300-token passages and an illustrative $3 per million input tokens, 5 passages plus instructions is about 2,000 tokens ($0.006 a question), 50 passages about 15,500 ($0.047), and a whole 200,000-token corpus $0.60. Better retrieval usually wins on cost and often on quality, because distractors hurt. Long context wins when the corpus is small and static: with prompt caching at, say, a tenth of the input price, the 200k corpus costs $0.06 a question and avoids retrieval errors altogether (cache-augmented generation). Follow-up: "Latency?" Prefill of 200k tokens takes seconds even when cached reads are cheap; time to first token often decides.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] How would you reduce hallucinations in a customer-facing RAG system by half?	Measure first: classify hallucinations by cause (retrieval miss, ignored context, out-of-scope question, invented details). Then: abstain on calibrated low-sufficiency signals, stricter grounding instructions, citation verification with regeneration, better retrieval for the miss category, and human handover for high-stakes topics. Show the arithmetic of abstention: answering 85% of questions at 90% accuracy cuts wrong answers from 20% of questions to 8.5%. Follow-up: "Why not threshold the top cosine score?" Scores are not comparable across queries; calibrate on labelled data or ask a sufficiency question.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] Explain RAG-sequence versus RAG-token.	Both treat the retrieved document as a latent variable and sum over the top k. RAG-sequence uses one document for the whole answer: Σ_z p(z|x) Π_i p(yᵢ | x, z, y₍<ᵢ₎). RAG-token sums over documents at every token: Π_i Σ_z p(z|x) p(yᵢ | …), so different tokens can draw on different documents. Example: with p(z) = 0.6/0.4 and per-token probabilities 0.9/0.1 for z₁ and 0.2/0.9 for z₂, sequence gives 0.126 and token gives 0.260. The retriever trains without labels through ∂log p/∂s_z = p(z | x, y) − p(z | x). Follow-up: "Why freeze the document encoder?" Re-encoding the index every step is too expensive; REALM refreshes it asynchronously.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] What do RETRO and FiD change compared with putting passages in the prompt?	FiD encodes each (question, passage) pair separately and fuses them in the decoder's cross-attention, so encoder cost is linear in the number of passages and 100 passages are affordable. RETRO retrieves neighbours for every 64-token chunk from a trillions-of-tokens database and reads them through chunked cross-attention during pretraining, reporting GPT-3-level perplexity on the Pile with 25× fewer parameters. Both show knowledge can move from weights to an index. Trade-off: they need architecture changes and joint training, whereas prompt-based RAG lets retriever and LLM improve independently. Follow-up: "Why is prompt-based RAG dominant?" Strong general LLMs plus long contexts make the prompt a good enough interface.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] Retrieved context contradicts the model's knowledge. Which should win, and how do you test it?	It depends on whether the corpus is the source of truth. A policy assistant must follow its documents; an open-web assistant must resist misleading or poisoned pages. Measure both: counterfactual passages (edit the answer and check whether the model follows) for faithfulness, and inserted plausible wrong passages for robustness. Models are inconsistent: some ignore context in favour of memory, while LLMs readily adopt coherent counter-evidence. Fix with explicit instructions, RAFT-style training with distractors, and source authority in ranking. Follow-up: "Can you have both?" Not fully: maximal faithfulness and maximal robustness to bad context pull in opposite directions; trust in the corpus sets the balance.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 31] How can an attacker compromise a RAG system, and how do you defend it?	By writing to the corpus: poisoned passages crafted to be retrieved for target questions and to carry a false answer (a handful per question sufficed in PoisonedRAG), or indirect prompt injection inside documents. Defend by controlling who can write to indexed sources, recording provenance, ranking authoritative sources higher, treating retrieved text as delimited data that can never trigger tools on its own, and checking outputs for links or actions the question did not call for. For agentic RAG, apply the lethal-trifecta test (Chapter 28.4). Follow-up: "How do you measure it?" Attack success rate on a red-team set of poisoned documents, per release.	chapter ch::31
[Ch 32] What does LangChain's LCEL give you over calling the API directly?	A common interface (invoke, batch, stream and async versions) for every component, and the pipe operator to compose prompts, models, retrievers and parsers into one runnable, so batching, streaming, retries and tracing come with the composition rather than being written by hand. The concrete win is concurrency: 1,000 calls of 2 seconds each take 2,000 s in a plain loop and about 250 s with batch(..., max_concurrency=8). The cost is indirection: errors surface deep in library frames and the final prompt is assembled out of sight, so log it. Follow-up: "would you use it in production?" For a simple flow, an SDK call inside a thread pool gives the same concurrency with fewer dependencies.	chapter ch::32
[Ch 32] What is the difference between LangChain and LangGraph?	LangChain supplies components and linear composition; LangGraph models a workflow as a state machine whose nodes read and update a typed state, with conditional edges, loops, checkpointing after each step, human-in-the-loop interrupts and streaming of intermediate steps. Use LangChain-style composition for a fixed pipeline and LangGraph when the flow loops (retry until a check passes), branches, or must pause and resume. Since LangChain 1.0 its agents run on LangGraph's runtime, so the line is now components versus orchestration. Follow-up: "what does the checkpointer buy you?" Resuming after a crash or an approval pause, and replaying a run from any step for debugging.	chapter ch::32
[Ch 32] When would you reach for LlamaIndex?	When the hard part is the data side of RAG: many sources to load (PDFs, wikis, databases), chunking and metadata, several index types, and query engines that route or decompose questions across them. It gets a prototype running in a few lines, but its defaults (chunk size, embedding model, top-k, prompt) are rarely right, and every one of them moves cost and quality: 5 chunks of 512 tokens is 2,560 context tokens a query, which at 100,000 queries a day and $3 per million tokens is about $770 a day. When not to: a single, well-structured corpus that one retriever and one prompt already handle. Follow-up: "what do you tune first?" Chunking and retrieval, measured with recall@k on a labelled set (Chapters 25 and 31).	chapter ch::32
[Ch 32] What does DSPy optimise?	The prompt of each module in a pipeline: its instruction text and its few-shot demonstrations, chosen to maximise a metric on labelled examples. MIPROv2 bootstraps demonstrations from successful traces, has an LLM propose instructions, and searches the combinations with Bayesian optimisation on validation minibatches; GEPA instead reflects on traces and edits instructions. A two-module compile is a few thousand LLM calls (about $12 in 32.5's example), and with 300 validation examples at 80% accuracy the noise is ±2.3 points of standard error, so hold out a test set. It does not change model weights. Follow-up: "when is it not worth it?" When you have no metric, or fewer than a few dozen labelled examples.	chapter ch::32
[Ch 32] Your team's RAG service is built on LangChain and debugging is painful. Would you migrate? How would you decide and how would you do it safely?	Measure first: list the incidents and the hours lost to the framework over a quarter, and the features you actually use; if it is three wrappers and a retriever, migration is cheap, and if you rely on many integrations, it is not. Migrate incrementally behind the same interface: rewrite one step at a time as plain functions, run old and new side by side on the evaluation set and in shadow mode on live traffic, compare traces, then switch. Size the comparison: on 500 evaluation questions at 90% correctness, the difference between two independent runs has a standard error of 1.9 points (±3.7 at 95%), and scoring both versions on the same questions shrinks it. When not to migrate: if the pain is missing tracing, add tracing first. Follow-up: "how long?" Days for a simple flow; the shadow period, not the code, sets the timeline.	chapter ch::32
[Ch 32] Design a refund-handling agent with a human approval step. Which tool would you use and how does state persist across the pause?	A LangGraph-style state machine: classify, gather evidence with read-only tools, propose a refund, check it against policy in code, and interrupt before the payment node when the amount or risk is above a threshold. A checkpointer keyed by conversation id stores the state after every step in a durable database, so the run survives restarts and resumes when the approver's decision arrives, with a timeout that escalates stale cases and an audit log of every step. Scale it: at 10,000 refunds a day with 5% needing approval, about 500 paused threads exist at once if approvals take a day, so the checkpoint store, not the model, is the component to size. Follow-up: "what if the order changed while paused?" Re-read the order before the payment node runs; never trust state captured before the pause.	chapter ch::32
[Ch 32] How do you version, test and roll out a prompt change in a framework-based app?	Keep prompts as versioned files, not strings inside library calls; run the evaluation set in CI with thresholds that block a merge; tag every trace with the prompt version; roll out as a canary and keep a one-line rollback (Chapter 47). Be honest about what the canary can detect: to see a resolution rate fall from 80% to 75% with 80% power needs about 1,090 conversations per arm, which is 11 days at 100 canary conversations a day, so the canary guards against crashes and gross failures while the offline evaluation guards quality. Follow-up: "what if the provider updates the model under you?" Pin the model version where the API allows, and rerun the evaluation set on a schedule to catch silent drift.	chapter ch::32
[Ch 32] How would you make framework choice a team decision rather than a personal preference?	Write a short RFC with explicit criteria: the flows the team must support, debuggability, tracing and evaluation integration, deployment fit, licence and dependency risk, and how hard it is to leave. Run a time-boxed spike that builds the same representative flow (for example, RAG with one tool call and an approval) two or three ways and measures lines of code, latency, and time to find a planted bug. Include the platform team's cost: supporting a second stack in tracing and deployment costs real time, and if it takes one engineer-week a month, that is about 23% of an engineer. Decide, record the decision and its review date. Follow-up: "what if a senior engineer disagrees?" Ask which criterion they weigh differently and test that one in the spike.	chapter ch::32
[Ch 32] Constrained decoding or validate-and-retry for structured output?	Constrained decoding masks tokens that would break a JSON schema or grammar at each sampling step, so the output always parses; validate-and-retry samples freely and re-asks on failure. Retrying is cheap when failures are rare: at a 3% failure rate the expected number of calls is 1/0.97 = 1.03. Constrained decoding guarantees syntax, not meaning, and a schema that forces the answer field first stops the model reasoning before it commits, so put a reasoning field first or keep the schema loose. When not to constrain: free-text tasks where the schema is only a wrapper. Follow-up: "where is it implemented?" As a logit mask in the serving engine (vLLM, SGLang, XGrammar, Outlines) or through a provider's structured-output mode (Chapter 21).	chapter ch::32
[Ch 33] What is an LLM agent?	A system where an LLM chooses actions (tool calls) in a loop, observes results, and decides the next step until the task is complete, rather than following a fixed code path. The defining property is that the model controls the control flow, which is also why reliability compounds: ten steps at 95% each give about 60% end to end. Follow-up: "so is a RAG pipeline an agent?" Not if retrieval always happens once in a fixed place; it becomes one when the model decides whether and what to retrieve.	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] How does function calling work?	The model receives tool schemas, outputs a structured call (name and JSON arguments), the application executes it and returns the result as a message, and the model continues. The model never executes code itself. Real APIs can return several calls in one turn, each with an id, and the results go back tagged with those ids. Follow-up: "what do you check before executing?" Validate the arguments against the schema and the policy in code; return errors to the model rather than raising.	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] What is ReAct?	Interleaving reasoning, actions and observations in a loop so the model plans, acts, and adjusts based on real results. The reasoning step decides the next action; the observation can change the plan, which is what lets an agent recover when a lookup fails. Modern tool-calling APIs and reasoning models implement the same loop natively, without the original text format. Follow-up: "why not plan everything up front?" Plans break on surprises; plan-then-execute with re-planning combines both (33.4).	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] Workflow or agent: how do you choose?	Workflow when steps are known and stable (cheaper, predictable, testable); agent when the path depends on what is found and cannot be enumerated. Start simple; add autonomy only where evaluation shows the need. A useful test: if you can draw the flowchart, build the workflow and put LLM calls inside its boxes. Follow-up: "what if 15% of cases don't fit the flowchart?" Route those to an agent or a human, and keep the workflow for the rest (33.12).	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] How do you design good tools for an agent?	Few, distinct tools; clear descriptions with units and limits; strict schemas with enums; concise outputs; actionable errors; idempotency; confirmation for irreversible actions; policy checks inside the tool. Output size is a cost lever: trimming a tool's output from 600 to 400 tokens per step cut the 20-step example in 33.9 from 33,000 to 26,000 billed tokens. Follow-up: "how do you know a description is good?" Watch which tool the model picks on an evaluation set and fix the descriptions where it confuses them.	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] What types of memory do agents use?	Working (context), episodic (past interactions), semantic (durable facts) and procedural (instructions, tools). Each needs rules for writing, expiry, privacy and retrieval. The risk is writing wrong "facts" into long-term memory, which poisons every later session, so write only confirmed facts. Follow-up: "how does memory relate to context engineering?" Files on disk are memory the agent reads on demand, which keeps the working context small (33.13).	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] What is MCP?	An open protocol for exposing tools, resources and prompts from servers to AI applications through a standard interface, replacing per-application integrations; it requires explicit trust, authentication and permission management. It turns an M × N integration problem (M applications, N systems) into M + N. Follow-up: "what is the security risk?" A third-party server's tool descriptions and outputs are untrusted input that can carry injected instructions (tool poisoning).	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] Why do agents fail more in production than in demos?	Errors compound over steps (pⁿ), real inputs are messier, tools fail, contexts grow, and demos are single lucky runs. At 95% per step a 10-step task succeeds 60% of the time, while a demo shows the 60% that worked. Two corrections make the answer senior: recovery raises the effective per-step rate (p = 0.95 with half of errors fixed gives 78% over 10 steps), and an average hides per-task spread (an average of 0.6 can mean half the tasks fail most of the time), so report pass^k and per-task success over repeated runs. Follow-up: "what do you change first?" Verification after risky steps and informative tool errors, because they raise the recovery rate.	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] How do you control an agent's cost?	Budgets per task, caching the stable prefix, trimming tool outputs and history, smaller models for simple steps, parallel calls, fewer steps through better tools, and a hand-off when the budget is reached. The quadratic term dominates: in the 33.9 example caching cuts 194,000 billed tokens to 33,000, and trimming takes it to 26,000. Watch cache expiry during slow tool calls, which can make caching cost more than it saves. Follow-up: "how do you set the budget?" From the value of the task: a 15-euro ticket cannot spend 2 euros of tokens.	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] Design an agent that handles "where is my order?" and refund requests.	Route first: "where is my order" is a workflow (lookup, format the answer), which is most of the traffic and needs no agent. Refunds use a state machine with LLM decisions inside steps: gather evidence with read-only tools, propose an action, validate it with a policy engine in code, auto-approve small amounts, require human approval above a threshold, make payment calls idempotent, keep a full audit log, and run in shadow mode before automating. Put numbers on it: a 12-step cap and €0.10 of tokens per case, with a typical 5-step case at about €0.04 (33.12), and evaluation on 500 replayed tickets. Follow-up: "how do you decide to automate a case type?" When shadow-mode agreement with humans exceeds a target, say 97%, on enough cases to bound the error.	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] A user's message contains instructions to call the refund tool. What stops it?	Refund eligibility and amounts are decided in code against order data, not by the model's reading of the message; caps and human approval for higher amounts; the message is treated as data; monitoring and injection tests. The deeper point is that a prompt-level defence lowers the attack success rate but never to zero, so the design must assume some injections succeed and bound the damage: a successful injection can at most request a capped refund that the policy engine would have allowed anyway. Follow-up: "how do you test it?" A red-team set of injected messages and tool outputs, measuring attack success rate over hundreds of attempts.	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] When would you use multiple agents?	When sub-tasks are independent and benefit from parallelism, need different tools or permissions, or exceed a single context; otherwise one agent with good tools is simpler, cheaper and easier to evaluate. Put a number on the cost: Anthropic reported that its multi-agent research system used about 15 times the tokens of a chat interaction, against about 4 times for a single agent, and that token usage alone explained most of the variance in performance on its browsing evaluation. So multi-agent pays when the task is valuable and parallelisable (broad research); it loses when sub-tasks depend on each other (most coding), where errors pass between agents and context is lost at every hand-off. A middle ground is one agent that calls sub-agents as tools for isolated searches. Follow-up: "how do you evaluate it?" End-to-end task success and cost per task against a single-agent baseline at matched token budget.	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] How do you take an agent from prototype to production?	Define success and budgets; build a sandbox and evaluation set from real cases; measure task success over repeated runs; add guardrails and audit logs; shadow mode against humans; gradual automation by risk tier; monitoring of success, cost, escalations and overrides; a rollback path. Size the evaluation: on 500 replayed cases at 90% success the standard error is 1.3 points, enough to see a 5-point regression, while 50 demo cases (±4.2 points) are not. Follow-up: "what is the first production metric you watch?" The human override rate by case type, because it moves before customer complaints do.	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] How does RL for multi-turn agents differ from single-turn RLVR?	Trajectories interleave generated actions with environment observations, so the loss is masked to the policy's own tokens (often only about 10% of a coding trajectory); the reward usually arrives only at the end, so credit assignment across 30–50 turns is noisy and calls for large groups, curricula or turn-level checks; rollout durations are heavy-tailed, so synchronous batches leave workers idle (about 14% utilisation in the 33.11 example) and teams move to asynchronous RL with bounded staleness; and environment throughput, not GPU time, often limits training. Reward hacking is the main risk: the verifier must grade in a clean sandbox with hidden tests. Follow-up: "how do you know the agent is hacking?" Training reward rising while held-out pass rate under a stricter grader stays flat.	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] How would you design an agent that reads untrusted content and still uses private data safely?	Start from the threat model: an agent that has access to private data, reads untrusted content (web pages, emails, tool outputs) and can communicate externally has all three parts of what Simon Willison called the "lethal trifecta", and an injection can make it exfiltrate data. Remove one leg where possible (no external communication from sessions that read untrusted content). Otherwise use an architecture, not a filter: the dual-LLM pattern, where a privileged model plans from the trusted request and a quarantined model reads untrusted text but cannot call tools, or CaMeL-style designs that also track where every value came from and enforce policies on tool arguments. Add the instruction hierarchy (system above user above tool output) and measure attack success rate on a benchmark such as AgentDojo: at 1,000 attempts, a 1% rate has a 95% interval of about ±0.6 points. Follow-up: "what does it cost?" Some utility, since the planner cannot react to content it never sees.	chapter ch::33
[Ch 33] Your coding agent degrades after an hour of work. What do you change?	Look at the context first: at hour one it is likely tens of thousands of tokens of stale tool output, which costs money on every step and dilutes attention. Clear old tool results and compact the history into a structured summary when the context passes a threshold, keep durable notes (progress, decisions, failed approaches) in files the agent can reread, and delegate search-heavy sub-tasks to sub-agents that return short reports. Do the edits in batches: in the 33.13 simulation, clearing at every step costs 1.33M token-equivalents against 0.74M for clearing at a threshold, because every edit invalidates the cache. Follow-up: "how do you know the summary is good?" Check for repeated work and forgotten user constraints after compaction, across repeated runs.	chapter ch::33
[Ch 34] How do you decide what to work on?	Impact, tractability, my edge and timeliness; a one-page proposal with a falsifiable hypothesis and the cheapest experiment that could kill it; time-boxed exploration with explicit continue/pivot/kill decisions. Impact is a product of how much the result matters and how likely it is to hold: an exciting optimiser claim with a low survival rate can be worth less than a modest data result the team will act on. When not to follow the framework: when a direction is cheap to try and the downside is a day, just run the experiment. Follow-up: "give an example of something you killed." Name the kill criterion and the experiment that triggered it (34.2's worked choice).	chapter ch::34
[Ch 34] A paper claims a 2-point gain. How do you assess it?	First, is 2 points larger than the noise? On a 500-question benchmark at 70% accuracy, one model's score has a standard error of √(0.7 × 0.3 / 500) = 2.0 points, so the difference between two independently evaluated models has a standard error near 2.9 points, and a 2-point gain is not distinguishable from noise unless the paper reports a paired comparison, several seeds or a larger set. Then check whether baselines were re-run and tuned with the same budget (a per-method learning-rate sweep), compute and data matched, results consistent across scales and tasks, the evaluation sound (contamination, judges), and whether the reported number is the best of several runs. Then try to reproduce the smallest setting. Follow-up: "what if they report 5 seeds?" Then ask for the interval and whether the seeds were paired across methods.	chapter ch::34
[Ch 34] Your change improves the score by 0.3 points on one seed. What next?	Estimate seed variance on the baseline first: if the seed SD is 0.4, a single comparison favours the new run 70% of the time even with this real gain, and 50% of the time with no gain. To detect 0.3 with 80% power at p < 0.05 needs n = 2(1.645 + 0.84)² × 0.4² / 0.3² ≈ 22 seeds per arm (23 with the exact t-test), so either reduce the noise (continuous metric, larger or paired evaluation) or test at a scale where the effect is larger. Do not claim anything until the interval excludes zero. When not to run more seeds: when even the optimistic end of the interval is too small to change the decision. Follow-up: "what if seeds are expensive?" Pair the seeds across arms and analyse differences.	chapter ch::34
[Ch 34] Why do one-at-a-time ablations mislead?	They miss interactions: a component that helps only in combination with another looks useless when added alone. In 34.5, B adds nothing to the baseline (−0.25 on one run, +0.03 in the factorial) but a full point when A is present. Use leave-one-out from the full system or factorial designs; with k components a full factorial costs 2^k cells, so use it for two to four components and leave-one-out beyond that. Follow-up: "how do you report interactions?" A 2 × 2 table of cell means with intervals.	chapter ch::34
[Ch 34] What is the winner's curse in hyperparameter sweeps?	The best observed configuration's score is biased upwards by noise, and re-running it gives a lower number. Its size is the expected maximum of k noise draws: about 1.63σ for 12 equally good configurations, so with σ = 0.5 the winner looks 0.81 points better than it is; for large k it grows like σ√(2 ln k). Confirm with fresh seeds and report the whole sweep. Follow-up: "does it matter if configurations really differ?" The bias shrinks but stays positive, so the fix is the same.	chapter ch::34
[Ch 34] How would you budget compute for testing a new idea?	A small-scale ladder with a few learning rates and seeds establishes the effect and its trend; a confirmation at the target scale follows only if the trend holds. Concretely, in 34.7: four sizes from 125M to 1.4B at 20 tokens per parameter, three learning rates and two seeds each, cost 1.9 × 10²¹ FLOPs or about 1,350 GPU-hours at 40% MFU on an H100, against 4,130 for one 7B confirmation run, so the ladder is 25% of the budget. When not to scale up: when the effect shrinks across the ladder, or when even the optimistic extrapolation is too small to change a decision. Follow-up: "how confident is the extrapolation?" Bootstrap the fit and report the interval (34.12).	chapter ch::34
[Ch 34] How do you make your experiments reproducible?	Config files, tracked code and data versions, seeds, automatic logging of all metrics and artefacts, versioned evaluation code, and documented variance where bit-exact determinism is impractical. A test that the setup works: two runs with the same seed give identical numbers (the check script's toy does), and the seed-to-seed spread is measured and reported. Follow-up: "what if the GPU kernels are non-deterministic?" Accept it, measure the variance it adds, and size the seed count accordingly.	chapter ch::34
[Ch 34] Describe a research result you got wrong, and what you changed afterwards.	(Behavioural, see Chapter 38.) Strong answers name a specific methodological lesson: added seeds, re-tuned baselines, changed evaluation. A typical strong story: a 0.4-point gain from one seed that vanished at five seeds, after which the speaker measured the noise floor before every study and pre-registered the seed count. Follow-up: "how did you find out?" Say who noticed and what check revealed it.	chapter ch::34
[Ch 34] Your 125M model scores at chance on the benchmark you care about. How do you run the ablation?	Use a metric that moves at this scale: held-out loss or bits per byte on domain text, or the probability assigned to the correct answer in a cloze format. At chance, a 4-option benchmark with 1,000 items has a standard error of 1.4 points, so 26% versus 27% is noise. Validate the proxy where the benchmark is above chance, and check that the effect keeps its sign across two or three ladder sizes before trusting the extrapolation. Follow-up: "what if the proxy and the benchmark disagree at the largest size?" Trust the benchmark there, and report the disagreement as a limit of the proxy.	chapter ch::34
[Ch 34] You ran 20 ablations and one is significant at p = 0.03. Do you believe it?	Not yet: with 20 comparisons and no real effects, the chance of at least one p < 0.05 is 1 − 0.95²⁰ = 64%, and a Bonferroni threshold would be 0.0025. Re-run that comparison with fresh seeds as a confirmation, and report all 20 alongside it. When a correction is too conservative for an exploratory grid, control the false discovery rate instead. Follow-up: "what if the effect is large?" A large effect with a modest p-value is usually a noisy estimate; the confirmation run will say.	chapter ch::34
[Ch 35] Why does a stable softmax subtract the maximum logit, and does it change the answer?	Softmax is invariant to adding a constant to every logit, because the factor e^(−m) cancels between numerator and denominator, so subtracting the max m changes nothing mathematically. It keeps the largest exponent at e⁰ = 1, so nothing overflows: e^89 already overflows fp32 (max about 3.4 × 10³⁸) and e^12 overflows fp16 (max 65,504). The same trick gives log-sum-exp, lse(x) = m + log Σ e^(xᵢ − m), which is what a stable cross-entropy uses. Follow-up: "and for log σ(x)?" Use F.logsigmoid; in fp16 the naive −log σ(−20) is infinite.	chapter ch::35
[Ch 35] In multi-head attention with a KV cache, what are the shapes at decode step t?	With batch B, H query heads, H_kv KV heads and head size d: the new query is (B, H, 1, d); the cache holds K and V of (B, H_kv, t, d) each; after repeating KV heads they are (B, H, t, d); the scores are (B, H, 1, t) and the output (B, 1, H·d) before the output projection. For B = 1, H = 32, H_kv = 8, d = 128 at t = 1,000, the scores are (1, 32, 1, 1000). No causal mask is needed at decode, because the single new token may see every cached position. Follow-up: "what does the cache cost?" 2·L·H_kv·d·T·bytes, 4.29 GB at 32k tokens for an 8B-class model in bf16 (35.12).	chapter ch::35
[Ch 35] Your top-p sampler is called with p = 1. What should happen, and what can go wrong?	Every token should stay eligible, so sampling equals plain temperature sampling. The usual implementation keeps a token if the mass before it is below p; in floating point the cumulative sum reaches exactly 1.0 before the tail, so tiny-probability tokens are dropped (for logits [10, 0, −20, −30] only two survive). That is harmless for quality but breaks tests that compare with unfiltered sampling, so short-circuit p ≥ 1. Follow-up: "and p = 0?" Keep at least the top token, otherwise every logit becomes −∞ and the softmax returns NaN.	chapter ch::35
[Ch 35] How do you test ML code you just wrote in an interview?	Three layers: a hand example with a known answer (AUC of perfectly separated scores is 1.0); a comparison with a reference or brute-force version on random inputs, including edge cases such as ties, padding and a single class; and an invariant (probabilities sum to 1, a causal model's earlier outputs do not change when a later token does). In 35.10 the brute-force pairwise AUC and scikit-learn agree to 10⁻¹². When not to: do not write a test framework; three assertions in the last five minutes are enough. Follow-up: "what would you add in production?" Property-based tests and a regression test for every bug you fixed.	chapter ch::35
[Ch 35] A language model's training loss drops to 0.00 within a few hundred steps. What do you check?	A loss far below the data's entropy is a leak until proven otherwise: the model can see what it predicts. Check the target shift first (targets must be inputs shifted by one), then the causal mask with a perturbation test (change token t + 1; logits at positions ≤ t must not move), then whether training and validation windows overlap. In 35.11 an unshifted target gives 0.000 and a reversed mask 0.096, against an honest floor of 2.39 nats. Do not start by tuning the learning rate: a falling loss is not evidence of learning. Follow-up: "and if the loss never moves?" Overfit one batch; if that fails, look for a missing zero_grad, a detached graph, a softmax before the loss or a learning rate of zero.	chapter ch::35
