[Ch 35] Your GRPO code computes the advantage as rewards - rewards.mean(). What is wrong, and when is it right?	The baseline must be the mean of each prompt's own group, not of the whole batch; the code is only right when the batch holds one prompt. With an easy prompt solved 4/4 and a hard one solved 1/4, the batch mean of 0.625 gives every easy response +0.375, which rewards the model for having been given an easy prompt, and the hard prompt's one success gets +0.375 instead of +0.75. Reshape rewards to (prompts, G) and subtract the row means. Follow-ups: whether to divide by the group std (original GRPO does; Dr. GRPO does not, because it over-weights nearly solved and nearly failed prompts), how to aggregate tokens (DAPO's batch token mean versus per-response means), and where the KL goes (in the loss, often dropped for verifiable rewards).	chapter ch::35
[Ch 35] What does grouped-query attention change relative to multi-head attention, and what does it cost?	Query heads keep their own projections, but groups of H/H_kv query heads share one key and value head, so the K and V projections and the KV cache shrink by H/H_kv. With 32 query heads and 8 KV heads the cache for a 32k-token sequence falls from 17.2 GB to 4.29 GB in bf16, and decode, which is bound by reading the cache, can serve up to about 4× more sequences per GPU at long context. The cost is a small quality loss, usually recovered by training with GQA from the start or uptraining an MHA checkpoint. When not to bother: with short contexts and small batches the cache is not the bottleneck. Follow-up: "how would you avoid copying K and V for each group?" Reshape q to (B, H_kv, rep, T, d) and broadcast.	chapter ch::35
[Ch 35] Fifteen minutes are left and your beam search returns wrong sequences. What do you do?	Say so, then shrink the problem: run beam size 1, which must equal greedy decoding, and print the beams after each step on a two-step hand example such as the table in 35.9 (greedy 0.175, beam 0.36). Most bugs are in the handling of finished beams (they must be carried forward unchanged) or in sorting the wrong way. If it still fails with five minutes left, stop editing, state the bug you suspect and the test that would confirm it: a clear diagnosis scores better than a silent last-minute edit. Follow-up: "how do you handle length bias?" Divide each score by len^α with α around 0.6–1.	chapter ch::35
[Ch 35] In a round where an AI assistant is allowed, how do you show your own skill?	Do the thinking the assistant cannot see: clarify, plan aloud, and give it small, well-specified pieces. Then read every generated line as if reviewing a colleague's code, and verify with a hand example and a reference test before moving on. A useful number to say: if each of five generated pieces is right 90% of the time, all five are right only 0.9⁵ = 59% of the time, so verification is the job. When not to use it: short functions you can write and explain faster yourself. Follow-up: "what if the assistant's code passes your test but you don't understand it?" Rewrite it or explain it line by line; never submit code you cannot defend.	chapter ch::35
[Ch 36] Design an experiment to test whether a data filter improves downstream reasoning.	Clarify the decision (include the filter in the next run or not) and the minimum gain that would justify the tokens it removes. State the hypotheses, including the boring ones: more tokens of anything, contamination, a style change. Then run matched-token anneals or small pretraining runs with and without the filter, plus a control filter that removes the same share of data at random or by an unrelated score. Use two scales and three seeds; evaluate reasoning, a regression suite and per-domain loss; state the decision rules in advance. Put a number on the noise: on a 3,000-item reasoning aggregate at 60%, two independent scores differ with a standard error of 1.3 points, so a 1-point effect needs paired evaluation. What I would not do: compare one filtered run with last quarter's baseline. Follow-up: "the filter helps at 400M but not 1.4B?" Run the third size before deciding; a shrinking effect is the most common outcome.	chapter ch::36
[Ch 36] Walk me through your most important paper as if I were a sceptical reviewer.	Problem, idea, strongest evidence with error bars, what you tried that failed, limitations, what you would do next, and your specific contribution. Lead with the weakest point before they find it ("our main result is at one scale; here is why I still believe it"), and know the numbers cold: the effect, its interval, the number of seeds, and the compute. When not to defend: if they find a real flaw, agree, say how you would fix it, and estimate how much it would change the result. Follow-up: "what would you do with ten times the compute?" Name the one experiment it buys and the result that would change your conclusion.	chapter ch::36
[Ch 36] Critique this paper's main table.	Ask whether baselines were re-run or copied, and tuned with the same budget; whether compute and data were matched; whether there are seeds and intervals; whether the gains are consistent across tasks and scales; whether contamination was checked; and which ablation is missing. Then quantify: a 2-point gain on a 500-item benchmark at 70% is within one standard error of a difference of two independent scores (2.9 points), so ask whether the evaluation was paired or repeated. End with the single experiment that would most change your belief. Follow-up: "anything good about it?" Name the strongest result and why it is convincing; a critique that finds nothing good reads as reflexive.	chapter ch::36
[Ch 36] You have one week and 64 GPUs to test an idea. How do you plan?	Start with the budget: 64 × 168 = 10,752 GPU-hours. With the numbers of Chapter 34.7, a four-size ladder from 125M to 1.4B with three learning rates and two seeds costs about 1,350 GPU-hours (about 21 hours on 64 GPUs if the runs pack well), and one 7B, 140B-token confirmation costs about 4,130 GPU-hours (about 65 hours). Both fit, using half the week's compute, which leaves room for reruns. Plan: day 1, a proposal with a kill criterion and the smallest experiment; days 2–3, the ladder with baselines and seeds; days 3–6, the 7B run only if the ladder's trend justifies it; day 7, analysis and memo. What I would not do: start the 7B run on day 1, or skip the baseline seeds. Follow-up: "the ladder is ambiguous on day 3?" Spend the remaining compute on more seeds at the two cheapest sizes rather than the 7B run.	chapter ch::36
[Ch 36] What would make you abandon a research direction?	A pre-stated kill criterion met; effects that vanish with better baselines or more seeds; scaling trends that flatten; or a cheaper method achieving the same result. Make the criterion numeric in advance: "stop if the 125M difference is below one seed SD and the 350M difference is smaller still". Distinguish a dead idea from a bad test: before killing, check that the experiment could have detected the effect (its power), since an underpowered null is not evidence of absence. Follow-up: "have you ever kept going past a kill criterion?" A good answer names a case and the new evidence that justified it.	chapter ch::36
[Ch 36] What would you work on in your first six months here?	Give a thesis, the problem it implies, why this lab is the place to solve it, a month-by-month plan with a cheap kill test in months 2–3, and what would change your mind (36.9). Tie it to the lab's assets and recent work, and give one number that drives a decision, such as the compute for the first kill test or the effect size that would justify scaling. What not to do: list five fashionable topics. Follow-up: "why hasn't someone already done this?" Say what is new (data, scale, a tool) or admit it may be in progress and say how your angle differs.	chapter ch::36
[Ch 36] How would you design a verifier for RL on a coding task so the policy cannot hack it?	Grade in a fresh sandbox: apply only the agent's diff to the non-test files of a clean checkout, restore the original tests, and run hidden tests the agent never saw; remove git history and network access so answers cannot leak; flag diffs that touch tests or hard-code outputs; and monitor the gap between training reward and the pass rate on a held-out set under a stricter grader. Treat the verifier as an adversarial target: before training, run a red-team policy against it. Follow-up: "how do you know it worked?" The training-reward versus held-out-pass-rate gap stays flat, and sampled high-reward trajectories read as genuine solutions (Chapter 33.11).	chapter ch::36
[Ch 36] Best-of-N sampling or longer thinking: how would you decide where to spend inference compute?	Compare them at matched compute on full curves, not single points, and per difficulty bucket. Best-of-N needs a verifier and is bounded by its quality: with a perfect verifier and a 30% per-sample success rate, best-of-8 solves 94% of problems, while majority voting helps much less when the correct answer is rarely the most common one. Longer thinking needs no verifier but saturates, and costs latency because it is sequential. Typically, longer thinking helps more on medium problems and sampling with a good verifier on hard ones. Follow-up: "what if the verifier is a learned reward model?" Then best-of-N over-optimises it as N grows, so plot the true score against N and stop before it turns down (Chapters 13.9 and 18.5).	chapter ch::36
[Ch 36] How would you test whether a model behaves differently when it thinks it is being evaluated?	Build pairs of prompts that differ only in evaluation cues and compare behaviour pairwise. With 500 pairs, if 40 behave safely only in the evaluation-styled version and 15 only in the deployment-styled one, McNemar's statistic is (40 − 15)² / 55 = 11.4, p < 0.001. Add reading of reasoning traces for verbalised awareness, and test for sandbagging by checking whether capability rises under stronger elicitation such as light fine-tuning (Chapter 27.7). Follow-up: "what if the model hides it?" Use realistic deployment-like settings and interpretability probes, and treat behavioural tests as a lower bound.	chapter ch::36
[Ch 38] Tell me about yourself.	Not a story but a 90-second map: now (your role and two or three kinds of problems), before (the path, in two sentences), the thread that connects them, and why this role is the natural next step. Include one number that signals level, such as "an evaluation harness three teams now use", and name at most three projects so the interviewer can choose one to probe. Weak answers recite the CV in order for five minutes. Follow-up you will get: "Tell me more about X", so every project you mention needs a full STAR story behind it.	chapter ch::38
[Ch 38] Tell me about a result that turned out to be wrong.	It tests rigour and honesty. Choose a real case where you found the error, or someone found it in your work; say how wrong it was in numbers, how fast and how openly you corrected it, and which process you changed (seeds, baselines, harness tests). Section 38.12's example: a +1.1-point single-seed gain became +0.3, interval −0.4 to +1.0, once seed noise (σ = 0.6 points) was measured; the standard error of a one-seed comparison was 0.85. Avoid trivial errors and errors that were someone else's. Follow-up: "Why didn't you catch it earlier?" Answer honestly, then say what you check first now.	chapter ch::38
[Ch 38] Tell me about a failure.	A real failure with a consequence, told in five parts: what happened, your part in it, what you did to fix it, what you do differently, and evidence that you did it differently the next time. The evidence is what makes it credible: in 38.12's example, a regression test that later caught a tokeniser change before results went out. Quantify the damage (2.4 of a reported 3 points came from the candidate's own bug) and the speed of disclosure. Avoid fake failures ("I care too much") and stories that blame others. Follow-up: "How did the people affected react?" Say what they said, including criticism.	chapter ch::38
[Ch 38] Why this lab, and not another?	Two or three specifics from the lab's own published work (papers, policies, model documentation, research agenda), why each matters to you, and how your skills fit a team there. Generic praise of "safety" or "scale" sounds unprepared, because every lab hears it. If your motivation is mainly the research problems, say so honestly and say how you think about the responsibility that comes with them. Follow-up: "What do you disagree with in our approach?" Have one considered disagreement with a reason, and one thing you find convincing.	chapter ch::38
[Ch 38] How do you think about the risks of the work you'd do here?	Name a concrete risk tied to your own role, not to AI in general: for an evaluation researcher, understating a dangerous capability because the elicitation was weak; for a post-training researcher, reward hacking that carries into deployment. Say how you would detect it (a stronger elicitation, held-out environments, reading high-reward samples) and where you are uncertain. Trade-off worth naming: caution costs speed, and you should say how you would judge which matters more in a given case. Follow-up: "What would make you raise an alarm?" A specific trigger and the channel you would use.	chapter ch::38
[Ch 38] Tell me about a time you raised a safety, ethics or integrity concern.	It tests courage and proportion: neither silence nor alarm. Structure: what you noticed, the evidence you gathered, whom you told and how fast, the fix you proposed, and what changed. Section 38.11's example: about 2% of a fine-tuning set still contained personal data; the candidate paused the pipeline, sent ten examples and a count to the owners, proposed a fix, and the run moved by four days. If you have no such story, say so and describe how you would act, but most people have a small one (data use, a misleading result, a shortcut in a review). Follow-up: "What if your manager had said ship anyway?" Escalate through the proper channel, with the evidence.	chapter ch::38
[Ch 38] Tell me about a time you disagreed with a more senior colleague.	Choose a disagreement about substance, settled by evidence rather than rank. Show that you understood their view, proposed a test both sides accepted, and committed to the outcome, especially if it went against you. Section 38.9 is a worked example: agreeing the evaluation first turned an argument into a hybrid that cost 15% more to serve instead of 100%. Weak answers end with "and I was right". Follow-up: "What would they say about you?" A fair account, including where they would criticise you.	chapter ch::38
[Ch 38] What is your biggest weakness?	A real weakness in how you do the job, not in how you interview, with what you are doing about it and evidence it is working. For a scientist, a good one is over-investing in rigour before checking whether the result matters; the fix is writing down which decision a result informs before starting, which in 38.6's example cut a three-week study to one. Avoid strengths in disguise and weaknesses that disqualify you for the role. Follow-up: "How would your manager describe it?" The same way, which is why it has to be real.	chapter ch::38
[Ch 38] Tell me about a project you stopped.	It tests judgement and compute stewardship. Give the kill criterion you set (ideally before starting), the evidence that met it, the cost you avoided, and what you salvaged: a dataset, a negative result others used, a tool. Example shape: "After two weeks the method gave +0.2 points at 400M, interval −0.3 to +0.7, against the +1 point we needed to justify a large run, so I stopped and wrote a two-page note; the data loader I built is now the team default." Follow-up: "Was it the right call in hindsight?" Say what you learnt later, even if it complicates the story.	chapter ch::38
[Ch 38] Tell me about a time your impact reached beyond your own work.	The question mid-level candidates find hardest. Use a mid-sized story told with senior behaviours (38.10): something you built that others adopted, a methodological catch that changed a process, or an ambiguous request turned into a decision. Give adoption in numbers (three teams, 40 runs a week), the people you persuaded and how, and the trade-off you chose. Do not inflate the scope; precision about what you owned is itself a senior signal. Follow-up: "What would have happened without you?" A concrete counterfactual: three teams still reporting scores 4 points apart.	chapter ch::38
[Ch 39] Tell me about a time you decided not to use ML.	Behavioural: use STAR (Chapter 38) and end with what you learned. A strong story names the simpler alternative, the number that made it good enough, and the trigger that would bring ML back. Example shape: "Three rules caught 85% of the cases at 99% precision. A model might have added a few points of recall, but it needed labels, a pipeline, monitoring and retraining. I shipped the rules, and wrote down that if volume doubled or recall fell below 80% we would build the model." Weak answers make the decision sound like laziness or luck. Follow-up: "Did you ever revisit it?" Say what the trigger showed, or that it never fired.	chapter ch::39
[Ch 39] Tell me about a trade-off you made between speed and quality. Would you make it again?	Behavioural: STAR, with both sides of the trade-off quantified (shipped three weeks earlier; 4 points lower accuracy on the long tail), who agreed to it, and how you limited the downside (a flag, a follow-up ticket with a date, monitoring of the weak segment). The second question is the real one: answer it honestly, and "no, I'd do it differently, because…" is a good answer if you can say what you learned. Follow-up: "Who paid the cost of the lower quality?" Name them and say what you did for them.	chapter ch::39
[Ch 39] Your manager asks for a model to predict which restaurants will churn, by next week. What do you do first?	Clarify the decision before the model: who acts on the prediction, how, and with what capacity. If account managers can call 200 restaurants a month, and with 10,000 active restaurants and 2% monthly churn about 200 churn each month, what matters is precision in the top 200, not AUC. Define churn (no orders for 28 days, say), then build a baseline this week: flag restaurants whose orders fell by more than half against the previous four weeks, and measure its precision on last quarter's data. Deliver that with its number and a plan, not a deep model. The senior point: prediction is useless unless calling helps, so propose a holdout in which half the flagged restaurants are not called, to measure whether the intervention reduces churn. Follow-up: "And if the rule is good enough?" Ship the rule and spend the time on the intervention.	chapter ch::39
[Ch 39] You disagree with a product manager about launching a model whose offline metrics look good but whose A/B result is flat. How do you handle it?	Trust the online result over offline metrics, but first check that "flat" is informative. With a 10% baseline conversion rate and 200,000 users per arm, the standard error of the difference is about 0.095 points and the minimum detectable effect at 80% power about 0.27 points, 2.7% relative; if the offline gain predicts +0.5% relative, the test could never have seen it. Then look for reasons (metric mismatch, novelty, opposing segments) and propose an option: a longer or targeted test, or a launch behind guardrails if the change is cheap to reverse. Whose decision it is matters: usually the PM owns the launch, and your job is to make sure it is informed. State your view once, in writing, with the numbers; if the decision goes the other way, commit and help measure it, and escalate only if users are put at risk or a guardrail is broken. Follow-up: "And if the PM wants to ship anyway?" If it is reversible and the guardrails hold, that is a reasonable call; say so.	chapter ch::39
[Ch 39] How do you explain a 95% confidence interval to a non-technical stakeholder?	The strict definition: if we repeated the test many times, intervals built this way would contain the true effect 95% of the time; it is not a 95% probability that this particular interval contains it (Chapter 37, question 9). For a stakeholder: "The test shows +1.2% orders; the effect is very likely between +0.3% and +2.1%. We're confident it's positive; the size is less certain." That plain reading is the approximate Bayesian one with a flat prior. It is close enough for a large A/B test with no strong prior information, and it is what the stakeholder needs to decide, but an interviewer may probe the distinction, so name it. When not to use it: small samples, or when past tests say effects this large are rare; then the winner's curse makes the flat-prior reading overconfident and the estimate should be shrunk. Follow-up: "So what's the probability it's positive?" Only a Bayesian analysis with a stated prior gives that.	chapter ch::39
[Ch 39] What's something you changed your mind about recently, technically?	It tests intellectual honesty and whether you update on evidence. Structure: what you believed and why it was reasonable, the evidence that changed it, and what you now do differently. Example: "I used to treat RL on verifiable rewards as the main lever for reasoning, because pass@1 kept rising. Then I compared pass@k curves: in a toy run pass@1 rose from 0.34 to 0.47 while pass@64 fell from 0.91 to 0.76, because a rare strategy was squeezed out (Chapter 19.6). Now I track pass@k and entropy in every RL run, and I treat the base model's coverage as a ceiling unless exploration is kept alive." Weak answers name a trivial change or none. Follow-up: "What would change your mind back?" Name the evidence, such as RL solving problems the base model never solves at large k.	chapter ch::39
[Ch 39] Your lead asks you to test method X so it can go into the next pretraining run, which is frozen in three weeks. How do you plan it?	Start from the decision and the bar, not the experiment: agree what result adopts X (for example a compute multiplier of at least 1.1× at the largest scale tested, holding with scale) and the default if unclear (keep the recipe). Then size the options: with 16 H100s, about 8,000 GPU-hours, a 150M/400M/1B ladder with per-arm learning-rate sweeps and 3 seeds costs about 750 GPU-hours, and one 3B run per arm another 1,500. Recommend the ladder first and the 3B pair only if 1B clears the bar, which is the kill criterion. Reject a gain that shrinks with scale even if it is positive everywhere (39.10). Follow-up: "Why tune X separately?" An untuned new method against a tuned baseline is the commonest false negative.	chapter ch::39
[Ch 39] A large pretraining run's loss spikes on day 12 of 30 and settles 0.03 above trend. What do you do?	Price both sides first: on 2,048 GPUs a 4-hour rollback costs about 8,200 GPU-hours (about $16,000 at $2 an hour), while the remaining 18 days are about 885,000 GPU-hours. Then check the hypotheses in order of cost: the data at the spike step, per-rank faults, gradient-norm and logit history, recent changes, all minutes from logs. Roll back and skip the batch or cordon the node if one of those explains it; apply a stability fix before continuing if logits were growing; otherwise continue with a pre-set threshold for rolling back. Tell the run owner within the hour, with a recommendation (39.11). Follow-up: "When would you just push through?" When the gap closes within a day and no cause points to lasting damage.	chapter ch::39
[Ch 40] How does a decision tree choose a split?	For each feature and threshold, it computes the weighted impurity (Gini or entropy for classification, variance for regression) of the two children and picks the split that reduces impurity most. Greedy, one level at a time. Number: a 5/5 node has Gini 0.5; a split into 4/1 and 1/4 leaves weighted Gini 0.32, a decrease of 0.18. Follow-up: "how do you make it fast?" Sort each feature once and sweep thresholds while updating class counts, O(n log n) per feature, or bin features into histograms as LightGBM does.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] Random forest vs gradient boosting?	Forest: deep trees on bootstrap samples with random feature subsets, averaged in parallel, reducing variance; robust with little tuning. Boosting: shallow trees added sequentially to fit the loss gradient, reducing bias; usually more accurate when tuned, more sensitive to hyperparameters. In numbers: averaging B trees with correlation ρ leaves variance ρσ² + (1 − ρ)σ²/B, so with ρ = 0.5 a forest can never get below half a single tree's variance, and lowering ρ matters more than adding trees. When not to boost: very noisy labels, very small data with leaf-wise growth, or a need to extrapolate. Follow-up: "can a forest overfit by adding trees?" No; more trees only reduce the second term. It overfits through tree depth and leaf size.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] Why is logistic regression a linear model if the sigmoid is non-linear?	The decision boundary is linear: the log-odds are a linear function of the features; the sigmoid only maps them to probabilities. The boundary p = 0.5 is the hyperplane w·x + b = 0, and any other threshold t is the parallel hyperplane w·x + b = log(t/(1 − t)). Follow-up: "how do you get a non-linear boundary?" Add features (interactions, splines, kernels); the model stays linear in its parameters, so the loss stays convex.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] What does k control in kNN?	Small k: flexible, noisy boundaries (high variance). Large k: smoother, possibly underfit (high bias). Choose by validation. At k = 1 the training error is zero (each point is its own neighbour), which is the clearest sign that training error is useless for choosing k. Follow-up: "what breaks in high dimensions?" Distances concentrate, so the nearest and farthest neighbours are almost equally far; use a learned embedding and an ANN index (Chapter 30.4).	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] What is PCA and when would you use it?	Projection onto the orthogonal directions of maximum variance, computed by SVD of centred data. For compression, visualisation, denoising and decorrelating features for linear models. Number: covariance [[2, 1], [1, 2]] has eigenvalues 3 and 1, so the first component (1, 1)/√2 explains 75%. When not to: before tree models, or when the predictive signal lies in a low-variance direction. Follow-up: "why centre?" Without centring, the first component points at the mean, not along the spread.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] Why does a smaller learning rate in boosting often generalise better?	Each tree contributes a small correction, so the ensemble approaches the fit gradually, acting like regularisation (shrinkage); combined with early stopping, it finds a better point at the cost of more trees. In the chapter's figure, learning rate 0.5 bottoms out at RMSE 1.57 after 73 trees; 0.1 reaches 1.30 after about 470. Trade-off: training and inference time grow roughly in proportion to the number of trees. Follow-up: "why not tune the number of trees with a fixed rate?" The optimal count varies by more than ten times with the rate, so let early stopping pick it.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] How do XGBoost, LightGBM and CatBoost differ?	XGBoost: second-order split finding with a regularised objective and native missing-value handling. LightGBM: histogram binning, leaf-wise growth and sampling tricks (GOSS, feature bundling) for speed. CatBoost: ordered target statistics for categoricals and symmetric (oblivious) trees, strong defaults. Trade-off: leaf-wise growth reaches a lower loss with the same number of leaves but overfits small data unless you cap leaves; symmetric trees are fast at inference and regularise, but each is less expressive. Follow-up: "why does CatBoost order its target statistics?" Encoding a category by its mean target over the same rows leaks the label; computing it only from rows earlier in a random order removes the leak.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] What is the kernel trick?	Replacing dot products with a kernel function lets a linear algorithm (SVM) learn non-linear boundaries as if features were mapped to a high-dimensional space, without computing that mapping. It works because the SVM dual depends on the data only through xᵢ·xⱼ. Number: (x·z)² for x = (1, 2), z = (3, 1) is 25, equal to φ(x)·φ(z) with φ = (x₁², √2x₁x₂, x₂²). Trade-off: the Gram matrix is n × n, so kernel SVMs stall beyond tens of thousands of rows. Follow-up: "what makes a function a valid kernel?" Mercer: symmetric, with a positive semi-definite Gram matrix on every finite set of points.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] Why is naive Bayes "naive", and why does it still work?	It assumes features are independent given the class. Classification under zero-one loss only needs the argmax right, not the probabilities, and Domingos and Pazzani (1997) showed naive Bayes can be optimal under zero-one loss even when independence is badly violated, as long as the violations do not flip the ranking of classes. What breaks is calibration: correlated features are double-counted. Number: a feature with likelihood ratio 3 gives posterior 0.75 from a 50/50 prior; three copies of it give 0.964. When it fails: strongly redundant features whose double-counting overwhelms the other evidence, or when you need probabilities. Follow-up: "how would you fix the probabilities?" Calibrate on held-out data, or use logistic regression on the same features, which learns to discount correlated evidence.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] How do you choose k in k-means?	Elbow of within-cluster error, silhouette score, stability across runs, and above all usefulness of the clusters for the purpose. Use k-means++ initialisation and several restarts. Trade-off: within-cluster error always falls as k grows (at k = n it is zero), so it cannot choose k alone. Follow-up: "what if the clusters are elongated or of different sizes?" Use a Gaussian mixture with full covariances, choosing the number of components by BIC, or HDBSCAN.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] How do trees handle missing values and categorical features?	CART needs imputation, but XGBoost and LightGBM learn a default direction for missing values at each split (they try sending the missing rows left and right and keep the higher gain); LightGBM and CatBoost handle categoricals natively (CatBoost with ordered target statistics, avoiding target leakage). Trade-off: a learned default direction treats "missing" as informative, which is right when missingness carries signal and wrong when it is a logging bug that changes at serving time. Follow-up: "one-hot or target encoding for a 50k-level category?" Not one-hot; use target encoding with out-of-fold statistics, or an embedding in a deep model.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] When would you choose logistic regression over gradient boosting in production?	When interpretability or regulatory explanation is required, data is small, features are already well engineered and roughly linear, calibrated probabilities are critical, latency or memory is extremely tight, or the gain from boosting is too small to justify complexity. Number: a logistic model with 200 features is 200 multiply-adds per prediction; 500 depth-6 trees are 3,000 node comparisons plus 500 memory-scattered lookups. Trade-off: the linear model needs hand-built interactions that a GBDT finds by itself. Follow-up: "how big must the gain be?" Compare it to the online effect you need and the noise of offline metrics; a 0.002 AUC gain is often invisible online.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] Deep learning or gradient boosting for a tabular ETA problem?	Gradient boosting first: it handles heterogeneous features well, trains fast, and is hard to beat on tabular data. Consider deep models when you have very large data, sequences (route traces), high-cardinality IDs needing embeddings, or want multi-task learning; often the best production system combines both (GBDT on top of learned embeddings). Two caveats: trees cannot extrapolate, so a model trained before a city's average trip lengthened will cap its predictions at the old range (predict a residual from a simple baseline instead); and on small tables, tabular foundation models such as TabPFN v2 now beat tuned GBDTs on many datasets of up to about 10k rows. Follow-up: "how would you validate?" A time-based split, never random, because ETA drifts with season and supply.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] How would you explain an individual prediction of a boosted model to an operations team?	SHAP values: additive per-feature contributions that sum exactly to the prediction minus the average prediction, each feature's Shapley value averaged over every order in which features could be added; TreeSHAP computes them exactly for tree ensembles in polynomial time. Example: a baseline of 10 minutes, feature contributions +6 and +3, prediction 19. Caveats: they explain the model, not causality; with correlated features, interventional SHAP evaluates the model on impossible inputs while conditional SHAP credits features the model does not use. Use monotonic constraints where the business expects a direction. Follow-up: "SHAP vs permutation importance?" Permutation importance is global and measured on validation data; SHAP is local and additive.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] Implement gradient boosting for log loss. What changes from squared loss?	The initial prediction is the log-odds of the base rate; each tree fits the negative gradient y − p (not y − log-odds); leaf values are better set with a Newton step (sum of gradients over sum of hessians p(1−p)), which is what XGBoost does; predictions pass through the sigmoid. The XGBoost version: expand the loss to second order, so leaf j contributes Gⱼw + ½(Hⱼ + λ)w², giving w* = −G/(H + λ) and split gain ½[G_L²/(H_L + λ) + G_R²/(H_R + λ) − G²/(H + λ)] − γ. Number: four rows at p = 0.5 with labels 1, 1, 1, 0 and λ = 1; splitting off the negative gives gain 0.493 and leaf values 0.857 and −0.400. Follow-up: "why add λ to H?" When all predictions in a leaf are confident, H is tiny and the Newton step explodes; λ caps it.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] Derive the variance of a bagged ensemble. Why does random feature selection help?	With B identically distributed trees of variance σ² and pairwise correlation ρ, the variance of the average is (1/B²)[Bσ² + B(B − 1)ρσ²] = ρσ² + (1 − ρ)σ²/B. The second term vanishes with more trees; the first is a floor. Random feature subsets lower ρ by forcing different trees to use different features. Number: σ² = 1, B = 100: ρ = 0.5 gives 0.505, ρ = 0.2 gives 0.208. Trade-off: fewer candidate features per split raise each tree's bias, so max_features has an optimum, usually near √d for classification. Follow-up: "where does the 37% out-of-bag figure come from?" A row is missed by all n bootstrap draws with probability (1 − 1/n)ⁿ → 1/e ≈ 0.368.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] Write the SVM dual. Why do only support vectors matter?	The dual maximises Σαᵢ − ½ΣᵢΣⱼαᵢαⱼyᵢyⱼ(xᵢ·xⱼ) subject to 0 ≤ αᵢ ≤ C and Σαᵢyᵢ = 0, with w = Σαᵢyᵢxᵢ. Complementary slackness forces αᵢ = 0 for every point strictly outside the margin, so only points on or inside it (support vectors) contribute to w. Number: for (1, 1) labelled +1 and (−1, −1) labelled −1, w = (0.5, 0.5), α = 0.25 each, margin width 2/‖w‖ = 2.83; adding (2, 2) labelled +1 leaves everything unchanged with α = 0. Trade-off: the number of support vectors drives prediction cost for kernel SVMs, and it grows with noise and with small C. Follow-up: "what do γ and C do in an RBF SVM?" γ sets the width of each Gaussian bump (large γ gives a wiggly boundary), C sets the price of margin violations; tune them together on a log grid.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 40] Why does L1 regularisation give sparse weights and L2 does not?	With an orthonormal design each weight solves ½(w − z)² + λ|w|, whose solution is soft-thresholding: sign(z)·max(|z| − λ, 0). The L1 penalty's slope stays at ±λ right up to zero, so any weight whose data pull |z| is below λ stays exactly zero; the L2 penalty's slope λw vanishes at zero, so ridge gives z/(1 + λ), never zero. Number: z = [3, 0.5, −1.2], λ = 1: lasso [2, 0, −0.2], ridge [1.5, 0.25, −0.6]. Trade-off: with correlated features the lasso picks one arbitrarily and is unstable across resamples; elastic net adds an L2 term to keep groups together. Follow-up: "how do you optimise a non-differentiable penalty?" Coordinate descent or proximal gradient, which applies soft-thresholding after each gradient step.	chapter ch::40
[Ch 41] How would you build a text classifier for 30 categories with 5,000 labelled examples?	TF-IDF (word and character n-grams) + logistic regression as a baseline, then embeddings + logistic regression and a fine-tuned encoder; compare macro-F1 and per-class results on a held-out set; choose by quality, latency and cost. Check label quality and class balance first. Number: 5,000 examples over 30 classes is about 170 per class on average, but a long tail may leave some classes with 20, where per-class F1 has an interval of ±0.2; report those classes separately. Follow-up: "would you use class weights?" Yes for recall on rare classes, but correct the probabilities (prior correction or recalibration) before using them for abstention.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] Why character n-grams?	They capture misspellings, morphology, transliterations and sub-word cues, which matter in short noisy texts like merchant names and queries. "farmasi" shares the 3-grams "far", "arm", "rma" with "farmacia" though the words differ. Trade-off: the feature space grows by an order of magnitude, so limit n to 3–5 and use char_wb (n-grams inside word boundaries). Follow-up: "why not just use subword embeddings?" You can, and you should compare; the character TF-IDF baseline costs seconds and sets the bar.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] What is BIO tagging?	Token labels marking the beginning (B-X), inside (I-X) and outside (O) of entity spans, turning span extraction into token classification. The B tag is what separates two adjacent entities of the same type ("B-DISH I-DISH B-DISH" is two dishes). Follow-up: "how do you align word labels to subword tokens?" Label the first subword of each word, set the rest to −100 so the loss ignores them, and read predictions from first subwords.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] Why is ROUGE a weak metric for LLM summaries?	It measures word overlap with a reference, so it rewards copying and is blind to factual errors and paraphrase: a summary reversing the meaning can score higher than a faithful paraphrase. In 41.9 the reversed summary scores ROUGE-1 0.95 against 0.57 for the faithful one. Use it, if at all, as a cheap regression check. Follow-up: "what instead?" Claim-level faithfulness checks with an entailment model or a judge calibrated on human ratings.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] LDA vs BERTopic?	LDA models documents as mixtures of word-distribution topics from bag-of-words counts: cheap and interpretable, weak on short texts and synonyms. BERTopic clusters sentence embeddings and describes clusters with class-based TF-IDF: better semantics, handles short texts, flags outliers. Trade-off: BERTopic gives each document one topic (LDA gives a mixture) and depends on UMAP and HDBSCAN settings, so check stability across seeds. Follow-up: "what do α and β do in LDA?" Small α means few topics per document; small β means few words per topic.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] When would you use an LLM instead of a fine-tuned classifier?	Low or moderate volume, many or frequently changing classes, little labelled data, or tasks needing reasoning over long context. At high volume with stable labels, use the LLM to label data and distil into a small model. Number: a 400-token call at $0.15/M input costs $6.6 × 10⁻⁵, so the real-time break-even against a $730-a-month GPU is about 370,000 calls a day; a realistic cached 5,000-token prefix roughly halves that. Latency also differs: an encoder answers in milliseconds, an API call in hundreds of milliseconds or more. When not to use an LLM: hard latency SLOs, data that cannot leave your network, adversarial inputs you cannot isolate. Follow-up: "what changes when the taxonomy changes monthly?" The LLM needs a prompt edit; the encoder needs re-labelling and re-training, which favours the LLM.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] How do you evaluate an LLM-based classifier before launch?	A fixed, expert-labelled evaluation set covering all classes and known hard cases; macro-F1, per-class metrics and confusion pairs; human vs LLM agreement compared with human vs human agreement; re-run on every prompt or model version. Human agreement is the ceiling: if two annotators agree 85% of the time where chance agreement is 40%, Cohen's κ = (0.85 − 0.40)/(1 − 0.40) = 0.75, and an LLM that matches humans as often as they match each other is as good as the labels can show. Size the set for the differences you need to see: at 90% accuracy, 500 items give a 95% interval of ±2.6 points, too wide to detect a 2-point change without a paired test. Report per slice (language, class frequency). Follow-up: "the LLM disagrees with the labels on 8% of items; who is wrong?" Adjudicate a sample of the disagreements; often a third are label errors.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] Design attribute extraction from 2 million menu items.	Define the schema and allowed values; LLM extraction with structured output on a sample, audited by people; normalisation to canonical values and taxonomy nodes; distil into a token-classification or seq2seq model for the full volume (or batch LLM calls if affordable); entity-level metrics per attribute; nulls when absent, never invented. Number: at 300 input tokens per item plus a cached 2,000-token schema prefix, 2 million items is 0.6B uncached tokens, about $45 in batch (half of $0.15/M), plus about $30 for the cached prefix, so a one-off LLM pass is affordable; the cost is in re-running on every menu update. Follow-up: "how do you stop invented allergens?" Every extracted value must appear in the source text, checked in code, and the schema allows null.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] How would you analyse customer reviews for restaurants?	Aspect-based sentiment with a fixed aspect list and evidence spans; aggregate per restaurant and aspect over time with code; route negative aspects to the right team; summaries generated from aggregates plus quotes; evaluate extraction per aspect. Trade-off: a fixed aspect list misses new complaint types, so run topic discovery monthly on the reviews the extractor leaves without aspects. Follow-up: "can you use star ratings as labels?" Only as weak signals: the rating often reflects delivery while the text is about food.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] You have noisy labels. What do you do?	Measure noise on a re-labelled sample; find likely errors via cross-validated predictions that confidently disagree with labels; fix or drop them; improve guidelines; use robust training (label smoothing) if noise remains. Number: if a re-labelled sample of 400 shows 10% disagreement, the interval is about ±3 points, enough to know whether noise caps your accuracy. Follow-up: "is the noise random or systematic?" Look at the confusion pairs; systematic noise (one class always mislabelled as another) needs a guideline fix, not a robust loss.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] How do you handle Arabic and English in one NLP system?	Multilingual models, per-language evaluation sets and metrics, transliteration variants, language-specific normalisation, native-speaker review; watch for code-switching within one text. The same applies to any multilingual catalogue (Hindi and English, Spanish and Catalan). Trade-off: one multilingual model is simpler to run, but a weaker language can lose several points against a dedicated model; measure per language before deciding. Follow-up: "what about transliterated text (Arabizi, Hinglish)?" Character n-grams and models trained on social-media text handle it far better than standard tokenisers; include it in the test set.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] For the merchant classifier in 41.3: why an LLM, and what would you change after a year in production?	Give the baseline numbers and the gap the LLM closed, the cost after batching, and how quality was measured. Then name a concrete next step: distillation for cost and latency, a confidence threshold with review, or drift monitoring as merchant types change. A strong answer brings the arithmetic: calls per week, tokens per call including the cached prefix, the batch discount, and the break-even volume for distillation. (Rehearse the same answer for a project of your own.) Follow-up: "what would make you switch to the distilled model tomorrow?" A real-time requirement, volume beyond about twice the break-even, or a vendor price change.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] How do you check an LLM summary for hallucinations at scale?	Split the summary into atomic claims; check each against the source with an entailment model or a calibrated LLM judge; require citations; track the share of unsupported claims as a metric; route summaries with unsupported claims to regeneration or review. Number: if 2% of claims are unsupported and summaries have 8 claims each, about 15% of summaries (1 − 0.98⁸) contain at least one, so measure per summary as well as per claim. Follow-up: "what if a review itself contains a false claim?" Faithfulness to the source is not truth; aggregate across reviews and only state claims that several support.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] A product team wants a "topics customers complain about" dashboard. How do you build it?	Aspect or theme extraction into a fixed, reviewed set of themes (seeded by clustering plus LLM naming), counts and trends computed in code, drill-down to example reviews, a monthly review of new clusters for emerging themes, and an evaluation sample per theme. Trade-off: re-clustering every month gives unstable themes that break trends; a fixed list with a monthly discovery pass keeps trends comparable. Follow-up: "how do you know a spike is real?" Compare against the review volume and a confidence interval on the share, and read ten examples.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] How do you decide between paying for LLM calls and fine-tuning a model?	Compute cost per month for each: tokens × calls × price (with batch and cache discounts) vs training plus serving cost; compare quality on the same evaluation set; factor in latency, how often labels change, team effort and vendor risk. Often the answer is both: an LLM for labels and hard cases, a small model for volume. Number from 41.11: a 5,000-token cached prefix plus a 300-token listing costs $1.26 × 10⁻⁴ per call, $1,134 a month in batch at 600,000 a day, against $730 for a GPU; the gap of about $400 a month is less than the engineering time to run a service, so the decision rests on accuracy, latency and taxonomy churn. Follow-up: "what if the prompt were not cached?" $7,209 a month in batch: distil.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] How do you get a calibrated confidence from an LLM classifier?	Constrain the output to the label set, request token log-probabilities and read each label's probability (summing token log-probabilities for multi-token labels, or using single-token label IDs). Then calibrate on a held-out set with temperature or vector scaling, because instruction-tuned models are often over-confident. Trade-off: one call instead of several samples, but some APIs expose only top-k log-probabilities or none, and reasoning models' final-token probability reflects the conclusion, not the reasoning. Number: if the raw probability says 0.95 on items that are right 85% of the time, a threshold set at "0.95 confidence" lets through three times the expected error rate (15% instead of 5%). Follow-up: "and if there are no log-probabilities?" Agreement between several samples or two prompts, at two to five times the cost.	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] You trained with class_weight="balanced". Can you threshold the probabilities?	Not directly. Weighting class k by cₖ makes the model estimate probabilities proportional to cₖpₖ, so rare classes are inflated: in the 41.2 simulation, the rarest class (about 4% of items) gets a mean predicted probability of 0.21 and ECE doubles. Fix by prior correction (divide by cₖ and renormalise, which needs no data) or by recalibrating on held-out data with vector scaling or per-class isotonic regression; temperature scaling alone cannot undo a per-class shift. Trade-off: the weights may still help ranking within rare classes, so keep them and correct, or drop them and use per-class thresholds. Follow-up: "the same with negative downsampling?" Yes: p = q/(q + (1 − q)/w) for a negative sampling rate w (Chapter 43.5).	chapter ch::41
[Ch 41] Your review summariser reads customer text. How do you protect it from prompt injection?	Treat reviews as untrusted data: put them in a delimited field marked as data, constrain the output (aggregates computed in code, claims checked against them), and require a second check before anything customer-facing or action-triggering. Measure the attack success rate on an adversarial test set every release. Trade-off: stricter output constraints make summaries less fluent; for customer-facing text that is the right side to err on. Follow-up: "can a better system prompt fix it?" It reduces naive attacks but is not a guarantee; the defence is limiting what a successful injection can do (Chapter 22.8).	chapter ch::41
[Ch 42] What is the difference between a container and a virtual machine?	A VM includes a full operating system on virtualised hardware; a container shares the host kernel and only packages the app and its dependencies, so it is smaller and starts in seconds. The trade-off is isolation: a VM's separate kernel isolates tenants more strongly, which is why cloud providers run containers inside VMs for untrusted workloads. Number: a slim Python image is a few hundred MB and starts in about a second; a VM image is several GB and boots in tens of seconds to minutes. Follow-up: "how does a container use a GPU?" The host has the NVIDIA driver; the container brings the CUDA user-space libraries and gets the device through the container runtime's GPU support.	chapter ch::42
[Ch 42] What do a Kubernetes Deployment, Service and HPA each do?	A Deployment declares the desired state (which image, how many replicas) and keeps it true: it replaces crashed pods and rolls out new versions gradually, a few pods at a time. A Service gives the changing set of pods one stable name and load-balances requests across those that pass readiness. The Horizontal Pod Autoscaler changes the replica count from a metric. Number: the HPA sets desired replicas = ceil(current × metric / target), so 6 pods at 90% CPU with a 60% target become ceil(6 × 90/60) = 9. Follow-up: "why is CPU a poor scaling signal for a GPU model server?" GPU utilisation reads high at moderate load and CPU barely moves; scale on queue depth or requests in flight instead.	chapter ch::42
[Ch 42] When would you use batch inference instead of an online API?	When predictions do not depend on request-time context, volume is large, or cost matters more than freshness. Nightly scores for 10 million users are one job that runs on cheap or spot machines and writes to a cache, instead of a fleet sized for peak traffic that sits idle at night. When not to: the prediction depends on what the user just did, or on context that only exists at request time (the basket, the location, the query). Follow-up: "what if only part of it needs freshness?" Hybrid: precompute the heavy part in batch (embeddings), combine it online with fresh features in a light model.	chapter ch::42
[Ch 42] What do p50 and p99 latency mean, and why report both?	p50 is the median: half of requests are faster. p99 is the value 99% of requests are faster than; the slowest 1% are slower. The mean hides the tail, and the tail is what users notice and what drives timeouts. Number: a page that makes 20 backend calls sees at least one call beyond its p99 on 1 − 0.99²⁰ = 18% of page loads, so the p99 of a backend shapes the typical experience of the page. Follow-up: "why not p99.9?" Report it for very high-traffic services, but it needs a lot of data to estimate: at 1,000 requests a minute, a one-minute p99.9 rests on one request.	chapter ch::42
[Ch 42] Why put a queue between two services?	It decouples them: the producer does not wait for the consumer, a slow or failed consumer does not slow or break the producer, and a burst is absorbed as a backlog instead of an outage. Messages can be retried, and failures go to a dead-letter queue instead of being lost. Number: if orders arrive at 1,000 a second for a 10-minute dinner peak and the consumer handles 600, the backlog reaches 240,000 messages; at 300 spare messages a second afterwards it drains in about 13 minutes. Trade-off: the result is asynchronous, so a queue does not suit work the user is waiting for, and you must handle duplicates (make consumers idempotent). Follow-up: "queue or stream?" A queue for jobs processed once; a stream (Kafka) for events that several consumers read and may replay.	chapter ch::42
[Ch 42] Your model API meets its p50 target but p99 is five times higher. What could cause it and what would you check?	A p99 far above p50 means some requests take a different path or wait. Candidates: queueing near saturation (p99 rises as 1/(1 − utilisation), so at 80% a single server's p99 is 2.5 times its value at 50%); fan-out, where a request waits for the slowest of many backends (with 100 backends, 63% of requests see some backend's p99); garbage-collection pauses; cold replicas just started by the autoscaler; batching windows; slow feature lookups; a few huge inputs; noisy neighbours. Confirm each with traces: is the slow span in one service, on particular replicas, at particular times, or for particular inputs? Fixes match the cause: more headroom or better load balancing, hedged requests after the p95, timeouts with partial results, warm-up before readiness, input size limits. Follow-up: "how do you know it is queueing?" p99 tracks utilisation and request rate, and the time is in the server's queue, not in its handler.	chapter ch::42
[Ch 42] Estimate how many replicas you need for 2,000 RPS with 80 ms latency and 4 concurrent requests per replica.	In flight = 2000 × 0.08 = 160; at 4 per replica and 60% headroom, 160 / 2.4 ≈ 67 replicas. Two clarifications make it a strong answer: the 80 ms must be the service time measured at low load (if it already includes queueing under load, the headroom double-counts it), and the 4 must come from a load test, the largest concurrency at which p99 still meets the SLO (for CPU-bound Python, roughly the number of worker processes). Then add redundancy across zones, so losing one zone does not overload the rest: with three zones, each must carry half the load, not a third. Follow-up: "what if latency doubles?" In flight doubles and so does the fleet: about 134 replicas.	chapter ch::42
[Ch 42] Design the infrastructure for scoring 10 million menu items with an LLM every week, robust to API failures.	Put items on a queue or in batch files; process them with workers or the provider's batch API; retry with exponential backoff; send repeated failures to a dead-letter queue; make writes idempotent, keyed by item id and model version; skip items whose text has not changed since the last run; monitor cost and failure rate. Cost first: at 500 input and 100 output tokens per item and $0.15/$0.60 per million, 10 million items cost about $1,350 a week in real time and half that in batch. Rate limits next: 6 billion tokens at a 2-million-tokens-per-minute limit take 50 hours, so the job must be spread over days or use batch quotas. Checkpoint progress (which items are done) so a crash resumes rather than restarts, and validate every output against its schema, re-queuing failures with a capped retry count. Follow-up: "the provider is down for six hours?" The queue holds the work; the job finishes later; nothing customer-facing depends on it.	chapter ch::42
[Ch 42] A new model version doubles CPU per request. What changes operationally, and how would you roll it out?	Twice the replicas or bigger machines for the same traffic; update resource requests and autoscaling limits; load-test; canary release with latency and error guardrails. Number: the 67-replica fleet of question 7 becomes about 134, so check cluster quota and node-pool limits before the rollout, not during it. Also check whether the extra CPU raises p99 more than p50 (larger inputs may now dominate), and whether the cost is justified by the online gain. Follow-up: "can you avoid doubling the fleet?" Optimise the model (quantise, distil, compile with ONNX Runtime), cache predictions for repeated inputs, or serve the new model only to the traffic where it helps.	chapter ch::42
[Ch 42] You serve an open 70B LLM on Kubernetes. Traffic doubles in five minutes. What happens, and how would you design for it?	New replicas need a GPU node (minutes to provision if none is free), a large image, and 140 GB of weights loaded into GPU memory: at 1 GB/s from object storage that is over two minutes of download alone. So reactive autoscaling arrives after the spike. Design for it with warm minimum capacity sized for the daily peak, scheduled scale-up ahead of known peaks, weights cached on node-local NVMe or a shared volume, readiness that includes a warm-up request, and a scaling signal that leads (queue depth, requests in flight, KV-cache use) rather than GPU utilisation. Meanwhile protect the replicas you have: admission control or a queue with a timeout, and a fallback (a smaller model, or a clear "busy" response). Trade-off: warm capacity costs money every hour it is idle. Follow-up: "spot GPUs?" Fine for batch and evaluation, risky for latency-critical serving unless on-demand capacity covers the baseline.	chapter ch::42
[Ch 42] Why must liveness and readiness probes check different things?	Readiness answers "can this pod serve now?": model loaded and warmed, essential dependencies reachable; failing it removes the pod from load balancing without a restart. Liveness answers "is this process stuck?": it should check only the process; failing it restarts the container. If liveness checked a shared dependency such as the feature cache, a cache outage would fail every pod's liveness at once, Kubernetes would restart all of them, and each would spend minutes reloading the model: a restart storm that turns a brief dependency outage into a full one. Follow-up: "the model takes three minutes to load and the pod is killed during start-up?" Add a startup probe, which holds off liveness until the first success.	chapter ch::42
[Ch 42] Slurm or Kubernetes for a 512-GPU training run, and what else does the run need from infrastructure?	Training needs gang scheduling (all 64 nodes start together or not at all), whole-node allocation and topology-aware placement; Slurm provides these natively, Kubernetes with gang-scheduling and queueing add-ons. Beyond the scheduler: an InfiniBand or RoCE fabric with NCCL for the collectives, NVLink within nodes for tensor parallelism, data streamed from object storage in large shards, and fast checkpoint storage. Number: a full checkpoint of a 70B model with Adam is about 1 TB; at 20 GB/s it takes about 50 seconds, so it must be written asynchronously and sharded, at an interval set by the failure rate (Chapter 17). Follow-up: "one node is 20% slower?" Every synchronous step waits for it; find it from per-rank step times and evict it.	chapter ch::42
[Ch 43] What is the difference between explicit and implicit feedback, and where do negatives come from with implicit data?	Explicit feedback is a stated preference (ratings), rare and biased towards extremes. Implicit feedback is behaviour (impressions, clicks, orders): abundant but with no explicit negatives. For ranking, the best negatives are items the user saw and did not choose. For retrieval, use sampled negatives (in-batch plus some random). Items that were unavailable are not negatives at all. Follow-up: "is an impressed-but-not-clicked item at position 20 a good negative?" Weak: it was probably never examined, so weight it by the examination probability or drop it (43.9).	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] Why do recommenders use multiple stages?	Latency and cost. A rich ranker cannot score a whole catalogue in 100 ms, so cheap high-recall models cut the set to thousands, rules remove what cannot be shown, a heavy model ranks hundreds, and re-ranking builds the page. Each stage optimises a different metric: recall early, precision and value late. Number: a ranker costing 50 µs per item scores 500 candidates in 25 ms but a 100,000-item catalogue in 5 seconds. Follow-up: "what is lost?" Anything retrieval misses can never be ranked, so measure retrieval recall separately.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] How does matrix factorisation work for implicit feedback?	Every user and item gets a k-dimensional vector and the score is their dot product. With implicit data every cell is a 0/1 preference weighted by a confidence that grows with interaction count, and ALS solves it by alternating closed-form ridge regressions for users and items. The trick that makes it scale: VᵀCᵤV = VᵀV + Vᵀ(Cᵤ − I)V, where VᵀV is shared and the correction touches only the user's nᵤ items, so each user costs O(k²nᵤ + k³) instead of O(k²·n_items), about 8,800 times less for a million items, k = 64 and nᵤ = 50. When not to use it: rich content or context matters more than co-occurrence, the catalogue is mostly cold, or new users must be scored in real time. Follow-up: "ALS or SGD?" ALS handles the all-cells implicit loss exactly and parallelises per user; SGD suits sampled losses like BPR.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] What metric would you use for the retrieval stage, and why not NDCG?	Recall@k (or HitRate@k) of the items the user later ordered. The retrieval stage's job is not to order; it is to not lose the right item. Order inside the candidate set is decided by the ranker. Number: if retrieval recall@500 is 60%, no ranker can find the other 40% of eventual orders, which caps the whole system. Follow-up: "how do you choose k?" From the ranker's latency budget, then check that recall@k has flattened by that k.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] What is cold start and how do you handle a new restaurant?	No interactions means an untrained ID embedding. Use content features in the item tower (cuisine, price, menu embeddings) so it gets a vector on day one, give it guaranteed exploration impressions, train with ID dropout so the model can score from content, and borrow signals from similar existing restaurants. Semantic IDs (43.11) give a new item an ID that shares parameters with similar items. Follow-up: "how many exploration impressions?" Enough to estimate its conversion rate to within the gap that matters: at a 5% rate, 400 impressions give a 95% interval of about ±2 points.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] Explain in-batch negatives in a two-tower model. What bias does it introduce and how do you fix it?	Other positives in the batch serve as negatives, giving a B × B logit matrix trained with softmax cross-entropy. Items appear as negatives in proportion to popularity, so popular items get over-penalised. The logQ correction subtracts each item's log sampling probability from its logit, which by importance sampling makes the sampled softmax a consistent estimate of the full softmax. Add a share of uniformly sampled corpus negatives (mixed negative sampling), because in-batch sampling almost never presents tail items as negatives. Mask false negatives: the same item twice in a batch, and near-duplicates. The temperature (τ = 0.05 with unit vectors) decides how much the hardest negatives dominate: at τ = 0.05 a negative with similarity 0.9 takes 98% of the gradient weight against ones at 0.7, 0.5 and 0.3. Evaluate tail recall separately (recall@k for items outside the top 10% by popularity). Follow-up: "why not only hard negatives?" They are often false negatives and make training unstable; mix a small share.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] Why can't a two-tower model use user-item cross features, and where do they go?	The towers only interact at the final dot product, which is what makes precomputing item vectors and ANN search possible. Cross features such as distance or past orders from this restaurant need both user and item at once, so they go into the ranker. Follow-up: "can you get some interaction into retrieval?" Partly: late-interaction or multi-vector models, or a light re-scoring of the top few thousand ANN results, at extra cost.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] GBDT or deep model for the ranker?	Start with GBDT: strong on tabular features, fast to train, easy to debug and explain. Move to a deep model with very large data, many sparse IDs, sequence features, or several objectives to learn together. Better features usually beat a fancier architecture. A common middle step: feed learned embeddings (from the two-tower model) into the GBDT as features. Follow-up: "how do you know the deep model is worth it?" An A/B test after matching the GBDT offline, and an honest cost of serving and maintaining it.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] How do you handle position bias?	Model examination explicitly: either add position as a training feature (or a separate bias tower) and neutralise it at serving, or weight clicks by inverse propensity of examination, estimated from a small randomisation experiment. For IPS, the weights belong in a rank-based loss over clicked items (Joachims), or in the unbiased pointwise loss with c/θ in both terms (Saito); weighting clicks by 1/θ and non-clicks by 1 in pointwise BCE is still biased (0.375 instead of 0.5 for r = 0.5 at θ = 1/3). Clip weights. Neither fixes items that were never shown; that needs exploration. Follow-up: "how do you get θ?" Swap adjacent positions at random for a small share of traffic and compare click rates of the same items.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] How would you combine click, order and basket value into one ranking?	Multi-task model with a head per target, calibrated; combine as p(click) × p(order | click) × expected value, or a weighted sum with weights chosen by A/B tests against the business goal. Mention ESMM to avoid selection bias when learning p(order | click), and guardrails such as delivery time. Expect the seesaw: tasks that share a bottom fight, so use MMoE or PLE and monitor every task when tuning one. Follow-up: "the order head was trained with 10% of negatives?" Undo it before multiplying: p = q/(q + (1 − q)/w).	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] How do you add diversity, and how do you measure whether it helped?	Re-rank with MMR or caps per cuisine and chain. Measure intra-list diversity and coverage offline, and online check that conversion holds or improves and that more restaurants receive orders. Diversity is a means; the test is orders and retention. Number: with λ = 0.7, a second pizza at score 0.85 and similarity 0.95 is worth 0.31, a sushi place at 0.7 and similarity 0.1 is worth 0.46, so sushi wins the second slot. Follow-up: "how do you set λ?" Sweep it offline to trace the NDCG–diversity curve, then A/B test two or three points on it.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] Offline NDCG improved by 3% but the A/B test is flat. What do you investigate?	Whether the offline candidate set matches production (availability, filters, re-ranking); leakage or point-in-time errors in new features; position bias in the evaluation labels; whether NDCG on clicks matches the online metric (orders); segment effects cancelling out; train-serve skew in features; and whether re-ranking rules override the change. Then check the test itself: power, novelty, sample ratio mismatch. Number: if the A/B test was powered for a 1% lift in orders and offline NDCG gains typically translate at a third or less, a 3% NDCG gain may simply be below the test's detection threshold. Follow-up: "how would you check translation?" Plot past launches' offline gains against their online effects.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] How would you run exploration in production, and how do you know what it costs?	Reserve a slot or two per page, or add an uncertainty bonus to new items for their first impressions, or use a bandit for small decisions. Log propensities. Measure cost as conversion loss in exploration slots against a holdout without exploration, and benefit as coverage and new-restaurant success over weeks. Number: in the banner simulation, ε-greedy with ε = 0.1 loses about 60 clicks over 20,000 impressions and Thompson sampling about 35. Follow-up: "which bandit for a feed?" A contextual one (LinUCB or neural Thompson sampling) on a few slots, not over the whole page.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] How would you evaluate a new ranking policy from logs before an A/B test?	Off-policy evaluation: IPS or doubly robust estimators using logged propensities. State the limits: it needs stochastic logging with overlap, variance explodes for slates, so use it to choose candidates for A/B tests, not to replace them. Interleaving is a cheaper online alternative for ranker comparisons. Number: if the new policy puts an item at the top that the old one showed there with probability 0.01, each such reward is weighted 100, so a handful of rows dominate the estimate; clip and report the effective sample size. Follow-up: "the logging policy was deterministic?" Then there is no overlap; add a little randomisation to logging first.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] How is "order again" different from "discover new restaurants" as a design problem?	Order again is mostly a recency-frequency problem with time and context (a strong heuristic or a small model wins), seen items are the point, and the metric is speed to reorder. Discovery needs retrieval beyond the user's history, exploration, diversity and novelty, excludes seen items, and is judged on new-restaurant conversion and long-term retention, not short-term clicks. The same split exists as "buy it again" vs "recommended for you" in e-commerce. Follow-up: "how do you allocate page space between them?" A/B test the mix, with retention as the deciding metric.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] Where would you use LLMs in this system, and where would you not?	Yes: offline enrichment of menus into attributes and embeddings (cold start), LLM judges for offline relevance evaluation after calibration, conversational search where users express intent in words, and explanations on short lists. No: scoring thousands of candidates per request in the main ranking path, where latency and cost rule it out; there, classic models (possibly distilled from LLM signals) stay. Watch the frontier: generative recommenders with semantic IDs (HSTU, OneRec) are moving into production at the largest platforms. Follow-up: "what does an LLM call per candidate cost?" At 500 candidates and even $10⁻⁵ per call, $0.005 per page view, more than most pages earn.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] Your CTR model was trained with 10% of negatives. How do you get calibrated probabilities?	The sampled data has p positives for every w(1 − p) negatives, so the model learns q = p/(p + w(1 − p)); invert it: p = q/(q + (1 − q)/w), or equivalently add log w to the logit. Number: a true CTR of 1% at w = 0.1 shows up as 9.2%; the formula maps 0.092 back to 0.010. Then verify calibration on unsampled recent traffic per segment, and add a small isotonic or Platt layer if needed, because drift breaks it again. Trade-off: heavier downsampling saves compute but raises variance on the negatives. Follow-up: "why does calibration matter so much in ads?" The auction charges by bid × pCTR, so a ninefold over-prediction misprices every impression and lets one advertiser's miscalibrated model win slots it should not.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] How do you handle an embedding table with a billion user IDs?	At 64 fp32 dimensions it is 256 GB, plus 512 GB of Adam state, so: give frequent IDs their own rows and hash the tail (with N IDs in M buckets, about 1 − e^(−N/M) of IDs collide), map IDs below a frequency threshold to a shared row or to content features, store in lower precision, shard rows across devices with an all-to-all exchange per step (DLRM, TorchRec), and use row-wise AdaGrad (4 GB of state instead of 512 GB). Keep the table fresh with continual training and on-the-fly insertion of new IDs with eviction. Trade-off: hashing saves memory but makes unrelated IDs share vectors; it hurts most on head IDs. Follow-up: "why row-wise AdaGrad?" Rows are updated at very different rates, an adaptive per-row step size handles that, and one accumulator per row is almost free.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] Your multi-task ranker improved orders but likes went down. What is going on and what do you do?	Negative transfer: the tasks share a bottom and their gradients conflict, so tuning for one moves the other (the seesaw). Check whether the drop is real with per-task metrics on the same test data. Then reduce sharing where tasks conflict: MMoE gives each task its own gate over shared experts, PLE adds task-specific experts; re-weight the task losses; or, if the tasks are truly at odds, accept a trade-off chosen explicitly against the business objective. Follow-up: "how do you choose loss weights?" Tune them against the online metric that combines the tasks, not against the sum of offline losses.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 43] How do you know a ranking change is not trading long-term retention for short-term clicks?	A two-week A/B test cannot see slow erosion. Use a long-running holdback (1–5% of users on the old system for months) to measure cumulative effects on retention; validated surrogate metrics that predict long-term outcomes; guardrails on bad engagement (cancellations, refunds, hides, "not interested"); and plot the treatment effect by day of exposure to separate novelty from lasting effects. Delayed conversions also need the attribution window to close before you read the test. Trade-off: holdbacks cost the holdback group the improvements, so keep them small and rotate them. Follow-up: "the holdback shows a retention gap but no single launch did?" That is the point of a holdback: many small, individually positive launches can add up to a negative.	chapter ch::43
[Ch 44] What is the difference between pointwise, pairwise and listwise learning to rank?	Pointwise predicts each document's relevance on its own (classification or regression). Pairwise learns which of two documents for the same query should rank higher (RankNet). Listwise optimises a property of the whole list, usually NDCG (LambdaRank, LambdaMART, ListNet). Pairwise and listwise optimise order, which is what users see; pointwise gives calibrated probabilities. The senior trade-off: when the score feeds arithmetic downstream (an ads auction multiplying pCTR by a bid, blending restaurants with dishes, a threshold), you need calibration, so either train pointwise, calibrate the listwise output on held-out data, or train a weighted sum of a listwise and a pointwise loss. Follow-up: "can a listwise model's scores be compared across queries?" Not as they stand, because the loss is unchanged by adding a constant per query.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] Explain BM25 in one minute.	A keyword score summing, over query terms, IDF (rare terms count more) times a term-frequency factor that saturates (k₁) and is normalised by document length (b). A term's contribution can never exceed (k₁ + 1)·IDF, so with k₁ = 1.2 the tenth mention adds almost nothing. It is a strong, cheap baseline and still the lexical half of most hybrid systems. Follow-up: "why the log(1 + …) in the IDF?" The classic form goes negative for terms in more than half the documents.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] Why combine lexical and semantic retrieval?	Lexical catches exact names, brands and numbers; semantic catches synonyms, paraphrases and vague queries. Each covers the other's failures. Fuse with reciprocal rank fusion, score 1/(60 + rank) summed over lists, because BM25 and cosine scores are on incomparable scales; then let the ranker weigh both scores as features. When not to bother: a catalogue of codes or names only, where BM25 plus fuzzy matching already gets recall near 1.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] What is NDCG and why the log discount?	DCG sums graded gains discounted by log₂(position + 1), because users look less at lower positions; NDCG divides by the best possible DCG so scores are comparable across queries. Graded gains (2^rel − 1) reward putting the very best results first: with grades 0–3, a grade-3 result is worth 7 and a grade-1 result 1, while the linear gain (rel) would make it only 3 times as valuable. That choice matters: the exponential gain weights the step from grade 2 to 3 (gain 3 to 7) four times as heavily as the step from 0 to 1, so the metric, and LambdaRank trained on it, cares mostly about the very best results; and it makes the metric sensitive to how labels are scaled, so fix the grade scale before comparing models. Follow-up: compute the ideal DCG from all known relevant documents, not only the retrieved ones.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] How would you handle misspelled queries?	Candidate corrections within one or two edits, ranked by popularity in successful queries and by context; learn corrections from reformulations (users who typed A then quickly B and ordered). Show the correction transparently and never overwrite exact matches to known restaurant names. Build the vocabulary from queries that led to orders, or common misspellings like "piza" become "correct".	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] Explain LambdaMART.	RankNet gives pairwise gradients from σ(sᵢ − sⱼ). LambdaRank multiplies each pair's gradient by the |ΔNDCG| of swapping the pair, λᵢⱼ = −σ/(1 + e^{σ(sᵢ − sⱼ)}) × |ΔNDCG_ij|, and sums them per document, so errors near the top matter most, without needing NDCG to be differentiable. LambdaMART fits gradient-boosted trees to those lambdas, with a Newton step per leaf using the second derivative. It is the default production ranker for tabular features. Follow-up: "is there a loss behind the lambdas?" Yes: the LambdaLoss framework (Wang et al., 2018) shows LambdaRank optimises a bound on an NDCG-based loss.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] Bi-encoder vs cross-encoder: when do you use each?	Bi-encoders encode query and documents separately, so document vectors are precomputed and searched with ANN: retrieval. Cross-encoders read query and document together: far more accurate but one forward pass per pair, so only for re-ranking the top 50–100. Re-ranking 100 candidates is 100 forward passes per query; at 1,000 QPS that is 100,000 passes a second, a GPU fleet of its own. In a marketplace, the cross-encoder score is often a feature for the final ranker.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] Which features would you build for restaurant search?	By family: query (intent, length, language), document (rating, popularity, prep time, price), query-document (BM25 per field, exact name match, embedding similarity, smoothed query-restaurant CTR and conversion), user and context (distance, ETA, past orders there, cuisine affinity, time of day). Smooth engagement features towards a prior: 2 clicks out of 3 impressions with a prior of 10% and 50 pseudo-impressions gives (2 + 5)/(3 + 50) ≈ 13%, not 67%. And compute them point in time, or the label leaks in.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] A user types a restaurant name and it is closed. What do you show?	The restaurant first, clearly marked closed with its opening time, then the most similar open alternatives nearby. Hiding it makes the user think search is broken, and they leave rather than browse. Measure it with MRR or success@1 on navigational queries, not NDCG.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] How do you get relevance labels for training and evaluation?
