Engagement for training at scale, debiased for position; a human-judged golden set (0–3 scale, guidelines, agreement checks) for evaluation; LLM judges to scale the golden set after calibrating against the human labels (report Cohen's kappa between LLM and humans; above about 0.6 is usable). The trade-off: engagement labels reflect price and ETA as well as relevance, while human labels reflect topical relevance only, which is one reason offline and online results disagree (Q15).	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] How do you measure search quality online?	Search conversion as the primary metric, with zero-result rate, reformulation rate, abandonment and position of first order as diagnostics; team-draft interleaving to compare rankers quickly; A/B tests for the final call; everything segmented by query type. Interleaving tells you which ranker users prefer, not by how much conversion changes, so it never replaces the A/B test.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] Search conversion dropped 2% last week. How do you investigate?	Check instrumentation and traffic mix first (app release, new city, marketing campaign bringing new users). Then segment: by query type, language, city, platform. Look at zero-result and reformulation rates for spikes, which point to retrieval or spelling breaking. Check supply (restaurants closed, a big partner offline) and recent ranking or index changes. Compare with conversion from browsing to separate a search problem from a demand problem. Size it first: on 2 million searches a week at 30% conversion, the standard error of a weekly rate is about √(0.3 × 0.7 / 2M) ≈ 0.03 points, so a 2% relative drop (0.6 points) is far outside noise, and the question is why, not whether. Follow-up: "everything is flat except one city": then look at that city's supply and index, not the model.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] How would you blend restaurants and dishes in one result page?	Estimate intent probabilities in query understanding. Either rank both types with one model that includes type and intent features, calibrated so scores are comparable, or rank separately and allocate slots by intent (more dishes for "margherita", more restaurants for "italian"). Calibration is the crux: two LambdaMART models trained on different lists produce scores on different scales, so map each to P(order) with isotonic regression before interleaving them. Test the blending policy online; offline metrics struggle with mixed lists. Follow-up: "why not one listwise model over both?" You can, but its scores are only comparable within one list, and the slot policy still needs probabilities.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] How would you add LLMs to restaurant search?	Start offline: LLM-generated labels to train and evaluate the ranker, menu enrichment for better matching, synonym and transliteration mining. Online, use them for query understanding of long natural-language queries ("something light and vegan under €12") turned into filters plus a semantic query. Keep the high-traffic ranking path on LambdaMART and distilled cross-encoders for latency and cost. The arithmetic: at 1,000 searches a second, even $0.0002 per LLM call is $0.20 a second, about $17,000 a day, so the LLM goes where the gain per call is largest (the long tail) or offline. Follow-up: cache LLM query rewrites for head queries; they repeat.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] Your new ranker wins offline on NDCG but loses an interleaving test. Why might that be?	The offline labels and online behaviour disagree: the golden set over-represents head queries; labels judge topical relevance while users also weigh ETA, price and fees; the offline candidate set ignores availability; or the new model is worse at navigational queries, where one failure is very visible. Look at per-segment interleaving results and at the queries where the two rankers disagree most. Then fix the offline set so it would have predicted the loss (add the failing slice, or switch labels to orders), because an offline metric that cannot predict online outcomes will mislead the next iteration too.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] Write the LambdaRank gradient and say what each part does.	For a pair where i should rank above j, λᵢⱼ = −σ/(1 + e^{σ(sᵢ − sⱼ)}) × |ΔNDCG_ij|. The logistic factor is RankNet's gradient: near σ when the pair is badly misordered, near zero once i is well above j. |ΔNDCG_ij| weights the pair by how much the metric would change if the two swapped. Each document sums its lambdas, adding λᵢⱼ where it is the better document and subtracting λⱼᵢ where it is the worse one. Example: an irrelevant document at score 1.0 above a grade-2 document at 0.5 gives logistic factor 0.622 and, with ΔNDCG 0.305, λ = −0.19 for the grade-2 document. Follow-up: "why not differentiate NDCG?" It is piecewise constant in the scores, so its gradient is zero almost everywhere.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] How would you estimate position propensities for unbiased learning to rank?	The cleanest way is randomisation: on a small slice of traffic, swap adjacent results (or shuffle the top k) and compare click rates of the same documents at different positions; the ratio gives θ_r/θ_{r+1}. Without randomisation, fit a position-based click model by EM, or use interventions that occur naturally when different rankers placed the same document at different positions (intervention harvesting). Then weight each click by 1/θ_r. The trade-off: small propensities give huge weights and high variance, so clip the weights (for example at 1/θ ≤ 10) and accept some bias. Follow-up: trust bias means clicks at the top are inflated beyond examination, which propensity weighting alone does not fix.	chapter ch::44
[Ch 44] How would you train a dense retriever for search from click logs?	Pairs of (query, clicked or ordered document) with in-batch negatives and a softmax loss, starting from a pretrained multilingual encoder; then add hard negatives mined with BM25 or the current model, refreshed as the model improves (ANCE). Guard against false negatives: relevant but unclicked documents among the mined negatives, so skip the top few mined results or filter with a cross-encoder. Evaluate on recall@100 for the retrieval stage, not NDCG, because the ranker fixes the order. Follow-up: popular documents appear in many batches as negatives and get over-penalised, which the logQ correction fixes (Chapter 43.4).	chapter ch::44
[Ch 45] How would you forecast hourly orders per zone?	Seasonal-naive baseline; then a global GBDT with calendar, lags at or beyond the horizon, rolling same-hour means, weather and events; backtest by time; WAPE and bias per zone; quantiles for staffing. On the chapter's synthetic data this takes WAPE from 0.136 (same hour last week) to 0.110. Follow-up: "why one global model rather than one per zone?" It shares patterns across zones, so small and new zones borrow strength from big ones; add the zone as a feature.	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] Why WAPE rather than MAPE for demand?	MAPE explodes for small actuals (quiet hours, small zones) and is undefined at zero; WAPE weights errors by volume (Chapter 4.5). An hour with 2 actual orders and a forecast of 4 contributes 100% to MAPE, the same as a peak hour off by half, while WAPE counts it as 2 orders out of the total. Report bias next to it, because WAPE hides a model that is always low.	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] What is the assignment problem?	Matching agents to tasks one-to-one at minimum total cost; solved exactly by the Hungarian algorithm (linear_sum_assignment) in O(n³); the core of batch dispatch. In the chapter's example, the optimal matching needs 45.1 pickup minutes against 50.0 for greedy. It stops being enough once a courier can take two orders, which turns it into a routing problem.	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] What would you predict for an ETA shown to customers?	A high quantile (P70–P90) or a range, chosen by experiment, rather than the mean, because lateness costs more than earliness. Train with the pinball loss at that quantile, or an asymmetric Huber loss, and check that the P80 really covers 80% per city and hour. Follow-up: too high a quantile loses conversion, so the level is a business choice measured online.	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] How do you handle censored demand in quick commerce?	Flag stock-out periods; exclude or impute them; model sales as min(demand, stock); use availability as a feature; treat forecasts as unconstrained demand. If you train on raw sales, the forecast learns the stock-outs and recommends ordering less, which causes more stock-outs: a feedback loop.	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] How do you make forecasts at store level add up to city level?	Hierarchical reconciliation: bottom-up, top-down, or optimal combination (MinT), and evaluate at the levels where decisions are made. MinT forecasts every level, then finds the closest coherent set, weighting levels by the inverse of their error covariance, so it usually beats both bottom-up (noisy) and top-down (blind to local patterns).	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] Decompose ETA or predict end to end?	Components help dispatch and diagnosis and allow targeted fixes; end-to-end often gives the best customer-facing number; combine by feeding component predictions into an end-to-end model. For travel time, predict the residual over a routing engine (as DeepETA does) rather than the total, because the residual is smaller and easier to learn.	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] Your P90 forecast covers 80% of outcomes. What do you do?	Recalibrate with conformal prediction or an empirical correction on recent residuals, check by segment and horizon, and investigate drift (new demand patterns, events). Concretely: compute yᵢ − q̂(xᵢ) on a recent calibration window and add its (1 − α)(1 + 1/n) quantile to every forecast. If demand is trending, the calibration window is not exchangeable with the future (the chapter's example only reaches 87.8%), so recompute on a rolling window or use an adaptive update.	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] How would you rebalance couriers?	Forecast need per zone for the next window, solve a min-cost transport LP from surplus to deficit zones, then translate moves into incentives or suggestions; evaluate with a switchback test. Couriers are independent workers, so the LP output is a target, not an instruction; the real decision variable is the incentive needed to make enough couriers move.	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] Design ETA prediction for a new city with little data.	Global model trained on all cities with city-level features; routing-engine travel times as features; prep-time priors by cuisine and restaurant size; wider quantiles initially; fast feedback loop; monitor late rate daily. Predicting the residual over the routing engine helps most here, because the routing engine already knows the new city's roads. Follow-up: shrink restaurant-level prep-time estimates towards the cuisine prior until each restaurant has a few dozen orders.	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] A new dispatch algorithm reduces average pickup time in simulation. How do you launch it?	Check simulation assumptions against real data (replay the current policy and confirm the simulator reproduces observed pickup times and late rates), then a switchback test across cities and time slots measuring delivery time, late rate, courier utilisation and earnings, cancellations; guardrails; gradual rollout. A user-level A/B test is wrong here, because treatment and control share couriers. Follow-up: switchback slots need a carryover buffer, since couriers dispatched in one slot are still busy in the next.	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] How do forecast errors translate into business cost, and how would you choose the loss?	Under-forecast causes late deliveries and lost orders; over-forecast causes idle courier pay or wasted stock. Estimate both costs, and choose the quantile q = c_u / (c_u + c_o) (the newsvendor result) as the forecast target. With c_u = €6 and c_o = €2 for a perishable item, q = 0.75. When the costs are in different units, as in courier staffing (euros per late order vs euros per courier-hour), convert through the decision: add couriers while the late orders each one avoids are worth more than its cost (45.6).	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] How would you decide delivery fees by zone and time?	Estimate elasticities with randomised experiments (fee jitter across slots, or switchbacks), never from observational data, where surge makes the fee a proxy for demand and the slope comes out with the wrong sign. Model demand and supply responses; optimise fees for marketplace-level objectives (orders delivered on time, contribution, fairness) under constraints; test with switchbacks; watch long-term retention, because users learn to avoid surge hours and couriers leave when earnings fall. Example: an elasticity of −0.30 means a 16.7% fee rise costs 5% of orders but raises fee revenue by 11%. When not to surge: emergencies, regulated markets, and new cities still building trust.	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] How many couriers do you need for 120 orders an hour, 25 minutes per order, 90% assigned within 5 minutes?	The offered load is 120 × 25/60 = 50 busy couriers on average, but staffing at 50 lets the queue grow without bound. Erlang C (M/M/c) gives P(wait) = C(c, a) and P(W > t) = C(c, a)·e^{−(cμ − λ)t}; at c = 56, C = 0.309 and P(W > 5 min) = 0.309 × e^{−1.2} = 0.093, so 56 meets the target and 55 (85.9%) does not. The assumptions are Poisson arrivals, exponential service and interchangeable couriers, all optimistic, so treat 56 as a starting point and calibrate with a simulator or observed service levels. Follow-up: pooling two such zones needs 107 rather than 112, because the buffer grows with √load.	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] How would you evaluate a new dispatch policy offline before a switchback?	Build a simulator that replays historical orders against a model of courier behaviour, and validate it twice: it must reproduce today's policy's observed outcomes by city and hour, and it should have predicted the direction of past switchback results. Then compare policies in the simulator on the same replayed demand (common random numbers reduce variance). The trade-off: simulators are weakest where the policy changes behaviour (courier acceptance, customer reactions to ETAs), so use them to filter candidates, not to decide.	chapter ch::45
[Ch 45] When would you use a time-series foundation model instead of GBDT?	For series with little history (new zones, new products), for many short series without useful covariates, and as a zero-shot baseline that costs nothing to try. GBDT usually wins when known covariates drive demand (weather, promotions, holidays), because the foundation model cannot see them. A practical setup uses the foundation model as a cold-start fallback and switches to the GBDT once a series has a few weeks of data.	chapter ch::45
[Ch 46] Why not rank every item with your best model? (M)	Latency and cost. Scoring a million items with a deep model per request is far beyond a 150 ms budget: at 1,700 QPS that is 1.7 billion scorings a second, against about 850,000 for 500 candidates. A cheap retrieval stage cuts to a few hundred candidates with high recall; the expensive model then only needs to be precise at the top. Follow-up: "what do you lose?" Items retrieval misses can never be ranked, so measure retrieval recall@500 separately from ranking NDCG.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] Your offline NDCG improved by 3%, but the A/B test is flat. Why? (M)	Common causes: the offline metric is computed on logged data that only contains items the old model showed, so it cannot judge new items; the offline label (click) differs from the online goal (order); a train/serve skew (a feature computed differently online); or the improvement sits in a segment too small to move the total. I would check feature parity first, then slice both results. Also check power: if the test could only detect a 2% change in orders and offline NDCG gains typically translate at a fraction of their size, "flat" may simply mean "too small to see".	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] How do you handle position bias in training data? (M)	Add the position as a feature during training and set it to a fixed value at serving time, so the model learns the item's quality separately from where it was shown. Alternatives: weight examples by the inverse of the estimated probability of being examined at that position, or collect unbiased data from a small randomised slice of traffic. The position-feature trick assumes the position effect is separable; a GBDT learns interactions between position and other features, so a shallow position tower (PAL-style, P(click) = P(seen | position) × P(click | seen)) trained jointly and dropped at serving is cleaner. Follow-up: IPS propensities themselves need randomised data, for example swapping adjacent results on 1% of traffic.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] How would you deal with cold start for a new restaurant? (M)	Use content features that exist from day one (cuisine, price, menu text embeddings, location), borrow signal from similar restaurants, and reserve a few exploration slots in the feed so the new restaurant collects interactions. A bandit can decide how much exposure to give as evidence comes in. Budget it: if a new restaurant needs about 1,000 impressions to estimate its click rate to within a couple of points, and the feed serves millions of impressions a day, a 1% exploration share is plenty.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] Pointwise, pairwise or listwise loss: which and why? (S)	Pointwise gives calibrated probabilities, which I need if I combine scores (P(click) × P(order)) or apply thresholds. Pairwise and listwise optimise order directly and usually do better on NDCG. In practice I often start pointwise with gradient boosting, then try LambdaRank and compare on NDCG and on the A/B test. The costs of listwise: scores are only meaningful within one request, so they cannot be multiplied by bids or compared across surfaces, and training needs query grouping and is slower. If I need both, I either calibrate the listwise output with isotonic regression on held-out data, or train a weighted sum of a listwise and a pointwise loss. When not to go listwise: when the score is an input to an auction or a threshold.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] How do you know the model isn't creating a feedback loop? (S)	Track catalogue coverage and the concentration of impressions over time (for example the share going to the top 1% of restaurants). Keep a small exploration share of traffic, and evaluate new models partly on that randomised data, which is free of the loop. A concrete alarm: if the top 1% of restaurants' share of impressions climbs from, say, 15% to 25% over a quarter with no change in their order share per impression, the ranker is amplifying itself. Follow-up: exploration costs a little short-term conversion; size it as the price of unbiased training data.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] When would you not use ML at all? (S)	When a rule captures most of the value, when there is too little data to beat it, when the decision must be fully explainable, or when the cost of maintenance outweighs the gain. I'd ship the rule first, log the data, and revisit when it stops scaling. Example: "sort by distance" for a city with 50 restaurants is hard to beat; at 5,000 restaurants and repeat users, personalisation starts to pay.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] How would you choose between real-time and batch inference? (M)	By how quickly the input changes and how much freshness is worth. Recommendations that depend on the current session or real-time availability need online scoring; a daily email or a nightly taxonomy classification can be batch. A common hybrid: batch-computed candidates, re-ranked online with session features. Batch is also cheaper: LLM batch APIs are often half price, and batch jobs run on spare capacity.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] How do you evaluate an LLM-based component before launch? (M)	Build a labelled eval set of a few hundred real cases, including hard ones; measure task metrics; for open-ended outputs, use an LLM judge that has been checked against human labels on a sample; then run an A/B test on the business metric. Error analysis by hand comes before and after. Size it: with 300 items at 80% accuracy the standard error is √(0.8 × 0.2 / 300) ≈ 2.3 points, so the eval can separate prompts about 6–7 points apart but not 2; compare prompts on the same items with a paired test to do better.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] Your LLM pipeline costs too much. What do you do? (S)	Measure where tokens go first. Then, in rough order of effort: shorten prompts and outputs, cache repeated calls, use batch APIs, route easy cases to a smaller model, and finally distil into a small fine-tuned model trained on the large model's outputs. Re-check quality on the eval set at each step. Typical sizes of each lever: batch APIs about 50%; prompt caching of a long shared prefix cuts input cost by most of the cached part; routing most traffic to a model several times cheaper cuts cost by a similar factor on that share; distillation can cut cost by an order of magnitude. When not to distil: when the task or its inputs shift often (new categories every month), because each shift needs a new labelled set and a retrain, while a prompted large model adapts with a prompt edit.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] A key feature is computed differently in training and serving. How would you catch it? (S)	Log the features actually used at serving time and compare their distributions with the training features (train/serve skew checks). Better still, compute features once in a feature store used by both paths, so the logic cannot diverge. The sharpest test: for a sample of logged requests, recompute the features with the training pipeline and diff them value by value; any feature whose values disagree on more than a fraction of a percent of rows is a bug.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] What would you build first, with a team of two and three months? (S)	A baseline with good logging: a simple retrieval (popularity plus "ordered before"), a gradient-boosted ranker on solid features, an offline evaluation pipeline and an A/B framework. Deep models and LLM components come after the data and evaluation loop works, because without them I cannot tell whether anything better is actually better. The trade-off is explicit: I am giving up maybe a few percent of eventual quality for a system I can measure and iterate on within the quarter.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] Your model was trained with negatives downsampled to 10%. How do you get calibrated probabilities? (M)	The model's odds are inflated by 1/w, so map each prediction q to p = q / (q + (1 − q)/w). With w = 0.1, q = 0.2 becomes 0.2 / 8.2 ≈ 2.4%. The correction is monotone, so it does not change rankings, only the scale, which matters for auctions, products of probabilities and thresholds. Follow-up: verify on a holdout that was not downsampled, because the formula assumes negatives were dropped uniformly at random.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] Size the GPUs to serve a 70B model at 10 million requests a day. (S)	Start from tokens: 10M × 300 output tokens / 86,400 ≈ 35,000 tokens a second, 70,000 at a 2× peak, and five times that in input tokens. Memory: 140 GB of bf16 weights, so four H100s per replica with about 148 GB left for KV cache at 0.33 MB per token, about 450,000 tokens. Decode is bandwidth-bound: reading 140 GB at 4 × 3.35 TB/s takes 10.4 ms per step, plus KV reads, so about 15 ms per step at batch 64 and roughly 2,500 tokens a second per replica in practice; about 28 replicas for decode plus about 100 GPUs for prefill, around 210 GPUs at peak. Trade-off: bigger batches cut cost but raise TPOT. Follow-up: FP8 weights halve the weight reads, and prefix caching removes the prefill of shared system prompts.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] Why train a 7B model on 49T tokens when the compute-optimal size is much larger? (S)	Because training compute is paid once and inference compute is paid per token served. The budget of 2 × 10²⁴ FLOPs is Chinchilla-optimal for about 130B parameters on 2.6T tokens, but a 130B model costs nearly 20 times as much per token to serve. If the model will serve billions of tokens a day, over-training a small model wins on total cost. The risks: 49T tokens is about 7,000 per parameter, which exceeds the supply of high-quality unique data (about 3 epochs over 15T), and returns diminish. The alternative to raise: train a larger model and distil it into 7B. Follow-up: "how would you decide?" Compare the 7B's loss at 49T tokens with the distilled student's on the same evaluations, using small-scale proxies before committing.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] How do you set the weights in a multi-objective ranking score? (S)	They encode business trade-offs, so tune them online: run a grid or a bandit over weight settings on a small share of traffic, plot the resulting orders, revenue and guardrail metrics, and choose from the Pareto front. Keep the heads calibrated, or a retrained head silently changes what the weights mean. Short tests favour engagement-heavy settings whose costs (retention, partner churn) appear later, so confirm the chosen setting with a long-running holdout.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 46] How often should a 2,000-GPU training run checkpoint? (S)	Use the Young/Daly rule, interval ≈ √(2 × MTBF × C), where C is the time training stalls per checkpoint. Scaling Llama 3's reported 419 interruptions in 54 days on 16,384 GPUs to 2,000 GPUs gives about one failure a day (MTBF about 25 hours); with a 1-minute asynchronous checkpoint the interval is √(2 × 1,520 × 1) ≈ 55 minutes and the total loss about 4% of the run. Cheaper checkpoints allow shorter intervals: at 15 seconds the loss drops to 2.5%. Follow-up: the restart time often dominates, so fast node replacement and data-loader resumption matter as much as checkpoint speed.	chapter ch::46
[Ch 47] What is training–serving skew and how does a feature store help?	Skew is a feature computed differently in training and in production: the training SQL uses a 7-day window and the online service a rolling 168 hours, or missing values are filled with 0 in one and the mean in the other. Offline metrics look good and online results disappoint, with no error anywhere. A feature store keeps one definition per feature and materialises it twice, offline with point-in-time lookups for training and online in a low-latency store for serving, so the logic cannot diverge. Without one, log the exact features used at prediction time and train on those logs. Follow-up: "how would you detect skew today?" Recompute a sample of logged requests' features with the training pipeline and diff them value by value.	chapter ch::47
[Ch 47] Explain shadow, canary and A/B deployments.	Shadow sends a copy of live traffic to the new model and logs its outputs without showing them: it catches crashes, latency and odd output distributions at zero user risk, but says nothing about user behaviour. A canary serves 1–5% of real traffic with automatic rollback rules: it catches errors and guardrail breaks on real users early, at small risk. An A/B test is a randomised split run long enough to measure the business metric causally. They answer different questions, so a release climbs all three. Number to know: at 5% canary traffic, detecting a rise in error rate from 0.13% to 0.23% takes about 24,000 canary requests, so small regressions need hours of traffic, not minutes.	chapter ch::47
[Ch 47] What is PSI and how do you read it?	The population stability index bins a feature by training-data deciles and sums (aᵢ − eᵢ)·ln(aᵢ/eᵢ) over bins, where eᵢ and aᵢ are the training and live shares; it equals the symmetric KL divergence between the binned distributions. Below 0.1 is stable, 0.1–0.25 worth watching, above 0.25 worth investigating; a mean shift of half a standard deviation reaches about 0.25. It measures effect size, which is why it works as an alert where p-values fail (a KS test calls a harmless shift significant at p = 10⁻²⁷ with 5,000 rows). Caveats: it depends on the bin count and on the clip for empty bins, and it needs a few hundred rows per window to sit well below 0.1 without any shift.	chapter ch::47
[Ch 47] What would you log with every prediction?	A request id, timestamp, model version and prompt or config version; the exact feature values (or a hash plus a sample of full values); the prediction, its score or probability, and any threshold applied; latency; and, for LLM calls, tokens, cost and retrieved document ids. Later, join the outcome label by request id. This log is the best training set (it has the serving-time features, so no skew), the first debugging tool and the input to drift monitoring. The trade-off is storage and privacy: sample full feature vectors if volume is huge, and redact personal data before it lands.	chapter ch::47
[Ch 47] Your ETA model's error jumped 20% this week. Walk through your investigation.	Check system health and recent deployments first (minutes): model, feature pipeline, upstream app releases. Then input pipelines (an hour): nulls, schema, a broken upstream feature, a new default value; PSI per feature points to the culprit fast. Then drift by feature and by city: is the error concentrated in one segment? Then external events: weather, a promotion, a new delivery mode. Then compare with the same season last year before calling it concept drift. Fix data bugs before retraining, because retraining on broken data learns the bug. The decision tree is ordered by likelihood and cost: most jumps are data bugs or deployments, and those are cheapest to rule out. Follow-up: "the inputs are clean and stable": then it is concept drift (the relation changed), so check recent labels by segment and retrain on recent data with a holdout from the new regime.	chapter ch::47
[Ch 47] Design the CI/CD process for a prompt-based classifier used by three teams.	Prompts as files in git with owners; a pinned model version; an evaluation set per team's use case with paired comparison against the current prompt and a tolerance the set can actually resolve (at 80% accuracy, a single 500-item run has a standard error near 2 points, so gate on the paired difference); a small must-pass set where any failure blocks; then shadow and canary with guardrails; traces tagged by prompt version; owners notified on change; rollback by flag. Run each eval item several times or at temperature 0 with repeats, because LLM evals are not deterministic and flaky gates get ignored. Follow-up: "a provider updates the model": re-run the full suite before switching the pin, and keep the old version as fallback.	chapter ch::47
[Ch 47] Labels arrive 30 days late. How do you monitor the model in the meantime?	Input and prediction drift as early warnings; proxy labels (early signals such as complaints or early repayment behaviour) whose relation to the real label you re-check monthly; a small fast-labelled random sample each week; and business metrics. Estimate performance without labels: if the model is calibrated, the expected accuracy of a batch is the mean confidence of its predictions (confidence-based estimation, valid under covariate shift but not concept drift); if only the class mix moved, black-box shift estimation recovers the new class proportions from the confusion matrix and the live prediction distribution. When the labels arrive, compare the estimates with the truth to learn how much to trust them next time.	chapter ch::47
[Ch 47] How would you decide when to retrain?	A schedule plus triggers: retrain when drift or performance crosses a threshold and the new model passes the gates. Choose the schedule from how fast performance decays: backtest by training on data up to week t and measuring error in weeks t + 1, t + 2, …, and retrain before the decay costs more than a training run. If a model loses 1% of its quality per week and retraining costs a few GPU-hours, weekly is cheap insurance; if it loses nothing for months, retraining only adds release risk. Never retrain automatically on data that failed validation.	chapter ch::47
[Ch 47] Why is re-running a z-test on a canary every five minutes a problem, and what do you do instead?	The z-test assumes one look at a pre-set sample size; checking repeatedly gives noise many chances to cross the threshold. In simulation, 25 looks at α = 1% roll back a healthy canary 8.3% of the time instead of 1%. Use a sequential test: Wald's SPRT accumulates the log-likelihood ratio of "regressed" vs "healthy" after each request and stops at pre-set bounds (ln((1 − β)/α) and ln(β/(1 − α))), or an always-valid p-value or confidence sequence comparing the two arms. These keep the false-rollback rate at α however often you look, and usually decide sooner: the SPRT caught a 0.42% error rate after about 3,900 canary requests, against 23,700 for the fixed test sized for a 0.23% rate.	chapter ch::47
[Ch 47] How large must an eval set be to gate a prompt change at a 2-point tolerance?	At 80% accuracy, two independent runs need about 6,300 items each for 80% power at α = 5%, because the standard error of a difference is √2 times that of each score. Pairing on the same items helps a lot: if the two prompts disagree on 10% of items, McNemar's test needs about 1,960. Most golden sets are a few hundred items, so either widen the tolerance to what the set can resolve (at 500 items, about 4 points paired with 10% discordance and 7 unpaired), grow the set, or gate on a smaller must-pass set and leave fine comparisons to online tests. Follow-up: sample each item a few times, because generation noise adds variance on top of item sampling.	chapter ch::47
[Ch 47] A 70B pretraining run shows a loss spike. What do you do?	First check whether it is recovering: many spikes are transient, so wait a pre-agreed budget of a few hundred steps while watching the gradient norm. If not, roll back to a checkpoint before the spike and skip the batches around it; if it recurs at the same data, inspect that data; if it recurs elsewhere, lower the learning rate or tighten clipping for a while. For frequent spikes, change the recipe for the next run: QK-norm and z-loss against attention-logit growth, a lower β₂ or a different ε for Adam. Log every intervention, because it is part of the model's provenance. Follow-up: "how do you tell a spike from a bug?" A bug (a corrupted shard, a bad node producing NaNs) reproduces deterministically at the same step after rollback.	chapter ch::47
[Ch 47] How often should a large training run checkpoint?	Balance checkpoint stalls against lost work: overhead per unit time is C/τ + τ/(2M), minimised at τ = √(2MC) (Young/Daly). With Llama 3's reported failure rate scaled to 2,000 GPUs, M ≈ 25 hours; with a 1-minute stall, τ ≈ 55 minutes and the overhead about 3.6% plus restart time. The overhead at the optimum is √(2C/M), so asynchronous checkpointing (smaller C) and fewer failures (larger M) both help, by square roots. Follow-up: retention: keep recent checkpoints densely for rollback, older ones sparsely for analysis, because storage for a large model's optimiser state adds up quickly.	chapter ch::47
[Ch 47] Your LLM-judge scores jumped 4 points overnight with no change to your system. What happened?	Most likely the judge changed: a provider updated the judge model behind an unpinned alias, or someone upgraded it. Other candidates: the judge prompt changed, the sampling temperature changed, or the eval data loader changed. Check the judge's version in the traces, re-score yesterday's outputs with today's judge (if the jump reproduces on old outputs, it is the judge), and compare both judges' agreement with human labels on a few hundred items. Then pin the judge, re-baseline the history, and add judge version to every score's metadata. Trade-off: a newer judge may be better aligned with humans; switch deliberately, not silently.	chapter ch::47
[Ch 48] Write SQL for each user's latest order and their order count in the last 30 days.	Two pieces joined on user: the latest order by ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY order_ts DESC, order_id DESC) = 1 (the second sort key breaks timestamp ties deterministically), and a 30-day count by COUNT() … WHERE order_ts >= datetime(:as_of, '-30 days') AND order_ts < :as_of GROUP BY user_id, left-joined so users with no recent orders get 0 through COALESCE. As of 2 March 2026 on the sample data, user 1's latest order is 4 with 3 orders in 30 days, user 2's is 6 with 1, and user 3's is 9 with 3, where orders 8 and 9 share a timestamp and the tie-breaker picks 9 (code/ch48_check.py has the full query). The traps interviewers watch for: a filter on the 30-day window applied before finding the latest order (users whose latest order is older disappear), an inner join that drops users with zero recent orders, and an "as of" date taken from now(), which makes the query unreproducible. Follow-up: "do it in one pass" with COUNT() FILTER (WHERE …) or a conditional SUM(CASE …) inside the ranked subquery.	chapter ch::48
[Ch 48] What is the difference between ROW_NUMBER, RANK and DENSE_RANK?	They differ only on ties. ROW_NUMBER gives every row a distinct number, breaking ties arbitrarily unless you add a tie-breaker. RANK gives tied rows the same rank and then skips. DENSE_RANK gives tied rows the same rank without skipping. For ratings 4.8, 4.6, 4.6, 4.5, 4.4: ROW_NUMBER gives 1, 2, 3, 4, 5; RANK gives 1, 2, 2, 4, 5; DENSE_RANK gives 1, 2, 2, 3, 4. Use ROW_NUMBER for "exactly one row per group", RANK for "top 3 including ties, as in a sports table", and DENSE_RANK for "the second-highest distinct value". Follow-up: ROW_NUMBER without a deterministic tie-breaker returns different rows on different runs, which makes training sets irreproducible.	chapter ch::48
[Ch 48] How would you sessionise clickstream events?	Order each user's events by time, flag an event as a new session when the gap from the previous event (from LAG(ts)) exceeds the threshold or there is no previous event, then take a running SUM of the flags per user: the running sum is the session id. With a 30-minute threshold, events at 10:00, 10:10, 11:00 and 11:05 form two sessions because of the 50-minute gap. The threshold is a modelling choice: check the distribution of gaps, which usually has a clear valley between within-session and between-session gaps. The same flag-then-cumulative-sum pattern solves streaks and other gaps-and-islands problems.	chapter ch::48
[Ch 48] What is Cohen's kappa and why not just use percentage agreement?	Kappa measures agreement beyond chance: κ = (p_o − p_e)/(1 − p_e), where p_e is the agreement expected if both annotators labelled at random with their own class rates. With imbalanced classes chance agreement is high: if both annotators label 80% of items positive, p_e = 0.8² + 0.2² = 0.68, so 90% raw agreement is only κ = 0.22/0.32 = 0.69. Rough reading: below 0.4 poor, 0.4–0.6 moderate, 0.6–0.8 substantial, above 0.8 strong. Use weighted kappa for ordinal scales and Fleiss' kappa or Krippendorff's alpha for more than two annotators. Follow-up: for rare classes, also report agreement on the positives themselves, which kappa can hide.	chapter ch::48
[Ch 48] Your model looked great offline and failed online. List the data causes you would check.	Leakage (future features, target-derived features), training-serving skew, different populations or time periods, label definition mismatch, missing values handled differently online. In order of how I would check them: first compare offline features with logged serving features for the same requests (skew shows up immediately); then audit the top features' availability times (leakage usually hides in the most important feature); then compare the offline test period and population with the live one (a random split over time, a city mix that changed); then confirm that the offline label means what the online metric measures (a click label for an order goal). A tell-tale number: an offline AUC far above anything the team has seen before (0.95 where 0.75 was normal) is more often leakage than genius.	chapter ch::48
[Ch 48] Explain point-in-time correctness and how you would guarantee it in a feature pipeline.	Every training row may use only feature values that were available at its prediction moment. Store features with valid-from timestamps and the time each value became available (a daily table built at 03:00 is not available at 01:00), and join with an as-of join on prediction time (merge_asof, ASOF JOIN, or a feature store's point-in-time lookup). To test it: assert feature_available_ts ≤ prediction_ts on every training row; recompute a random sample of features from raw logs using only earlier events and diff them against the training table; compare offline features with the values logged at serving time for the same requests; and investigate any feature whose importance is suspiciously high. Shuffling timestamps and watching accuracy is not a valid leakage test: a leaky feature still leaks after the shuffle. Follow-up: ties at the prediction timestamp need an explicit rule (strictly before, or before with a lag).	chapter ch::48
[Ch 48] A Spark job joining orders to restaurants takes three hours. What do you look at?	The query plan and the stage timeline: skew on hot keys, a missing broadcast of the small restaurants table, too few or too many partitions, Python UDFs, reading unpartitioned data. Concretely: 100,000 restaurants at a few hundred bytes each is tens of megabytes, small enough to broadcast, which removes the shuffle of the large orders table entirely. If one task runs for hours while the rest finish in minutes, it is skew (one giant city or chain): salt the key, or let adaptive query execution split the skewed partition. If the job reads every date to use one week, add a partition filter. Follow-up: check whether the job needs Spark at all; a week of orders may fit in DuckDB on one machine.	chapter ch::48
[Ch 48] Design a labelling process for 300 food attributes across 2M menu items in Arabic and English.	Guidelines and a clear attribute taxonomy; an LLM first pass with confidence; human review of a stratified sample and low-confidence items by bilingual annotators; kappa per attribute; adjudication; active learning to pick the next batch; a frozen gold set for evaluation. The budget forces the design: 2M items × 300 attributes is 600M decisions, impossible by hand, so humans can only calibrate and correct. Measure the LLM against adjudicated human labels per attribute and per language, accept it where it matches human agreement, and route the rest. Keep the gold set random and separate from the active-learning batches, which are biased towards hard items by design. Follow-up: attributes such as "spicy" are partly subjective, so expect lower kappa and decide whether a probabilistic label is acceptable.	chapter ch::48
[Ch 48] How would you collect preference data for a reward model, and how good can the reward model get?	Pairwise comparisons of two responses to the same prompt, shown in random order, on a graded scale, with a rubric that puts correctness before helpfulness and style, plus gold items to track each rater and regular calibration sessions. Trained raters agree on roughly 60–75% of open-ended comparisons (InstructGPT reported about 73%). If two independent raters each match the true preference with probability q, agreement is q² + (1 − q)², so 70% agreement implies q ≈ 0.82: a perfect model of the consensus would still score only about 82% against single-rater labels. So judge reward-model accuracy against that ceiling, evaluate on multiply-labelled comparisons, and drop or down-weight near-ties. Follow-up: length bias, since raters without a rubric prefer longer answers and the reward model learns it.	chapter ch::48
[Ch 48] Write the cohort-retention query.	Find each user's first month as their cohort; for every order compute the month offset from the cohort using year and month (not days / 30); count distinct users per (cohort, offset); divide by the cohort size from the first-order table. On the chapter's data, the January cohort of 3 retains 0.67 in months 1 and 2, and the February cohort of 2 retains 0.50 in month 1. The traps: counting orders instead of distinct users, and taking the denominator from the activity table, which goes wrong when the cohort is defined by sign-up. Follow-up: the newest cohorts have not reached the later offsets, so plot the triangle rather than averaging across cohorts.	chapter ch::48
[Ch 48] When is synthetic data safe to train on?	When it is filtered by a check that does not share the generator's blind spots: a verifier for maths and code, tests, or agreement with a source document, and when real data stays in the mix. Rejection sampling with a verifier (generate eight candidates, keep those that pass) can produce near-guaranteed-correct reasoning traces; filtering with an LLM judge is weaker, because the generator and the judge can make correlated errors. Training repeatedly on a model's own outputs loses the tails of the distribution (model collapse), so accumulate rather than replace real data. Never evaluate on data from the same generator, and check the provider's terms before training on its outputs.	chapter ch::48
[Diagnostic 1] A fraud detector has 95% recall and a 1% false-positive rate. Fraud is 0.1% of transactions. What fraction of flagged transactions are fraud?	Bayes: true positives 0.001 × 0.95 = 0.00095; false positives 0.999 × 0.01 = 0.00999; precision 0.00095 / 0.01094 ≈ 8.7%. At low prevalence, precision is dominated by the false-positive rate; report precision at the real base rate, not on a balanced test set (Ch 4).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 2] Training with cross-entropy minimises a KL divergence. Which one, in which direction, and what does that direction do to the model's behaviour? Where does the other direction appear in LLM training?	Cross-entropy on data = H(p_data) + KL(p_data ‖ p_model); the entropy is fixed, so training minimises forward KL. Forward KL is mode-covering: the model must put mass wherever the data does, including unlikely corners. Reverse KL (mode-seeking) appears as the KL penalty in RLHF and in on-policy distillation (Ch 1, 18).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 3] A colleague checks an A/B test every day and stops when p < 0.05. What goes wrong, roughly how badly, and what should they do instead?	Repeated peeking inflates the false-positive rate well above 5%: an A/A test checked daily for 20 days reaches "significance" about 25% of the time. Fix: a pre-registered sample size from a power analysis and one look at the end, or a sequential method designed for peeking (alpha spending, always-valid p-values) (Ch 2). Partial credit: a 1 for "peeking inflates false positives" without a remedy, or a remedy without saying why peeking is wrong.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 4] On the same 500 test items, model A scores 82% and model B 84%. Is B better? How would you decide?	Standard error of each accuracy ≈ 1.7 points; an unpaired difference has SE ≈ 2.4 points, so 2 points is within noise. Because both models see the same items, use a paired test (McNemar, or a paired bootstrap over items), which depends only on items where they disagree and has lower variance. If still unclear, collect more items or look at slices (Ch 2, 23). Partial credit: a 1 for computing the standard error but not using a paired test, or for "use McNemar" with no numbers.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 5] A model has 0.97 validation AUC and performs badly in production. List the causes you would check, in order.	In order: leakage (features from after the prediction time, target leakage, duplicates or the same users across splits, a random split where a time split was needed); training–serving skew (features computed differently online); a label definition that differs from the production outcome; distribution shift since the data was collected; and the metric itself (AUC fine, but the operating threshold or calibration wrong) (Ch 3, 47). Partial credit: a 1 for naming leakage but not training–serving skew and label mismatch; ordering matters, because leakage is both the most common and the cheapest to check.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 6] A 10-class classifier's loss at step 0 is 7.5. What should it be, and what does 7.5 suggest?	About ln 10 ≈ 2.30. A loss of 7.5 means the initial logits are far too large or confident: wrong output-layer initialisation, a missing or double softmax, labels misaligned or out of range. Fix the start before tuning anything (Ch 5).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 7] Write the AdamW update. Why is its weight decay "decoupled", and why does that matter?	m ← β₁m + (1 − β₁)g; v ← β₂v + (1 − β₂)g²; bias-correct; θ ← θ − η(m̂/(√v̂ + ε) + λθ). In Adam with L2 the decay term passes through the adaptive scaling, so weights with large gradient variance are barely regularised; AdamW applies decay directly to the weights, so it acts uniformly and is tunable independently of the learning rate (Ch 7).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 8] Why does Adam need learning-rate warm-up? What does a warmup–stable–decay (WSD) schedule give you that cosine does not?	Early second-moment estimates are poor and small, so the first steps are large and noisy; warm-up keeps them small until v is reliable. WSD holds the learning rate constant and decays only at the end, so any checkpoint in the stable phase can be decayed into a finished model: you can extend runs and branch experiments without retraining (Ch 7).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 9] Why is bf16 preferred to fp16 for training, and why keep fp32 master weights anyway?	bf16 has fp32's exponent range, so gradients do not overflow or underflow and no loss scaling is needed; fp16 has more mantissa but a narrow range. bf16 has only 8 bits of precision, so small updates to weights near 1 round away; fp32 master weights accumulate them (Ch 7).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 10] You double the width of a model under the standard parametrisation and keep the learning rate. What happens, and how does µP change it?	Under SP, each Adam step changes the output roughly in proportion to width, so the learning rate tuned at the smaller width becomes too large and training can diverge; every width needs its own sweep. µP scales initialisation and per-layer learning rates (hidden-matrix learning rates ∝ 1/width) so the best learning rate transfers across widths (Ch 7).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 11] Estimate the KV cache for one 8,192-token sequence: 32 layers, 8 KV heads, head dimension 128, bf16. How do GQA and MLA change it?	2 (K and V) × 32 × 8 × 128 × 8,192 × 2 bytes ≈ 1.07 GB. With 32 full heads (MHA) it would be 4× larger; GQA shares KV heads across query heads; MLA caches a small latent per token instead of keys and values, a further several-fold cut (Ch 10, 11, 21).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 12] Why is the attention score divided by √d_k?	With unit-variance components, q·k has variance d_k; unscaled scores grow with dimension, the softmax saturates towards one-hot and its gradients vanish. Dividing by √d_k keeps the variance near 1 (Ch 10).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 13] How does RoPE encode position? Why does a model trained at 8k tokens degrade at 32k, and what are two fixes?	RoPE rotates pairs of query and key dimensions by angles proportional to position, so q·k depends only on relative distance. Beyond the training length, low-frequency dimensions reach rotation angles never seen in training. Fixes: position interpolation, NTK-aware scaling, YaRN, followed by a short fine-tune on long sequences (Ch 10).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 14] You have 1e23 training FLOPs. Using Chinchilla's rule of thumb, what model size and token count are compute-optimal? Why do most open models train far smaller models on far more tokens?	C ≈ 6ND with D ≈ 20N gives C ≈ 120N², so N ≈ √(1e23/120) ≈ 29B parameters and D ≈ 580B tokens. Open models train smaller models far longer because lifetime inference cost dominates: a smaller model trained past compute-optimal is cheaper to serve at the same quality (Ch 13).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 15] A mixture-of-experts layer has 64 experts with top-2 routing. What scales with the number of experts and what with k? Why does it need load balancing, and how is it done?	Memory (parameters) scales with the number of experts; compute per token scales with k (2 experts' FFNs). Without balancing, the router collapses onto a few experts, which then overload while others go untrained. Fixes: an auxiliary load-balancing loss (Switch: E · Σ f_e P_e), capacity limits, or auxiliary-loss-free per-expert bias terms (DeepSeek-V3) (Ch 11).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 41] The same paragraph costs 25 tokens in English and 60 tokens in another language with your tokeniser. Why, and what are the consequences for cost, context and quality? What would you change at the tokeniser level? (Chapter 9)	BPE merges are learned from a training corpus, mostly English, so frequent English words become single tokens while words in under-represented scripts are split into short pieces or bytes: here 60/25 = 2.4 tokens for every English one. Consequences: the same request costs 2.4 times as much and takes longer to generate; the effective context window is 2.4 times shorter; each word takes more prediction steps, which tends to lower quality; and users of that language pay more for the same service. Fixes at the tokeniser level: train it on a language-balanced corpus, enlarge the vocabulary (at the cost of a larger embedding and output layer), and track fertility (tokens per word) per language as a release metric; for an existing model, add vocabulary with new embeddings initialised from the pieces they replace, followed by continued pretraining (Ch 9). Partial credit: a 1 for the frequency explanation and one consequence; a 2 needs cost, context and quality, and a concrete fix.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 42] Write CLIP's training loss for a batch of N image–text pairs. Why does batch size matter so much, and what does SigLIP change? (Chapter 14)	With unit-norm image and text embeddings, S_ij = cos(image_i, text_j)/τ for a learned temperature τ. The loss is the average of two cross-entropies with the diagonal as the targets: softmax over each row (image to text) and over each column (text to image). Each pair's negatives are the other N − 1 items in the batch, so a larger batch gives more and harder negatives, which is why CLIP used batches of 32,768 and why the N × N matrix must be gathered across devices. SigLIP treats every image–text pair as an independent binary classification with a sigmoid (label 1 on the diagonal, 0 elsewhere, with a learned temperature and bias), so there is no normalisation over the batch: it is cheaper in memory and communication and works well at smaller batch sizes (Ch 14). Partial credit: a 1 for one direction of the cross-entropy only, or for the loss without the batch-size argument.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 16] How much GPU memory does training a 7B-parameter model with Adam in mixed precision need, excluding activations? How does ZeRO-3 on 8 GPUs change that?	About 16 bytes per parameter (bf16 weights and gradients, fp32 master weights and two Adam moments): 7e9 × 16 ≈ 112 GB, more than one 80 GB GPU. ZeRO-3 shards all of it across the 8 GPUs, ≈ 14 GB each, at the cost of gathering weights layer by layer (Ch 17).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 17] Estimate the time to train a 7B model on 2T tokens on 256 H100s (989 TFLOP/s dense bf16 peak) at 40% MFU.	6ND = 6 × 7e9 × 2e12 = 8.4e22 FLOPs. Throughput 256 × 989e12 × 0.4 ≈ 1.0e17 FLOP/s. Time ≈ 8.3e5 s ≈ 9.6 days, plus restarts and evaluation (Ch 17).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 18] How does MinHash deduplication find near-duplicate documents, and why does deduplication improve models?	Split each document into shingles (word n-grams), hash with many hash functions and keep the minimum per function; the share of matching minima estimates Jaccard similarity. Locality-sensitive hashing (bands) finds candidate pairs without comparing every pair. Duplicates waste compute, raise memorisation and distort the data mix (Ch 16). Partial credit: a 1 for the MinHash mechanism without banding (LSH), or without a reason deduplication helps.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 19] Data, tensor and pipeline parallelism: what does each split, what does each communicate, and where in a cluster does each run?	Data parallelism replicates the model and splits the batch, all-reducing gradients (any network, across nodes). Tensor parallelism splits each layer's matrices, all-reducing activations in every layer (needs NVLink, inside a node). Pipeline parallelism splits the layers into stages and sends activations between them (tolerates slower links; costs a pipeline bubble). Large runs combine all three, often with ZeRO/FSDP sharding on the data-parallel axis (Ch 17). Partial credit: a 1 if one of the three is wrong about what it communicates or where it runs.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 20] A new model scores far above its predecessor on a public benchmark. How would you check for contamination?	n-gram or embedding overlap between the training data and the test set; compare performance on the public set against a freshly written or rephrased set of equal difficulty; test items released after the training cutoff; canary strings; check whether the model can complete test questions verbatim. A large gap between original and rephrased items is the strongest evidence (Ch 16, 23). Partial credit: a 1 for overlap checks only; a 2 needs a behavioural test (rephrased or post-cutoff items).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 21] Derive the policy gradient for J(θ) = E_(y∼π_θ)[R(y)]. Why does subtracting a baseline leave it unbiased?	∇J = Σ_y ∇π(y) R(y) = Σ_y π(y) ∇log π(y) R(y) = E[R(y) ∇log π(y)]. For any b independent of y, E[b ∇log π(y)] = b ∇Σ_y π(y) = b ∇1 = 0, so subtracting b changes the variance, not the mean (Ch 18.4).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 22] Derive the DPO loss from the KL-regularised RL objective in a few lines.	The KL-regularised optimum is π*(y) = π_ref(y) exp(r(y)/β)/Z. Solve for r: r = β log(π*/π_ref) + β log Z. Substitute into the Bradley–Terry likelihood; Z cancels in the difference, leaving −log σ(β[log(π(y_w)/π_ref(y_w)) − log(π(y_l)/π_ref(y_l))]) (Ch 18.6).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 23] A GRPO group of four responses gets rewards [1, 0, 0, 0]. What are the advantages? What happens when all four rewards are equal, and why does it matter for the prompt set?	Mean 0.25, sample standard deviation 0.5: advantages +1.5, −0.5, −0.5, −0.5 (+1.73 and −0.58 with the population std). If all rewards are equal, every advantage is zero and the prompt gives no gradient. Prompts the policy always or never solves waste generation: filter by pass rate and resample (Ch 19).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 24] What is reward over-optimisation? How would you detect it and limit it?	Optimising a learned reward past the point where true quality improves: proxy reward keeps rising while human or gold-model ratings fall (length, flattery, format tricks). Detect with a held-out gold judge or human evaluation plotted against KL from the reference. Limit with a KL penalty or KL budget, early stopping, reward-model ensembles, length control and fresh preference data on the current policy (Ch 18.5). Partial credit: a 1 for the definition and a KL penalty without a way to detect it (a gold judge or human ratings plotted against KL).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 25] How many trainable parameters does a rank-16 LoRA add to a 4096 × 4096 weight matrix? Why does such a small update work, and what does QLoRA add?	2 × 4,096 × 16 = 131,072, about 0.8% of the matrix's 16.8M. Fine-tuning updates have low intrinsic rank, so a low-rank update captures most of the change. QLoRA keeps the frozen base in 4-bit (NF4) and trains LoRA adapters in higher precision, so large models fit on one GPU (Ch 20).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 26] Why is the prefill phase compute-bound and the decode phase memory-bandwidth-bound? What does that imply for batching?	Prefill processes many tokens per weight read (high arithmetic intensity, matrix–matrix work). Decode generates one token per sequence per step, so every step reads all weights and the KV cache for little compute (matrix–vector work). Batching many sequences in decode raises arithmetic intensity, which is why continuous batching and KV-cache memory set serving throughput (Ch 21).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 27] How does speculative decoding produce exactly the target model's distribution? When does it stop helping?	A small draft model proposes several tokens; the target scores them in one pass; each is accepted with probability min(1, p_target/p_draft), and on rejection a token is resampled from the normalised residual max(0, p_target − p_draft). The result is exactly the target's distribution. It stops helping when acceptance is low (a poor draft) or when the system is already compute-bound at large batch sizes (Ch 21).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 28] When does majority voting over sampled answers (self-consistency) make accuracy worse?	When a single wrong answer is more probable than the right one, voting amplifies it: with 30% correct and one wrong answer taking 70%, majority over 25 samples is right about 2% of the time. Voting also needs answers that can be compared for equality (Ch 19.8).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 29] You want an LLM judge to score answers in your application. How do you show the judge can be trusted?	Label a sample (a few hundred items) with expert humans; measure judge–human agreement (accuracy or Cohen's kappa) against human–human agreement; check known biases (position, length, self-preference) with swapped and controlled pairs; fix the rubric and prompt version; re-validate when the model or task changes; report judge scores with confidence intervals (Ch 24). Partial credit: a 1 for "compare with human labels" without chance-corrected agreement (kappa) and a human–human ceiling, or without any bias check; a 2 needs agreement against a ceiling, at least two bias controls, and revalidation when the judge or task changes.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 30] An agent succeeds at each step with probability 0.95. What is its success rate on a 20-step task, and what does that imply for how you evaluate and design agents?	0.95²⁰ ≈ 36%. Errors compound with length, so long tasks need checkpoints, verification and recovery steps; evaluate on end-to-end success and on reliability across repeated runs (pass^k), not single-step accuracy (Ch 25, 33).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 31] A linear probe recovers "is this sentence negated" from layer 12 with 95% accuracy. What does that show and not show? How would activation patching add to it?	The information is linearly decodable at layer 12, not that the model uses it: probes can pick up correlates. Activation patching swaps that activation between runs that differ only in negation and measures the effect on the output, which tests whether the representation is causally used (Ch 26). Partial credit: a 1 if you say the probe shows the model uses negation, or describe patching without what it is compared with.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 32] What is an induction head, and what two components form the circuit?	A head that, at token A, attends to the token after the previous occurrence of A and copies it (… A B … A → B). It needs a previous-token head in an earlier layer (writing "the token before me was A" into each position) and the induction head that matches on that information through K-composition (Ch 26).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 33] When does BM25 beat dense retrieval and vice versa? How would you combine them?	BM25 wins on exact terms, rare names, codes and out-of-domain text; dense retrieval wins on paraphrase and semantic matches. Run both and fuse the ranked lists (reciprocal rank fusion), then re-rank the top candidates with a cross-encoder; evaluate with recall@k on your own queries (Ch 30).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 34] A RAG system answers a question wrongly although the right document is in the index. In what order do you debug?	Check retrieval first: is the right chunk in the top k (recall@k)? If not: chunking split the answer, the query needs rewriting, a metadata filter excluded it, or the embedder misses the vocabulary. If it was retrieved: was it in the context sent to the model (truncation, ordering)? If so, a generation problem: the prompt, conflicting chunks, or the model ignoring context. Fix the earliest failing stage (Ch 31). Partial credit: a 1 for checking retrieval only, or for listing causes without the order (retrieval, then context assembly, then generation).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 35] An agent reads a user's emails and can send messages. How would you defend against indirect prompt injection?	Treat everything from email as data, never instructions; give the agent least privilege (read-only by default, scoped tools); require human confirmation for consequential actions such as sending; restrict where data can flow (no arbitrary URLs, allow-listed recipients); separate the model that reads untrusted content from the one that can act; log and red-team with injection attempts (Ch 22, 28, 33). Partial credit: a 1 for prompt-level defences only ("tell the model to ignore instructions in emails"); a 2 needs at least one architectural control: least privilege, confirmation for sending, or separating the reader from the actor.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 36] A new ranker improves offline NDCG by 3%, but the A/B test is flat. Give the likely reasons, in order.	Offline labels are biased by the old ranker's exposure (position bias, never-shown items); the offline metric does not match the online objective; the gain sits in positions or queries that carry little traffic; training–serving skew or latency regressions; too little power to detect the effect; novelty or interference effects. Check skew and power first, then whether offline and online metrics agree historically (Ch 43, 44). Partial credit: a 1 for listing reasons without saying which to check first, or for missing exposure (position and selection) bias; a 2 orders the checks, cheapest and most likely first (skew and power), and names offline–online metric mismatch.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 37] Why do large recommender systems use separate retrieval and ranking stages? What model is typical for each?	Scoring every item with a heavy model is too slow at millions of items. Retrieval (two-tower embeddings with approximate nearest-neighbour search, plus other candidate sources) narrows to hundreds or thousands with high recall; a ranker (gradient-boosted trees or a deep cross network using user–item interaction features) orders them precisely; a final re-ranking layer applies business rules and diversity (Ch 43). Partial credit: a 1 for the funnel without a typical model at each stage, or without the reason (latency over a large catalogue).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 38] What is a point-in-time join, and what goes wrong in training data without one?	Joining each training label to feature values as they were known at the label's timestamp, not as they are now. Without it, features computed later leak the future into training (for example a restaurant's rating after the order you are predicting), giving inflated offline scores and a model that fails in production (Ch 48).	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 43] A feature's production distribution over three bins is 10% / 40% / 50%; at training time it was 30% / 40% / 30%. Compute the population stability index. What do you do next? (Chapter 47)	PSI = Σ (a − e) ln(a/e) = (0.1 − 0.3) ln(1/3) + 0 + (0.5 − 0.3) ln(5/3) = 0.220 + 0.102 ≈ 0.32, above the 0.25 "investigate" line. First rule out a pipeline change (an upstream schema or unit change, new default values, a broken join), since most sudden shifts are bugs. If the shift is real, check whether the model's predictions and its performance on recently labelled data have moved; retrain or recalibrate only if they have, and compare with the same season last year before calling it drift (Ch 47). Partial credit: a 1 for the right number with no plan, or a sound plan with an arithmetic slip.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 44] A product costs 2 to buy and sells for 5; unsold units are thrown away. Daily demand is roughly normal with mean 100 and standard deviation 20. How many units do you stock, and why not 100? (Chapter 45)	Each unit short loses the margin 5 − 2 = 3; each unit over loses its cost, 2. The newsvendor quantile is cost_under / (cost_under + cost_over) = 3/(3 + 2) = 0.6, and the 60th percentile of a normal with mean 100 and standard deviation 20 is 100 + 0.253 × 20 ≈ 105 units. Not 100, because running out costs more than throwing one away, so the optimum sits above the mean; if unsold units could be returned for most of their cost, it would sit higher still (Ch 45). Partial credit: a 1 for the right critical ratio with a slip in the quantile, or for "stock a bit above the mean" without the ratio.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 39] "Tell me about a time you were wrong." What separates a senior answer from a mid-level one?	A real, consequential mistake owned without blaming others; how you found out you were wrong (data, a review, a user); what you did to fix it and limit the damage; and what you changed afterwards in your process or the team's, with evidence it worked. Mid-level answers pick a trivial mistake or stop at the fix (Ch 38). Partial credit: a 1 for a real mistake with a fix but no change in process, or for a story whose "mistake" is really a strength; a 2 needs ownership, how you found out, the fix, and a lasting change with evidence it worked.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 40] A product manager asks for a classifier with 99% accuracy by next month. How do you respond?	Do not say yes or no to the number: ask what decision the classifier drives and the cost of each error type, which defines the metric (99% accuracy may be trivial at 1% prevalence, or impossible). Check data and label quality, build a quick baseline to see what is achievable, then propose a staged plan: a first version with an agreed precision–recall target and human review of uncertain cases, with a date to revisit (Ch 39). Partial credit: a 1 for asking about the decision and costs but not proposing a plan, or for a plan with no baseline or data check; a 2 reframes the metric (prevalence), checks data and labels, offers a staged plan with a review date, and does all of it without simply refusing.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 45] Your new method beats the baseline by 0.8 points on one seed. The baseline's seed-to-seed standard deviation is 0.5 points. What do you conclude, and what do you run next? (Chapter 34)	With one seed per arm, the difference between two runs has standard deviation 0.5 × √2 ≈ 0.71 points, so 0.8 is about one standard deviation: no evidence yet. Run at least five seeds of each, with the baseline tuned as hard as the method: the standard error of the difference then falls to 0.5 × √(2/5) ≈ 0.32, and a true 0.8-point gain would show as about 2.5 standard errors. Then check that the gain holds on a second evaluation and at a second scale, at matched compute (Ch 34). Partial credit: a 1 for "run more seeds" without the arithmetic, or for the arithmetic without a plan beyond seeds.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 46] You suspect a new pretraining-data filter improves reasoning. Design the smallest experiment that would convince a sceptical colleague. (Chapters 16, 34)	State the hypothesis and the metric before running: "the filter improves reasoning benchmarks X and Y at matched tokens". Train proxy models (for example 1B parameters on 25B tokens) with and without the filter, identical in everything else, and match the number of training tokens, since a filter that removes data changes how often the rest repeats. Use several seeds to measure the noise floor and evaluations that are above chance at that scale. Add a dose–response check (two or three filter thresholds) and confirm the winner at a second scale. Check that the filter does not simply select text that resembles the benchmarks (contamination by similarity), and report all evaluations, including any that got worse (Ch 16, 34). Partial credit: a 1 for a with/without comparison that misses seeds, matched tokens or the contamination check.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 47] Implement a numerically stable log-softmax over the rows of a 2-D array in NumPy, and say what goes wrong in the naive version. (Chapter 35)	Subtract each row's maximum before exponentiating: z = x - x.max(axis=1, keepdims=True); return z - np.log(np.exp(z).sum(axis=1, keepdims=True)). Subtracting a constant from a row leaves the softmax unchanged, and after it the largest exponent is exp(0) = 1, so nothing overflows. The naive version computes exp(x) directly: in float32 anything above about 88.7 overflows to infinity and the result becomes NaN, and very negative logits underflow to 0 so the log gives −∞. Test it: exp of each output row sums to 1, adding 1,000 to a row leaves the output unchanged, and it matches scipy.special.log_softmax (Ch 35). Partial credit: a 1 for the max-subtraction trick without explaining why it is valid or without tests.	diagnostic
[Diagnostic 48] A language model's training loss sits at ln(vocabulary size) from step 0 and never moves. Which bugs do you check, in what order, and with what quick test? (Chapters 6, 35)	ln(vocabulary size) is the correct loss for a uniform prediction at initialisation, so the model is not learning at all. Check, in order: the optimiser does not update the weights (optimizer.step() missing, zero_grad() called after backward, parameters not passed to the optimiser, or the forward pass run under torch.no_grad() or on detached tensors); the learning rate is effectively zero (a warm-up that starts at 0 and lasts the whole run, or a scheduler stepped wrongly); the labels are wrong (not shifted by one for next-token prediction, or padding and masking that leave almost nothing to predict); and the data loader returns the same or empty batch every step. The quick test: try to overfit a single batch for 100 steps, print the gradient norm, and check that one weight tensor actually changes between steps (Ch 6, 35). Partial credit: a 1 for two or three plausible bugs without the one-batch overfit test.	diagnostic
